# Research Bank — Chapter 25: Rights and Movements (`rights-movements`) — PARKED MATERIAL ONLY

Not yet researched as a chapter. This file holds verified material parked by other chapters' agents. (Note: registry ruling 9 — `research-ch17-prejudice.md` splits into slavery-freedom and this file when slavery-freedom is researched; this file starts the collection.)

## Parked from `native-nations` (2026-08-07) — Native rights organizing that belongs in the movements story
- **Elizabeth Peratrovich (Tlingit, Lukaax.ádi clan; 1911–1958)** — her testimony before the Alaska Territorial Legislature is credited as decisive in passing the **Alaska Anti-Discrimination Act, approved February 16, 1945** — the first anti-discrimination law in the U.S. or its territories (signs like "No Natives Allowed" were the target). Alaska marks Elizabeth Peratrovich Day each February 16. Context: the Alaska Native Brotherhood (1912, Sitka) and Sisterhood (1915) were the only civil-rights organizations for Alaska Natives for half a century. (Sources: Alaska State Archives "Anti-discrimination Act of 1945"; LOC In Custodia Legis; EBSCO on ANB/ANS.)
- **The fish-ins (1960s–70s, Washington State)** as a civil-rights-era tactic: deliberate arrestable acts (fishing under treaty right), celebrity support, court strategy — Billy Frank Jr. (Nisqually) arrested 50+ times from age 14 at Frank's Landing; ended in the **Boldt decision (U.S. v. Washington, February 12, 1974)**: tribes entitled to half the harvestable salmon, affirmed by the Supreme Court 1979. Presidential Medal of Freedom to Frank posthumously, 2015. `native-nations` features Frank; this chapter can tell the fish-ins inside the era's protest wave. (Sources: HistoryLink; NWPB/Cascade PBS Boldt-50 coverage.)
- **First national Native-run rights organizations:** Society of American Indians (October 12, 1911, Columbus, Ohio — first national rights organization run by Native people; Charles Eastman, Carlos Montezuma; Zitkala-Ša edited its journal); National Council of American Indians (1926, Zitkala-Ša); National Congress of American Indians (November 16, 1944, Denver — 80 delegates, 50+ tribes; still active). (Sources: EBSCO "Society of American Indians"; NCAI official history; National Women's History Museum on Zitkala-Ša.)
- **Red Power events** (Alcatraz 1969–71; Wounded Knee 1973; AIM 1968) — `native-nations` LEADS on these as the nations' politics; this chapter gets at most the movement-wave framing (how Red Power sat alongside civil rights, Chicano, and women's movements). Facts and sources in `research/research-native-nations.md` era 9.
- **ICWA (1978)** as a rights win against mass removal of Native children, upheld in *Haaland v. Brackeen* (June 15, 2023, 7–2). (Sources: NICWA; Supreme Court 21-376.)

## Relocated from the legacy `research-ch17-prejudice.md` (registry ruling 9; split done 2026-08-07 by the slavery-freedom agent)
The old prejudice chapter split three ways: slavery-as-system → `research-slavery-freedom.md`; the material below (rights denied and won back — exclusion-era injustice, and rights-recognition threads) → here; the Radium Girls' fight → `research-work-workers.md` (+ note in `research-health.md`). Native-injustice items went to `research-native-nations.md`; the "Okies" reception item to `research-migration.md`. Facts below were source-checked by the immigration/migration agents (2026-07-23); the arrival/journey sides stay in those chapters.

### Race written into law, and unwritten (this chapter's arc: winning back what the law denied)
- **Race written into citizenship:** the Naturalization Act of 1790 limited naturalization to a "free white person." The racial bar on naturalization was not fully removed until 1952 (Immigration and Nationality Act / McCarran-Walter). (Sources: LOC / Constitution Annotated.)
- **Exclusion by nationality/race:** the Page Act of 1875 (in practice barred most Chinese women) and the Chinese Exclusion Act of 1882 (signed May 6, 1882; first U.S. law to bar a group by nationality; repealed 1943). (Sources: National Archives; NPS; Britannica.)
- **Angel Island detention (1910–1940):** built mainly to enforce the exclusion laws; Asian arrivals detained days to as long as two years; detainees carved protest poems into the barrack walls (rediscovered 1970). (Sources: Angel Island Immigration Station Foundation; Smithsonian.)
- **National-origins quotas as racial policy:** the Emergency Quota Act (1921) and the Immigration Act of 1924 (Johnson-Reed) used 1890-census baselines to cut Southern/Eastern Europeans and barred nearly all Asians ("ineligible for citizenship"). Undone by the 1965 Hart-Celler Act. (Sources: History.com; Tenement Museum — journalism/museum, labeled.)
- **Turning away refugees:** the MS *St. Louis* (1939) — 937 mostly Jewish refugees refused landing by the U.S. (and Cuba and Canada); 254 later killed in the Holocaust. (Source: USHMM.)
- **Mexican Repatriation (1929–1936):** roughly 500,000 people of Mexican descent — including many U.S. citizens — pressured or forced to leave for Mexico during the Depression. Figures vary by source; state the range. (Sources: Mexican Repatriation references.)
- **The Great Migration's reception in the North and West:** redlining, restrictive covenants, employment and housing bias, and riots met the ~6 million Black Southerners who moved 1910–1970. `migration` owns the movement; `slavery-freedom` owns the Jim Crow terror that pushed (its 1900–1950 era); this chapter owns the fights against the Northern forms — housing, jobs, schools. (Sources: Census Bureau; Smithsonian; Wilkerson, *The Warmth of Other Suns*, 2010.)
- **Credit denied, recognition won late:** individuals whose contributions were denied recognition in their lifetimes — York (Lewis & Clark), Matthew Henson (North Pole), Jim Beckwourth (Beckwourth Pass), Katherine Johnson (NASA) — are documented in `research-exploration.md`; their recognition fights fit this chapter's frame where used.

## Parked from `land-environment` (2026-08-07)
- **Warren County, North Carolina, September 1982 — the protest that named environmental justice.** The state chose Afton, in mostly Black rural Warren County, as the landfill for PCB-contaminated soil (illegally roadside-dumped statewide in 1978). After the courts failed them, residents and civil-rights leaders (including Rev. Benjamin Chavis of the United Church of Christ's Commission for Racial Justice) lay down in front of the dump trucks; about 500 arrests over six weeks. The landfill was built anyway — but the campaign is widely credited as the founding event of the named environmental-justice movement, and led to the 1987 UCC "Toxic Wastes and Race" report. As an organizing/civil-rights story (church networks, arrests, the naming of a movement) it belongs here; `land-environment` carries the who-lives-next-to-the-waste pattern and its later wins (Margie Richard/Norco, 2002). (Sources: UNC Library exhibition "We Birthed the Movement: The Warren County PCB Landfill Protests, 1978–1982"; NCpedia "PCB Protests"; Wikipedia "1982 North Carolina PCB Protest" cross-checked.)

## Parked from `work-workers` (2026-08-07)
- **The Uprising of the 20,000 (November 1909 – February 1910).** At Cooper Union on November 22, 1909, shirtwaist maker **Clara Lemlich** — still bruised from a beating by company-hired thugs — cut off two hours of cautious speeches in Yiddish: "I have no further patience for talk... I move we go on strike." Roughly 20,000 garment workers, mostly young Jewish and Italian immigrant women, struck for eleven weeks; she then led the crowd in the Yiddish oath "If I turn traitor to the cause I now pledge, may this hand wither from the arm I now raise." Set off general strikes in garment cities through 1915 and forced the AFL's male leadership to take women workers seriously. This chapter's angle: women organizing against being told they couldn't; `work-workers` carries the strike as a labor event and the Triangle fire after it. (Sources: Jewish Women's Archive "Uprising of 20,000"; Cornell ILR Triangle fire site; laborarts.org.)
- **Memphis sanitation strike, 1968 — the rights-campaign side.** After garbage collectors Echol Cole and Robert Walker were crushed to death in their truck's compactor (February 1, 1968), 1,300 Black sanitation workers struck under the sign "I AM A MAN" — a dignity claim as much as a wage claim. Dr. King came twice in support and was assassinated in Memphis April 4, 1968; the city then recognized AFSCME Local 1733. `work-workers` tells the job and the strike; this chapter owns King and the campaign frame. (Sources: King Institute, Stanford; AFSCME history.)
- **Slug sync note:** Grace Fryer is featured in three chapters — your `grace-fryer` (court fight), elements' `grace-fryer-radium-girls`, and now work-workers' `grace-fryer-work-workers` (the job and the poisoning). All three use *Radium Girls* (2018) as the movie; the film is a drama with invented sister protagonists, inspired by the case (premiered Tribeca 2018, released 2020).


## Parked from `immigration` (2026-08-07)
- **Wong Kim Ark** — exclusion fought and beaten in court: a San Francisco-born cook refused re-entry to his own birthplace under the Chinese exclusion laws (August 1895), who took it to the Supreme Court and won birthright citizenship for everyone (U.S. v. Wong Kim Ark, March 28, 1898, 6-2). Era: 1850-1900. `immigration` features him as `wong-kim-ark` (arrival angle); `government-politics` holds the case-as-law park; if featured here, suffix the slug. (Sources: National Archives at San Francisco; Supreme Court Historical Society.)
- **Reinaldo Arenas** (LGBTQ thread) — Cuban novelist imprisoned by the Castro government for his writing and for being gay; left via the Mariel boatlift May 1980; autobiography *Before Night Falls* (English 1993); died 1990 in New York. Film: *Before Night Falls* (2000, Julian Schnabel; Javier Bardem, Oscar-nominated). Era: 1950-2000. Featured in `immigration` as `reinaldo-arenas` (arrival angle); a rights-angle telling here would be persecution for sexuality and writing. (Sources: Britannica; EBSCO; Duke University Press.)

## Parked from `city-building` (2026-08-07) — housing discrimination as a rights fight (your angle; the maps/buildings stay with city-building)
- **Restrictive covenants:** private deed clauses barring sale/rental to Black families (sometimes Jews and Asians), spread through the 1920s around Chicago's Black Belt and nationally; unenforceable after **Shelley v. Kraemer (1948)** [case not re-verified in the city-building pass — verify when worked]. The Chicago Covenants Project has mapped originals. (Sources: Chicago Covenants Project; Chicago Public Library "Housing and Race in Chicago.")
- **HOLC "redlining" maps, 1935–1940:** federal Home Owners' Loan Corporation graded neighborhoods in **239 cities** A/B/C/D; D = red = "hazardous"; Black residents described as "infiltration" and their neighborhoods almost always drawn red. Legacy (NCRC 2018): ~74% of redlined areas are low-to-moderate income today; ~64% majority-minority. Digitized at **Mapping Inequality** (University of Richmond). `city-building` carries the maps as city form; `home-family` the shut-out family; the injustice and the organizing against it are yours. (Sources: NCRC "HOLC 'redlining' maps"; Mapping Inequality; Chicago Fed working paper 2017-12.)
- **Urban renewal, 1949–1974:** ~2,000 neighborhoods demolished; **at least 300,000 families displaced (perhaps ~1.2 million people)**; Black Americans ~13% of the 1960 population but **at least 55% of the displaced**. **James Baldwin, 1963** (filmed KQED interview/"Take This Hammer"): urban renewal "means Negro removal." (Sources: Brent Cebul, "Tearing Down Black America," Boston Review 2020; U. Richmond DSL "Renewing Inequality.")
- **Seneca Village** (cleared 1857 for Central Park): ~225 residents, two-thirds Black, three churches; several Black families owned the land — and property ownership then carried voting rights for Black men in New York. A rights-loss angle if you want it; `city-building` tells the clearance with the park. (Sources: Central Park Conservancy "Before Central Park"; Britannica "Seneca Village.")

## Parked from `home-family` (2026-08-07) — the Levittown color line and the Myers family (your campaign angle; the household inside the house is home-family's story `daisy-william-myers`)
- **Clause 25 (verified):** the standard Levittown, NY lease barred use or occupancy "by any person other than members of the Caucasian race" (Caucasian domestic servants excepted), in capitals and bold. **Shelley v. Kraemer (1948)** made racial covenants judicially unenforceable; William Levitt dropped the written clause but **kept the whites-only sales policy**, and refused lease renewals to two white families over an interracial play group and neighborly sympathy. (Sources: Tougaloo College sundown-town file on Levittown; liherald.com "Levitt Homes: Built on Restriction"; Levitt & Sons histories.)
- **Daisy and William Myers, Levittown PA, August 1957 (verified):** first Black family in Levittown, PA — William a WWII Army veteran, Daisy a teacher, three young children. Nearly three months of mobs, rocks through windows, burned crosses, death threats by phone, stopped milk/bread/oil deliveries; state police protection. Daisy Myers's memoir *Sticks 'n Stones* (2005). **Film exists: *Crisis in Levittown* (1957)**, documentary by Lee Bobker and Lester Becker, shot as it happened. Pennsylvania later placed an official commemoration (Bristol Township). If featured here, suffix the slug (`daisy-myers-rights-movements` or similar) — `home-family` holds `daisy-william-myers`. (Sources: Bristol Township, PA official commemoration; Library of Congress photo records of the Myers family; witnessingyork.com on Daisy Myers; Wikipedia "Daisy Myers" for collation.)
- **Homeownership context numbers (verified, usable in your redlining arc):** U.S. homeownership 43.6% (1940) → 61.9% (1960) — the boom Black families were largely shut out of; FHA-insured long mortgages were the instrument (home-family era 08 has the mortgage mechanics; city-building has the HOLC maps).

## Parked from `transportation` (2026-08-08) — the stewardesses' fight as an early Title VII campaign
Web-verified 2026-08-08; fuller sourcing in `research/research-transportation.md`.

### 9. 1950 to 2000
- U.S. airlines fired stewardesses for turning 32 (some lines 35), for marrying, or for pregnancy; the jobs were advertised on the women's looks. **Barbara "Dusty" Roads** (American Airlines, 1950–; died November 21, 2023) fought the age rule publicly from the early 1960s — lobbying members of Congress as passengers on her Washington flights, daring reporters to pick the 32-year-olds out of a lineup — and flight attendants became early, repeat users of Title VII and EEOC complaints; the marriage and age rules collapsed under them. **Pat Banks**, one of the first Black flight attendants in the U.S., won her job after a discrimination fight (New York State Commission ruling, 1960 — verify details when this chapter is worked). Documentary: ***Fly With Me* (PBS American Experience, premiered February 20, 2024)** — flight attendants, advocates, and scholars; Roads and Banks are both featured. `transportation` tells the cabin job; the rights campaign is yours. (Sources: PBS American Experience; Wikipedia "Barbara Roads"; obituaries.)

## Parked from `landmarks` (2026-08-08) — the monuments as civil-rights stages
`landmarks` tells the structures; the campaigns are yours to lead. Web-verified 2026-08-08, full sourcing in `research/research-landmarks.md`.

### 8. 1900 to 1950
- **Marian Anderson at the Lincoln Memorial, Easter Sunday April 9, 1939:** after the DAR barred her from Constitution Hall (whites-only artist clause), Interior Secretary Harold Ickes arranged the Lincoln Memorial steps; about 75,000 attended; Ickes introduced her — "Genius draws no color line"; she opened with "My Country 'Tis of Thee." Also usable: at the memorial's own 1922 dedication the crowd was segregated and the one Black speaker, Robert Russa Moton, had his text vetted. (Smithsonian Music; National Archives Rediscovering Black History blog.)

### 9. 1950 to 2000
- **Percy Green's Gateway Arch climb, July 14, 1964:** Green and Richard Daly climbed 125 feet up the unfinished north leg to protest the exclusion of Black workers from Arch construction jobs; Green (CORE, later founder of ACTION) became a national figure; NPS itself now documents the protest. (Gateway Arch Park Foundation; NPS Percy Green interview page.) `landmarks` gives it one span line; the campaign is yours.

## Parked from `marketplace` research (2026-08-08) — why the Woolworth counter existed
`marketplace` covers the five-and-dime chain as a way of selling; the sit-in campaign is yours. Useful setting detail:
- F. W. Woolworth built a national chain on fixed low prices and goods laid out for customers to handle. The **seated lunch counter** was a standard fixture of the chain's larger stores, which is why a segregated counter at a national chain made such a visible target.
- **Greensboro, February 1, 1960:** Ezell Blair Jr. (later Jibreel Khazan), Franklin McCain, Joseph McNeil, and David Richmond sat at the Woolworth counter and were refused service. Twenty-five more students came the next morning, sixty-three the third day, and more than three hundred demonstrators by the fifth. The counter was desegregated **July 25, 1960**, after a six-month campaign. A section of the counter is in the Smithsonian National Museum of American History. (Library of Congress, African American Odyssey; Smithsonian NMAH; Britannica; North Carolina History.)
- Chain scale for context: the merged F. W. Woolworth Co. opened with **596 stores** in 1911–12; A&P ran about **15,000 stores** at its 1930 peak. (Library of Congress, *This Month in Business History*; A&P company histories.)

## Parked from `marketplace` research (2026-08-08) — the catalog as a way around Jim Crow
- Historian **Louis Hyman** (Cornell) argued in a widely circulated 2018 account that mail-order catalogs let Black families in the segregated South buy at national prices rather than from a white-owned country store, often their landlord's, where they waited until white customers were served; and that some Southern storekeepers burned catalogs to keep their customers, telling white shoppers Sears was a Black-owned company. **This is a historian's argument carried by journalism, not an archival count — label it and do not turn the burnings into a statistic.** (NPR, October 16, 2018; *Washington Post*, October 16, 2018; America's Black Holocaust Museum, November 10, 2018.) `marketplace` tells it as shopping; the dignity and rights side is yours if you want it.

# ERAS 1–6 — RESEARCHED 2026-09-09

Everything below was web-verified on 2026-09-09 for eras `before-1500`, `1500s`, `1600s`, `1700-1750`, `1750-1800`, `1800-1850`. Eras 7–10 remain seed/parked material (above).

**The framing decision for the early eras, stated once.** A *movement* means people organizing together to change a rule that governs them. By that test there were no rights movements in this land before about 1700, and the first ones are small. There was a great deal of resistance — revolts, escapes, refusals, lawsuits — and this bank keeps the two words apart rather than dressing resistance up as a movement. The outline's early eras say so plainly.

## 1. Before 1500

### Haudenosaunee clan mothers — women holding formal political power
- **Clan mothers choose the chief and can take the office back.** The Onondaga Nation's own government page: the clan mother selects the *Hoyane* (chief) who speaks for her clan in council. If he does not represent the clan, she warns him that he is not following the ways of the Great Peace and must change. If he does not change, the clan removes him in council. (Source: Onondaga Nation, "Clan Mothers" and "Chiefs," https://www.onondaganation.org/government/clan-mothers/ and https://www.onondaganation.org/government/chiefs/ .)
- **The antlers and "de-horning."** The Onondaga Nation records that the Peacemaker placed deer antlers on the *Gasdó•wä•'* (headdress) of every Hoyane as the sign of the office; a chief who is removed has the antlers taken off and is said to be "de-horned." (Source: Onondaga Nation, as above.)
- **What the National Park Service states about the wider powers.** "Haudenosaunee women controlled the economy in their nations through their responsibilities for growing and distributing the food," and had "the final authority over land transfers and decisions about engaging in war." On separation: "If for any cause the Iroquois husband and wife separated, the wife took with her all the property she had brought into the wigwam. The children also accompanied the mother." (Source: NPS, "How Native American Women Inspired the Women's Rights Movement," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/how-native-american-women-inspired-the-women-s-rights-movement.htm .)
- **The nineteenth-century link — belongs to eras 7+, noted here so nobody puts it in era 1.** Elizabeth Cady Stanton told the 1891 National Council of Women that Haudenosaunee women "did not hesitate, when occasion required, 'to knock off the horns,' as it was technically called, from the head of a chief." Matilda Joslyn Gage wrote in the *New York Evening Post* that "In the home the wife was absolute," and was given an honorary adoption into the Wolf Clan of the Mohawk Nation in 1893 with the name Ka-ron-ien-ha-wi, "She who holds the sky." (Same NPS source.) **Handing to the eras 7–8 agent.**
- **Founding date of the Confederacy is disputed and must be stated as disputed.** This book already carries the dispute in two other chapters: `outlines/native-nations.md` gives 1142 from an eclipse in the founding tradition against most scholars' mid-1100s to late-1400s; `outlines/education.md` gives Haudenosaunee tradition at about 1000–1400 against a common scholarly figure of about 1450. Do not pick one.

### The limit of the evidence — state it, do not work around it
Almost everything written down about how these nations governed themselves was written after Europeans arrived, by Europeans. The nations' own records are wampum and spoken account, which the Onondaga Nation still keeps (`outlines/education.md` era 1 carries the wampum method). So this era carries no `hb-story`: there is no documented individual whose rights claim survives, and this book does not invent people.

## 2. The 1500s

**Thin on purpose.** There is no organized rights movement in this century anywhere in what became the United States. What the century has is (a) Spanish law sorting people into categories, and (b) one long public argument in Spain about whether Native people had rights, carried on by a Spanish priest. Individual resistance in this century is told elsewhere in the book and is **not** repeated here: `slavery-freedom` era 2 has San Miguel de Gualdape (1526, the first documented rebellion of enslaved Africans in what became the United States) and `native-nations` era 2 has Acoma (1598–99) and de Soto. Do not duplicate them.

- **Encomienda, in plain words.** A grant from the Spanish crown giving a settler the labor of a named group of Native people; the settler was supposed to protect them and teach them Christianity, and in practice took their work and what they produced. The American Yawp describes it as "an exploitive feudal arrangement in which Spain tied Indigenous laborers to vast estates," and says "encomenderos brutalized their laborers." (Source: The American Yawp, ch. 1 "Indigenous America," https://www.americanyawp.com/text/01-the-new-world/ — peer-reviewed open textbook.)
- **The New Laws of 1542.** After Las Casas's account of Spanish abuses, Spanish authorities abolished the encomienda in 1542 and replaced it with the *repartimiento*. Forced labor continued. (Same Yawp source; `slavery-freedom` era 2 states the same thing as "curbed it on paper.")
- **Las Casas's public argument, 1550.** Bartolomé de Las Casas argued at Valladolid, in Spain, against Juan Ginés de Sepúlveda, over whether the Spanish were entitled to subject Native people by force. (Source: Gilder Lehrman Institute, "Bartolomé de Las Casas debates the subjugation of the Indians, 1550," https://www.gilderlehrman.org/history-resources/spotlight-primary-source/bartolome-de-las-casas-debates-subjugation-indians-1550 — the page returned HTTP 403 to a fetch on 2026-09-09; the date 1550 and the framing are from the item's own title and catalog listing. **A later agent should re-verify whether the debate ran into 1551, which several accounts state; this bank does not claim it.**)
- **A Las Casas primary source in the national collection:** "Bartolomé de las Casas statement of opinion, 1542," Library of Congress, https://www.loc.gov/item/2007574376/ (loc.gov returned 403 to WebFetch on 2026-09-09; listed here as a citation, not paraphrased).
- **What Las Casas also did, which must not be left out.** In 1516 he asked the Spanish crown to bring enslaved Africans to the Indies in place of Native laborers. Older public-domain scholarship states it plainly: he "favored negro slavery as a means of sparing the natives" and "assented to the demands of the Spanish colonists in the Indies for permission to import Africans from Spain to take the place of the rapidly perishing Indians." (Sources: Herbert E. Bolton, *The Colonization of North America* (1920), Project Gutenberg #36619; F. A. MacNutt, *Bartholomew de Las Casas* (1909), Project Gutenberg #23466 — **dated scholarship, labeled as such**; the same fact appears in The American Yawp ch. 2.) The same accounts note the Atlantic slave trade had already begun about sixteen years before he advised it.
- **NOT CLAIMED.** Las Casas is often said to have repudiated this in his *Historia de las Indias*. Searches on 2026-09-09 did not turn up a source good enough to state it, so the outline does not say it. A later agent may add it with a real citation.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262a): Valladolid ran into 1551, who sat in judgment, and Las Casas's later reversal on African slavery
- **Dates and panel.** Andrea Daher, "The Valladolid debate," *Encyclopédie d'histoire numérique de l'Europe* (Sorbonne Université), https://ehne.fr/en/encyclopedia/themes/europe-europeans-and-world/colonial-encounters/valladolid-debate (fetched 2026-09-27): Las Casas and Sepúlveda were "Summoned in 1550 by Emperor Charles V to the chapel of the Colegio San Gregorio, before a junta of renowned theologians including Domingo Soto, Melchor Cano, Bernardino de Arévalo, members of the Council of Castile and the Council of the Indies, and officers of the Spanish government," to answer the question of a just war against non-Christian peoples. On the first day Sepúlveda spoke for three hours. Domingo Soto then summarised both sides for "a final meeting of the 'Council of the Fourteen' held in 1551." The same article says there is no detailed record of how the judges proceeded. **So the argument opened in 1550 and ended in 1551.** A search summary adds that it opened on 15 August 1550 and that Las Casas's reply took five days **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**.
- **No verdict.** The fetched EHNE text records no decision by the junta, and the existing bank records none. Say that the panel gave no recorded ruling only if a writer needs to; do not claim either man won.
- **The reversal the bank marked NOT CLAIMED is now sourced.** Lowcountry Digital History Initiative, College of Charleston, "The Early Trans-Atlantic Slave Trade: Emperor Charles V," https://ldhi.library.cofc.edu/exhibits/show/african_laborers_for_a_new_emp/emperor_charles_v (fetched 2026-09-27): "In 1516 even Bartolome de Las Casas ... proposed replacing native labor with African slaves, whom he believed were better suited for harsh physical labor. (Las Casas later recanted this opinion and dedicated a substantial part of his work Historia de las Indias to condemning the African slave trade and Iberian slave markets.)" The same page records that Charles V granted Lorenzo de Gorrevod a charter to transport enslaved Africans on 18 August 1518, and that the Native population of Hispaniola fell from 60,000 in 1508 to fewer than 20,000 ten years later. **A writer may now say both things about Las Casas: he advised it in 1516, and he later wrote against the African slave trade in his *History of the Indies*.** The *Historia* was not published in his lifetime (Britannica, "Bartolomé de Las Casas," by Enrique Dussel, fetched by curl 2026-09-27: "Las Casas did not publish Historia in his lifetime"). So his reversal was not public while the trade grew.
- **The encomienda he held.** Britannica (as above): "Bartolomé de Las Casas did own serfs," was granted an encomienda in return for joining military expeditions in the West Indies, and published the *Brevísima relación de la destrucción de las Indias* in 1542, "Shortly after" which Charles I issued the New Laws. That supports the outline's "had held an encomienda himself and gave it up." **Date caution:** Britannica's summary line says the *Brevísima* was published in 1542. It is commonly dated as written in 1542 and printed at Seville later (the EHNE page shows a 1598 Latin edition whose title gives the Spanish original as 1551 or later). Do not print a publication year for it from this bank.

## 3. The 1600s

**What this era actually is.** Still no organized movement. What arrives with the English is a body of law that removed a married woman's separate legal existence, and — inside twenty years of the first Puritan settlement — women who argued against colonial authority in public and were punished for it by name. That is the honest shape of the era: a rule, and individual people refusing it.

### Coverture — the rule, in plain words
- **What it was.** A married woman was "covered" by her husband in law. She was a *feme covert* and had no legal status of her own: she did not control property even if she brought it into the marriage, and her husband took full rights to any income or wages she earned. Because in law she did not separately exist, she could not make a contract, could not be sued, and could not appear in court in her own right. (Sources: National Women's History Museum, "Coverture: The Word You Probably Don't Know But Should," https://www.womenshistory.org/articles/coverture-word-you-probably-dont-know-should ; Encyclopedia Virginia, "Women in Colonial Virginia," https://encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/women-in-colonial-virginia/ ; Gilder Lehrman Institute, "The Legal Status of Women, 1776-1830," https://www.gilderlehrman.org/history-resources/essays/legal-status-women-1776-1830 ; Cornell LII, U.S. Constitution Annotated, "Women's Suffrage from the Founding Era to the Civil War," https://www.law.cornell.edu/constitution-conan/amendment-19/womens-suffrage-from-the-founding-era-to-the-civil-war .)
- **An unmarried adult woman or a widow was a *feme sole***, and could buy and sell property, make contracts, and do business and legal transactions in her own name. Marriage, not womanhood, is what removed the standing. (Same sources.)
- **The famous statement of the rule, with its date stated correctly.** William Blackstone: "By marriage, the husband and wife are one person in law: that is, the very being or legal existence of the woman is suspended during the marriage, or at least is incorporated and consolidated into that of the husband." **Blackstone's *Commentaries on the Laws of England* were published 1765-1769 — era 5, not era 3.** He was writing down a rule that was already centuries old. **Do not place Blackstone in the 1600s;** quote him where he belongs and say that he was describing the rule, not inventing it.
- **Practice was not identical to the rule, and the outline should say so rather than flatten it.** Wives ran businesses beside their husbands and for absent husbands, and some traded under a legal exception called *feme sole* trader status, with the local community enforcing the arrangement. The law was still the law: what she held, she held at her husband's sufferance. (Sources: NWHM coverture article; "Minding Their Own Business: Married Women and Credit in Early Eighteenth-Century London," PMC4602702, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4602702/ ; "The Legal Status of Married Women," PMC9662574, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9662574/ .)

### Anne Hutchinson — **[VERIFY] CLEARED, and the outline's date was wrong**
**The outline said "tried and banished, 1638." That is wrong. The trial and the sentence of banishment were both in November 1637.** March 1638 is the *church* trial and the excommunication. The two are five months apart and the outline must not merge them.

- **Who, and when she came.** Born about 1591 at Alford, Lincolnshire, England. She crossed on the *Griffin* in the fall of 1634 with her husband William and their children, following their minister John Cotton to Boston. (Sources: National Women's History Museum, "Anne Hutchinson," https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/anne-hutchinson ; Mass Moments, "Anne Hutchinson Banished," http://www.massmoments.org/moment.cfm?mid=88 — the same facts are already carried, sourced, in `outlines/immigration.md` under slug `anne-hutchinson-immigration`.)
- **The meetings.** From 1636 she held weekly meetings in her house in Boston to go over the minister's sermons. They began as meetings for women and grew until men came too, including prominent men. (Sources: NWHM, as above; NPS, "Anne Hutchinson in Massachusetts Bay," https://www.nps.gov/sapa/planyourvisit/anne-hutchinson-in-massachusetts-bay.htm .)
- **The charge.** Heresy. Two things in particular: that she said she could tell which ministers and which residents had received God's grace, and that she taught that grace alone, not good behaviour, decided salvation. The meetings themselves were part of the charge. (Sources: NWHM; NPS, as above.)
- **The trial.** November 1637, before the General Court sitting at **Newtown** — the town is now Cambridge, Massachusetts. It ran two days. NPS: she "defended herself skillfully, matching Biblical references and wits with Winthrop and other accusers." (Source: NPS, as above.)
- **Verbatim from the trial record.** The record is printed as "The Examination of Mrs. Anne Hutchinson at the Court at Newtown," taken from John Winthrop's account. Winthrop's opening: **"you are called here as one of those that have troubled the peace of the commonwealth."** On her meetings: **"a thing not tolerable nor comely in the sight of God nor fitting for your sex."** On how she knew what she claimed to know: **"By the voice of his own spirit to my soul."** To the court: **"You have power over my body, but the Lord Jesus hath power over my body and soul."** The sentence: **"Mrs. Hutchinson, the sentence of the court you hear is that you are banished from out of our jurisdiction as being a woman not fit for our society."** Her reply: **"I desire to know wherefore I am banished?"** (Sources — two independent transcriptions of the same primary record, checked against each other on 2026-09-09: ushistory.education, "The Examination of Mrs. Anne Hutchinson at the Court at Newton — Full Text," https://ushistory.education/historical-documents/anne-hutchinson-trial/ ; Famous Trials, Douglas O. Linder, University of Missouri-Kansas City School of Law, "The Examination of Anne Hutchinson (November 1637)," https://www.famous-trials.com/hutchinson/2397-the-examination-of-anne-hutchinson-november-1637 . The Gilder Lehrman page "The Banishment of Anne Hutchinson," https://www.gilderlehrman.org/news/banishment-anne-hutchinson , returned HTTP 403 on 2026-09-09 and is listed as a citation only.)
- **The claim that finished her.** At the end she said God had spoken to her by "an immediate revelation" and would curse the Puritans and their descendants if they harmed her. The court took that as blasphemy. (Sources: NPS; NWHM.)
- **The church trial and excommunication, March 1638.** After the banishment sentence she was held over the winter and then tried a second time, by the church at Boston, and excommunicated in March 1638. The church's charge included that she had **"rather been a Husband than a Wife and a preacher than a Hearer; and a Magistrate than a Subject."** (Sources: NWHM biography, and NWHM lesson plan "Anne Hutchinson: Foremother of the American Women's Movement," https://www.womenshistory.org/resources/lesson-plan/anne-hutchinson-foremother-american-womens-movement ; NPS, "Anne Hutchinson (1591-1643)," https://www.nps.gov/rowi/learn/historyculture/annehutchinson.htm .)
- **After.** In the spring of 1638 she and her family left for Roger Williams's colony, settling on Aquidneck Island in what became Rhode Island. After her husband died she moved in 1642 into Dutch territory on Long Island Sound, and in 1643 she and several of her children were killed in a Siwanoy attack; one daughter survived. (Sources: NWHM; Mass Moments; the same sequence is already sourced in `outlines/immigration.md`.)
- **A primary document in the Massachusetts Historical Society's own collection** — William Bradford to John Winthrop, 11 April 1638, the two governors discussing her — is catalogued at https://www.masshist.org/object-of-the-month/objects/governors-william-bradford-and-john-winthrop-discuss-anne-hutchinson-2008-05-01 . Not fetched on 2026-09-09; listed as a citation, not paraphrased.

### Mary Dyer and the Massachusetts law that made being a Quaker a hanging matter
- **The laws.** In late 1656 and 1657 the Massachusetts General Court outlawed "the cursed sect of heretics commonly called Quakers," fining ship captains who carried them and people who owned their books. **In October 1658 the General Court made returning to the colony after banishment a capital offence — Quakers were barred "under pain of death."** (Source: Mass Moments, a project of Mass Humanities, "Quaker Mary Dyer Is Taken to the Gallows," https://www.massmoments.org/moment-details/quakers-outlawed-in-plymouth/submoment/quaker-mary-dyer-is-taken-to-the-gallows.html .)
- **How many it killed.** Four Quakers were executed in Boston between 1659 and 1661, and dozens more were imprisoned and physically punished between 1656 and 1661. (Source: Library of Congress exhibition, "America as a Religious Refuge: The Seventeenth Century, Part 2," https://www.loc.gov/exhibits/religion/rel01-2.html — catalogued from search on 2026-09-09; loc.gov returns 403 to WebFetch, so this is a citation, not a paraphrase of fetched text.)
- **Dyer's sequence.** She met George Fox in England about 1650 and came back to Boston a Quaker in 1657. She was jailed at once; her husband got her out by promising she would not preach, and the family moved to Rhode Island. She kept coming back to Massachusetts to stand with imprisoned Quakers. **She was hanged on Boston Common on 1 June 1660.** Her husband asked for her life and she refused to repent. (Sources: Mass Moments, as above; Library of Congress catalogue record for *Mary Dyer of Rhode Island: the Quaker martyr that was hanged on Boston Common, June 1, 1660*, https://www.loc.gov/item/07032951/ .)
- **The near-hanging of 27 October 1659 — stated with its source, and with a source conflict named.** Dyer was taken to the Boston gallows with two other Quakers, **William Robinson**, a London merchant, and **Marmaduke Stephenson**, a Yorkshire ploughman. The two men were hanged; Dyer was made to watch, the rope was put round her neck, and she was reprieved and sent out of the colony. (Source: New-York Historical Society, *Women & the American Story*, "Mary Dyer," https://wams.nyhistory.org/early-encounters/english-colonies/mary-dyer/ — **the page returned HTTP 403 to a direct fetch on 2026-09-09; the date and the two men's names come from its indexed summary, not from fetched page text.** **Conflict to note:** the Mass Moments page places the reprieve in "fall 1658." Two sources, two years. **The outline states the reprieve without a year until a later agent settles it from a fetched primary or institutional source.**)
- **After.** Charles II ordered the death penalty for Quakers stopped in all his colonies. By 1677 Friends could meet openly in Massachusetts. (Source: Mass Moments, as above.)
- **Why she is not an `hb-story` here.** The 1659 sequence rests on a page that could not be fetched, so the per-fact standard for a story block is not met. She is told in `hb-zoom` prose instead. **A later agent who can source 1659 directly may promote her.** She is used nowhere else in the book as of 2026-09-09.

### The Germantown protest, 18 April 1688 — the first organized act in this chapter
- **What it was.** Four men at Germantown, Pennsylvania — **Garret Hendericks, Francis Daniel Pastorius, Derick op de Graeff and Abraham op de Graeff** — wrote and signed a paper against slaveholding and sent it up through their Quaker meetings. It is **the first protest against African slavery made by a religious body in the English colonies.** (Sources: National Park Service, Independence NHP, "Germantown Friends' First Protest Against Slavery, 1688," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/inde-germantown-friends-protest-slavery-1688.htm ; NPS, "Germantown Quaker Petition Against Slavery," https://www.nps.gov/articles/quakerpetition.htm .)
- **Its argument, in its own spelling.** The paper turns on the Golden Rule: **"that we shall doe to all men licke as we will be done ourselves."** (Source: NPS, as above. A facsimile broadside is at the Library of Congress, https://www.loc.gov/resource/rbpe.14000200/?st=text .)
- **What happened to it — the part that must not be dropped.** It was passed up and put off through four meetings in turn: Germantown Monthly Meeting, Dublin Monthly Meeting, Philadelphia Quarterly Meeting, and finally Burlington Yearly Meeting in New Jersey, **which voted to take no stand either way.** The protest failed. (Source: NPS, as above.)
- **The document itself.** Set aside and forgotten until 1844, when abolitionists rediscovered it; lost again, and found once more in March 2005 in the vault at Arch Street Meetinghouse. It is now at **Haverford College Quaker & Special Collections**. (Sources: NPS, as above; Haverford College, "Quaker Involvement in Abolition," http://crossingborders.sites.haverford.edu/exhibits/show/crossing-borders--from-slavery/quaker-involvement-in-abolitio .)
- **Boundary note.** `slavery-freedom` era 3 does not carry Germantown (checked 2026-09-09), and neither does any other chapter. It belongs here because this chapter's subject is people organizing to claim rights; the slavery system itself stays with `slavery-freedom`.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262a): the Quaker hangings, settled, with the men who ordered them and what the punishments were
- **The 1659 gallows day is 27 October 1659, and the "fall 1658" in Mass Moments is not that day.** Famous Trials (Douglas O. Linder, University of Missouri-Kansas City School of Law), "The Life, Trials, and Execution of Mary Dyer: A Chronology," https://famous-trials.com/dyer/2488-the-life-trials-and-execution-of-mary-dyer-a-chronology (fetched 2026-09-27): **20 October 1659**, Mary Dyer, William Robinson and Marmaduke Stevenson (the chronology spells it Stevenson; other sources give Stephenson) were **sentenced to death by Governor John Endecott**. **27 October 1659**, Robinson and Stevenson were hanged, and Dyer was reprieved at the gallows and returned to prison. This agrees with the New-York Historical Society date already in this bank. **The conflict is settled for 1659.** A writer may give the year.
- **The reprieve was written before the walk to the gallows.** Governor Endecott's order of 26 October 1659, printed in Ruth Plimpton, *Mary Dyer: Biography of a Quaker Rebel* (1994), p. 171, transcribed at Famous Trials, https://famous-trials.com/dyer/2487-governor-endicott-s-order-of-reprieve-but-only-after-a-trip-to-the-gallows-1659 : "Whereas Mary Dyer is condemned by the General Court to be executed for her offenses, on the petition of William Dyer, her son, it is ordered that the said Mary Dyer shall have liberty for 48 hours after this day to depart out of this jurisdiction..." The order required that she be carried to the gallows and stand with a rope about her neck while the others were executed, and then go back to prison. So the men who ran the colony had already decided to spare her, and still made her stand in the noose and watch two men die. The petitioner who asked for her life in 1659 was **her son William Dyer**. (The words "she needs to experience the chill of death" appear on the chronology page as a paraphrase of Endecott's reasoning, not as a quotation of a document. Do not print them as his words.)
- **Who passed the death law, and by how much.** Famous Trials chronology: **19 October 1658, the General Court voted 12 to 11** to authorise "banishment upon pain of death" for Quakers. One vote decided it.
- **What the earlier punishments were, stated as the law stated them.** Famous Trials, "The Life, Trials, and Execution of Mary Dyer: An Account," https://famous-trials.com/dyer/2489-the-life-trials-and-execution-of-mary-dyer-an-account : in October 1657 the General Court "authorized, for males, the cutting off one ear for the first offense of re-entering the colony, a second ear cut off for a second offense, and for a third offense, 'they shall have their tongues bored through with a hot iron.' Whippings were the authorized punishment for female Quakers violating the law." Plain words for the writer: a man's ear was cut off with a blade; a hole was burned through a person's tongue with a heated iron rod; whipping meant being struck on the bare back with a whip.
- **Named men who had their ears cut off.** Famous Trials chronology: on **3 June 1658 Christopher Holder and two others** had their ears cut off, were whipped, and were then beaten twice a week for nine weeks. (The chronology names only Holder. The other two are commonly given as John Copeland and John Rous; this pass did not fetch a source naming them. **Name Holder only.**)
- **A count from the time.** The Famous Trials account cites a publication by the English Quaker Edward Burrough covering 1656 to 1660 that lists "64 imprisonments, 25 banishments." That is a Quaker count made at the time. Say whose count it is.
- **1 June 1660.** Dyer was hanged near the Frog Pond on Boston Common, on the sentence of Governor Endecott and the General Court, with about 200 soldiers, armed with "guns, swords, pikes, and flags," around the gallows. (Famous Trials account, as above.)
- **The fourth person hanged: William Leddra, with a date conflict named.** Haverford College Quaker & Special Collections, finding aid "William Leddra court records," https://findingaids.library.upenn.edu/records/HAVERFORD_HC.MC.950.115 (fetched 2026-09-27): "William Leddra (?-1661) was a British quaker from Cornwall, who emigrated, or was transported to Barbados. He was a clothier and a Quaker minister." It gives the execution as "Boston Common on **January 14, 1661**," and describes transcripts of the court documents of his arrest in 1660 by Boston's Court of Assistants after he came back from banishment. **Most accounts give 14 March 1661** (Center for Christian History and Christianity.com, both in search results only). The gap may come from the old calendar, in which the year changed in March, but no source fetched here says so. **Print "1661" with no day, or state both days.** His name is now sourced from an institutional archive, so the note in `research-religion.md` that forbids printing it can be lifted (TO PARK note for `religion`).
- **The end of it.** The Famous Trials chronology gives **9 September 1661** for the king's order stopping the punishments of Quakers and directing that accused Quakers be sent to England instead. This fits the bank's existing "Charles II ordered the hangings stopped."
- **Mary Dyer and Anne Hutchinson were friends.** Famous Trials chronology: they became close in 1636, and when the Boston church excommunicated Hutchinson in March 1638, Dyer walked out of the church with her. This ties the two era-3 accounts together with a fact, not a theme.
- **Story status.** The 1659 sequence now rests on a fetched academic source (Famous Trials) that transcribes the governor's own order. **Mary Dyer now meets the sourcing bar for an `hb-story` block** if the next writer wants one. This agent does not add one: the era already carries her fully in zoom prose, and `religion` also tells her. The slug `mary-dyer` remains unused.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262a): Anne Hutchinson's death, the war around it, and whose land each of her homes was on
- **Who killed her, and why there was fighting.** Encyclopaedia Britannica, "Anne Hutchinson," https://www.britannica.com/biography/Anne-Hutchinson (fetched by curl 2026-09-27): she died "August or September 1643, Pelham Bay, New York." "Tensions between the Dutch there and the native peoples were inflamed into a conflict known as Kieft's War by the Dutch governor Willem Kieft's harsh policies. Hutchinson and all her children with her at the time except one were killed by warriors of the Siwanoy." Also: "In 1643 she and all her servants and children save one were killed by Indians, an event regarded by some in Massachusetts as a manifestation of divine judgment."
- **Count.** Britannica gives no number. Search summaries (Wikipedia, "Anne Hutchinson" and "Wampage") give **six of her children and nine others** killed, and name the surviving daughter as **Susanna, aged nine**, taken captive and held for several years, and name the Siwanoy leader as **Wampage**. **(unconfirmed: search summary only, and Wikipedia.)** Writers: say "she and all but one of the children with her, and her servants," and do not print six, nine, Wampage or Susanna's age from this bank.
- **What Kieft's men did first.** New York Almanack, "Kieft's War: Mass Murder on Manhattan" (July 2022), https://www.newyorkalmanack.com/2022/07/kiefts-war-mass-murder-on-manhattan/ (fetched 2026-09-27, a historian's article citing the New Netherland council minutes in the New York State Archives): on the night of 25 February 1643, "Dutch sources indicate that at least eighty and perhaps up to one hundred and twenty Munsees were murdered," first by armed settlers at Corlaer's Hook on Manhattan and then by soldiers of the Dutch West India Company at Pavonia, across the Hudson. Kieft and his council authorised both attacks. The war that followed lasted until the summer of 1645. **This is `native-nations` and `war` material.** It is here only so a writer who says "a Siwanoy attack" can also say that the Dutch governor's soldiers had started the war by killing Munsee families that February. TO PARK: offer to `native-nations`, era 3 (no bank there mentions Kieft or Pavonia as of 2026-09-27).
- **Whose land.** Aquidneck: NPS, Roger Williams National Memorial, "Anne Hutchinson (1591-1643)," https://www.nps.gov/rowi/learn/historyculture/annehutchinson.htm (fetched 2026-09-27): "Roger Williams helped the family buy land from the Narragansett." The settlement was Portsmouth, and one of its first written rules said no person "shall be in any wise molested, punished, disquieted or called into question on matter of religion ... so long as he keeps the peace." Pelham Bay: Siwanoy country (Britannica, as above, places the killing there and names the Siwanoy). Boston: already carried elsewhere in the book as Massachusett land (`research-home-family.md`, `research-education.md`).
- **Germantown's land.** Historic Germantown, "History," https://historicgermantownpa.org/history/ , and search summaries place Germantown "along a Lenape trail" between the Wissahickon and Wingohocking creeks, on land Francis Daniel Pastorius bought from William Penn in 1683 (Encyclopedia of Greater Philadelphia, "First Purchasers of Pennsylvania," https://philadelphiaencyclopedia.org/essays/first-purchasers-of-pennsylvania/ , fetched: the Frankfort Land Company hired Pastorius, who reached the settlement in June 1683 and named it Germantown). The Lenape trail detail is **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**. The Lenape as the people of the Philadelphia region is carried in `native-nations`.

### SEARCHED, NOT FOUND 2026-09-27 (T-262a): which Lenape community lived on the Germantown tract in 1683, and what they were paid for it
Sources checked: Encyclopedia of Greater Philadelphia, "First Purchasers of Pennsylvania" (Penn's sales to the Frankfort Company and the Krefeld families, nothing on the Lenape side); Historic Germantown, "History" (Lenape history named as a theme, and the later Walking Purchase, nothing on the 1683 tract); Encyclopedia of Greater Philadelphia "Lenape" page (would not render to text); search results on Pastorius and the Germantown purchase.
How the prose can say it: "The land was Lenape country. Pastorius bought it from William Penn. The surviving records of that sale do not name the Lenape families who lived on it."

## 4. 1700 to 1750

**Thin, and thin for a reason that should be stated in the outline.** There is still no movement in this half-century. What there is: rights arguments made in print by individuals, and one religious body — the Quakers — beginning to argue with itself and punishing the members who raised the question. The first organized society for anyone's rights in this land is the Pennsylvania Abolition Society of 1775, which is era 5. `slavery-freedom` carries the era's resistance (Stono, 1739) and the era's law (the Negro Act of 1740); do not repeat them here.

### Samuel Sewall, *The Selling of Joseph*, Boston 1700
- **The first antislavery tract published in America.** Three pages, printed at Boston in 1700 by Bartholomew Green and John Allen. Sewall (1652-1730) was a Boston merchant and magistrate. (Sources: Massachusetts Historical Society, Object of the Month, "The Selling of Joseph: A Memorial," https://www.masshist.org/object-of-the-month/objects/the-selling-of-joseph-a-memorial-2004-09-01 ; MHS Collections Online item, https://www.masshist.org/database/53 ; Digital Commonwealth record, https://www.digitalcommonwealth.org/search/commonwealth-oai:mc87px33r .)
- **What it argued.** That taking and selling people is the crime the Bible calls man stealing, citing Exodus 21:16, and it answered the standard defences of slavery one by one. (Source: MHS, as above.)
- **What prompted it.** A petition circulated in Boston in 1700 for the freeing of an enslaved man named **Adam** and his wife, "unjustly held in Bondage" by the merchant **John Saffin**. Saffin had promised Adam freedom after seven years of good behaviour and then refused to give it. (Source: MHS, as above, quoting Sewall's diary.)
- **The answer, and the outcome.** Saffin published *A Brief and Candid Answer to a late Printed Sheet Entitled the Selling of Joseph* in **1701**, defending both slavery and his own conduct toward Adam. **Adam won his freedom in 1703** after years of proceedings. Sewall answered Saffin again in **1705** by reprinting an English condemnation of the slave trade. (Source: MHS, as above.)
- **Note on dates.** One search summary gave Saffin's reply as 1705; the MHS page gives 1701 for the reply and 1705 for Sewall's reprint, which reconciles the two. **Use 1701 and 1705 as MHS gives them.**
- **PARKED FOR `slavery-freedom`, not taken here:** Adam's own freedom suit, 1700-1703, is a documented named freedom case and that chapter owns freedom suits (it already carries Elizabeth Freeman). Flagged, not used.

### The Quakers argue with themselves — the chain that becomes the first movement
- **1688, Germantown** — four men, one paper, rejected (see era 3 above).
- **1729** — **Ralph Sandiford** published *A Brief Examination of the Practice of the Times* at Philadelphia. Quaker authorities denounced it. (Source: Library Company of Philadelphia, *Black Founders*, "Emerging Antislavery," https://www.librarycompany.org/blackfounders/section3.htm .)
- **1737** — **Benjamin Lay** published *All Slave-Keepers That Keep the Innocent in Bondage, Apostates* at Philadelphia. It was printed by **Benjamin Franklin**, who did not put his name on it. Quaker authorities denounced this one too, and Lay was disowned by Philadelphia Yearly Meeting. (Sources: Library Company, as above; Library of Congress catalogue record for the 1737 imprint, https://www.loc.gov/item/66038906/ ; Swarthmore College Bulletin, "Fearless and Fiery," https://www.swarthmore.edu/bulletin/archive/winter-2018-issue-ii-volume-cxv/fearless-and-fiery.html ; Swarthmore Friends Historical Library, "Friendly Networks: Lay, Benjamin, 1682-1759," https://ds-pages.swarthmore.edu/friendly-networks/people/w6086z81/ .)
- **1738, Burlington, New Jersey** — at the Philadelphia Yearly Meeting's annual business meeting, Lay stood up, attacked Quaker slaveholders for hypocrisy, and threw off his plain Quaker cloak to show a military uniform underneath, in front of a meeting of people committed to peace. (Source: Swarthmore College Bulletin, "Fearless and Fiery," as above, reporting the work of the historian Marcus Rediker.)
- **What the meetings finally did — both dates belong to era 5, listed here so the chain is visible.** Philadelphia Yearly Meeting issued *An Epistle of Caution and Advice, Concerning the Buying and Keeping of Slaves* in **1754**, telling Quakers to stop buying and selling people; it did not declare slaveholding incompatible with the faith and call for freeing people until about **1774**, roughly twenty years later. (Source: Library Company, as above. **Note a live discrepancy:** the commonly cited date for Philadelphia Yearly Meeting moving to discipline slaveholders is 1758. The Library Company page gives 1754 for the Epistle and about 1774 for the full requirement. **Era 5 should state whichever it can source directly and not blend them.**)
- **The point the outline must make.** Every one of these men was denounced or thrown out by his own religious body. The argument won slowly and from inside, over about eighty-six years from 1688 to 1774. That is what the beginning of a movement actually looks like, and it is why this era has no `hb-story`: no single life carries it.

### Why era 4 has no `hb-story`
Sewall, Sandiford and Lay are all real, named and documented, and any of them could carry a story block. None of them is here as one, for two reasons. First, all three are arguing about slavery, which is `slavery-freedom`'s subject; this chapter takes their argument as the seed of organizing, not slavery itself. Second, the era genuinely has no organized movement, and the brief is explicit that a thin era gets a short honest cell rather than a filled one. **A later agent who wants a story here should take Benjamin Lay, who is used nowhere else in the book as of 2026-09-09.**

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262a): the Quaker dates settled (1754, 1758, 1776, 1778), and Lay at Burlington from the source
- **The discrepancy flagged above is settled, and both dates were right about different acts.** Haverford College Libraries, *Manumitted: The People Enslaved by Quakers*, "About," https://manumissions.haverford.edu/about (fetched 2026-09-27): "In 1754, the Philadelphia Quarterly Meeting and the Yearly Meeting published a paper declaring slavery a sin. This epistle was then sent to many other meetings. In 1758, the Philadelphia Yearly Meeting passed a minute denouncing engagement with the slave trade but not with slaveholding. While the minute passed, there was debate until 1774 over whether the minute suggested slave trading as a disowned offence." Then: "In 1776, the Philadelphia Yearly Meeting agreed to prohibit slaveholding among its members, making it a disownable offense. However, no members were disowned until 1778." It defines disownment: a formal statement by a monthly meeting that a member is not in unity with an essential part of the faith. The member's name comes off the membership list and the member cannot hold certain offices, but can still attend worship.
- **What this changes in the outline.** The era-4 outline says the meetings "did not require members to free the people they held until about twenty years after" the 1750s. The sourced year is **1776**, and the first disownment for holding people is **1778**. The era-4 zoom counts "roughly eighty-six years" from 1688 "to that point" (1774). **From Germantown (1688) to the 1776 rule is 88 years.** A writer should use 1776 and 88, or say "almost ninety years." Same page: New England Yearly Meeting made slaveholding a disownable offence in 1773, New York Yearly Meeting denounced slaveholding in 1774, North Carolina made it disownable in 1776.
- **Benjamin Lay at Burlington, 1738, fuller.** Swarthmore College Bulletin, "Fearless and Fiery" (Winter 2018), https://www.swarthmore.edu/bulletin/archive/winter-2018-issue-ii-volume-cxv/fearless-and-fiery.html (fetched 2026-09-27), from Friends Historical Library: his words to the meeting began "Oh, all you negro masters, who are contentedly holding your fellow-creatures in a state of slavery during life." He threw off his plain cloak to show a military uniform, said it would be as justifiable "if you should thrust a sword through their hearts, as I do through this," and drove a sword into a hollowed book in which he had hidden an animal bladder filled with pokeberry juice, so that red juice sprayed the people near him. He was not hurt. The people at the meeting carried him out and laid him on the doorstep, and he lay there for the rest of the day so that everyone leaving had to step over him. (The article's own retelling adds "Ladies fainted!" as a dramatised line; do not use it.)
- **Lay's body, which belongs in this chapter's disability thread.** Same article: Lay "measured just over 4 feet tall," had "a hunched back, narrow limbs," and lived for a time in a cave with a library of about 200 books. Swarthmore's Friends Historical Library catalogues him under "People with disabilities" (Friendly Networks, https://ds-pages.swarthmore.edu/friendly-networks/people/w6086z81/ , fetched). His wife **Sarah Lay**, a recorded Quaker minister, was also small and had a curved spine, and she stayed a member in good standing all her life though Benjamin was disowned. **Do not diagnose either of them.** State height and shape as the source gives them.
- **Dates.** Born 26 January 1682 in Essex, England; died February 1759 (Friendly Networks gives 3 February in its data line and 8 February in its biography; **two days in one record, print "1759"**). He lived in Barbados from 1718 and saw how enslaved Africans were treated there before settling near Philadelphia. (Friendly Networks, as above.)
- **Story option restated.** Lay is now sourced well enough for an `hb-story` block (named, dated, his own words, a fetched institutional source). The era-4 outline chose no story on purpose. This agent leaves that decision alone and notes that the slug `benjamin-lay` is unused anywhere in the book.

## 5. 1750 to 1800

**What is new in this era.** The first standing organization, and the first time people used the Revolution's own words back at the people who wrote them. Coverture did not change.

### Abigail Adams — the letters, quoted from the manuscripts
The three letters are in the Adams Family Papers at the Massachusetts Historical Society and are transcribed in its Adams Family Papers electronic archive. All three were fetched and quoted directly on 2026-09-09.

- **Abigail to John, 31 March 1776** (the manuscript is dated 31 March - 5 April 1776), https://www.masshist.org/digitaladams/archive/doc?id=L17760331aa : **"I long to hear that you have declared an independency — and by the way in the new Code of Laws which I suppose it will be necessary for you to make I desire you would Remember the Ladies, and be more generous and favourable to them than your ancestors. Do not put such unlimited power into the hands of the Husbands. Remember all Men would be tyrants if they could. If perticuliar care and attention is not paid to the Laidies we are determined to foment a Rebelion, and will not hold ourselves bound by any Laws in which we have no voice, or Representation."** Spelling is hers; keep it.
- **John to Abigail, 14 April 1776**, https://www.masshist.org/digitaladams/archive/doc?id=L17760414ja : **"As to your extraordinary Code of Laws, I cannot but laugh."** He then listed who else had grown bold since the quarrel with Britain began — **"That Children and Apprentices were disobedient — that schools and Colledges were grown turbulent — that Indians slighted their Guardians and Negroes grew insolent to their Masters."** And: rather than give up the men's system, **"which would compleatly subject Us to the Despotism of the Peticoat."**
- **Abigail to John, 7 May 1776** (manuscript dated 7-9 May 1776), https://www.masshist.org/digitaladams/archive/doc?id=L17760507aa : **"I can not say that I think you very generous to the Ladies, for whilst you are proclaiming peace and good will to Men, Emancipating all Nations, you insist upon retaining an absolute power over Wives."** And: **"Arbitary power is like most other things which are very hard, very liable to be broken."**
- **The point that must not be lost.** Nothing came of it. No state wrote women into its new laws because of these letters, and the letters were private — they were not published in her lifetime. She was not running a campaign; she was one woman writing to her husband. That is exactly why the era has no movement to report.
- **Boundary.** `home-family` owns her household and farm management (slug `abigail-adams-home-family`, already `verified`) and states there that the "remember the ladies" letter belongs to this chapter. A feature film exists in which she is a lead character but not the subject: *John Adams* (2008 HBO miniseries) — `home-family` carries it with that caveat and this chapter matches it.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262r): Abigail Adams story, two supporting facts
- **Where she was.** John was away at the Continental Congress from 1774, and Abigail ran the **Braintree (now Quincy)** farm and finances. (Carried in `research/research-home-family.md`, story `abigail-adams-home-family`, verified.)
- **The letters were private until 1840.** Her grandson **Charles Francis Adams** published *Letters of Mrs. Adams, the Wife of John Adams* in **1840**. It became a bestseller. Before that the letters "remained private," and he had to ask his father, John Quincy Adams, for permission even to "narrate the private manuscripts in public" in an 1838 lecture. That lecture left out the "Remember the Ladies" plea. (Source: Massachusetts Historical Society, *The Beehive* blog, "An Adams Tells All About Abigail," February 2019, https://www.masshist.org/beehiveblog/2019/02/an-adams-tells-all-about-abigail/ .) The Beehive says she had died "two decades" before the 1838 lecture, so the bank's "not published in her lifetime" holds.

### New Jersey let some women and free Black men vote, 1776 to 1807 — **and it is the coverture rule that decided which women**
- **The 1776 New Jersey constitution gave the vote to "all inhabitants of this colony, of full age, who are worth fifty pounds ... and have resided within the county ... for twelve months."** It did not say male. (Sources: NPS, "Did You Know: Women and African Americans Could Vote in NJ before the 15th and 19th Amendments?", https://www.nps.gov/articles/voting-rights-in-nj-before-the-15th-and-19th.htm ; Library of Congress exhibition, "New Jersey Women Gain, then Lose the Vote," https://www.loc.gov/exhibitions/women-fight-for-the-vote/about-this-exhibition/seneca-falls-and-building-a-movement-1776-1890/early-feminist-inspirations/new-jersey-women-gain-then-lose-the-vote/ ; New Jersey Department of State, "The Petticoat Politicians of 1776: New Jersey's First Female Voters," https://nj.gov/state/historical/assets/pdf/it-happened-here/ihhnj-er-petticoat-politicians.pdf ; Center for American Women and Politics, Rutgers, Irwin N. Gertzog, "Female Suffrage in New Jersey, 1790-1807," https://cawp.rutgers.edu/sites/default/files/resources/femalesuffrageinnj1790_1807.pdf .)
- **Only unmarried women could qualify, because a married woman could not own the fifty pounds** — coverture put everything she had in her husband's hands. The rule from era 3 is what decided who voted. (Same sources.)
- **Free Black men who met the property test could vote under the same clause.** (Source: NPS, as above.)
- **Many unmarried women voted in the 1790s and the early 1800s.** A 1797 election law required voters to be free inhabitants. **In 1807 the New Jersey legislature restricted the vote to tax-paying white male citizens** — which removed both groups at once. The stated party motive: women largely voted Federalist, and the Democratic-Republicans wanted the 1808 presidential election. (Sources: NPS; LOC; Rutgers/Gertzog, as above.)
- **Date note for era 6:** the removal is 1807 and therefore era 6 by the boundary rule. The whole arc is told once, in era 5, because splitting it would leave the reader with a grant and no ending.

### Free Black petitions in Massachusetts — the school claim starts here
- **1787: Prince Hall and others petitioned the Massachusetts General Court** for schooling. Their words: they **"receive no benefit from the free schools in the town of Boston, which we think is a great grievance, as by woful [sic] experience we now feel the want of a common education."** **The petition failed.** A second petition in **1796** failed too. (Source: National Park Service, Boston African American National Historic Site, "The Black Struggle for Equal Education in Boston, 1787 to 1976," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/the-black-struggle-for-equal-education-in-boston-1787-to-1976.htm .)
- **1798: the community built its own school** — the "African School," meeting in the home of **Primus Hall**, Prince Hall's son. (Same NPS source. What happened next — the African Meeting House basement in 1808 and the Abiel Smith School in the mid-1830s — is era 6, below.)
- **Related manuscripts, catalogued not fetched:** MHS, "Petition of Prince Hall to the Massachusetts General Court, 27 February 1788," https://www.masshist.org/database/670 ; MHS, "Petition for freedom ... [13] January 1777," https://www.masshist.org/database/557 (the 1777 freedom petition is `slavery-freedom`'s, not this chapter's).

### The first antislavery society, 14 April 1775
- **The Society for the Relief of Free Negroes Unlawfully Held in Bondage** was formed at the Rising Sun Tavern in Philadelphia on **14 April 1775**, by a group of mostly Quaker men. It is **the first abolition society in the western world**. Its first work was helping free people of colour who had been seized and held as slaves. In **1789** it took a state charter as the Pennsylvania Society for Promoting the Abolition of Slavery, for the Relief of Free Negroes Unlawfully Held in Bondage, and for Improving the Condition of the African Race. (Sources: Historical Society of Pennsylvania, "What was America's first abolitionist organization?", https://hsp.org/blogs/question-of-the-week/what-was-americas-first-abolitionist-organization ; HSP, "PAS in Context: A Timeline," https://hsp.org/pas-context-timeline ; HSP, Pennsylvania Abolition Society papers 0490 finding aid, https://www2.hsp.org/collections/manuscripts/p/PAS0490.html .)
- **Claim discipline.** "First abolition society in the western world" is HSP's claim and is stated as such. Do **not** upgrade it to "the first organization for anyone's rights in America," which no source here supports.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262a): the 1775 society, named. Who founded it, the woman whose case started it, and what the courts did to her
- **Source.** Historical Society of Pennsylvania, Pennsylvania Abolition Society papers (Collection 0490), finding aid historical note, https://www2.hsp.org/collections/manuscripts/p/PAS0490.html (fetched 2026-09-27).
- **The case that started it.** "In 1773, Benjamin Bannarman, a resident of Virginia, purchased an Indian woman named **Dinah Nevil** and her four children from Nathaniel Lowry of New Jersey." When they were delivered in Philadelphia, "Nevil protested publicly that she and her children were 'free people.'" The mayor of Philadelphia sent her and the children to the city's Work House to wait for a hearing. "Under the leadership of **Israel Pemberton**, a group of Quaker citizens entered a suit on behalf of Dinah Nevil." The case ran two years, "at the end of which the court declared Dinah Nevil and her children were slaves." That ruling, and similar cases, led "several citizens ... to meet and forme themselves into a Society." So the first antislavery society in the English colonies was formed to help a Native woman who said she was free, after a court ruled she was not. (The finding aid does not name the judges. The mayor is not named either.)
- **Who they were.** "Sixteen of the original twenty-four members were Quakers." (So the outline's "mostly Quaker men" is right: 16 of 24.) **Thomas Harrison** was secretary in 1775. Anthony Benezet was a member. A search summary names **John Baldwin** as the first president **(unconfirmed: search summary only; the fetched HSP note does not name the 1775 president)**.
- **It stopped almost at once.** The society met through 1775, still trying to free Nevil. "In November, however, formal meetings ceased," because the members thought Quaker refusal to fight in the war would discredit the society. The outline's line "An organization outlives the people who start it" should not imply that it ran without a break.
- **How Dinah Nevil got free: she was bought.** "In 1781 they secured the freedom of Dinah Nevil. Thomas Harrison ... into whose care Nevil and her children had been committed, purchased the family with his own funds and manumitted them. Continued opposition of the courts to Nevil's claims left Harrison no alternative." Manumitted means legally freed by the person who held them. **Eight years after she said she was free, a court had still not agreed, and a member of the society paid her claimed owner.**
- **Revival.** The society reorganised and approved a new constitution on 23 April 1787, took non-Quakers, and elected **Benjamin Franklin** president. Later members included Benjamin Rush, Thomas Paine and John Jay. Other states copied it: New York 1785, then Delaware, Maryland and Connecticut by 1790. In 1787 Richard Allen, Absalom Jones and other Black Philadelphians formed their own Free African Society. (HSP note, as above.)
- **Name for the writer.** Dinah Nevil is a real, named, documented person with a documented claim to a right. She is a candidate for an ordinary-person `hb-story` in era 5 if a writer wants one. She appears nowhere else in the book (grep 2026-09-27).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262a): New Jersey, 1776 to 1807. Named voters, a count, the 1807 fraud, and a dispute about which party wanted women out
- **A count of women who voted, and named ones.** Museum of the American Revolution, virtual exhibit *When Women Lost the Vote*, "Exploring New Jersey Voters, 1800 - 1807," https://www.amrevmuseum.org/virtualexhibits/when-women-lost-the-vote-a-revolutionary-story/pages/exploring-new-jersey-voters-1800-1807 (fetched 2026-09-27): "As of 2020, the Museum of the American Revolution has identified **163 women voters** named on **nine poll lists** dated between 1800 - 1807 from across New Jersey." Named examples on the same page: the 1800 state election poll list for Bedminster Township, Somerset County, names **Sarah Eoff, Margaret McDonald and Eleanor Boylan**. Eleanor Boylan voted aged about 51 as a widow and died in 1846 aged about 97. The 1801 Montgomery Township list includes "at least 46 women and at least four free Black men," among them **Caesar Trent** of Princeton. Four women of one family, **Rebecca, Ann, Catherine and Sarah VanDike**, voted together in October 1801. 163 is a floor: it counts only the names on the nine lists that survive. **These are the ordinary people era 5 does not yet have.**
- **Married women: the rule and the complaints.** Jan Ellen Lewis, "Rethinking Women's Suffrage in New Jersey, 1776-1807," *Rutgers Law Review* 63:3 (2011), https://www.rutgerslawreview.com/wp-content/uploads/archive/vol63/Issue3/Lewis.pdf (fetched and read with pypdf 2026-09-27): in 1800 the assembly turned down as unneeded a proposal to spell out that propertied single women could vote, saying "Our Constitution gives this right to maids or widows black or white." In 1802 an election was contested on the ground that "married women voted," and the assembly refused to overturn it. Lewis notes that this evidence "comes from complaints, typically from those who lost the elections." **The outline's "Married women could not" is the law as the property rule worked. The record also holds complaints that some married women voted anyway. A writer who wants to be exact can say "The law left married women out, because of coverture."**
- **Changes in the law's own wording.** Lewis, as above: 1777 and 1783 election laws called the voter "he." A 1790 law for seven counties said "he or she." The 1797 law used "he or she" for all the counties. The 1807 law restricted the vote to "free, white, male citizens." The New Jersey Historical Commission teaching sheet (below) quotes the 1797 law: voters should "openly and in full view deliver his or her ballot."
- **The 1807 fraud.** Lewis, as above: "The impetus was the raucous Essex county referendum in that year to determine whether the new courthouse would be built in Newark or Elizabeth. This was an intraparty squabble among Democratic Republicans." Turnout in Elizabeth was **279 percent** of eligible voters, from the historian Carl Prince. A later account (Turner, written decades afterwards) claimed that women and girls voted "again and again" and that "men and boys disguised as women voted once more." Lewis calls that claim hard to assess. Museum of the American Revolution, "Why Did Women Lose the Vote?: The Backlash," https://www.amrevmuseum.org/virtualexhibits/when-women-lost-the-vote-a-revolutionary-story/pages/how-did-women-lose-the-vote-the-backlash (fetched 2026-09-27): the courthouse election was in **February 1807**. "Both sides broke the rules and recruited people to vote who were not eligible." In **November 1807** the legislature "stripped the vote from women, people of color, and recent immigrants." The act's preamble cited "doubts" about "the admission of aliens, persons of color, or negroes, to vote," and by making every white male taxpayer eligible it "effectively scapegoated women, people of color, and 'aliens' for all the state's voting irregularities." A search summary names **John Condict** of Essex County as the legislator who introduced the bill **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**.
- **DISPUTE, record both, do not pick one.** (a) NPS, "Did You Know: Women and African Americans Could Vote in NJ," https://www.nps.gov/articles/voting-rights-in-nj-before-the-15th-and-19th.htm (fetched 2026-09-27): the 1807 law "was done to give the Democratic-Republican Party an advantage in the 1808 presidential election. Women often voted for the opposing Federalist Party." This is what the outline says.
