(b) New Jersey Historical Commission, *It Happened Here: New Jersey*, "The Petticoat Politicians of 1776," https://nj.gov/state/historical/assets/pdf/it-happened-here/ihhnj-er-petticoat-politicians.pdf (fetched and read 2026-09-27): after Jefferson won in 1800, "Federalists came to believe female voters benefitted the Republican cause," and the 1807 change was made "less to limit the rights of women and more to try to control party politics." (c) Museum of the American Revolution and Lewis: the 1807 law followed an internal Democratic-Republican fraud fight and was framed as election reform. **All three agree the motive was party politics and not a debate about women's rights. They disagree about which party women helped.** The outline's single sentence ("women mostly voted Federalist, and the Democratic-Republicans wanted the 1808 presidential election") states one side of a dispute as settled. The writer should say that the men who passed the law were fighting over party advantage and a fraud scandal, and that historians disagree about which party women's votes helped.
- **Other states' 1776 wording, for the "first" claim.** NJ Historical Commission, as above: other 1776 constitutions used "freeman" (Pennsylvania, Maryland, Delaware, North Carolina), "man" (Vermont), "white male inhabitants" (Georgia) or "free white man" (South Carolina), and "all of them excluded women." That supports "the only state where women voted."

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262a): two rights claims from era 5 the bank lacked
- **Paul and John Cuffe, Dartmouth, Massachusetts, 1780: no taxation without a vote.** Petition text: Gilder Lehrman Institute, Hamilton Education Program, "Petition against Taxation without Representation for Free Black Men, 1780," https://hamilton.gilderlehrman.org/supporting-document/petition-against-taxation-without-representation-free-black-men-1780 (fetched 2026-09-27), transcribed from William Cooper Nell, *The Colored Patriots of the American Revolution* (1855). Addressed to the Massachusetts General Court, "The petition of several poor negroes and mulattoes, who are inhabitants of the town of Dartmouth," it says they are "chiefly of the African extract, and by reason of long bondage and hard slavery, we have been deprived of enjoying the profits of our labor or the advantage of inheriting estates from our parents," and that they are taxed although "we are not allowed the privilege of freemen of the State, having no vote or influence in the election of those that tax us." It adds: "many of our colour (as is well known) have cheerfully entered the field of battle in the defence of the common cause." Signers: **John Cuffe, Adventur Child, Paul Cuffe, Samuel Gray, Pero Howland, Pero Russell and Pero Coggeshall** (Gray, Howland and Russell signed with a mark). The New Bedford Light, "The revolutionary imprisonments of Paul Cuffe" (Colin Hogan, June 2026), https://newbedfordlight.org/the-revolutionary-imprisonments-of-paul-cuffe/ (fetched, journalism drawing on Jeffrey A. Fortin's 2024 biography *Captain Paul Cuffe, Yeoman*): the petition went in **February 1780**; the Massachusetts constitution of March 1780 did not answer it; the brothers then wrote to the Bristol County court and the Dartmouth selectmen, calling themselves both "Indian men" and "free Negroes"; and "for withholding their taxes, the Cuffes were thrown in jail." The jail date given in search summaries is December 1780 **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**. Who jailed them: the fetched article says "his Dartmouth neighbors imprisoned him," meaning the town's tax officials; it names no one. Shared with `slavery-freedom` (freedom petitions of the same years) only by context. **This is the clearest era-5 case of people quoting the Revolution's own words back at the men who wrote them, which is the era-5 zoom's claim. The outline currently supports that claim only with Abigail Adams's private letter.**
- **Judith Sargent Murray, "On the Equality of the Sexes," 1790.** National Women's History Museum, "Judith Sargent Murray," https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/judith-sargent-murray (fetched 2026-09-27): born 1 May 1751 in Gloucester, Massachusetts; the essay ran in the *Massachusetts Magazine* in 1790 (Boston: I. Thomas and E. T. Andrews, vol. II, pp. 132-135) under the pen name "Constantia." It argued that men and women have equal minds and that women were held back by being denied education: "in what the minds of females are so notoriously deficient, or unequal." Search summaries add that she wrote a first version in 1779 and passed it among friends before printing it **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**. Unlike Abigail Adams's letters, this argument was printed and public in 1790. `education` may also carry it: grep 2026-09-27 found "Sargent Murray" in no bank or outline.

## 6. 1800 to 1850

### [VERIFY] the American School for the Deaf, 1817 — CLEARED
Already researched and sourced in this project's `research/research-education.md` §6j; restated here so this chapter stands on its own.
- **The American School for the Deaf opened at Hartford, Connecticut on 15 April 1817**, in Bennett's City Hotel. Founders: **Thomas Hopkins Gallaudet** and **Laurent Clerc**, a deaf French teacher Gallaudet brought back from Paris, backed by **Dr. Mason Fitch Cogswell** and ten other Hartford men. Cogswell's deaf daughter **Alice Cogswell** was among the first pupils. **Seven full-time students** in the first classes. Teaching was in sign language, on the French method. (Sources: American School for the Deaf, "History & Cogswell Heritage House," https://www.asd-1817.org/about/history--cogswell-heritage-house ; Today in Connecticut History, https://todayincthistory.com/2020/04/15/april-15-american-school-for-the-deaf-founded-in-hartford/ ; Gallaudet University Museum, https://gallaudet.edu/museum/history/ .)
- **Public money followed:** the Connecticut General Assembly made the first annual state grant in **1819**, the first state aid to a school in America, and **Congress gave the school a land grant in the Alabama Territory in 1820**, the first federal aid to special education. (Same source.)
- **`education` leads on the school as a school.** This chapter's angle is that a group of deaf people schooled together in one place produced a signing community — the beginning of deaf people acting as a group, which is where American disability organizing starts.

### [VERIFY] female academies and seminaries — named founders, schools and dates — CLEARED
- **Emma Willard.** In **1819** she wrote *A Plan for Improving Female Education*, addressed to the New York State legislature, arguing that young women needed the same subjects young men were taught. With support from Governor DeWitt Clinton she moved her school to Troy, New York and founded the **Troy Female Seminary in 1821**. (Sources: Emma Willard School, "History," https://www.emmawillard.org/about/history ; National Park Service, "Emma Willard School," https://www.nps.gov/places/willardschool.htm ; Library of Congress, "Emma Hart Willard: Pioneering Geography Educator," https://blogs.loc.gov/maps/2016/03/emma-hart-willard/ ; Thomas Jefferson's reply to her, 18 December 1819, Founders Online, https://founders.archives.gov/documents/Jefferson/03-15-02-0266 .)
- **Catharine Beecher.** Opened the **Hartford Female Seminary on 20 May 1823** with her sister Mary. Pupils did geography, world history, Latin, rhetoric, philosophy and the natural sciences rather than the manners-and-fine-arts course usual in girls' schools. **100 full-time pupils within three years.** (Sources already in `research/research-education.md` §6d: Today in Connecticut History, https://todayincthistory.com/2020/05/20/may-20-catharine-beecher-opens-hartford-female-seminary/ ; National Women's History Museum, https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/catharine-esther-beecher .) **`education` already carries Beecher as a story (`catharine-beecher-education`, verified) — do not duplicate her as a story here.**
- **Mary Lyon.** Began raising money in **1834** and founded **Mount Holyoke Female Seminary at South Hadley, Massachusetts in 1837**. She raised it in **donations from six cents to $1,000, plus quilts and bedding from women's sewing circles and stoves and furniture from trustees.** Tuition was **$60 a year**. Mount Holyoke's own account says she "endured ridicule from those who felt her ambitious undertaking would be 'wasted' on women," and that "The idea of a college for women was horrifying to many people in the 1830s." (Source: Mount Holyoke College, "The Founding of Mount Holyoke Female Seminary," https://offices.mtholyoke.edu/marylyon/founding ; "Notable Women Educators of Mary Lyon's Era," https://offices.mtholyoke.edu/marylyon/women_educators .) **The exact opening day (commonly given as 8 November 1837) and the first-year enrolment were not confirmed from a fetched source on 2026-09-09; the outline says 1837 and no more.**

### [VERIFY] the first college to admit women alongside men — CLEARED, and the usual one-line version is wrong
- **Oberlin Collegiate Institute, in Oberlin, Ohio, enrolled women beside men from its opening in 1833** — but not in the degree course. Women were put in what the college called the **Ladies Course**, which gave a diploma, not a bachelor's degree.
- **In 1837 four women entered the Collegiate Department, the course that led to a bachelor's degree: Mary Kellogg, Mary Caroline Rudd, Mary Hosford and Elizabeth Prall.** Three of them graduated in **1841**; Kellogg had left for lack of money and came back later. Oberlin was **the first college to grant bachelor's degrees to women in a coeducational programme (1841)**, and the first to adopt a policy of admitting Black students (**1835**).
- (Sources: Oberlin College, "Beyond Coeducation: Oberlin College and Women's History," https://www2.oberlin.edu/external/EOG/womenshist/women.html ; Oberlin College, "Oberlin History," https://www.oberlin.edu/about-oberlin/oberlin-history ; Oberlin College 175th anniversary timeline, https://www2.oberlin.edu/175/timeline.html .)
- **Write the two dates, not one.** "Oberlin admitted women in 1833" and "Oberlin gave women degrees in 1841" are both true and mean different things. Collapsing them into one date is gist extraction.

### [VERIFY] "study would damage a girl's health" — **NOT CLEARED. The claim is being removed from era 6, not hedged.**
Searching on 2026-09-09 turned up no quoted example of this argument from 1800-1850 in a usable source. What the record does give is later: the medical argument that study made women infertile or broke their health is documented from the 1850s onward, and its classic statement is **Edward H. Clarke, *Sex in Education; or, A Fair Chance for the Girls* (Boston, 1873)** — **era 7, not era 6.** Mary Putnam Jacobi's answer to it is also era 7. (Pointers: National Women's History Museum, "Mary Putnam Jacobi," https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/mary-putnam-jacobi ; nineteenth-century medical-journal reprints at PMC6919709, PMC6004221, PMC8846743.)
**Action taken:** the claim is out of the era-6 outline. What era 6 can honestly say instead is what Mount Holyoke's own account says — that the idea of a college for women was horrifying to many people in the 1830s and that Mary Lyon was ridiculed. **HANDED TO THE ERAS 7-8 AGENT: Clarke 1873, with a verbatim quotation, belongs in era 7.**

### [VERIFY] free Black children shut out, and the schools their communities built — CLEARED (Boston, named throughout)
The whole chain, from one NPS source: National Park Service, Boston African American National Historic Site, "The Black Struggle for Equal Education in Boston, 1787 to 1976," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/the-black-struggle-for-equal-education-in-boston-1787-to-1976.htm .
- **1787** — Prince Hall and others petitioned the Massachusetts General Court: they **"receive no benefit from the free schools in the town of Boston, which we think is a great grievance, as by woful [sic] experience we now feel the want of a common education."** Failed. **1796** — a second petition. Failed. (Era 5.)
- **1798** — the community founded the **African School** in the home of **Primus Hall**, Prince Hall's son. (Era 5.)
- **1808** — the school moved to **the basement of the African Meeting House**. (Era 6.)
- **mid-1830s** — it moved into the **Abiel Smith School**, which became the segregated school where most Black pupils in Boston were concentrated. (Era 6.)
- **late 1840s** — **William Cooper Nell** and others organised **boycotts and petition drives** against the Smith School, and **Benjamin Roberts sued the city for his daughter Sarah.** (Era 6; `education` already tells the Roberts case as a story, `sarah-roberts-education`, verified — do not duplicate it here.)
- **1855** — the Massachusetts legislature outlawed segregated public schools. (Era 7. **Handed to the eras 7-8 agent.**)

### [VERIFY] Prudence Crandall — CLEARED, with names, town and dates
- **Autumn 1832, Canterbury, Connecticut.** **Sarah Harris**, a young Black woman whose father distributed the abolitionist newspaper *The Liberator*, asked to attend the Canterbury Female Boarding School, which Prudence Crandall ran. Harris had finished primary school and wanted enough learning to teach Black children at Norwich. Her words: **"I want to get a little more learning, if possible enough to teach colored children,"** and, **"If you think it will be the means of injuring you, I will not insist on the favor."** Crandall admitted her. The white families pulled their daughters out. (Sources: National Park Service, "(H)our History Lesson: Prudence Crandall, Sarah Harris, and a Struggle for Black Women's Education," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/-h-our-history-lesson-prudence-crandall-sarah-harris-and-a-struggle-for-black-women-s-education.htm ; Connecticut History, a CT Humanities project, https://connecticuthistory.org/prudence-crandall-fights-for-equal-access-to-education/ .)
- **Spring 1833.** Crandall went to Boston to see William Lloyd Garrison, then travelled New England recruiting Black families. Newspaper advertisements said that from **1 April 1833** the school would reopen for **"young ladies and little misses of color."** Enrolment reached about **24 pupils**, most of them boarders. (Sources: Connecticut History; NPS, as above; Today in Connecticut History, https://todayincthistory.com/2018/06/27/june-27-prudence-crandall-arrested/ .)
- **24 May 1833 — the Black Law.** The Connecticut General Assembly made it illegal for a school in the state to take Black pupils from outside Connecticut without the town's permission. The law was written to shut this one school. (Sources: Connecticut History; NPS.)
- **27 June 1833 — arrested, and one night in jail.** (Sources: NPS; Today in Connecticut History, which carries the date in three separate dated articles. **Conflict named:** connecticuthistory.org gives 23 August 1833 for the arrest, which is more likely the first trial. The outline uses June 1833 for the arrest and does not give a day for the first trial.)
- **The trials.** First trial: the jury could not agree. **October 1833: convicted**, after **Judge David Daggett** instructed that Black Americans were not citizens and so had no claim to the protections Crandall's lawyers relied on. **26 July 1834: the Connecticut Supreme Court threw the case out on a technical point** — not on the merits, so the Black Law stood. (Sources: NPS; Connecticut History.)
- **The attacks.** Shopkeepers refused to sell to the school. Pupils were pelted with stones and eggs. **January 1834: someone tried to burn the building.** **The night of 9 September 1834: a mob broke more than ninety windows and went through the house with weapons while Crandall and her pupils were inside.** She closed the school the next morning. (Sources: NPS; Connecticut History.)
- **Afterwards.** Crandall married the Baptist minister Calvin Philleo and moved to Illinois and then Kansas. Connecticut voted her a $400 yearly pension four years before she died, in **1890**, in Kansas, aged 87. **Sarah Harris became an abolitionist and an Underground Railroad conductor**, and in **1877** travelled to Kansas to see Crandall; the two wrote to each other about their former pupils. (Sources: NPS; Connecticut History; Connecticut state portal, "The State Heroine — Prudence Crandall (1803-1890)," https://portal.ct.gov/About/State-Symbols/The-State-Heroine---Prudence-Crandall-1803-1890/ ; Prudence Crandall Museum, https://portal.ct.gov/decd/content/historic-preservation/04_state_museums/prudence-crandall-museum .)
- **[VERIFY] on the target story "a teacher prosecuted for teaching Black or female pupils" is CLEARED by this.** The slot is filled with **Prudence Crandall and Sarah Harris**, both real and named. **The slug was changed from `teacher-driven-out` to `prudence-crandall`** so it names the person; the only other file carrying the old slug is the generated `outlines/BOOK-OUTLINE.md`.

### Elizabeth Cady Stanton and Lucretia Mott, and the Seneca Falls convention of 1848
All from the National Park Service, Women's Rights National Historical Park, and the Library of Congress, fetched 2026-09-09.

- **Stanton.** Born **12 November 1815 at Johnstown, New York**; died **1902**. She moved to Seneca Falls in **1847**. On what pushed her: **"The general discontent I felt with women's portion as wife, mother, housekeeper ... impressed me with a strange feeling that some active measures should be taken."** (Source: NPS, "Elizabeth Cady Stanton," https://www.nps.gov/wori/learn/historyculture/elizabeth-cady-stanton.htm .)
- **Mott.** Born **1793 on Nantucket, Massachusetts**; died **1880**. Quaker; schooled at Nine Partners, a Quaker boarding school. She helped found the **Philadelphia Female Anti-Slavery Society in 1833** and was a delegate to the World Anti-Slavery Convention in London in **1840**. **She was 55 at Seneca Falls.** In 1866 she became the first president of the American Equal Rights Association. (Source: NPS, "Lucretia Mott," https://www.nps.gov/wori/learn/historyculture/lucretia-mott.htm .)
- **How they met, and what was done to the women delegates in London, 1840.** Elected women delegates **"were refused admission because of their sex."** After argument, **"it was decided that the women would sit at the rear of the hall, but not participate."** Stanton, on her honeymoon, met Mott in the women's section. They **"agreed to hold a woman's rights convention on their return to America."** (Sources: NPS Stanton page; NPS Mott page, as above.)
- **The tea, 9 July 1848.** **Five women** met at a social gathering at Waterloo, New York and decided to hold a convention: **Stanton, Mott, Mary Ann M'Clintock, Martha Coffin Wright and Jane Hunt.** They put a call in the local paper inviting people to **"...a Convention to discuss the social, civil and religious rights and condition of woman."** (Sources: NPS, "Declaration of Sentiments: The First Women's Rights Convention," https://www.nps.gov/articles/declaration-of-sentiments-the-first-women-s-rights-convention.htm ; NPS Mott page.)
- **The convention.** **19 and 20 July 1848, at the Wesleyan Chapel in Seneca Falls, New York**, three miles east of Waterloo. **Over 300 people came.** **One hundred signed the Declaration of Sentiments — 68 women and 32 men.** Frederick Douglass was there. (Sources: NPS, "The First Women's Rights Convention," https://www.nps.gov/wori/learn/historyculture/the-first-womens-rights-convention.htm ; NPS Declaration article, as above.)
- **The Declaration of Sentiments** was written by Stanton with the M'Clintock family and **modelled on the Declaration of Independence.** It opens: **"When, in the course of human events, it becomes necessary for one portion of the family of man to assume among the people of the earth a position different from that which they have hitherto occupied..."** Grievances quoted verbatim from the NPS text: **"He has never permitted her to exercise her inalienable right to the elective franchise."** · **"He has taken from her all right in property, even to the wages she earns."** · **"He has denied her the facilities for obtaining a thorough education - all colleges being closed against her."** (Source: NPS, "Declaration of Sentiments," https://www.nps.gov/wori/learn/historyculture/declaration-of-sentiments.htm .)
- **The one resolution that was fought over.** Eleven resolutions passed unanimously. **The resolution calling for the vote met opposition, and passed only narrowly.** **Frederick Douglass stood with Stanton and argued for it.** The Library of Congress dates the seventy-two years of organized suffrage campaigning from that vote to the Nineteenth Amendment in 1920. (Sources: Library of Congress exhibition, *Shall Not Be Denied*, "Frederick Douglass Speaks in Support," https://www.loc.gov/exhibitions/women-fight-for-the-vote/about-this-exhibition/seneca-falls-and-building-a-movement-1776-1890/seneca-falls-and-the-start-of-annual-conventions/frederick-douglass-speaks-in-support/ ; U.S. House of Representatives, History, Art & Archives, "The Women's Rights Movement, 1848-1917," https://history.house.gov/Exhibitions-and-Publications/WIC/Historical-Essays/No-Lady/Womens-Rights/ .)
- **The grievance about property and wages is coverture,** the rule set out in era 3. Say so in the outline; it is the same rule, still in force in 1848.
- **NOT CLAIMED here:** who chaired the convention. The NPS pages fetched on 2026-09-09 do not say, and this bank does not repeat the common statement that James Mott presided because a woman could not.

### Dorothea Dix — what she found, in her own words
**Clinical-word note (policy §3b).** The word used at the time was *insane*, and the outline must say what it meant and what was done. It meant people whose minds worked in ways their families or their town could not manage. What was done to them is stated below in Dix's own words, with the instruments named.
- **28 March 1841.** Dix went to teach a Sunday school class at the East Cambridge jail in Massachusetts and found women who were mentally ill locked in with convicted criminals, in "a bare, foul-smelling, unheated jail room." The authorities denied her account and then improved those women's conditions. (Source: Mass Moments, a project of Mass Humanities, "Dorothea Dix Begins Her Crusade," https://www.massmoments.org/moment-details/dorothea-dix-begins-her-crusade.html .)
- **What she did next.** She went round **every jail, poorhouse and house of correction in Massachusetts** and wrote down what she saw in each. (Same source.)
- **The Memorial to the Legislature of Massachusetts, January 1843 — verbatim.** **"I come as the advocate of the helpless, forgotten, insane men and women held in cages, closets, cellars, stalls, pens! Chained, naked, beaten with rods, and lashed into obedience!"** Her specific entries include **a woman in a cage at Lincoln**, and at **Medford** one person **"chained, and one in a close stall for seventeen years."** (Sources: Mass Moments, as above; the printed Memorial itself, Library of Congress, https://www.loc.gov/item/11006306 and https://www.loc.gov/item/20020815 ; National Library of Medicine digital copy, https://resource.nlm.nih.gov/101174442 .)
- **Plain-words gloss the outline must supply:** a stall and a pen are enclosures built for animals; a rod is a stick used for beating. "Lashed" means whipped.
- **What came of it.** Publishers reprinted the Memorial as a pamphlet and newspapers ran extracts, and it caused an uproar. **The Massachusetts legislature voted money to enlarge the state hospital at Worcester.** In **1843 there were 13 mental hospitals in the United States; by 1880 there were 123**, and **Dix had a direct hand in founding 32 of them.** (Source: Mass Moments, as above.)
- **Dates.** Born **1802**, died **1887**, aged 85. (Source: Mass Moments.)
- **HANDED TO LATER-ERA AGENTS:** the institutions Dix campaigned for became, by the twentieth century, places where disabled people were held for life in conditions of their own. That is era 8-9 material and is not stated in era 6, where it would be an unsourced judgment on her.
- **Boundary:** `health` leads on medicine and hospitals as medicine. This chapter's angle is that Dix's method — go and look, write down what you see, publish it, and hand the count to lawmakers — is the same method Ida B. Wells later used on lynching, and that it is a rights campaign rather than a medical advance.

### Married women's property acts — where coverture starts to break, and where it started
- **Mississippi, 1839 — the first state.** The Mississippi Married Women's Property Act of 1839 let a married woman hold property in her own name by bequest, gift, purchase or inheritance, so long as it did not come from her husband. Mississippi was the first state to do it. (Sources: New-York Historical Society, *Women & the American Story*, "Married Women's Property Act," https://wams.nyhistory.org/expansions-and-inequalities/politics-and-society/married-womens-property-act/ ; NPS, "Betsy Love — The First Married American Woman to Gain Rights to Property," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/betsy-love-the-first-american-woman-to-gain-rights-to-property.htm .)
- **The case behind it, and the fact that must not be dropped.** **Betsy Love** was Chickasaw, through her mother Sally Colbert. Her husband owed money to a man named **John Fisher**, who moved to have **Toney — an enslaved person Betsy Love had inherited from her father Thomas Love and given to her daughter Susan — sold at auction to pay the husband's debt.** In **1837 the Mississippi Supreme Court ruled that Betsy Love was the legal owner and that her husband had no rights over her personal property**, because Chickasaw society is matrilineal, because Mississippi had recognised Chickasaw marriage contracts in 1830, and because "Property in the Chickasaw Nation was owned by the women of the tribe." (Source: NPS, as above.)
- **State it whole.** The first crack in coverture in American law came from a Chickasaw woman's case, and the property the court was arguing over was a human being. Writing this as "a woman won the right to keep her property" would be sanitization by omission.
- **New York, 1848.** New York passed its Married Women's Property Act in **1848, months before the Seneca Falls convention.** Its terms: the real and personal property a woman owned at marriage would not be subject to her husband's disposal, would not be liable for his debts, and would continue as her sole and separate property. It became the model for other states. It did not end coverture; it removed one part of it. (Sources: New-York Historical Society, as above; National Women's History Museum, "AN ACT for the effectual protection of the property of married women," https://www.womenshistory.org/resources/primary-source/act-effectual-protection-property-married-women .)
- **NOT CLAIMED:** that the 1848 New York act protected a wife's wages. One search summary said so; the quoted statutory language covers property, not earnings. **A later agent should check the 1860 New York act for wages before any chapter states it.**

### Sojourner Truth — **the era-6 story slot is REPLACED, and here is the reason**
The workspace asked the director to confirm her placement. **Decision taken 2026-09-09: her `hb-story` block leaves era 6 and the slug `sojourner-truth` is reserved for era 7.**
- **What era 6 actually contains for her.** She was born **Isabella Bomfree, 1797, Ulster County, New York**; died **26 November 1883 at Battle Creek, Michigan**. In **1843** she said the Spirit had called her to preach the truth and took the name Sojourner Truth, then travelled through Long Island, Connecticut and Massachusetts speaking and singing. In **1844** she joined the **Northampton Association of Education and Industry** in Massachusetts, a community founded by abolitionists that supported women's rights, religious tolerance and pacifism and was a stop on the Underground Railroad. It broke up in **1850**. (Sources: National Women's History Museum, "Sojourner Truth," https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/sojourner-truth ; NPS, "Sojourner Truth," https://www.nps.gov/people/sojourner-truth.htm ; NPS, Women's Rights NHP, "Sojourner Truth," https://www.nps.gov/wori/learn/historyculture/sojourner-truth.htm .)
- **What era 6 does not contain.** NWHM: **her formal lecture tour began in 1851**, and the Ohio Women's Rights Convention at Akron was part of it. Her *Narrative* was published in 1850. **Both are era 7 by the project's boundary rule.** Her rights-movement work is an era-7 subject; era 6 has the renaming and the community she joined, and that is not enough to carry a featured story in this chapter.
- **So:** era 6 carries her in `hb-zoom` prose. **Era 7 gets the story block, slug `sojourner-truth`, and it is free.**
- **HANDED TO THE ERAS 7-8 AGENT, and this matters.** The famous version of the Akron speech — the one with "And ain't I a woman?" repeated through it — is **not** a transcript. It is Frances Dana Gage's rendering, published in 1863, twelve years after the speech, and it puts a Southern dialect into the mouth of a woman who grew up speaking Dutch in New York. A report printed at the time reads differently. **Do not quote the 1863 version as if Truth said those words that day.** State the two versions and say which is which; this is exactly what the book's `how-we-know` afterword is for. NWHM quotes the Gage version ("And ain't I a woman? Look at me! Look at my arm! I have ploughed and planted, and gathered into barns" and "I have borne thirteen children, and seen most all sold off to slavery") without that caution — check it before using it.
- **Boundary already fixed elsewhere:** `slavery-freedom` carries her as `sojourner-truth-slavery-freedom` (verified) for walking to freedom in 1826 and winning her son Peter back in court in 1828.

### Sarah and Angelina Grimké — **the person who fills the freed era-6 story slot**
Used nowhere else in the book as of 2026-09-09.
- **Who they were.** Daughters of a wealthy South Carolina slaveholder. They became Quakers and abolitionists in the North and exiled themselves from their home state. (Source: Mass Moments, a project of Mass Humanities, "Angelina Grimké Addresses Legislature," https://www.massmoments.org/moment-details/angelina-grimke-addresses-legislature.html .)
- **The first women to be agents of the American Anti-Slavery Society**, and among the first American women to speak in public at all. They first addressed a mixed audience of men and women at **Poughkeepsie, New York**. (Source: Gilder Lehrman Institute, "Angelina and Sarah Grimké: Abolitionist Sisters," https://www.gilderlehrman.org/history-resources/essays/angelina-and-sarah-grimke-abolitionist-sisters ; South Carolina Encyclopedia, "Grimké, Sarah Moore and Angelina Emily Grimké," https://www.scencyclopedia.org/sce/entries/grimke-sarah-moore-and-angelina-emily-grimke/ .)
- **The scale of the tour.** In **23 weeks in 1837-1838 they spoke at at least 88 meetings in 67 towns, to more than 40,000 people face to face.** (Source: Mass Moments, as above.)
- **The Pastoral Letter, July 1837.** The Congregational General Association of Massachusetts issued a letter that ministers read from their pulpits, saying the sisters threatened **"the female character with wide spread and permanent injury"** and regretting **"females to bear an obtrusive and ostentatious part in measures of reform."** (Source: Mass Moments, as above; Gilder Lehrman, as above.)
- **Sarah's answer.** The first of her **"Letters on the Equality of the Sexes"** appeared in the *New England Spectator* in **July 1837**, the same month as the Pastoral Letter. (Source: Gilder Lehrman, as above.)
- **21 February 1838, the Massachusetts State House, Boston.** Angelina Grimké presented antislavery petitions signed by about **20,000 Massachusetts women** to a committee of the legislature, before an overflow crowd. **It is the first time a woman addressed a legislative body in American history.** Her words: **"We are citizens of this republic and as such our honor, happiness, and well-being are bound up in its politics, government, and laws."** (Source: Mass Moments, as above.)
- **After.** Both sisters lived to see slavery abolished and died before American women had the vote. (Source: Mass Moments, as above.)
- **Why they belong in this chapter and not in `slavery-freedom`.** The fight they started was over whether women could speak in public and petition their government at all. The Pastoral Letter attacked them for being women, not for being abolitionists, and Sarah answered it with a book about the equality of the sexes. That is a rights movement forming out of an antislavery campaign, which is this era's whole argument. Ten years later the Seneca Falls declaration made the same demand about petitioning and the vote.
- **Angelina's 1838 speech is also the answer to the "who spoke first" question** the outline needed: a woman addressing a legislature, ten years before Seneca Falls.
- **Related primary source, catalogued not fetched:** Angelina Grimké, *Letters to Catherine E. Beecher*, Library of Congress, https://www.loc.gov/resource/gdcmassbookdig.letterstocatheri00grim/ — note that the Catharine Beecher she is answering is the same one who founded the Hartford Female Seminary and opposed votes for women (`education`, slug `catharine-beecher-education`).

### One correction flagged, not silently fixed (2026-09-09)
`research/research-education.md` §6j (and this bank above, restating it) says **"Congress gave the school a land grant in the Alabama Territory in 1820."** **The Alabama Territory ceased to exist when Alabama became a state on 14 December 1819**, so "Alabama Territory in 1820" cannot both be right. The claim comes from the American School for the Deaf's own history page. **The outline therefore says only "Congress gave it a grant of land in 1820,"** dropping the territory. Per `CLAUDE.md`, this is named here for the audit phase rather than repaired in `research-education.md`. A later agent should check the act of Congress and settle whether the year is 1819 or 1820 and how the land was described.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262r): Grimké and Dix story checks
- **Grimké family and agency.** Sarah born **26 November 1792**, died 23 December 1873. Angelina born **20 February 1805**, died 26 October 1879. Their father was **John Faucheraud Grimké, jurist and cotton planter**, of Charleston. Angelina left Charleston for Philadelphia in **November 1829**. They joined the American Anti-Slavery Society in **1835** and **"became the first female antislavery agents."** In **July 1835 a Charleston mob broke into the post office and burned antislavery literature.** Angelina's letter to Garrison after it, printed in *The Liberator*, made them unwelcome in South Carolina. At the State House in 1838 she said: **"I stand before you as a southerner, exiled from the land of my birth by the sound of the lash and the piteous cry of the slave."** (Source: South Carolina Encyclopedia, "Grimké, Sarah Moore and Angelina Emily Grimké," https://www.scencyclopedia.org/sce/entries/grimke-sarah-moore-and-angelina-emily-grimke/ .)
- **Not found:** that the sisters "at first spoke to rooms of women" before Poughkeepsie. Gilder Lehrman returned 403 on 2026-09-27. The outline sentence was taken out, and the outline now names Maria W. Stewart (1832) as having spoken to a mixed audience before them.
- **Dix.** The outline's "families kept them at home and locked them up" had no source in the bank. It was replaced with what the Memorial documents: jails, poorhouses and houses of correction.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262r): who attacked the abolitionists, 1835 to 1838, with names, counts and outcomes
The era's organizers were attacked for organizing. This is the "who opposed them and how" the era needs. `slavery-freedom` leads on slavery as a system. These are attacks on people campaigning.
- **Boston, 21 October 1835.** The Boston Female Anti-Slavery Society (founded 1834 by Maria Weston Chapman, her sisters Caroline, Anne and Deborah Weston, and eight other women) met at its room on Washington Street, where the British abolitionist George Thompson was expected. Thompson cancelled and William Lloyd Garrison was put up in his place. (Sources: Unitarian Universalist History and Heritage Society, *Dictionary of Unitarian and Universalist Biography*, "Maria Weston Chapman," archived at https://web.archive.org/web/20110316004834/http://www25.uua.org/uuhs/duub/articles/mariawestonchapman.html ; Wikipedia, "Boston Female Anti-Slavery Society," which cites TeachUSHistory.)
  - **The count.** Deborah Weston called it **"the day when 5,000 men mobbed 45 women."** A report in the *Hampshire Gazette* (28 October 1835) also gives about 5,000 and calls the crowd citizens "calling themselves 'respectable'." (Sources: UUHHS, as above; TeachUSHistory, "The Boston Riot of 1835," http://www.teachushistory.org/second-great-awakening-age-reform/resources/boston-riot-1835 .) Pro-slavery men had posted **nearly 500 notices offering $100** to the first person to lay violent hands on Thompson. (Wikipedia, as above. Single source, not otherwise confirmed.)
  - **What the women did.** They opened their meeting with scripture and prayer. When Mayor **Theodore Lyman** asked them to leave, Chapman said: **"If this is the last bulwark of freedom, we may as well die here as anywhere."** They were escorted out through a hissing crowd and finished the meeting at the Chapman house. Afterwards shop clerks shouted "odious epithets" at Chapman in the street. Chapman wrote years later that **"the members of Dr. Channing's congregation were the mob."** (UUHHS, as above.)
  - **What was done to Garrison.** The mob **"dragged him through the streets at the end of a rope"** (TeachUSHistory, quoting the *Hampshire Gazette*). Wikipedia says a noose was put round his neck. Mayor Lyman and other men took him to the **Leverett Street Jail** and held him overnight for his own safety. (TeachUSHistory; Wikipedia.)
  - **Named actors in the mob:** none found. **Prosecutions:** none found in these sources.
- **Alton, Illinois, 7 November 1837: Elijah Parish Lovejoy killed.** Presbyterian minister and editor of the *St. Louis Observer*. He moved across the river to Alton for safety, and mobs there smashed three of his printing presses. He was killed defending a fourth. (Source: St. Louis Walk of Fame, "Elijah Lovejoy," http://stlouiswalkoffame.org/inductee/elijah-lovejoy/ .) Further detail from Wikipedia, "Elijah Parish Lovejoy" (not confirmed on a second page): the mob was pro-slavery. He was **struck by five bullets** and died within minutes. He was **34**. A man in the mob named **Bishop** was killed by the defenders' return fire. The attackers were tried from **19 January 1838** and found **not guilty**. The man who fired the fatal shots is not named. Background: in **April 1836** in St. Louis, a white mob **burned to death Francis McIntosh**, a free Black man, and **Judge Luke Lawless** refused to convict anyone and blamed abolitionists such as Lovejoy.
- **Philadelphia, 17 May 1838: Pennsylvania Hall burned.** Built with about **$40,000** raised by the Pennsylvania Hall Association, the Philadelphia Female Anti-Slavery Society raising a large share. It opened **14 May 1838** at Sixth and Haines Streets and housed the **Anti-Slavery Convention of American Women** that week. Angelina Grimké married Theodore Dwight Weld on 14 May nearby. Three days after opening, a mob set it on fire, using the building's own gas lighting. **Crowd: "between ten thousand and fifteen thousand," of whom "two hundred to three hundred people" took part in the attack.** Mayor **John Swift** made "half-hearted efforts" to clear the crowd. Sheriff **John G. Watmough** arrested about a dozen men and the crowd took them from him. **"No one was convicted of the crime, though dozens were arrested, and five men were investigated."** In **1847** the Pennsylvania Supreme Court made the county pay damages of **$27,942.27**. (Source: *Encyclopedia of Greater Philadelphia*, "Pennsylvania Hall," https://philadelphiaencyclopedia.org/essays/pennsylvania-hall/ .) The rioters are named only as "dock workers and white rioters." None is named.
- **How the prose can say it:** the mobs were white men of the towns, many of them described at the time as "respectable." No record found names a Boston or Philadelphia rioter, and no one was convicted for Boston, Alton or Philadelphia.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262r): Maria W. Stewart, the woman who spoke in public five years before the Grimkés
- Born **1803 in Hartford, Connecticut**, **"left an orphan at five years of age."** In summer **1831** Garrison's *Liberator* printed her essay "Religion and the Pure Principles of Morality." In **September 1832** she spoke at **Franklin Hall** in Boston, where the New England Anti-Slavery Society met, to an audience that included men. NPS calls it **"one of the first recorded instances of an American woman—of any race—speaking in public."** (Sources: NPS, "Maria W. Stewart," https://www.nps.gov/people/maria-w-stewart.htm ; West End Museum, Boston, "Maria Stewart," https://thewestendmuseum.org/history/era/west-boston/maria-stewart/ .) (The NPS quote contains an em dash. Paraphrase it in book prose.)
- **Her words (NPS):** "How long shall the fair daughters of Africa be compelled to bury their minds and talents beneath a load of iron pots and kettles?" and "Sue for your rights and privileges... You can but die, if you make the attempt; and we shall certainly die if you do not."
- **Who opposed her.** She "frequently encountered hostile audiences when she openly chastised black men for intemperance. As a result, her speaking career was short." (VCU Social Welfare History Project, "Stewart, Maria Miller," https://socialwelfare.library.vcu.edu/woman-suffrage/stewart-maria-miller/ .) The objection was that a woman speaking before men was improper (West End Museum). No named opponent found.
- **21 September 1833, farewell address.** She said it was **"no use for me as an individual, to try to make myself useful among my color in this city,"** and asked, **"What if I am a woman"** (VCU, as above). The fuller sentence, "What if I am a woman; is not the God of ancient times the God of these modern days? Did he not raise up Deborah, to be a mother, and a judge in Israel?", is **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**. Scholarly text: Marilyn Richardson, *Maria W. Stewart: America's First Black Woman Political Writer* (Indiana University Press, 1987), 45-49. She moved to New York in **1834**, taught, later worked as matron of the Freedmen's Hospital in Washington, and died there on **17 December 1879** (NPS).
- **For the outline:** the Grimké story says speaking to men and women together was something "almost no American woman had done." That holds, and Stewart is the example who did it first. The outline should name her rather than let the Grimkés read as first to speak. The "first woman to address a legislative body" claim for Angelina Grimké (1838) stands: the House Historian says she "became the first woman in the United States to speak before a legislative body" (see the petition PATCH below).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262r): Prudence Crandall's opponents, named, and two conflicts in the record
- **The leader: Andrew T. Judson.** He was a lawyer and Democratic politician who lived next door to the school. He led the opposition, pushed for the Black Law and led the prosecution. He told the abolitionist minister **Samuel J. May**: **"we mean there shall not be such a school set up anywhere in our State."** He also said: **"The colored people can never rise from their menial condition in our country . . . . They are an inferior race of beings, and never can or ought to be recognized as the equals of whites. Africa is the place for them."** He later sat in Congress (1835 to 1836) and was the federal district judge (1836 to 1853) who ruled for the African captives in the *Amistad* case in 1840. (Sources: Connecticut History, https://connecticuthistory.org/prudence-crandall-fights-for-equal-access-to-education/ , which names Judson as leader of the opposition; Online Review of Rhode Island History, "Prudence Crandall, Sarah Harris Fayerweather and Ann Hammond," https://smallstatebighistory.com/prudence-crandall-sarah-harris-fayerweather-and-ann-hammond-their-pre-civil-war-struggle-for-equality-for-black-people/ , citing Donald E. Williams Jr., *Prudence Crandall's Legacy*; Wikipedia, "Andrew T. Judson.")
- **9 March 1833 town meeting.** Canterbury voted that its inhabitants "protest against [the school] in the most earnest manner" and called on Crandall "to abandon the project." Judson spoke. **(unconfirmed: search summary only**, from a search on the ct.edu paper "Connecticut's Canterbury Tale," https://www.ct.edu/files/csus/92-139.pdf .)
- **A named pupil threatened with whipping: Ann Eliza Hammond,** 16, from Providence, enrolled **12 April 1833**. The town used Connecticut's vagrancy law against her, whose penalty was whipping **"on the naked body not exceeding ten stripes."** Crandall paid so that the sentence was not carried out. (Online Review of Rhode Island History, as above.) "Stripes" means lashes of a whip.
- **Other harassment** (same source): a dead cat hung at the school door, things thrown at the house at night, and a fire that damaged the building in **January 1834**.
- **CONFLICT on the 9 September 1834 attack.** NPS and Connecticut History (in the bank above) say **more than ninety windows** were broken. The Online Review of Rhode Island History says armed men broke **"five main first-floor windows."** Record both. **No attacker was ever charged:** "none were ever charged with their crime" (Online Review of Rhode Island History). No record found names them.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262r): women's first petition campaigns, 1829 to 1838, and the men who answered them
- **Against Indian removal, 1829 to 1830.** Catharine Beecher wrote the *Circular Addressed to Benevolent Ladies of the United States* (Boston, dated **31 December 1829**). It said: **"It may be, that female petitioners can lawfully be heard, even by the highest rulers of our land,"** and that "the Indian must perish unless their destruction can be averted." **More than 1,500 women signed petitions sent to Congress.** (Source: Teaching American History, "Circular: Addressed to Benevolent Ladies of the United States," https://teachingamericanhistory.org/document/circular-addressed-to-benevolent-ladies-of-the-united-states/ , intro by Sarah A. Morgan Smith.) The women came "from seven northern states" and "conducted the first national women's petition campaign in United States history" **(unconfirmed: search summary only**, attributed to Mary Hershberger, "Mobilizing Women, Anticipating Abolition," *Journal of American History*, 1999). Beecher wrote it at the urging of **Jeremiah Evarts** of the American Board of Commissioners for Foreign Missions **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**.
- **One petition, quoted.** Women of **Steubenville, Ohio, 15 February 1830**: "we, the feeblest of the feeble, appeal with confidence to those who should be the representatives of national virtues..." (Source: U.S. House of Representatives, History, Art & Archives, "'Congress Took No Further Action': Women and the Right to Petition," 5 June 2017, https://history.house.gov/Blog/Detail/15032448893 .) The House Historian says **"Women first petitioned on political issues"** in this debate.
- **Who ridiculed them.** "Members of Congress publicly ridiculed their efforts." Senator **Thomas Hart Benton** of Missouri: **"I would recommend to these ladies, not to douse their bonnets, and tuck up their coats, for such a race, but to sit down on the way side, and wait for the coming of the conquerors."** The Indian Removal Act passed in 1830. (House Historian, as above.) `native-nations` leads on removal itself.
- **The gag rule, 1836 to 1844.** In 1836 the House adopted a rule that **"immediately tabled all antislavery petitions,"** so none was read or acted on. Representative **John Patton** of Virginia renewed it on **21 December 1837**. The House renewed it each Congress until **1844**. **John Quincy Adams** led the opposition. (Sources: House Historian blog, as above; House Historian, "The House 'Gag Rule'," https://history.house.gov/Records-and-Research/Listing/lfp_046/ .) The 1836 sponsor and vote were not confirmed on a fetched page.
- **The Brookline women, 14 February 1838.** Women of Brookline, Massachusetts petitioned the House to rescind the rule, which they met with "astonishment and alarm." They called it **"an assumption of authority, at once dangerous and destructive to the fundamental principles of republican government, to the rights of minorities, to the sovereignty of the People."** **The first two signatures are Sarah M. Grimké and Angelina E. Grimké.** At the 1837 women's antislavery convention Angelina had moved that **"the right of petition is natural and inalienable."** Days after signing, she addressed the Massachusetts legislative committee, and the House Historian calls her **"the first woman in the United States to speak before a legislative body."** (House Historian blog, as above.) This supports the Grimké story's "first" and ties the story to the petition fight.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262r): Seneca Falls, who chaired it, and what happened to the signers
- **Who chaired: James Mott.** The convention's own printed report: **"JAMES MOTT, of Philadelphia, in the Chair"** on the second day. **Mary M'Clintock** was secretary. The first day was **"by women exclusively,"** and on the second "men participated." The resolutions "finally passed by a large majority." (Source: NPS, Women's Rights NHP, "Report of the Woman's Rights Convention," https://www.nps.gov/wori/learn/historyculture/report-of-the-womans-rights-convention.htm .) This settles the earlier **NOT CLAIMED** note above: a man chaired. The bank still does not claim the reason usually given, that a woman could not.
- **They expected the attack.** The Declaration of Sentiments says the signers anticipated "misconception, misrepresentation, and ridicule." (NPS report, as above.)
- **The backlash.** "A storm of sarcasm and protest broke out after the convention." **James Gordon Bennett**, publisher of the *New York Herald*, printed the whole declaration **"as a gesture of ridicule."** Some signers **"later removed their names after being subjected to intense criticism."** (Source: *West's Encyclopedia of American Law* via Encyclopedia.com, "Seneca Falls Convention 1848," https://www.encyclopedia.com/social-sciences-and-law/law/law/seneca-falls-convention-1848 .) That newspapers printed the signers' names to shame them, and that family pressure played a part, is **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**. **How many withdrew, and who: SEARCHED, NOT FOUND** (see below). Frederick Douglass's *North Star* editorial of **28 July 1848**, calling the declaration "the basis of a grand movement for attaining the civil, social, political, and religious rights of women," is **(unconfirmed: search summary only)**.
- **Land.** Seneca Falls and Waterloo are in Seneca County, New York, Cayuga homeland. By a **1789** treaty with New York the Cayuga Nation kept a **64,000-acre** reservation in Seneca and Cayuga counties. New York bought substantially all of it in **1795** and the last three square miles in **1807**. In 2017 a federal court (W.D.N.Y., *Cayuga Indian Nation v. Seneca County*, 30 April 2017) found those purchases were never ratified by Congress as the 1790 Trade and Intercourse Act required. (Source: Native American Rights Fund, National Indian Law Library, https://www.narf.org/nill/bulletins/federal/documents/cayuga_ny_v_seneca_ny.html .) Whether the Wesleyan Chapel's lot itself lay inside the 1789 reservation was not found. Say "Cayuga land."

### SEARCHED, NOT FOUND 2026-09-27 (T-262r): how many of the 100 Seneca Falls signers withdrew their names, and who they were
Sources checked: NPS "Signers of the Declaration of Sentiments" (lists 68 women and 32 men, says nothing about withdrawals); NPS "Report of the Woman's Rights Convention" (nothing after the event); Encyclopedia.com / *West's* ("some later removed their names"); search summaries (Britannica, LibreTexts, HistoryNet) say "many" with no count or names.
How the prose can say it: "Some of the signers later took their names off. The records do not say how many."

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262r): the deaf school's public money. Two conflicts, and the land
- **State money: 1816, not 1819.** The school's own 1893 history: **"In October, 1816, the Legislature of Connecticut was appealed to for aid and made an appropriation of five thousand dollars."** (Source: *A Brief History of the American Asylum, at Hartford, for the Education and Instruction of the Deaf and Dumb* (1893, Job Williams), Disability History Museum, https://www.disabilitymuseum.org/dhm/lib/detail.html?id=1371&page=all .) The Disability History Museum essay (John Crowley) calls that $5,000 "the first public funding for the education of Americans with disabilities" (https://www.disabilitymuseum.org/dhm/edu/essay.html?id=38), and the ASD Pioneers timeline dates it 1816 (https://asdpioneers.com/history/timeline-3/). **CONFLICT:** the bank above (from the ASD site) says the first annual state grant came in 1819. Both may be true: one $5,000 grant in 1816, annual grants from 1819. The outline's "annual grant in 1819, the first state money for a school in America" should be checked by the writer. The sourced version is: Connecticut gave $5,000 in October 1816.
- **Federal land: 1819, 23,000 acres.** 1893 history: **"Congress of the United States, under a motion made by Henry Clay, gave to the school twenty-three thousand acres of public land."** The school then renamed itself **"The American Asylum at Hartford, for the Education and Instruction of the Deaf and Dumb"** in 1819. (Disability History Museum, as above.) The Crowley essay places the land **in Alabama**. ASD Pioneers dates the grant **1819** and says the school **sold the land to build its endowment**. A House committee reported on the school's petition on **22 February 1819** (H. Doc. 15-142, U.S. Serial Set, https://www.govinfo.gov/app/details/SERIALSET-00024_00_00-006-0142-0000 , not opened). **This resolves the "Alabama Territory in 1820" flag above: the weight of sources is 1819, and Alabama was still a territory until 14 December 1819.** The outline's "1820" should become 1819, or the writer should say "in 1819 or 1820" and name the conflict.

### SEARCHED, NOT FOUND 2026-09-27 (T-262r): whose land were the deaf school's 23,000 Alabama acres
Sources checked: Disability History Museum essay (says "in Alabama" only); 1893 *Brief History* (says "public land" only); ASD Pioneers timeline ("a land grant in Alabama"); ASD's own history page ("Alabama Territory"); search for county, township or cession returned nothing specific. The land was federal public land in Alabama, which came almost wholly from Creek, Cherokee, Chickasaw and Choctaw cessions, but which one is not recorded in these sources.
How the prose can say it: "Congress gave the school 23,000 acres of public land in Alabama, land the United States had taken from Native nations, and the school sold it." Only if the writer states "taken from Native nations" as general context. Otherwise: "The records found do not say which nation's land it had been."

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262r): Oberlin's land
Oberlin was founded in 1833 in northern Ohio. The college's Allen Memorial Art Museum says it "stands on unceded ancestral lands originally stewarded by moundbuilders, Erie, Wyandotte, Mingo, and numerous other confederated Indigenous Nations and Peoples." (Source: Allen Memorial Art Museum, "Land Acknowledgment," https://amam.oberlin.edu/about/land-acknowledgment .) The United States took the land west of the Cuyahoga River by the **Treaty of Fort Industry, 4 July 1805**, from the Ottawa, Potawatomi, Chippewa, Wyandot, Munsee, Delaware and Shawnee **(unconfirmed: search summary only**, Wikipedia and Wooster Digital History Project). Whether Oberlin's own township lay in that cession was not confirmed on an opened page.

## HANDOFF — for the agent doing eras 7 and 8 (2026-09-09)

Everything above the era-7 line is done. These five items were found while researching eras 1-6 and belong to you.

1. **Sojourner Truth's story block is yours.** The slug `sojourner-truth` is free and reserved. Her lecture tour begins 1851 and the *Narrative* was published 1850, both era 7. Era 6 carries only her 1843 renaming and the Northampton Association, in zoom prose. **Handle the Akron speech carefully:** the famous "And ain't I a woman?" text is Frances Dana Gage's 1863 rendering, not a transcript, and it puts a Southern dialect on a woman raised speaking Dutch in New York. State the two versions.
2. **The "study damages a girl's health" argument is era 7, not era 6.** The documented statement is **Edward H. Clarke, *Sex in Education; or, A Fair Chance for the Girls* (Boston, 1873)**, and Mary Putnam Jacobi answered it. Era 6's tag was removed rather than hedged. Get a verbatim quotation.
3. **The Boston school chain ends in your era.** In **1855 the Massachusetts legislature outlawed segregated public schools** — the thing Prince Hall's 1787 petition had asked for, 68 years later. Same source as era 6 uses: NPS, "The Black Struggle for Equal Education in Boston, 1787 to 1976," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/the-black-struggle-for-equal-education-in-boston-1787-to-1976.htm . `education` already has Sarah Roberts as a verified story; take the campaign, not the case.
4. **The Haudenosaunee-to-suffragists link is yours, and it is sourced.** Elizabeth Cady Stanton told the 1891 National Council of Women that Haudenosaunee women "did not hesitate, when occasion required, 'to knock off the horns,' as it was technically called, from the head of a chief." Matilda Joslyn Gage wrote in the *New York Evening Post* that "In the home the wife was absolute," and was adopted into the Wolf Clan of the Mohawk Nation in 1893 as Ka-ron-ien-ha-wi, "She who holds the sky." (NPS, https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/how-native-american-women-inspired-the-women-s-rights-movement.htm .) Era 1 deliberately left it alone.
5. **Blackstone belongs to era 5 if any chapter wants him in place.** *Commentaries on the Laws of England*, 1765-1769. Era 3 quotes him with the date stated so no reader thinks he wrote in the 1600s.

**Two people who are documented, unused anywhere in the book, and available if a later editor wants more stories in the early eras:** **Mary Dyer** (era 3 — needs the 27 October 1659 gallows day sourced from a page that can actually be fetched) and **Benjamin Lay** (era 4).
