# ERA 7 — 1850 to 1900 — RESEARCHED 2026-09-09

Researched by the era-7 agent. Everything below is fetched and cited per fact. Where a
source could not be fetched, that is said. Eras 8-10 remain untouched.

## 7. 1850 to 1900

### [VERIFY] Wyoming, 1869 — CLEARED, and the one-line version needs a second sentence
- **The act.** *An Act to Grant to the Women of Wyoming Territory the Right of Suffrage and to Hold Office.* Introduced in the first Wyoming territorial legislature by **William H. Bright**, a Democrat from South Pass City. **Approved by Governor John Allen Campbell on 10 December 1869.** It is the first law in United States history that explicitly granted women the vote, and it granted the right to hold office in the same act. (Sources: Wyoming State Museum, "Suffrage Wyoming," https://wyomuseum.wyo.gov/suffrage/Suffrage.html ; Library of Congress, "Today in History — December 10," https://www.loc.gov/item/today-in-history/december-10 — **fetched only through search summary; a direct fetch of the loc.gov page returned 403 on 2026-09-09.** The act's own text at https://www.loc.gov/item/awhbib000036/ also returned 403, so **no verbatim wording of the statute is claimed here.**)
- **Wyoming granted first; Utah voted first. Both facts, or neither.** Utah Territory's legislature approved a women's suffrage law unanimously two months later; **the acting territorial governor signed it on 12 February 1870**, and **two days after that, on 14 February 1870, a Salt Lake City schoolteacher named Seraph Young cast a ballot in the Salt Lake City municipal election — the first woman in the United States to vote under a law giving women the vote on equal terms with men.** Wyoming's first election came later that year: **Louisa Swain of Laramie voted on the morning of 6 September 1870.** (Sources: Utah Division of Archives and Records Service, "150 Years of Utah Suffrage: Stories of Utah Women," https://archives.utah.gov/2020/02/14/150-years-of-utah-suffrage-stories-of-utah-women/ ; Utah State Capitol, "Seraph Young Votes," https://utahstatecapitol.utah.gov/seraphyoungvotes/ ; I Love History (Utah state history site), "Women's Suffrage: 1870-1896," https://ilovehistory.utah.gov/1870-1896-womens-suffrage/ ; Wyoming Secretary of State, Louisa Swain anniversary release, 6 September 2019, https://sos.wyo.gov/Media/2019/WY%20SOS_Louisa_Swain_Anniversary_09_06_2019.pdf .)
- **Wyoming women also served on juries first, and then stopped.** In the spring of 1870 women in Laramie were called to petit and grand juries, the first women in the nation to serve. A later reading of the qualification rule — "male citizens over 21 years of age" — kept women off Wyoming juries **until 1949**. (Source: Wyoming State Museum, as above.)
- **Esther Hobart Morris was appointed justice of the peace at South Pass City on 17 February 1870**, the first woman in the country to hold that office. (Source: Wyoming State Museum, as above; National Park Service, "Wyoming and the 19th Amendment," https://www.nps.gov/articles/wyoming-women-s-history.htm , which adds that she later served as a vice-president of the National American Woman Suffrage Association.)
- **Utah women's vote was taken away by Congress**, not by Utah: the **Edmunds-Tucker Act of 1887** stripped it, as part of the federal campaign against the Mormon church. Utah women got it back in the state constitution when **Utah became a state on 4 January 1896.** (Source: I Love History, Utah, as above.)
- **Why the NPS framing matters and should not be dropped:** the western legislatures that did this were not converted by the suffrage campaign. NPS records that Wyoming lawmakers had mixed motives, among them attracting more women to a territory with very few of them. Say the motive as well as the act.
- **Wyoming kept it at statehood, 1890** — the first state where women could vote. (Source: NPS, "Wyoming and the 19th Amendment," as above.)

### The split of 1869 — what actually broke, and the ugly part that must not be trimmed
- **The 14th Amendment put the word "male" into the Constitution.** Section 2, ratified 1868, is the first place the Constitution uses it, and it tied a state's representation in Congress to its "male inhabitants" who could vote. (Source: National Park Service, "Why the Women's Rights Movement Split Over the 15th Amendment," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/why-the-women-s-rights-movement-split-over-the-15th-amendment.htm .)
- **The 15th Amendment** passed Congress **26 February 1869** and was **ratified 3 February 1870**. Section 1: "The right of citizens of the United States to vote shall not be denied or abridged by the United States or by any State on account of race, color, or previous condition of servitude." **It says nothing about sex.** (Source: National Archives, "15th Amendment to the U.S. Constitution," https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/15th-amendment .)
- **The break happened at a meeting, and the date is known: 12 May 1869**, the third annual meeting of the American Equal Rights Association, the organisation abolitionists and women's rights campaigners had built together in 1866. (Source: NPS, as above.)
- **What was said, verbatim.** Frederick Douglass: **"I do not see how any one can pretend that there is the same urgency in giving the ballot to woman as to the negro. With us, the question is a matter of life and death, at least, in fifteen States of the Union ... when they are in danger of having their homes burnt down over their heads; when their children are not allowed to enter schools; then they will have an urgency to obtain the ballot equal to our own."** Susan B. Anthony: **"If you will not give the whole loaf of suffrage to the entire people, give it to the most intelligent first."** Elizabeth Cady Stanton: **"If intelligence, justice, and morality are to have precedence in the government, let the question of women brought up first and that of the negro last ... Mr. Douglass talks about the wrongs of the negro; but with all the outrages that he today suffers, he would not exchange his sex and take the place of Elizabeth Cady Stanton."** (Sources: NPS, "Why the Women's Rights Movement Split Over the 15th Amendment," as above; NPS, "Fighting for Suffrage: Comrades in Conflict," https://www.nps.gov/articles/comrades-in-conflict.htm ; NPS, "Fraught Friendship: Susan B. Anthony and Frederick Douglass," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/fraught-friendship-susan-b-anthony-and-frederick-douglass.htm .)
- **NPS states plainly that Stanton used racist language and stereotypes, arguing that African Americans were "ignorant of the laws and customs," and that this angered Black suffragists including Douglass and Frances Ellen Watkins Harper.** **Do not write around this.** The two most famous women in this chapter argued that educated white women should get the vote before Black men, and they lost allies over it. (Source: NPS, "Why the Women's Rights Movement Split," as above.)
- **Frances Ellen Watkins Harper, at the 1866 meeting:** **"You white women speak here of rights. I speak of wrongs."** (Source: NPS, as above.)
- **The two organisations, both founded in 1869.** The **National Woman Suffrage Association**, Stanton and Anthony, went for a federal constitutional amendment. The **American Woman Suffrage Association**, Lucy Stone, Henry Blackwell and Julia Ward Howe, went state by state and supported the 15th Amendment. **They merged in 1890 as the National American Woman Suffrage Association**, brokered in part by two daughters of the founders, **Alice Stone Blackwell** (Lucy Stone's daughter) and **Harriot Stanton Blatch** (Stanton's daughter). (Sources: Library of Congress, "The National American Woman Suffrage Association," https://www.loc.gov/collections/national-american-woman-suffrage-association/articles-and-essays/the-national-american-woman-suffrage-association/ ; Library of Congress, NAWSA Records, https://www.loc.gov/collections/national-american-woman-suffrage-association-records/about-this-collection ; NPS, "Alice Stone Blackwell," https://home.nps.gov/people/alice-stone-blackwell.htm .)
- **Minor v. Happersett.** **Virginia Minor** sued **Reese Happersett**, the registrar in St. Louis, Missouri, who refused to register her to vote. The Supreme Court decided the case unanimously against her, reported at **88 U.S. 162**; **Cornell's Legal Information Institute dates the decision to the Court's October Term 1874, and the case is usually cited as 1875** — the outline gives the citation and the term rather than picking one year silently. The holding: **"the Constitution of the United States does not confer the right of suffrage upon any one,"** and a state may limit voting to men. Women were citizens; citizenship did not carry the vote. That closed the route Anthony had tried in 1872, and left a constitutional amendment as the only way. (Source: Cornell Legal Information Institute, *Minor v. Happersett*, 88 U.S. 162, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/88/162 .)

### The laws that shut people out, 1873 to 1887 — dates, wording, and who enforced them
- **The Page Act, signed by President Ulysses S. Grant on 3 March 1875.** The first restrictive federal immigration law in United States history. Three provisions: federal agents at ports could stop and question **"any subject of China, Japan, or any Oriental country"** to decide whether the person had come **"without their free and voluntary consent, for the purpose of holding them to a term of service"**; a person suspected of coming for **"lewd and immoral purposes"** could be turned away; and the **"importation"** of women **"for the purposes of prostitution"** was banned, with immigration officials left to judge which women those were. **In practice officers applied it to Asian women generally, and it became a near-total bar on Chinese women entering the country.** (Sources: National Park Service, "Ulysses S. Grant, Chinese Immigration, and the Page Act of 1875," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/ulysses-s-grant-chinese-immigration-and-the-page-act-of-1875.htm ; NPS, San Francisco Maritime NHP, "Chinese Women, Immigration, and the First U.S. Exclusion Law: The Page Act of 1875," https://www.nps.gov/safr/blogs/chinese-women-immigration-and-the-first-u-s-exclusion-law-the-page-act-of-1875.htm .)
- **The Chinese Exclusion Act, signed by President Chester A. Arthur on 6 May 1882.** It suspended the coming of Chinese labourers for **ten years**, and **Section 14 said "no State court or court of the United States shall admit Chinese to citizenship."** Extended ten more years by the **Geary Act of 1892**, made permanent in **1902** with a requirement to carry a registration certificate, and **repealed in 1943** when China was a wartime ally — replaced by a quota of **105 Chinese immigrants a year**. (Source: National Archives, "Chinese Exclusion Act (1882)," https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/chinese-exclusion-act .)
- **`immigration` leads on arrival and exclusion as immigration policy.** This chapter's angle is narrower and must be said plainly: **this is the only time the United States wrote one named nationality out of citizenship by statute**, and the campaign to undo it runs through the next two eras.
- **The Comstock Act, 3 March 1873.** Full title as the National Archives gives it: **"An Act for the Suppression of, trade in, and circulation of obscene literature and articles of immoral use."** It banned sending certain things through the United States mail. Among them: anatomy books, novels, photographs of boxing matches, **and information about contraception** — how to avoid pregnancy. **The act never defined "obscene."** **Anthony Comstock (1844-1915)**, who had lobbied Congress for it, was appointed **a special agent of the Post Office** with broad power over the mails, and decided in practice what the word meant. In court he often kept jurors from seeing the item he had seized, on the ground that it was too vile to show them. **He claimed to have seized 150 tons of books, made 4,000 arrests, and driven 15 people to suicide.** (Source: National Archives Museum, "Vicecapades: 150th Anniversary of the 1873 Comstock Act," https://visit.archives.gov/whats-on/explore-exhibits/vicecapades-150th-anniversary-1873-comstock-act .)
- **Why Comstock is a rights-movements subject and not only a `health` one:** it made it a federal crime to put in the mail the information a woman needed about her own body, and the fight to repeal it runs through Margaret Sanger in era 8 and the birth-control cases of era 9. State what it banned, not "restricted publications."
- **Standing Bear v. Crook, 1879 — the first time a Native American sued the federal government, and the first federal ruling that a Native person is a person in law.**
  - **1877:** the United States forced the Ponca off their land at the mouth of the Niobrara River in Nebraska and moved them to Indian Territory. They had never been at war with the United States and had never sold that land. **One-third of the tribe died after they arrived**; Standing Bear's daughter died on the way. (Sources: Nebraska State Historical Society, "Chief Standing Bear and the Declaration of Independence," https://history.nebraska.gov/chief-standing-bear-and-the-declaration-of-independence/ ; NPS, Missouri National Recreational River, "Chief Standing Bear," https://home.nps.gov/mnrr/learn/historyculture/standingbear.htm .)
  - **Late December 1878:** his son **Bear Shield**, aged 16, died of fever in Indian Territory. **Early January 1879:** Standing Bear set out on foot for Nebraska carrying his son's bones, to bury him at home. The walk took about two months. He and those with him were arrested and held at **Fort Omaha** under General **George Crook**. (Source: NPS, MNRR, as above.)
  - **The trial began 1 May 1879 and ran into 2 May, before Judge Elmer S. Dundy of the United States District Court in Omaha**, on a writ of habeas corpus — the old rule that a person held in custody can make the government show its legal reason. **Dundy ruled on 12 May 1879 that "an Indian is a person within the meaning of the laws of the United States"** and ordered the Ponca released. (Sources: Nebraska State Historical Society, as above; NPS, MNRR, as above; Library of Congress law blog, "Chief Standing Bear and His Landmark Civil Rights Case," https://blogs.loc.gov/law/2019/11/chief-standing-bear-and-his-landmark-civil-rights-case/ — **catalogued from search results only; a direct fetch returned 403 on 2026-09-09.**)
  - **Standing Bear's own words in court**, as NPS gives them: **"That hand is not the color of yours. But if I pierce it, I shall feel pain ... I am a man. The same god made us both."** (Source: NPS, MNRR, as above.)
  - **From June 1879 he went on a lecture tour** through Chicago, Pittsburgh, Philadelphia, Boston, New York, Washington and Baltimore, with the journalist **Thomas Tibbles** and **Susette La Flesche (Bright Eyes)** of the Omaha, who interpreted, and her brother **Frank**. (Source: NPS, MNRR, as above.)
  - **Shared with `native-nations` (lead — what was done to the Ponca and how the nation endured) and `government-politics` (the ruling as law).** This chapter takes the campaign: a man who went to a federal court, won, and then toured the country arguing his case in public.
  - **Do not overstate the win.** The ruling covered Standing Bear and those with him. **Elk v. Wilkins (1884)** went the other way on Native citizenship and voting — flagged here, **not** researched by this agent; a later agent should source it before any chapter states it. (Pointer only: Nebraska State Historical Society, "Elk v Wilkins, A Setback for Indian Citizenship," https://history.nebraska.gov/wp-content/uploads/2022/05/doc_publications_NH1986IndianVote.pdf .)

### [VERIFY] women's colleges and state universities opening to women — CLEARED, with the dates that are actually documented
- **Vassar.** Matthew Vassar established it at Poughkeepsie, New York in **1861**; it **opened on 26 September 1865 with 353 students**, who paid **$350** for tuition and residence. Vassar's own history does not claim it was the first degree-granting college for women, and this bank does not claim it either. (Source: Vassar College, "About — History," https://www.vassar.edu/about/history .)
- **Smith and Wellesley both opened in 1875. Bryn Mawr was founded in 1885.** (Source: Vassar Encyclopedia, "The Founding of the Seven Sisters," https://vcencyclopedia.vassar.edu/notable-events/the-seven-sisters/ , via search summary; the page itself is about the 1926 consortium and **does not carry the individual founding dates**, so these three dates rest on the search result and on Smith's own account below, which dates Smith's opening to 1875. **A later agent should replace them with each college's own page before the prose stage.**)
- **Spelman.** **11 April 1881**, in the basement of Friendship Baptist Church in Atlanta, Georgia. Founded by **Sophia B. Packard and Harriet E. Giles**, New England schoolteachers and Baptist missionaries, helped by the church's pastor **Frank Quarles** and funded by the Woman's American Baptist Home Mission Society. It was called the **Atlanta Baptist Female Seminary**; it became Spelman College in **1924**. **The first student body was eleven: ten women, some of them formerly enslaved, and one girl.** (Source: Spelman College, "History in Brief," https://www.spelman.edu/about/history-in-brief.html and "About Us," https://www.spelman.edu/about/index.html .)
- **State universities — and Iowa's own archive contradicts the usual one-line claim.** The University of Iowa says: **"Since its founding, the University of Iowa has admitted women as well as men, the first public university in the U.S. to do so,"** and separately that **"Beginning in 1860, women were admitted on an equal basis with men."** Those are two different statements and the outline gives both rather than the flat "1855." (Source: University of Iowa Libraries, Special Collections and Archives, "Resource Guide to University 'Firsts'," https://www.lib.uiowa.edu/sc/archives/faq/faqfirsts/ .) Iowa also records **Mary Hickey Wilkinson graduating from its College of Law in 1873**, "one of the first women in the U.S. to receive a law degree."
- **Michigan: February 1870, Madelon Stockwell**, from Kalamazoo, the first woman to enrol at a university that had been all male since it opened in 1817. (Source: University of Michigan Heritage Project, "Madelon's World," https://heritage.umich.edu/stories/madelons-world/ ; Bentley Historical Library exhibit, "As to the Woman Question: The Admission of Women to the University of Michigan," https://exhibits.bentley.umich.edu/s/admissionofwomen/page/about .)
- **Cornell: 1870, and it did not hold.** Cornell admitted its first woman in 1870 — **Jennie Spencer of Cortland**, who won a state scholarship and then withdrew because there was nowhere for her to live near the campus. **Women were admitted regularly from 1872.** (Source: Cornell University, "Our Historic Commitment," https://diversity.cornell.edu/about-us/our-historic-commitment?field_decade_value=1850-1899 .)
- **The pattern to write, not a list of dates:** a college saying it admits women and a woman actually being able to take the degree and live there are two different things, and in this era they are usually years apart. Oberlin 1833 and 1841 (era 6), Cornell 1870 and 1872, Iowa's founding and 1860.

### [VERIFY] Edward H. Clarke, 1873 — CLEARED, with verbatim quotation. Handed forward from the era-6 agent; this is where it belongs.
- **The book.** *Sex in Education; or, A Fair Chance for the Girls*, by **Edward H. Clarke, M.D. (1820-1877)**, a professor at Harvard Medical School. **Boston: James R. Osgood and Company, 1873. 181 pages.** (Sources: National Library of Medicine Digital Collections catalogue record, https://collections.nlm.nih.gov/catalog/nlm:nlmuid-61410300R-bk ; Library of Congress catalogue record, https://www.loc.gov/item/07031164/ .)
- **EDITION TRAP, recorded so nobody repeats it.** The Project Gutenberg transcription (https://www.gutenberg.org/files/18504/18504-h/18504-h.htm) carries **1875** on its title page and spells the subtitle "A Fair Chance for Girls." **It is a later printing.** The first edition is 1873, per NLM and LC. Quote from the text; date the book 1873.
- **What Clarke actually claimed, verbatim from the text.**
  - **"The results are monstrous brains and puny bodies; abnormally active cerebration, and abnormally weak digestion."**
  - **"If excessive labor, either mental or physical, is imposed upon children, male or female, their development will be in some way checked."**
  - **"Unless the work is accomplished at that period, unless the reproductive mechanism is built and put in good working order at that time, it will never be perfectly accomplished afterwards."**
  - **"a girl upon whom Nature ... imposes so great a physiological task, will not have as much power left for the tasks of the school, as the boy."**
  - Of his case "Miss A": **"She graduated at nineteen, the first scholar, and an invalid."**
  (Source: the text itself, Project Gutenberg transcription as above, fetched 2026-09-09.)
- **The claim stated plainly, for a young reader.** Clarke argued that a girl's body spends its teenage years building the organs of reproduction, that studying the way boys study takes the energy that work needs, and that a girl who does it will end up permanently damaged. Smith College's own account summarises what he warned of: a woman doing "sustained vigorous mental activity — studying in a 'boy's way' — risked atrophy of the uterus and ovaries, masculinization, sterility, insanity, and even death," and he held that women should **study no more than four hours a day** and not study at all while menstruating. (Source: Smith College, "Pushing Beyond Gender Boundaries," https://www.smith.edu/news-events/news/pushing-beyond-gender-boundaries .)
- **Define the words in plain English at first use, per the hard-subjects policy.** *Atrophy* means an organ shrinking and stopping working. *The uterus and ovaries* are the organs a woman's body uses to carry a pregnancy. *Sterility* means being unable to have children. *Menstruating* is the monthly bleeding that starts in the teenage years. Clarke's claim was that school would do this. He was a Harvard Medical School professor, so the claim carried a doctor's authority.
- **What happened as a result — what is documented, and what is not.**
  - **Documented:** it was aimed at the colleges just then opening to women, and the colleges answered it. Smith's founding president **Laurenus Clark Seelye** said at the college's opening in **1875**: **"It is to preserve her womanliness that this College has been founded,"** and built a supervised residential system in response to exactly these anxieties. (Source: Smith College, as above.)
  - **Documented:** **Mary Putnam Jacobi** answered it twice. First with an essay, **"Mental Action and Physical Health," in *The Education of American Girls* (1874)**. Then with research: she sent out **a thousand questionnaires** through women's rights networks and got answers from hundreds of women, rich and poor, rural and urban, and used the data rather than anecdotes. The resulting essay, ***The Question of Rest During Menstruation*, won Harvard's Boylston Prize in 1876** — she was the first woman to win it — and found no support for the claim that menstruation made women unfit to study or work. Jacobi's own credentials: first woman in the United States to take a degree in pharmacy (**New York College of Pharmacy, 1863**), **M.D., Female Medical College of Pennsylvania, 1864**, admitted to the École de Médecine at the University of Paris **1868**, graduated **1871**. (Source: National Women's History Museum, "Mary Putnam Jacobi," https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/mary-putnam-jacobi .)
  - **NOT DOCUMENTED and NOT CLAIMED here:** the frequently repeated figures for how many printings the book went through, and the Association of Collegiate Alumnae's 1885 health survey of college women. Searching on 2026-09-09 found the ACA's founding (**Boston, 1882**) in the Smith College finding aid for its records, but **no fetched source for an 1885 survey and its findings**. The outline does not state either. (Pointer: Smith College Finding Aids, "Association of Collegiate Alumnae records," https://findingaids.smith.edu/repositories/2/resources/713 .)

### [VERIFY] the first women to take degrees in medicine and law — CLEARED, and the era-6 date matters
- **Medicine begins in era 6, not era 7.** **Elizabeth Blackwell took her M.D. from Geneva Medical College in New York in January 1849** — era 6 by the boundary rule. This chapter's era-7 material starts after her. (Pointer: National Library of Medicine, "Rise, Serve, Lead!", https://www.nlm.nih.gov/exhibition/riseservelead/collection-detail.html .)
- **Rebecca Lee Crumpler, 1 March 1864.** Born **Rebecca Davis at Christiana, Delaware in 1831**, raised by an aunt in Pennsylvania, worked as a nurse **from 1852 to 1860**, mostly in Charlestown, Massachusetts. Admitted to the **New England Female Medical College in 1860**; on **1 March 1864** she received a **"Doctress of Medicine."** **She was the first African American woman in the United States to earn an M.D., and the only African American woman ever to graduate from that college.** After the Civil War she moved to **Richmond, Virginia**, and worked with the **Freedmen's Bureau** treating people just freed from slavery who had no other access to a doctor. In **1883** she published *A Book of Medical Discourses*, believed to be the first medical text written by an African American author. (Sources: National Library of Medicine, "Changing the Face of Medicine — Dr. Rebecca Lee Crumpler," https://cfmedicine.nlm.nih.gov/physicians/biography_73.html and https://www.nlm.nih.gov/exhibition/changing-the-face-of-medicine/physicians/biography_rebecca_crumpler.html?id=item-77 ; National Park Service, "Dr. Rebecca Lee Crumpler," https://www.nps.gov/people/dr-rebecca-lee-crumpler.htm ; National Women's History Museum, https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/rebecca-lee-crumpler .)
- **Law: Arabella Mansfield, Iowa, 1869.** She apprenticed at her brother's law firm, **took the Iowa bar examination on 15 June 1869**, and became **the first woman admitted to a state bar in the United States.** (Source: Library of Congress law blog *In Custodia Legis*, "Arabella Mansfield, First Female Lawyer," https://blogs.loc.gov/law/2024/01/arabella-mansfield-first-female-lawyer/ — **catalogued from search summary; loc.gov blog pages returned 403 to direct fetch on 2026-09-09.**)
- **Charlotte E. Ray, 1872.** A graduate of **Howard University School of Law**, admitted to the **District of Columbia bar in 1872**, the first Black woman lawyer in the United States. **She applied under the name "C. E. Ray" and the admissions committee took her for a man.** (Source: *In Custodia Legis*, "Women in History: Lawyers and Judges," https://blogs.loc.gov/law/2015/03/women-in-history-lawyers-and-judges/ — same fetch caveat.)
- **Myra Bradwell, and the Supreme Court's answer.** Bradwell **passed the Illinois bar examination in 1869** and the Illinois Supreme Court refused her a licence **because she was married**. She took it to the Supreme Court of the United States, arguing the 14th Amendment. **She lost, 8 to 1.** The case is **Bradwell v. The State, 83 U.S. 130**; **Cornell dates it 1872 (the Court's October 1872 term) and it is usually cited as 1873** — the outline gives the citation and does not pick silently. Justice Samuel F. Miller wrote for the Court that regulating who may practise law is a state matter and not protected by the 14th Amendment. **Justice Joseph Bradley wrote separately, and this is the passage to quote:** **"The paramount destiny and mission of woman are to fulfil the noble and benign offices of wife and mother. This is the law of the Creator."** He also wrote: **"The natural and proper timidity and delicacy which belongs to the female sex evidently unfits it for many of the occupations of civil life,"** and **"A married woman is incapable, without her husband's consent, of making contracts which shall be binding on her or him"** — which is coverture, still doing work in the 1870s. (Source: Cornell Legal Information Institute, *Bradwell v. The State*, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/83/130 ; supporting: Supreme Court of the United States exhibition, "In Re Lady Lawyers: The Rise of Women Attorneys and the Supreme Court," https://www.supremecourt.gov/visiting/exhibitions/LadyLawyers/section1.aspx .)
- **Belva Lockwood, 1876 and 3 March 1879.** In **1876** the justices of the Supreme Court refused to admit her to its bar, saying **"none but men are permitted to practice before [us] as attorneys and counselors."** She lobbied Congress until it passed **"An Act to relieve certain legal disabilities of women,"** and on **3 March 1879**, on the motion of the Washington attorney **Albert G. Riddle**, she became **the first woman admitted to the bar of the Supreme Court of the United States.** (Sources: National Archives, *Prologue*, "Belva Lockwood," https://www.archives.gov/publications/prologue/2005/spring/belva-lockwood-1 ; Supreme Court of the United States, "In Re Lady Lawyers," https://www.supremecourt.gov/visiting/exhibitions/ladylawyers/Section2.aspx .)
- **The shape of this material:** a woman could pass the examination and still be refused the licence, and the answer in every case was to change the law rather than to persuade the court. That is the chapter's method again.

### [VERIFY] the earliest Black-parent and Chinese American school cases — CLEARED, with one date correction and one honest limit
The outline's note said "a Massachusetts case in the 1840s and a California case over a Chinese American girl in the 1880s." Both are real. The Massachusetts one is era 6 and already belongs to `education`; the California one is era 7 and is this era's story.

- **Massachusetts, 1855 — the campaign wins after the case loses.** *Roberts v. City of Boston* was brought in the late 1840s by **Benjamin Roberts** for his daughter **Sarah** and **the family lost**. The community kept going: **William Cooper Nell** ran boycotts of the Abiel Smith School and petition drives, and **"succeeded in getting the Massachusetts legislature to outlaw segregated public schools in 1855."** That is **68 years after Prince Hall's 1787 petition** asking for the same thing. (Source: National Park Service, Boston African American National Historic Site, "The Black Struggle for Equal Education in Boston, 1787 to 1976," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/the-black-struggle-for-equal-education-in-boston-1787-to-1976.htm .) **`education` already carries Sarah Roberts as a verified story (`sarah-roberts-education`) — this chapter takes the boycott-and-petition campaign and the 1855 law, not the case.**
- **The same NPS page says the law did not settle it:** in the decades after, "race-based segregation policies also impacted the public schools, despite federal and state laws which forbade them." Say that; do not end the Boston thread on a victory.
- **San Francisco's own chronology, from the school district.** **1851** — the first public schools open, for white children only. **1854** — the first segregated school for Black children. **1859** — the first segregated school for Chinese children. **1870** — the district closes the segregated Chinese school, which is what leaves Chinese American children with no public school at all. **1885** — after *Tape v. Hurley*, "SFUSD reopened the segregated Chinese school as a way to avoid integrating white schools." (Source: San Francisco Unified School District, "Facing Our Past, Changing Our Future, Part I: A Century of Segregation in San Francisco Unified School District (1851-1971)," https://www.sfusd.edu/facing-our-past-changing-our-future-part-i-century-segregation-san-francisco-unified-school-district .)
- **Ward v. Flood, 1874 — NAMED BUT NOT FULLY SOURCED, and the outline treats it accordingly.** The case was brought for **Mary Frances Ward**, a Black girl, against **Noah F. Flood**, principal of the **Broadway Grammar School in San Francisco**. The California Supreme Court held in **1874** that separate schools were lawful, but that where no separate school existed a Black child could not be kept out of the white school. **Every attempt to fetch a full source for this on 2026-09-09 failed:** the NPS *Five Views* chapter (https://www.nps.gov/parkhistory/online_books/5views/5views2b.htm) returned a different page, the Berkeley law catalogue record (https://lawcat.berkeley.edu/record/417699?ln=en) returned 403, and the Library of Congress *Brown v. Board at Fifty* exhibition page (https://www.loc.gov/exhibits/brown/brown-segregation.html) returned 403. **The claim rests on search-result summaries of those pages only.** The outline therefore names the case and the year and does not quote the ruling. **A later agent should source it properly or cut it.**
- **Tape v. Hurley, 1885 — fully sourced, and it fills this era's "parent who sued a school board" story slot.** See the story entry below.
- **Two more Chinese American cases in this era, from the same NPS page, for the era zoom:** **Yick Wo v. Hopkins (1886)** — Yick Wo had run a laundry at 349 Third Street in San Francisco for 22 years and was jailed under an 1885 San Francisco ordinance requiring a permit for laundries not built of brick or stone; the Supreme Court struck the ordinance down because "the permits were preferentially denied to Chinese laundries," a violation of the 14th Amendment's equal protection clause. **United States v. Wong Kim Ark (1898)** — Wong Kim Ark was born in San Francisco in 1873 to Chinese parents and was refused re-entry after a trip abroad; the Supreme Court held that "under the Fourteenth Amendment, which grants citizenship to all persons born or naturalized in the United States, Wong Kim Ark was a U.S. citizen by birthright." (Source: National Park Service, "Chinese Americans Use the Legal System to Fight Back," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/chinese-americans-use-the-legal-system-to-fight-back.htm .) **Shared with `immigration` and `government-politics`; this chapter's angle is that people barred by statute went to court and used the 14th Amendment against the bar.**

### STORY — Mary and Joseph Tape, and Mamie Tape — **fills the `target` slot "a parent who sued a school board." The slot is filled with real, named people; the slug changes from `parent-who-sued-school-board` to `mary-and-joseph-tape`.**
- **Mary Tape.** Born **1857 near Shanghai, China**. She came to the United States **alone, aged eleven, in 1868**. The **Ladies' Protection and Relief Society** took her in; she learned English there and took the name **Mary McGladery**, after a caretaker. (Source: National Park Service, "Mary Tape," https://www.nps.gov/people/mary-tape.htm .)
- **Joseph Tape.** Born **Jeu Dip**; he took the name Joseph Tape after arriving in **1864**. He worked as a domestic servant, then ran a milk delivery business, and later worked as a broker and interpreter for the Imperial Consulate of China. (Same source.)
- **The family.** Four children — **Mamie, Frank, Emily and Gertrude** — living in **Cow Hollow, San Francisco**, in American dress and American habits. (Same source.)
- **1884: the refusal.** The Tapes tried to enrol **eight-year-old Mamie** at **Spring Valley Primary School**, the all-white school near their home. The school refused her **because of her Chinese ancestry**. (Same source.)
- **The lawsuit.** **Joseph Tape sued the San Francisco Board of Education, the superintendent Andrew Jackson Moulder, and the principal Jennie Hurley**, on Mamie's behalf. (Same source.)
- **9 January 1885 — they won.** The Superior Court of California ruled that excluding "children of Chinese parents" from the public schools broke state law **and** the equal protection clause of the 14th Amendment. **The California Supreme Court upheld it on appeal.** (Sources: NPS, "Mary Tape," as above; NPS, "Chinese Americans Use the Legal System to Fight Back," as above. **The exact date of the California Supreme Court's decision was not established from a fetched source on 2026-09-09; the outline says the Superior Court date and that the state Supreme Court upheld it, without giving a second date.**)
- **What the state did about winning.** Superintendent **Moulder telegraphed the state legislature the moment the decision came down**, and the legislature passed a law setting up **"separate schools for children of Mongolian or Chinese descent."** (Source: NPS, "Mary Tape.")
- **8 April 1885 — refused again**, this time on the grounds that Mamie had no vaccination certificate and the classes were full. (Source: NPS, "Mary Tape.")
- **13 April 1885 — Mamie and her brother Frank became the first pupils at the new Chinese Primary School in Chinatown.** They won the case and were sent to a separate school anyway. (Source: NPS, "Mary Tape.")
- **Mary Tape's letter, 8 April 1885, printed in the *Daily Alta California* on 16 April 1885, page 1 — the primary document, quoted with her spelling as printed.**
  > "To the Board of Education—Dear Sirs: I see that you are going to make all sorts of excuses to keep my child out of the Public schools. Dear sirs, Will you please to tell me! Is it a disgrace to be Born a Chinese? Didn't God make us all!!! What right have you to bar my children out of the school because she is a chinese Decend. They is no other worldly reason that you could keep her out, except that. I suppose, you all goes to churches on Sundays! Do you call that a Christian act to compell my little children to go so far to a school that is made in purpose for them. ... It seems no matter how a Chinese may live and dress so long as you know they Chinese. Then they are hated as one. There is not any right or justice for them. ... It seems to me Mr. Moulder has a grudge against this Eight-year-old Mamie Tape. ... May you Mr. Moulder, never be persecuted like the way you have persecuted little Mamie Tape. Mamie Tape will never attend any of the Chinese schools of your making! Never!!! I will let the world see sir What justice there is When it is govern by the Race prejudice men! ... I guess she is more of a American then a good many of you that is going to prevent her being Educated.  Mrs. M. Tape."
  (Source of the full transcription: *The American Yawp Reader*, "Chinese Immigrants Confront Anti-Chinese Prejudice," https://www.americanyawp.com/reader/19-american-empire/chinese-immigrants-confront-anti-chinese-prejudice-1885-1903/ , which cites "Chinese Mother's Letter," *Daily Alta California*, April 16, 1885, page 1, available at the California Digital Newspaper Collection, https://cdnc.ucr.edu/?a=d&d=DAC18850416.2.3 . **The CDNC page itself returned 403 to a direct fetch on 2026-09-09**, but the National Park Service quotes the sentences beginning "It seems no matter how a Chinese may live and dress" **word for word from the same letter**, which cross-checks the transcription against a second independent source.)
- **Do not correct her spelling.** She was writing in her second language, in 1885, to the men who had shut her daughter out. The spelling is part of the document. If the prose modernises it, say so.
- **Shared with `immigration` (Chinese exclusion and Chinese American community life) and `education` (San Francisco's segregated schools as schools).** This chapter's angle: a mother and father who took a school board to court and won, and what winning got them.

### STORY — Sojourner Truth — the slug reserved by the era-6 agent, now filled
Era 6 carries her renaming in 1843 and the Northampton Association in 1844, in zoom prose. `slavery-freedom` carries `sojourner-truth-slavery-freedom` for 1826-1828. **This is her rights-movement story and the slug is `sojourner-truth`.**

**The documented era-7 chronology** (Source: National Park Service, "Sojourner Truth," https://home.nps.gov/people/sojourner-truth.htm , unless another source is named):
- **1850** — published *The Narrative of Sojourner Truth: a Northern Slave* and bought a house in Northampton, Massachusetts. She paid for it partly by selling photographs of herself, captioned **"I sell the shadow to support the substance."**
- **28 May 1851** — spoke at the Ohio Women's Rights Convention at the **Old Stone Church in Akron, Ohio**. The National Women's History Museum records that **she had not been invited.** (Source: National Women's History Museum, "Sojourner Truth" exhibit, https://www.womenshistory.org/exhibits/sojourner-truth .)
- **1857 onward** — moved to Michigan. During the Civil War she recruited Black men for the Union Army, among them her grandson **James Caldwell**, who volunteered for the Massachusetts 54th in April 1863. In 1863 she collected Thanksgiving food door to door in Detroit for the First Michigan Regiment of Colored Soldiers.
- **1863** — **arrested in Indiana** for breaking a state law that barred people of African descent from entering the state. **Held ten days, then released.** (Source: NWHM exhibit, as above.)
- **1864** — worked for the **National Freedmen's Relief Association** in Washington, D.C. **She met President Abraham Lincoln at the White House on 29 October 1864** (date from the NWHM exhibit). **In Washington she rode the public streetcars to force them to carry Black passengers.** (NPS wording: she "traveled on public streetcars to support desegregation." **NPS does not describe an injury or a lawsuit, and neither does the outline** — the commonly told version of a conductor injuring her shoulder was not confirmed from a fetched source on 2026-09-09.)
- **1870 onward** — campaigned for **federal land grants in the West for people freed from slavery. It failed.** She met President Ulysses S. Grant at the White House.
- **1872** — back in Battle Creek, worked on Grant's re-election, **went to vote, and was turned away at the polls.** That is the same year Susan B. Anthony voted in Rochester and was arrested for it. **Put the two side by side; the difference is the point.**
- **26 November 1883** — died at Battle Creek, Michigan, aged about 86, buried at Oak Hill Cemetery. NWHM records that **she herself believed she was 105.**

**THE AKRON SPEECH — the two versions, and the rule for this book.** This is the item the era-6 agent handed forward, and the caution was right.
- **The convention met on 28 May 1851** at the Old Stone Church, Akron, Ohio. (Source: NPS, "Ain't I A Woman — Teachers," https://www.nps.gov/teachers/classrooms/aint-i-a-woman.htm .)
- **Version one, and the closer one: Marius Robinson**, a secretary of the convention, printed his report in the ***Anti-Slavery Bugle* of 21 June 1851**, about three weeks after the speech. Truth stayed with Robinson and his wife Emily in Ohio that year, and **it is believed he went over his version with her before printing it.** (Sources: NPS, "Ain't I A Woman — Teachers," as above; NPS, "Lesson Plan: Ain't I A Woman?", https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/aint-i-a-woman-lesson-plan.htm ; Library of Congress, "Sojourner Truth's Most Famous Speech," https://blogs.loc.gov/headlinesandheroes/2021/04/sojourner-truths-most-famous-speech/ — **catalogued from search summary; loc.gov blog returned 403 to a direct fetch on 2026-09-09.**)
- **Robinson's text, verbatim as far as the NPS pages carry it:** **"May I say a few words? I want to say a few words about this matter. I am a woman's rights. I have as much muscle as any man, and can do as much work as any man. I have plowed and reaped and husked and chopped and mowed ..."** and, at the end, **"man is in a tight place, the poor slave is on him, woman is coming on him ..."** Robinson himself wrote that it was **"impossible to transfer it to paper, or convey any adequate idea of the effect it produced upon the audience."** (Sources: the two NPS pages above.) **The full Anti-Slavery Bugle column could not be fetched on 2026-09-09** — Chronicling America at loc.gov returned 403. **A later agent should get the full column from Chronicling America, LCCN sn83035487, issue of 21 June 1851, and put the whole passage in this bank.**
- **Version two, and the famous one: Frances Dana Gage**, who had presided over the convention, published her account **twelve years later, in 1863.** **Sources disagree on where:** NPS's teachers page and the NWHM exhibit say the *New York Independent*; a Library of Congress summary says the *National Anti-Slavery Standard* of **2 May 1863**. Both are recorded here; neither is picked. Gage was in Union-occupied South Carolina when she wrote it, and did not check it with Truth. **Gage called her own account "but a faint sketch of her speech."**
- **The two things Gage's version does that are not true of Sojourner Truth.** First, it writes her in a Southern dialect. **Truth grew up in Ulster County, New York, nearly 100 miles north of New York City, and spoke only Dutch until she was nine.** She was never enslaved in the South. Second, **it has her say she bore thirteen children. NWHM says she had five.** NWHM's word for the Gage version is that Gage **"fabricated"** it. (Sources: LC summary via search, as above; NWHM exhibit, as above.)
- **Neither the 1851 report nor the 1863 version, as the LC summary puts it, actually contains the phrase that became the speech's title — "Ain't I a Woman" is derived from Gage's later article.** **NPS's own page on Truth reproduces only the Gage version.** So the most-quoted sentence attributed to a Black woman in nineteenth-century America comes from a white woman's memory twelve years afterwards, printed in a dialect that was not hers.
- **THE RULE FOR THIS BOOK:** do not quote the 1863 version as words Truth said that day. Name both versions, date both, say who wrote each and what each is, and quote Robinson. **This is exactly the sort of case the `how-we-know` afterword exists for — park a cross-reference there.**

### STORY — Susan B. Anthony — CLEARED from the court record itself
Primary source throughout: **Ann D. Gordon, "The Trial of Susan B. Anthony," Federal Judicial Center, Federal Trials and Great Debates in United States History series**, https://www.fjc.gov/sites/default/files/trials/susanbanthony.pdf (PDF fetched and read in full on 2026-09-09). Supporting: Federal Judicial Center, "Circuit Court Opinions: Associate Justice Ward Hunt, *United States v. Anthony* (1873)," https://www.fjc.gov/history/exhibits/circuit-court-opinions/United-States-v-Anthony ; National Archives Museum, "Courting Confrontation: The Arrest of Susan B. Anthony," https://visit.archives.gov/whats-on/explore-exhibits/courting-confrontation-arrest-susan-b-anthony ; the printed trial account in the Library of Congress NAWSA collection, https://www.loc.gov/resource/rbnawsa.n2152/?st=list . Case citation: ***United States v. Anthony*, 24 F. Cas. 829 (C.C.N.D.N.Y. 1873).**

- **The plan was not to vote.** Under a strategy the National Woman Suffrage Association adopted in **1871**, Anthony expected to be refused registration and then to sue in federal court. Registration officials let her register. Then the polling officials let her vote. The plan changed because she succeeded.
- **The argument being tested.** The 14th Amendment says who is a citizen and forbids states to take away "the privileges and immunities of citizens of the United States." Suffragists argued voting was one of those privileges. This is the same argument Virginia Minor was running in Missouri at the same time.
- **5 November 1872, the first district of the Eighth Ward of Rochester, New York.** **Susan B. Anthony and fourteen other women voted** in the United States election, which included seats in Congress. They had registered a few days earlier. A poll watcher named **Sylvester Lewis** challenged Anthony. The three inspectors of election did what state law required on a challenge: they put her under oath and asked whether she was a citizen, whether she lived in the district, and whether she had been bribed. Her answers satisfied them and **they put her ballots in the boxes.**
- **The charge, in the indictment's own words:** voting "without having a lawful right to vote," in violation of section 19 of the Enforcement Act of 1870, she "being then and there a person of the female sex."
- **Nine days after the election** U.S. Commissioner **William Storrs** issued warrants for **Anthony and all fourteen other women.**
- **18 November 1872 — the arrest.** A deputy federal marshal came to her house and asked her to come downtown to see the commissioner. Anthony's own account of it, which she used in lectures: **"'What for?' I asked. 'To arrest you,' he said. 'Is that the way you arrest men?' 'No.' Then I demanded that I should be arrested properly."** She was taken to the commissioner's office **on the streetcar, at government expense.**
- **All fifteen women were arrested. Only Anthony's actions were examined.** At the examination on **29 November 1872**, Assistant U.S. Attorney **John Pound** insisted on asking the inspectors, over the defence's objection, **"Was Miss Anthony dressed in the apparel of a woman and had she the appearance of a woman?"** **The two Republican inspectors of election were themselves already under arrest for letting the women vote.**
- **26 December 1872** — Storrs ruled Anthony "must give bail to appear and answer to indictments, if bills be found by the Grand Jury." On **30 December** the other fourteen posted bail. Anthony did not, which left her alone in the marshal's custody; **he did not jail her.**
- **The habeas attempt.** **2 January 1873** — attorney **John Van Voorhis** petitioned the district court for a writ of habeas corpus, aiming to reach the Supreme Court. **Judge Nathan Hall** heard it at Albany on **21 January 1873** and refused to release her.
- **24 January 1873 — indicted.** Not guilty plea; bail set at **$1,000**, which **Henry Selden**, her lawyer, paid.
- **She campaigned to the jury pool, because the law would not let her speak at her own trial.** Federal courts before 1878 did not let a criminal defendant testify or address the jury. **During March and April 1873 Anthony spoke in twenty-nine villages and towns of Monroe County**, giving a lecture called **"Is It a Crime for a U.S. Citizen to Vote?"** A Rochester daily printed it in full.
- **22 May 1873** — U.S. Attorney **Richard Crowley** moved the case from the district court to the circuit court sitting in Ontario County in June. That put it in front of **Ward Hunt, an associate justice of the Supreme Court of the United States riding that circuit**, and moved it away from the jurors Anthony had been talking to. **She responded by scheduling a speech in every village in Ontario County.**
- **Why the timing mattered to the government:** in mid-April 1873 the Supreme Court had decided the **Slaughter-House Cases** and **Bradwell v. Illinois**, both reading the privileges and immunities of United States citizenship narrowly. That is the ground Hunt then stood on.
- **17-18 June 1873, Canandaigua, New York, a jury of twelve men.** Crowley called an inspector of election to prove she had voted. Selden had himself sworn and testified that he had advised her the Constitution let her vote. **At the close of the government's case Justice Hunt read out an opinion he had written**, holding that the right to vote is not among the privileges and immunities protected by the 14th Amendment, that states keep the power to bar citizens from voting except on the grounds named in the 15th, and that because Anthony knew New York enfranchised only men she had knowingly broken the law. **He then directed the jury to return a verdict of guilty, and the clerk refused Selden's request to poll the jury.** The jury never deliberated.
- **19 June 1873.** Selden, having worked through the night, moved for a new trial on the ground that Hunt had denied Anthony a jury trial. Hunt — who was being asked to rule on his own conduct — denied it, saying the right to a jury trial **"exists only in respect of a disputed fact."**
- **THE SENTENCING EXCHANGE — verbatim, with its provenance stated.** The FJC volume states plainly: **"Three reports of what Anthony said survive, and none of them comes from the stenographer's transcript of the trial. This, the longest version, is what she chose to publish herself."** Its document source is Ann D. Gordon, ed., *Against an Aristocracy of Sex, 1866 to 1873*, vol. 2 of *Selected Papers of Elizabeth Cady Stanton and Susan B. Anthony* (Rutgers University Press, 2000), 613-16. **Quote it, and say what it is: her own published version, not the court stenographer's.**
  - Judge Hunt: **"Has the prisoner anything to say why sentence shall not be pronounced?"**
  - Anthony: **"Yes, your honor, I have many things to say; for in your ordered verdict of guilty, you have trampled under foot every vital principle of our government. My natural rights, my civil rights, my political rights, my judicial rights, are all alike ignored. Robbed of the fundamental privilege of citizenship, I am degraded from the status of a citizen to that of a subject."**
  - Hunt: **"The Court cannot listen to a rehearsal of arguments the prisoner's counsel has already consumed three hours in presenting."**
  - Anthony: **"May it please your honor, I am not arguing the question, but simply stating the reasons why sentence cannot, in justice, be pronounced against me."**
  - Hunt: **"The Court cannot allow the prisoner to go on."**
  - Anthony: **"May it please the Court to remember that since the day of my arrest last November, this is the first time that either myself or any person of my disfranchised class has been allowed a word of defense before judge or jury—"**
  - Hunt: **"The prisoner must sit down—the Court cannot allow it."**
  - Anthony, on the jury: **"All of my prosecutors, from the 8th ward corner grocery politician, who entered the complaint, to the United States Marshal, Commissioner, District Attorney, District Judge, your honor on the bench, not one is my peer, but each and all are my political sovereigns ... native or foreign born, white or black, rich or poor, educated or ignorant, awake or asleep, sober or drunk, each and every man of them was my political superior; hence, in no sense, my peer."**
  - Hunt: **"The Court must insist—the prisoner has been tried according to the established forms of law."**
  - Anthony: **"Yes, your honor, but by forms of law all made by men, interpreted by men, administered by men, in favor of men, and against women."**
  - Hunt: **"The Court orders the prisoner to sit down. It will not allow another word."**
  - Anthony: **"failing, even, to get a trial by a jury not of my peers—I ask not leniency at your hands—but rather the full rigors of the law."**
  - Hunt: **"The prisoner will stand up. ... The sentence of the Court is that you pay a fine of one hundred dollars and the costs of the prosecution."**
  - Anthony: **"May it please your honor, I shall never pay a dollar of your unjust penalty."** She went on to say she owed a $10,000 debt from publishing her newspaper *The Revolution*, that she would pay every dollar of that honest debt, and **"not a penny shall go to this unjust claim."**
  - Hunt: **"Madam, the Court will not order you committed until the fine is paid."** **That refusal was itself a tactic — jailing her for non-payment would have opened a route to the Supreme Court.**
- **She never paid it.** In **July 1873** the deputy marshal reported back: **"I have made diligent search and can find no goods or chattles [sic] land or tenements"** to seize. The government dropped it.
- **The three election inspectors were tried straight after her**, convicted, and fined **$25 each**. They refused to pay too. **On 25 February 1874 the marshal jailed them.** Rochester supporters brought them food and coffee; Anthony telegraphed Representative **Benjamin Butler** and Senator **Aaron Sargent**; **on 3 March 1874 President Grant pardoned them, and on the same day the voters of the Eighth Ward re-elected them to their posts.**
- **January 1874** — Anthony petitioned Congress to remit her fine. Butler brought a bill to the House floor; **it failed.** In the Senate, Matthew Carpenter said Congress could not reverse a federal court, **but submitted a report condemning Justice Hunt's conduct.**
- **Do not round this off into "she was arrested for voting and fined $100."** The shape of it is: fifteen women voted, all fifteen were arrested, one was singled out for trial, the judge took the verdict away from the jury, the fine was never collected because collecting it would have let her appeal, and the men who let the women vote went to jail while she did not.
- **Her death and the 19th Amendment belong to era 8.** This agent did not source either; do not carry a death year out of this entry.

### STORY — Ida B. Wells — CLEARED, and her own 1892 pamphlet corrects the version the reference pages give
- **Born 16 July 1862 at Holly Springs, Mississippi**, to **James Wells** and **Lizzie Wells**. **Born into slavery**; freed in 1865. (Sources: National Park Service, "Ida B. Wells," https://www.nps.gov/people/idabwells.htm ; National Women's History Museum, "Ida B. Wells-Barnett," https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/ida-b-wells-barnett .)
- **The railroad case, 1884.** She had bought a first-class ticket and **the train crew ordered her into the car for African Americans. She refused, and was forcibly removed from the train.** She sued, **won $500 in the circuit court, and the Tennessee Supreme Court overturned it.** (Source: NPS, as above.) **Two details conflict between sources and both are recorded:** NWHM dates the removal to **4 May 1884** and names the railroad the **Chesapeake and Ohio Railroad**; the Tennessee State Museum names it the **Chesapeake, Ohio and Southeastern Railroad** and dates the reversal to **1887**. (Source: Tennessee State Museum, "Three Events That Defined Ida B. Wells' Fight for Social Justice," https://tnmuseum.org/Stories/posts/ida-b-wells-fight-for-social-justice .) **The outline gives the year and the facts, not the railroad's exact corporate name.**
- **1891 — she was fired** from her job teaching in a segregated Memphis public school for writing about the conditions in the schools. (Source: NPS.)
- **9 March 1892, Memphis — the lynching at the Curve. Say what lynching was, in plain words, at first use:** a killing carried out by a crowd, outside the law, with no trial. **Method, from the record:** the three men were **taken out of their jail cells in the early hours and shot to death.** (Sources: Tennessee State Museum, as above — "in the early morning hours of March 9, the three men were forcibly removed from their cells and shot to death"; NPS — "taken from their cells and murdered.")
- **Why they were killed.** **Thomas "Tom" Moss** was a part-owner of the **People's Grocery**, a co-operative store run by Black people in a mostly Black neighbourhood. It was taking business from a white-owned store nearby. **William Barrett**, one of the rival owners, got a judge to declare the People's Grocery a public nuisance, was deputized by that judge, and marched on the store with a group of plain-clothes deputies. Shots were fired in the confusion and several men were hurt. Moss and two others were arrested and jailed, and killed in their cells. **They were killed for competing in business.** (Source: Tennessee State Museum, as above.)
- **THE THIRD MAN'S NAME IS NOT SETTLED, and the outline says so.** NPS names the three **Tom Moss, Calvin McDowell and Will Stewart.** NWHM names them **Thomas Moss, Calvin McDowell and Henry Steward.** **Give the two names for the third man rather than picking one.** Wells's own 1892 pamphlet does not name them at all — she calls them "the president, the manager and clerk of the grocery."
- **THE MOST IMPORTANT CORRECTION IN THIS ERA: what happened to the *Free Speech* is not what the reference pages say.** NPS: **"A mob stormed her newspaper office and destroyed all of her equipment."** NWHM: **"A white mob subsequently set fire to the *Free Speech* office."** **Wells's own account, printed in 1892, says something different:**
  > "Wednesday evening May 24, 1892, the city of Memphis was filled with excitement. Editorials in the daily papers of that date caused a meeting to be held in the Cotton Exchange Building; a committee was sent for the editors of the Free Speech an Afro-American journal published in that city, and the only reason the open threats of lynching that were not carried out was because they could not be found."
  > "Mr. Fleming, the business manager and owning a half interest the Free Speech, had to leave town to escape the mob, and was afterwards ordered not to return; letters and telegrams sent me in New York where I was spending my vacation advised me that bodily harm awaited my return. Creditors took possession of the office and sold the outfit, and the Free Speech was as if it had never been."
  (Source: Ida B. Wells, *Southern Horrors: Lynch Law in All Its Phases*, 1892, Project Gutenberg ebook 14975, https://www.gutenberg.org/files/14975/14975-h/14975-h.htm , fetched 2026-09-09.)
  **So, from the woman who was there: a committee went looking for the editors and would have lynched them if they had been found; her business partner J. L. Fleming fled Memphis and was told never to come back; she was in New York and was warned not to return; and the paper ended because creditors seized the office and sold the equipment.** **NPS's and NWHM's fire-and-mob version is not supported by her own contemporaneous account. Write hers, and if a later agent wants the fire, it needs its own source.**
  - **Calendar check:** her printed text says **"Wednesday evening May 24, 1892."** **24 May 1892 was a Tuesday; 25 May 1892 was the Wednesday.** The pamphlet's day-name and date do not agree. **The outline gives 24 May 1892 and no weekday.**
- **The editorial that caused it**, from the *Free Speech*, May 1892, quoted in *Southern Horrors*: **"Nobody in this section of the country believes the old thread-bare lie that Negro men rape white women."** (Source: *Southern Horrors*, as above.)
- **Her method, and it is the same method as Dorothea Dix's in era 6: count, publish, hand people a number they cannot argue with.** From *Southern Horrors* (1892), verbatim: **"the dark and bloody record of the South shows 728 Afro-Americans lynched during the past eight years"** (that is, 1884 to 1892 as she reckoned it), and **"not less than one hundred and fifty have been known to have met violent death at the hands of cruel bloodthirsty mobs during the past nine months."** And the sentence that destroys the excuse: **"only one-third of the 728 victims to mobs have been charged with rape, to say nothing of those of that one-third who were innocent."** (Source: *Southern Horrors*, as above.)
- **What the excuse was, and what she said it really was.** The public justification for lynching was that Black men raped white women. Wells's answer was that two-thirds of the people killed were never even accused of it, and that in many of the remaining cases the relationship had been consensual. **"White men lynch the offending Afro-American, not because he is a despoiler of virtue, but because he succumbs to the smiles of white women."** (Source: *Southern Horrors*, as above.) **Saying this in print in Memphis in 1892 is what made the mob come for her.** For a book at this reading level: say that she wrote that white men were killing Black men over relationships white women had chosen, and that saying so in print was what put her in danger.
- **The books.** ***Southern Horrors: Lynch Law in All Its Phases*, 1892** — how mobs formed and what excuses officials gave. ***A Red Record: Tabulated Statistics and Alleged Causes of Lynchings in the United States, 1892-1893-1894*, 1895** — **100 pages**, a history and a statistical record. (Sources: NWHM; NPS.)
- **1893** — she published ***The Reason Why the Colored American Is Not in the World's Columbian Exposition***, **in three languages**, for the Chicago world's fair. **1894** — she toured the United Kingdom lecturing on lynching, and a **London Anti-Lynching Committee** was formed as a result. (Source: NWHM.)
- **1895** — married **Ferdinand Barnett**; four children. **1909** — attended the founding conference of the NAACP and then broke with it because she thought it was not acting. (Source: NPS.)
- **The wider count, for scale — sourced but not fetched.** The NAACP's *Thirty Years of Lynching in the United States, 1889-1918* (1919) gives **3,224 people lynched in those thirty years, 2,522 of them Black and 702 white.** (Pointer: Library of Congress classroom materials PDF, https://www.loc.gov/static/classroom-materials/naacp-a-century-in-the-fight-for-freedom/documents/lynching.pdf — **403 to a direct fetch on 2026-09-09; figure taken from the search summary of that document. Era 8 should verify it before using it, since the period is mostly era 8's.**)
- **Her death date is disputed between the two main reference pages and belongs to era 8 anyway:** NPS gives **25 March 1931**, NWHM gives **15 March 1931**, both Chicago, both kidney disease. **Do not state a day until era 8 settles it.**
- **Also era 8, handed forward:** the **1913 Woman Suffrage Procession in Washington**, where Black suffragists were told to march at the back and Wells refused and walked with the Illinois delegation. (Source: NWHM; see also NPS, "A Noble Endeavor: Ida B. Wells-Barnett and Suffrage," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/a-noble-endeavor-ida-b-wells-barnett-and-suffrage.htm .)
- **Shared with `crime-justice` (lynching as killing outside the law) and `news-media` (investigative journalism). This chapter leads on the campaign.**

### STORY — Sarah Winnemucca — CLEARED
- **Who she was.** **Thocmetony**, meaning **Shell Flower** — also spelled Tocmetoni — of the **Northern Paiute**, born **1844** in what became Nevada. **Daughter of Chief Winnemucca and granddaughter of Chief Truckee.** Died **1891.** Her grandfather and father guided white travellers, including the explorer John C. Fremont. (Sources: National Park Service, "Sarah Winnemucca," https://www.nps.gov/people/sarah-winnemucca.htm ; Architect of the Capitol, "Sarah Winnemucca Statue," https://www.aoc.gov/explore-capitol-campus/art/sarah-winnemucca-statue .)
- **What she saw as a child, in the NPS's own list:** "the murder of two uncles, the attempted rape of her sister, the Pyramid Lake War and Bannock War, and the burning of Paiute food supplies." (Source: NPS, as above.) **Do not compress this list. Each item is a separate documented thing.**
- **Her work.** She had a strong facility with languages and worked in the 1870s as **an interpreter for the Indian Office and a negotiator between the Paiute and the United States Army** — including persuading bands to move onto reservations. **She was inside the system before she attacked it, and the outline says so.** (Sources: NPS; AOC.)
- **1878, the Bannock War.** She **found her father's band, held by Bannock fighters, and led them out to the Army's protection — 230 miles in three days.** (Source: AOC, as above.)
- **The removal, and this is the part that must not be softened.** After the Bannock War, Paiutes who had **not** fought were removed from the **Malheur Reservation**, inside their own homeland, to the **Yakama Reservation, 350 miles north**. **About 550 Paiute men, women and children travelled for nearly a month through the snow and over two mountain ranges. The women and children rode in wagons. Some of the men were made to walk in leg irons. Five children, one woman and an elderly man died on the road, and their bodies were left beside it because the ground was too cold to bury them. The historian Sally Zanjani estimated that more than a fifth of the group died in exile at Yakama, most of them from hunger and disease.** (Sources: Oregon Historical Society, Oregon History Project, "Report by W.V. Rinehart, 1879," https://www.oregonhistoryproject.org/articles/historical-records/report-by-wv-rinehart-1879/ ; NPS, "Sarah Winnemucca," for the removal itself.) **W.V. Rinehart was the agent for the Malheur Reservation. His report of 15 August 1879 complains about the cost of the removal and notes that the Paiute left under-equipped although supplies were on the way.**
- **This is what turned her from an interpreter into a campaigner.** NPS: the removal of Paiutes "who remained peaceful during the Bannock War ... incited her to action."
- **The campaign.** In the early 1880s she gave **more than 300 speeches**. **In 1880 she met President Rutherford B. Hayes and the Secretary of the Interior, Carl Schurz.** NPS records the outcome plainly: **the president did not change his position.** (Sources: NPS; AOC.)
- **The book. *Life Among the Piutes: Their Wrongs and Claims*, 1883**, written with the help of the reformers **Elizabeth Peabody and Mary Peabody Mann**. **The Architect of the Capitol calls it "the first book written by a Native American woman"; the Oregon Historical Society's phrasing is "the first autobiography published by a Native American woman."** Both are recorded; the outline uses the narrower claim.
- **Her demand, verbatim:** **"sweep away the [Indian] agency system; give us homes to live in, for God's sake and for humanity's sake."** (Source: NPS, as above. The bracketed word is NPS's.)
- **The school.** She founded a school for Paiute children and **taught them in Paiute and in English**. **It closed after three years for lack of money.** (Source: NPS.) **This is the exact opposite of what the Indian boarding schools were doing at the same time — Carlisle opened in 1879 and set out to stop children speaking their own languages. `native-nations` leads on the boarding schools; the contrast belongs here because it is a rights argument being made by building a school.**
- **Her statue stands in the United States Capitol for the state of Nevada**, showing her at 35 with a shellflower held up in her right hand and a book at her side. (Source: AOC.)
- **Shared with `native-nations` (lead — the Paiute and the removal) and `education` (the school). This chapter takes the campaign: an interpreter who turned on the system she had worked for, spoke 300 times, and put it in a book.**

### Emancipation and what replaced it — this chapter's slice, with the boundary to `slavery-freedom` respected
`slavery-freedom` hands off at 1877. Its material is emancipation itself and what freedom looked like. **This chapter takes what organized people did about the roll-back — and the best-documented example in the era is a court case that a committee planned on purpose.**
- **What was won in the amendments.** The National Archives puts it: during Reconstruction, Black Americans' political rights "were affirmed through three constitutional amendments and numerous Congressional laws attacking racial discrimination broadly." (Source: National Archives, "Plessy v. Ferguson (1896)," https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/plessy-v-ferguson .)
- **The Civil Rights Act of 1875** made it illegal to deny **"the full and equal enjoyment of any of the accommodations, advantages, facilities, and privileges of inns, public conveyances on land or water, theaters and other places of public amusement."** **In 1883 the Supreme Court struck it down**, holding that the 14th Amendment did not let Congress stop discrimination by private individuals. (Same source.)
- **Louisiana's Separate Car Act, Act 111, passed 10 June 1890.** It required railroads to give "equal but separate" accommodation by race. **Black New Orleanians protested it and there were 16 Black members of the state legislature at the time; it passed anyway.** Penalty for a passenger who broke it: **a $25 fine or 20 days in jail.** (Sources: National Park Service, "Homer Plessy," https://www.nps.gov/people/homer-plessy.htm ; National Archives, as above.)
- **The committee. In September 1891 a group of French-speaking men of African descent in New Orleans formed the Comité des Citoyens — the Citizens' Committee — to test the law in court.** The National Archives gives its full name as **the Citizens' Committee to Test the Constitutionality of the Separate Car Law**, and records that **they hired the attorney Albion W. Tourgée.** (Sources: NPS, as above; National Archives, as above; 64 Parishes, an encyclopedia of the Louisiana Endowment for the Humanities, "Comité des Citoyens," https://64parishes.org/entry/comite-des-citoyens .)
- **Homer Plessy.** Born **Homère Patris Plessy, 17 March 1863, New Orleans**, to free people of colour; a shoemaker. **He volunteered.** (Source: NPS, as above.)
- **7 June 1892 — an arrest arranged in advance.** Plessy bought a ticket to Covington, Louisiana on the **East Louisiana Railroad** and sat in the car for white passengers. **The railroad company was in on it** — it disliked the cost of running extra cars. **A private detective had been hired to make the arrest**, so that the charge would be exactly the one the committee wanted to argue. (Sources: NPS, as above; National Archives, as above.)
- **Judge John H. Ferguson** of the Orleans Parish criminal court ruled against Plessy's constitutional argument. That is whose name is on the case. (Source: National Archives.)
- **Decided 18 May 1896, argued 13 April 1896. Vote: 7 to 1**, Justice Brewer not taking part. Citation **163 U.S. 537**. **Justice Henry Billings Brown** wrote for the Court: **"Laws permitting, and even requiring, their separation, in places where they are liable to be brought into contact, do not necessarily imply the inferiority of either race to the other,"** and, on the argument that segregation stamps Black people as inferior, **"If this be so, it is not by reason of anything found in the act, but solely because the colored race chooses to put that construction upon it."** (Sources: Cornell Legal Information Institute, *Plessy v. Ferguson*, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/163/537 ; National Archives; NPS.)
- **Justice John Marshall Harlan dissented alone. Verbatim:** **"But in view of the constitution, in the eye of the law, there is in this country no superior, dominant, ruling class of citizens."** **"Our constitution is color-blind, and neither knows nor tolerates classes among citizens."** **"What can more certainly arouse race hate, what more certainly create and perpetuate a feeling of distrust between these races, than state enactments?"** (Source: Cornell LII, as above.)
- **NPS: the ruling stood for nearly sixty years, until *Brown v. Board of Education* in 1954. Plessy died in New Orleans in 1925.**
- **The chapter's angle, stated so it does not duplicate anyone:** `government-politics` has *Plessy* as law; `transportation` has the railroad; `slavery-freedom` has what freedom turned into. **This chapter has the committee: people who read a new law, raised money, hired a lawyer, chose a volunteer, arranged the arrest, and lost — and whose losing argument is the one the Court finally accepted in 1954.** Note also that this is the third organized test case in this era alone: Anthony in 1872, the Tapes in 1884, the Comité in 1892.
- **Two era-7 items deliberately NOT taken from `slavery-freedom` or `native-nations`:** the Freedmen's Bureau schools (theirs) and the Dawes Act of 1887 (native-nations). **This agent did not research either and the era-7 outline does not state them.**

**All four era-7 `[VERIFY]` tags are cleared and removed from the outline. Era 7 carries zero `[VERIFY]` tags. Five stories, all `verified`, all sourced above.**
