**Things this agent could not settle, and what the outline does about each**
1. **Full text of Marius Robinson's 1851 report of the Akron speech.** Only the fragments the two NPS pages quote could be fetched; Chronicling America at loc.gov returned 403. The outline quotes the fragments and does not present them as the whole speech. **Next agent: Chronicling America, LCCN sn83035487, 21 June 1851.**
2. **Where Frances Dana Gage published her 1863 version.** NPS and NWHM say the *New York Independent*; a Library of Congress summary says the *National Anti-Slavery Standard* of 2 May 1863. Both recorded; the outline says only "published in 1863."
3. **The third man killed at the Curve in Memphis, 9 March 1892.** NPS: **Will Stewart.** NWHM: **Henry Steward.** The outline gives both and says the name is not settled.
4. **The date of the California Supreme Court's decision in *Tape v. Hurley*.** Not established from a fetched source. The outline gives 9 January 1885 for the Superior Court and says the state Supreme Court upheld it, with no second date.
5. ***Ward v. Flood*, 1874.** Named from search summaries only — three attempted fetches (NPS *Five Views*, Berkeley law catalogue, LC *Brown v. Board at Fifty*) failed. The outline names the case, the parties and the year and quotes nothing.
6. **Smith, Wellesley and Bryn Mawr founding dates.** From a search summary of the Vassar Encyclopedia page, which does not itself carry them; Smith's own page corroborates 1875. Should be replaced with each college's own page before prose.
7. **The years on two Supreme Court cases.** *Minor v. Happersett*, 88 U.S. 162, and *Bradwell v. The State*, 83 U.S. 130: Cornell dates them to the October 1874 and October 1872 terms; both are usually cited a year later. The outline gives citations and terms, not a chosen year.
8. **Ida B. Wells's death date.** NPS: 25 March 1931. NWHM: 15 March 1931. Era 8's problem; do not state a day until it is settled.
9. **NAACP figure of 3,224 lynched 1889-1918** — from a search summary of a loc.gov PDF that returned 403. **Not used in the era-7 outline.** Era 8 should verify before using.

**Fetch blocks hit on 2026-09-09, so nobody repeats them:** every `loc.gov` page tried returned **403** (item pages, exhibitions, the law and Headlines & Heroes blogs, Chronicling America, and static PDFs). Also 403: `blogs.loc.gov`, `cdnc.ucr.edu`, `jcsw.hms.harvard.edu`, `lawcat.berkeley.edu`, `chineseamerican.nyhistory.org` (returned empty). **402:** `facinghistory.org` PDFs. **404:** `nps.gov/people/ida-b-wells.htm` (the working URL is `idabwells.htm`), `nps.gov/people/standing-bear.htm`, `spelman.edu/about-us/history`, `womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/sarah-winnemucca`. **Worked well:** `nps.gov` article and people pages, `archives.gov` and `visit.archives.gov`, `fjc.gov` (including its PDFs — extract with `pdftotext`), `law.cornell.edu`, `gutenberg.org`, `americanyawp.com`, state historical societies (`history.nebraska.gov`, `oregonhistoryproject.org`, `archives.utah.gov`), university pages, `aoc.gov`, `supremecourt.gov`, `sfusd.edu`, `spelman.edu/about/`.

**Cross-chapter material parked on 2026-09-09:** `research-news-communication.md` (the *Free Speech* correction and Wells's method) · `research-education.md` (Clarke 1873 and Jacobi, the coeducation dates and their traps, Vassar, Spelman, the SFUSD chronology, Massachusetts 1855) · `research-native-nations.md` (the Ponca removal and *Standing Bear v. Crook*; the Malheur-to-Yakama removal with its numbers; Sarah Winnemucca's biography).

**Handed to the era-8 agent**
- **Ida B. Wells continues into era 8:** the **1913 Woman Suffrage Procession** in Washington, where Black suffragists were told to march at the back and she refused, and the founding of the NAACP in 1909, which she attended and then broke with. Her death date is disputed (see 8 above).
- **Susan B. Anthony's death and the 19th Amendment are yours.** This agent did not source either. Do not carry a death year out of the era-7 entry.
- **The exclusion laws run on:** the Geary Act of 1892 extended Chinese exclusion, 1902 made it permanent with a registration certificate, and **1943 repealed it, replacing it with a quota of 105 Chinese immigrants a year.**
- **The Comstock Act is still in force in era 8** and is the law Margaret Sanger was prosecuted under. Its text banned information about contraception from the mail and never defined "obscene."
- ***Plessy v. Ferguson* stands for nearly sixty years** and is the wall the NAACP's legal campaign is built to knock down. The era-7 material gives you the committee that lost it.
- **The pattern this era establishes and era 8 inherits:** the organized test case. Anthony 1872, the Tapes 1884, the Comité des Citoyens 1892. All three lost, and each left an argument standing.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262b): Reconstruction violence, where it is told, and the boundary with `slavery-freedom`
- **Owner.** The registry and `slavery-freedom`'s own outline set the handoff at 1877: `slavery-freedom` "Keep out: later civil rights campaigns (`rights-movements`, from Reconstruction's end onward, the handoff sits at 1877)." That chapter's outline and bank already carry, with actors and counts: the Memphis Massacre, 1 to 3 May 1866 (mobs including city policemen, firemen and businessmen; 46 Black residents killed; 91 homes, 4 churches and 8 schools burned, by the congressional committee's count; five women raped, by the same committee's count; Frances Thompson's testimony); New Orleans, 30 July 1866 (Sheriff Harry Hays's deputized police and white mobs; 34 Black people and 3 white delegates killed); the Ku Klux Klan (founded December 1865, Pulaski, Tennessee); the Colfax Massacre, 13 April 1873 (Christopher Columbus Nash and more than 150 armed white men; between 62 and 153 Black men killed, the count uncertain); and the end, the Compromise of 1877 and the withdrawal of federal troops. (Checked 2026-09-27 in `outlines/slavery-freedom.md`, era 1850-1900, and `research/research-slavery-freedom.md`.)
- **What this chapter does with it.** Do not tell those events again. This chapter's era-7 span "what was won, and how it was taken back" may name them in one sentence with the pointer to `slavery-freedom`, and then take the organizing that came after 1877: the test cases, Wells's campaign, and the violence aimed at Black political organizing after 1877, which no chapter yet tells (Wilmington, 1898, below).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262b): Wilmington, North Carolina, 10 November 1898. An elected government overthrown by force, and a Black newspaper burned
Not told anywhere in the book's outlines (grep of `outlines/` for "Wilmington" on 2026-09-27 found only unrelated places). It is violence against Black political organizing and a Black press after 1877, so it sits in this chapter's era 7.
- **The newspaper.** Alex Manly edited the *Daily Record*, Wilmington's only Black newspaper in the 1890s. He published an editorial that "challenged white concepts of interracial relationships," which the Democratic Party used to inflame white voters. (Sources: NCpedia, LeRae Umfleet, "The Wilmington Coup, 1898," revised August 2022, https://www.ncpedia.org/history/cw-1900/wilmington-massacre-1898 ; the "only Black newspaper" wording is from a search summary of NC DNCR, "Wilmington Coup," https://www.dncr.nc.gov/blog/2016/11/10/wilmington-coup (unconfirmed: search summary only).)
- **The leader, named.** Alfred Moore Waddell, "a former Confederate officer and U.S. Congressman," led it. (Source: NC Department of Natural and Cultural Resources, "1898 Wilmington Coup," https://www.dncr.nc.gov/1898-wilmington-coup .) On 7 November 1898 at the county courthouse he told a crowd: "Men, the crisis is upon us. You must do your duty. This city, county, and state shall be rid of negro domination once and forever." (unconfirmed: search summary only, from the DNCR and NCpedia search results.)
- **The count of attackers, two figures.** DNCR: "About 500 white men assembled at the armory" and "the crowd swelled to perhaps 2,000." NCpedia: Waddell organized "as many as 2,000 white people to march on the *Record* printing office." Give both.
- **What they did.** They broke into the *Record* office in Free Love Hall and set it on fire (NCpedia: "broke in and burned the building"; DNCR: "a fire broke out, and the top floor of the building was consumed"). The Red Shirts, a group of Democratic Party supporters, had "intimidated Black people and forced white people to vote for Democratic Party candidates" in the election two days earlier. (Sources: NCpedia; DNCR, as above.)
- **The count of the dead, every figure and whose.** The coroner counted 14. NCpedia: "At least 14 and perhaps as many as 60 men were murdered," and "no official count of dead can be ascertained due to a lack of records." The state's 2006 commission report settled on "as many as sixty." Other reports ran to "scores or even hundreds," unconfirmed. (Sources: DNCR; NCpedia.)
- **The government taken.** The Republican mayor, Silas P. Wright, the aldermen and other officers, Black and white, were forced to resign that afternoon. The new board of aldermen made Waddell mayor. Prominent Black citizens and white Republicans were banished from the city. Manly and "numerous other blacks" fled. (Sources: DNCR; NCpedia.)
- **The response.** Governor Daniel Russell did not ask for federal help and President William McKinley sent no troops. "The U.S. Attorney General's Office investigated, but the files were closed in 1900 with no indictments." No one was punished. (Source: NCpedia.)
- **The record.** The 1898 Wilmington Race Riot Commission, set up by the state, released a draft report in December 2005 and its final report of about 500 pages in May 2006. (Source: DNCR.)
- **Plain wording for the prose.** Call it what the sources call it: a coup, the overthrow of an elected city government by armed force. The killers are unnamed in the sources opened; the leader is named. "No one was ever charged."
- **Parallel for the writer (fact, not commentary):** six years after the Memphis committee hunted for the *Free Speech* editors, a Wilmington mob burned the *Daily Record* over an editorial on the same subject.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262b): Frances Thompson, 1866 and 1876. The LGBTQ thread's first era-7 fact
- **1866 is `slavery-freedom`'s.** Thompson testified to the congressional committee investigating the Memphis Massacre that she and Lucy Smith were raped by seven white men, among them police officers (the parked note says three; a search summary of BlackPast and Wikipedia says two; do not state a number of officers without opening the committee report). `slavery-freedom` tells the testimony. (Sources: parked note from `slavery-freedom`, 2026-09-27; American Yawp Reader, "A case of sexual violence during Reconstruction, 1866," https://www.americanyawp.com/reader/reconstruction/a-case-of-sexual-violence-during-reconstruction-1866/ , catalogued, not re-read.)
- **1876 is this chapter's.** Memphis police arrested Thompson on **10 July 1876** under the city's public indecency law, for wearing women's clothing. The maximum fine was $50. **Dr. Joseph Nuttall, a doctor under contract to the city, and three other doctors examined her body** at the central police station on Adams Street and declared her a man. She could not pay the fine and was sentenced to **100 days on the chain gang at 50 cents a day**, cleaning streets. Crowds gathered to look at her. After harassment from other prisoners and the public she was taken off the chain gang and served the rest at the police station. **She died of dysentery in the city hospital on 1 November 1876.** (Source: MLK50: Justice Through Journalism, "They charged Frances Thompson with indecency when all she wanted to do was live a safe life," 30 May 2024, https://mlk50.com/2024/05/30/they-charged-frances-thompson-with-indecency-when-all-she-wanted-to-do-was-live-a-safe-life/ , which cites the *Memphis Daily Appeal* and *Daily Memphis Avalanche* archives and interviews the historian Hannah Rosen of William & Mary.)
- **Why it matters here.** Conservative Memphis newspapers reported that she had been given a medical exam and "pronounced a member of the male sex," and "Multiple articles used Thompson's identity to try to undermine her testimony to Congress." She told police and reporters she had lived her whole life as a woman and believed she was "of double sex." The city's recorder fined her $50. Authorities made her pose for pictures in men's and women's clothing and the pictures were printed in newspapers. After her release her neighbours found her ill and took her to the hospital. CNN also gives two police officers among the seven men in 1866, and calls her housemate a teenager. (Source: CNN, Chelsea Bailey, "A Black transgender woman's testimony helped ratify the 14th Amendment," 16 February 2025, https://www.cnn.com/2025/02/16/us/frances-thompson-transgender-memphis-massacre , fetched with a browser user-agent 2026-09-27, quoting the historian Hannah Rosen.) The claim that Southern papers used it to call all the 1866 reports invented: (unconfirmed: search summary only, BlackPast, 403).
- **Words.** Sources today describe her as a transgender woman. CNN: "She is believed to be the first Black transgender woman to testify before Congress." A hedged claim from journalism. Do not state that "first" in the prose. For an 11-year-old: say she lived as a woman, that police arrested her for it, that doctors examined her body against her will to decide what she was, and that officials then used that to call her testimony about the rape a lie.
- **Named actors:** the Memphis police (no officer named in the source); Dr. Joseph Nuttall and three unnamed doctors; the court (no judge named in the source).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262b): the reformers who pushed allotment, and what they got. Native resistance in court (Elk v. Wilkins)
`native-nations` leads on allotment as land taken (its bank: 138 million acres in 1887 down to 48 million by 1934, ILTF; 137 to 47 million, Meriam Report). This chapter's angle is that people who called themselves a rights movement for Native Americans organized for the policy that took the land.
- **Women's National Indian Association, 1879.** Founded in Philadelphia by **Mary Lucinda Bonney and Amelia Stone Quinton**, out of the Home Mission Circle of the First Baptist Church of Philadelphia. (Source: Encyclopedia of Greater Philadelphia, Beth A. Twiss Houting, "Indian Rights Associations," https://philadelphiaencyclopedia.org/essays/indian-rights-associations/ .) Its petition against white settlers moving onto land set aside in Indian Territory, on the duty to keep the treaties, was circulated in sixteen states and presented to President Rutherford B. Hayes and the House of Representatives in 1880 (unconfirmed: search summary only, Wikipedia "Women's National Indian Association").
- **Indian Rights Association, 15 December 1882.** Thirty or forty wealthy Philadelphia men met at the home of Judge John Welsh; his son **Herbert Welsh** and **Henry S. Pancoast** had just visited the Sioux in Dakota Territory. Its stated aim: "to secure to the Indians of the United States the political and civil rights already guaranteed to them by treaty and statutes." Its members "believed assimilation and Americanization within a Protestant context were the way for Indians to survive." (Source: Encyclopedia of Greater Philadelphia, as above.)
- **Helen Hunt Jackson.** In 1879 she heard Standing Bear lecture in Boston describing how the Ponca were driven off their land. Her book *A Century of Dishonor* came out in 1881; she sent President Garfield an inscribed copy on 25 January 1881 and sent a copy to every member of Congress with the note "Look upon your hands: they are stained with the blood of your relations." NPS: it "did little to dissuade American politicians" at first, but led readers "to petition their government for better treatment of the Indians." (Source: NPS, "A Century of Dishonor by Helen Hunt Jackson," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/a-century-of-dishonor-by-helen-hunt-jackson.htm .) The Standing Bear lecture tour of 1879 is therefore where this reform wave started.
- **Lake Mohonk, 1883.** Albert K. Smiley, a Quaker who owned the Lake Mohonk resort in New York, hosted a yearly Conference of Friends of the Indian from 1883 to 1916. "Virtually everyone who was prominent in the movement for the reform of Indian policy" came. It pressed for education, citizenship, and "the allotment of reservation land in individual homesteads," and backed industrial boarding schools and breaking tribal bonds. The source names no Native participants. (Source: EBSCO Research Starters, Mark S. Joy, "Analysis: Address of the Lake Mohonk Conference on Indian Affairs," 2019, https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/politics-and-government/analysis-address-lake-mohonk-conference-indian-affairs/ .)
- **What they got: the Dawes Act, 8 February 1887,** sponsored by Senator Henry Dawes of Massachusetts. A quarter-section (160 acres) to each head of a family, an eighth-section to each single adult and orphan; land "not allotted" could be bought from the tribes and sold to settlers; every allottee "is hereby declared to be a citizen of the United States." (Source: National Archives, "Dawes Act (1887)," https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/dawes-act .) The land it took: `native-nations` era 7.
- **How the prose can say it plainly:** the reformers meant to help, organized petitions, conferences and a lobby, and what they won was a law that took about two-thirds of the land Native nations still held. Name them. Do not call them villains and do not call them heroes.
- **Elk v. Wilkins, 3 November 1884. CLEARS the era-7 flag "flagged here, not researched."** **John Elk**, a Native man who had left his tribe and lived in Omaha, Nebraska, applied in **April 1880** to register to vote in the Fifth Ward. **Charles Wilkins**, the ward's registrar, refused him because he was Native. The Supreme Court ruled against Elk **7 to 2**, Justice Horace Gray writing, Justices John Marshall Harlan and William B. Woods dissenting. Holding: Native people born into their nations were no more "born in the United States and subject to the jurisdiction thereof," within the meaning of the 14th Amendment, than children of foreign diplomats, so birth did not make Elk a citizen. (Source: Cornell Legal Information Institute, *Elk v. Wilkins*, 112 U.S. 94, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/112/94 .) This is the case the outline calls "a case five years later" that "went the other way."

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262b): the Ponca removal, who carried it out, and whose land they were sent to
Replaces "the United States forced" with named actors. (Source for all: Nebraska State Parks marker "The Forced Removal," Niobrara State Park, transcribed at the Historical Marker Database, https://www.hmdb.org/m.asp?m=312195 , fetched 2026-09-27.)
- **Why the land was taken:** the 1868 treaty with the Sioux had assigned the Ponca reservation to the Sioux (search summary of the Wikisource text "Removal of the Ponca Indians," https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Removal_of_the_Ponca_Indians , unconfirmed: search summary only).
- In 1876 **Congress authorized $25,000 to move the tribe to Indian Territory "if they consented to go."** Eight chiefs were taken south to choose a new site. They refused every site and asked to go home. They were refused, and walked the 500-plus miles back to Nebraska in winter in 40 days, "with only a few dollars and one blanket each."
- "Despite many pleas, the tribe was ordered to move, using Army troops if necessary." **Indian Inspector E. C. Kemble** took the first group of **170** south, leaving **30 April 1877**, and ordered **Agent E. A. Howard** to remove the rest. They left on **16 May 1877 under an escort of 25 soldiers**, leaving their homes, crops and burial grounds. Plains History (Nebraska) adds that the "consent" came from "the two elderly and blind Chiefs" Kemble had brought back with him, not from the chiefs who refused (https://plainshistory.org/items/show/39 , fetched).
- **Whose land:** they crossed into Indian Territory on 9 July 1877 onto **Quapaw land**, and later chose **101,894 acres of former Cherokee land** on the Salt Fork of the Arkansas River.
- Counts: the bank's "a third of the tribe died" stands (NPS MNRR). A search summary gives about 720 Ponca in all (170 plus 550) and nine deaths on the 54-day march, among them Standing Bear's daughter Prairie Flower (unconfirmed: search summary only). NPS MNRR quotes a report that the Ponca had "already lost 36" in 1877.
- **Standing Bear's lawyers, named:** **John L. Webster and Andrew J. Poppleton** of Omaha. The group arrested with him was "a few members of his tribe, mostly women and children" (NPS, https://home.nps.gov/mnrr/learn/historyculture/standingbear.htm ). A figure of "30+" and an arrest date of 27 March 1879 appear only on an advocacy site (Native Hope, https://blog.nativehope.org/chief-standing-bears-legacy-civil-rights-leader-of-his-time-part2 ).
- **THE "FIRST" IN THE OUTLINE IS NOT SUPPORTED.** The outline said Standing Bear "did something no Native American had done before: he sued the federal government." NPS MNRR makes no "first" claim. The U.S. Courts page (https://www.uscourts.gov/data-news/judiciary-news/2020/10/29/chief-standing-bear-a-hero-native-american-civil-rights) says only that he "persuaded a federal judge to recognize Native Americans as persons with the right to sue for their freedom." Only Native Hope, an advocacy site, calls him "the first Native American to file a writ of habeas corpus against the U.S. government." The outline sentence was changed to drop the "first."

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262b): the Paiute removal, who ordered it, and Sarah Winnemucca's written promise
- **Who ordered it.** Sarah Winnemucca wrote that she learned "just a little before Christmas" of 1878 that **President Rutherford B. Hayes** had ordered her people taken to the Yakama Reservation under military escort. "My people were only given one week to get ready in." She was told to say nothing until three days before they left. (Her *Life Among the Piutes*, 1883, as quoted in the *Yakima Herald-Republic*, "More than 500 Northern Paiute were forced to Yakama Reservation in 1879," 2024, https://www.yakimaherald.com/news/local/more-than-500-northern-paiute-were-forced-to-yakama-reservation-in-1879/article_01696c50-f8ef-11ee-a712-6b522fc09e7b.html .)
- **Why:** the Paiute "were punished for the participation of a small number of the tribe" in the Bannock War. (Source: U.S. House of Representatives, History, Art & Archives, "Land of Misfortune: Sarah Winnemucca Petitions Congress," 26 March 2018, https://history.house.gov/Blog/Detail/15032450781 .)
- **The promise broken.** In 1880 in Washington she "received a written promise from Secretary of the Interior Carl Schurz that the Paiutes could return to Malheur, but Schurz reneged on his assurance, and this never happened." In **1884** she petitioned Congress to restore her people to Malheur, in a tone "of righteous demand rather than supplication." (Source: House History, as above.) The outline's "the president did not change his position" stays; add Schurz's broken written promise.
- **Whose land:** Malheur was created in 1872 by President Grant's executive order inside Paiute country; **President Chester Arthur returned it to the public domain in 1882 and 1883**, so it was opened to white settlers; by 1883 every removed Paiute had left Yakama (some to Warm Springs or Nevada, some back to the Harney Basin near Burns). The Yakama Reservation they were sent to was the Yakama Nation's. (Sources: Oregon History Project, "Malheur Indian Reservation," https://www.oregonhistoryproject.org/articles/historical-records/malheur-indian-reservation/ ; House History, as above.)

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262b): Chinese Americans' legal fights, the numbers, and the largest refusal to obey a law in the era
- **Yick Wo v. Hopkins, 10 May 1886, unanimous, Justice Stanley Matthews writing.** **Peter Hopkins was the sheriff of San Francisco** who held Yick Wo. About **200 Chinese laundry owners** applied for permits under the ordinance and **all were refused**; about **80 non-Chinese** applied and **all but one** were granted (the one refused was Mary Meagles). **More than 150 Chinese** were arrested for working without consent. The Court: if a law "is applied and administered by public authority with an evil eye and an unequal hand, so as practically to make unjust and illegal discriminations between persons in similar circumstances," it denies equal protection. (Source: Cornell LII, *Yick Wo v. Hopkins*, 118 U.S. 356, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/118/356 .) Actors: the San Francisco Board of Supervisors refused the permits; the sheriff jailed the men.
- **United States v. Wong Kim Ark, 28 March 1898, 6 to 2.** Justice Horace Gray wrote for the Court. **Chief Justice Melville Fuller dissented, joined by Justice John Marshall Harlan.** Justice Joseph McKenna did not take part, having joined the Court after argument. Wong Kim Ark was born at **751 Sacramento Street, San Francisco, in 1873**, and was refused landing at the port of San Francisco in **August 1895** on his return from a visit to China. **Henry Foote**, named by the Society as the District Attorney for San Francisco, brought the case against him. (Sources: Cornell LII, *United States v. Wong Kim Ark*, 169 U.S. 649, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/169/649 ; Supreme Court Historical Society, "United States v. Wong Kim Ark (1898)," https://civics.supremecourthistory.org/article/united-states-v-wong-kim-ark/ .) Note: Harlan, who dissented alone in *Plessy* two years earlier, joined the dissent here.
- **The Geary Act, 1892, and the refusal to register.** The act extended exclusion and required Chinese laborers to register and carry a **certificate of residence** at all times. Anyone caught without one faced **up to a year of hard labor and deportation**, unless he could prove his residence through **two white witnesses** (KQED) or "at least one credible white witness" (the 1892 act as quoted in *Fong Yue Ting*). The **Chinese Six Companies** (the Chinese Consolidated Benevolent Association) told people **not to register**, collected **$1 from each Chinese resident** to pay lawyers, and put out leaflets declaring "The Geary Act is an unjust law." Of about **100,000** Chinese residents, only about **13,000** registered in the first period, roughly **85 percent refusing**. Historians call it the first mass resistance to the American deportation system and one of the largest campaigns of civil disobedience in United States history. (Source: KQED, Cecilia Lei, "Chinese Immigrants Led the First Mass Resistance to America's Deportation System," 25 August 2026, https://www.kqed.org/news/12096279/chinese-immigrants-led-the-first-mass-resistance-to-americas-deportation-system , citing the historians Kelly Lytle Hernández (UCLA), Alexander Jin (Dartmouth) and Russell Jeung (San Francisco State).)
- **The test case lost: Fong Yue Ting v. United States, 15 May 1893, 5 to 3,** Justice Gray writing, Justices David Brewer and Stephen Field and Chief Justice Fuller dissenting (dissenters' names from a search summary; the vote from KQED; Brewer's dissent from Cornell). The three men chosen for the test were **Fong Yue Ting** (in the country since about 1879), **Wong Quan** (since about 1877) and **Lee Joe** (since about 1874). Lee Joe had applied for a certificate in April 1893 and was **refused because his only witnesses were Chinese**. The Court held that the power to expel foreigners is the same as the power to exclude them. Brewer called deportation punishment: "removal from home, from family, from business, from property." (Source: Cornell LII, *Fong Yue Ting v. United States*, 149 U.S. 698, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/149/698 .) The Six Companies had selected the three men (unconfirmed: search summary only).
- **The pattern:** the same shape as Plessy, a community picking its test case and paying for it, and losing.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262b): Ida B. Wells. Who killed the three men, what Memphis did, and the Willard dispute
- **The killers.** About 2:30 a.m. on 9 March 1892, **75 men in black masks** surrounded the Shelby County Jail and **nine went in** (unconfirmed: search summary only, Wikipedia "People's Grocery lynchings" via search). The men were taken to the Chesapeake and Ohio rail yards. **Calvin McDowell was shot at close range with a shotgun and Will Stewart was shot in the neck.** Thomas Moss's last words were reported as "Tell my people to go west. There is no justice for them here." (Source: The Lynching Sites Project of Memphis, "The People's Grocery Lynchings," https://lynchingsitesmem.org/lynching/peoples-grocery-lynchings-thomas-moss-will-stewart-calvin-mcdowell , which names the third man **Will Stewart**, as NPS does.)
- **No one was charged.** Wells, 1892: "The Afro-Americans of Memphis denounced the lynching of three of their best citizens, and urged and waited for the authorities to act in the matter and bring the lynchers to justice. No attempt was made to do so." (Source: *Southern Horrors*, Project Gutenberg 14975, fetched 2026-09-27.)
- **What happened before the lynching, in her words:** after the shooting at the store, in which three deputies were wounded, "Thirty-one men were arrested and thrown in jail as 'conspirators.'" (Same source.)
- **What Black Memphis did about it. Organizing, sourced:** "the black men left the city by thousands, bringing about great stagnation in every branch of business. Those who remained so injured the business of the street car company by staying off the cars, that the superintendent, manager and treasurer called personally on the editor of the Free Speech, asked them to urge our people to give them their patronage again." (Same source.) A walk-out and a streetcar boycott, both in 1892. The *Free Speech* had advised people to leave.
- **The editorial's date.** Wells gives it herself in *A Red Record* (1895): printed in the *Free Speech* on **21 May 1892**. (Source: *A Red Record*, Project Gutenberg 14977, https://www.gutenberg.org/files/14977/14977-h/14977-h.htm , fetched 2026-09-27.)
- **Exile, in her words (supports the outline's line that she did not go back):** "the writer hereof became an exile; her property destroyed and her return to her home forbidden under penalty of death." (Source: *A Red Record*, 1895, as above.)
- **Her count kept going.** *A Red Record*, using the *Chicago Tribune*'s yearly lists: "In 1892 there were 241 persons lynched"; for 1893 she lists the Black victims by the alleged cause, totalling 159. (Same source.)
- **Wells and Frances Willard, 1890 to 1894. Clears the parked `drugs-alcohol` item from the woman's own text.** Frances Willard, president of the Woman's Christian Temperance Union, told the *New York Voice* on 23 October 1890, after a Southern tour: "The colored race multiplies like the locusts of Egypt. The grogshop is its center of power. The safety of woman, of childhood, the home, is menaced in a thousand localities at this moment." Wells printed that in Britain in 1894 when asked what American reformers had done against lynching. In her address to the WCTU convention at Cleveland on 5 November 1894 Willard answered that Wells's "zeal for her race" had "clouded her perception" and that Wells had "put an imputation upon half the white race in this country that is unjust." Wells answered in *A Red Record*, chapter 8, "Miss Willard's Attitude": "At no time, nor in any place, have I made statements 'concerning white women having taken the initiative in nameless acts between the races.'" (Source: *A Red Record*, as above.) The point for the prose: the most famous women's reformer in the country used the same excuse for lynching Wells was fighting, and fought her in public.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262b): Ward v. Flood, 1874, now sourced from the decision's text
- The decision is transcribed at BlackPast, "(1874) Ward v. Flood," https://blackpast.org/african-american-history/ward-v-flood-1874/ (fetched 2026-09-27 with a browser user-agent): *Mary Frances Ward, by A. J. Ward, her Guardian, ad litem, v. Noah F. Flood, Principal of the Broadway Grammar School*, Supreme Court of California, **January 1874**.
- **Her mother, Harriet A. Ward, swore the petition:** Mary Frances was "between eleven and twelve years" old; the family were "of African descent, colored citizens of the United States and of the State of California," had lived in San Francisco for thirteen years, and lived at 1006 Pacific Street; the Broadway Grammar School was the nearest public school.
- **The rule it applied:** California's school law, Section 56: "The education of children of African descent, and Indian children, shall be provided for in separate schools." The San Francisco Board of Education's rule, Section 117: "Children of African or Indian descent shall not be admitted into schools for white children."
- **The Wards lost.** The court denied the writ. It said the record showed she was not far enough in her studies to enter any class, and went on to hold that separate schools were lawful, and that a Black child could be kept out of a white school only where a separate school existed. San Francisco had one. The decision was by Chief Justice William T. Wallace (unconfirmed: search summary only). The outline now says the family lost.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262b): the "firsts" in the era-7 outline, checked
- **Chinese Exclusion Act.** The outline said it was "the only time the United States wrote one named nationality out of citizenship by statute." No source opened says that. Britannica: "the first and only major federal legislation to explicitly suspend immigration for a specific nationality" (https://www.britannica.com/topic/Chinese-Exclusion-Act , fetched with a browser user-agent 2026-09-27). Immigration History (University of Texas): Chinese were "the first such group identified by race and class for severely limited legal entry and ineligibility for citizenship," and the 1882 law "affirmed the 1790 Nationality Act's bar against naturalization by Asians" (https://immigrationhistory.org/item/an-act-to-execute-certain-treaty-stipulations-relating-to-chinese-aka-the-chinese-exclusion-law/). National Archives: "the first significant law restricting immigration into the United States." **Conflict to record:** NPS calls the 1875 Page Act the first restrictive federal immigration law; the National Archives calls the 1882 act the first *significant* one. Both can stand if worded that way. The outline sentence was changed to Britannica's claim.
- **Smith, Wellesley, Bryn Mawr, replaced with each college's own page (clears the era-7 caveat).** Smith: founded 1871; "Smith College opened in the fall of 1875 with 14 students and six faculty under the presidency of Laurenus Clark Seelye" (https://www.smith.edu/about-smith/smith-history). Wellesley: founded 1870 by Pauline and Henry Fowle Durant; "opened its doors to 314 students on September 8, 1875" (https://www.wellesley.edu/about-us/150 ; https://www.wellesley.edu/about/collegehistory). Bryn Mawr: a Quaker college for women funded by the bequest of Dr. Joseph W. Taylor; its opening ceremonies were in September 1885 (Bryn Mawr *Bulletin*, "140 Years of Bryn Mawr," https://www.brynmawr.edu/bulletin/140-years-bryn-mawr).
- **Arabella Mansfield.** Now from pages that open: she passed the bar on **15 June 1869** at Mount Pleasant, Iowa. The Iowa law described "any white male citizen" as eligible; she argued there was nothing in it forbidding a woman, and was admitted, with her husband John Melvin Mansfield. She never practised law and became a college teacher and administrator (Iowa Wesleyan, DePauw). (Sources: Harris County Law Library, "Friday Firsts: Arabella 'Belle' Mansfield," 6 March 2020, https://www.harriscountylawlibrary.org/ex-libris-juris/2020/3/6/friday-firsts-arabella-belle-mansfield-first-woman-admitted-to-the-bar-in-the-us ; *The Gazette* (Cedar Rapids), "Time Machine: 'Petticoats at the bar'," https://www.thegazette.com/history/time-machine-petticoats-at-the-bar-as-nations-first-female-lawyer-was-an-iowan/ .)
- **Charlotte E. Ray.** DC Office of the Attorney General: she "graduated from the Howard University School of Law in 1872 and was admitted to the District of Columbia bar" and "was forced to close her practice because, as a woman of color, she could not obt[ain enough clients]" (https://oag.dc.gov/blog/black-history-month-celebrating-charlotte-e-ray). Graduation date **27 February 1872**, Howard Law's first woman graduate, and the "C.E. Ray" application: (unconfirmed: search summary only, BlackPast and others). The outline keeps the "C. E. Ray" detail; the writer must mark it as the usual account or cut it.
- **Standing Bear "first to sue":** not supported, see the Ponca PATCH. Outline changed.
- **Seraph Young, Esther Morris, Wyoming 1869, Crumpler, Jacobi, Lockwood, Iowa, Michigan, Cornell:** already sourced to pages that opened (era-7 entries above). Left as they are.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262b): carry-overs from the earlier handoffs, settled
- **Marius Robinson's full 1851 text, now in the bank.** NPS prints the whole of it (NPS, "Lesson Plan: Ain't I A Woman?", https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/aint-i-a-woman-lesson-plan.htm , fetched 2026-09-27): "May I say a few words? I want to say a few words about this matter. I am a woman's rights. I have as much muscle as any man, and can do as much work as any man. I have plowed and reaped and husked and chopped and mowed, and can any man do more than that? I have heard much about the sexes being equal. I can carry as much as any man, and can eat as much too, if I can get it. I am as strong as any man that is now. As for intellect, all I can say is, if a woman have a pint and a man a quart--why can't she have her little pint full? You need not be afraid to give us our rights for fear we will take too much, for we can't take more than our pint will hold. The poor men seem to be all in confusion, and don't know what to do. Why children, if you have woman's rights, give it to her and you will feel better. You will have your own rights, and they won't be so much trouble. I can't read, but I can hear. I have heard the Bible and have learned that Eve caused man to sin. Well, if woman upset the world, do give her a chance to set it right side up again. The lady has spoken about Jesus, how he never spurned woman from him, and she was right. When Lazarus died, Mary and Martha came to him with faith and love and besought him to raise their brother. And Jesus wept--and Lazarus came forth. And how came Jesus into the world? Through God who created him and woman who bore him. Man, where is your part? But the women are coming up blessed by God and a few of the men are coming up with them. But man is in a tight place, the poor slave is on him, woman is coming on him, and he is surely between a hawk and a buzzard." Robinson added: "Those only can appreciate it who saw her powerful form, her whole-souled, earnest gestures, and listened to her strong and truthful tones." (The double hyphens are the printed text's; a writer must not render them as dashes in book prose.) The Chronicling America page (LCCN sn83035487) still returns 403.
- **Where Gage published, 1863.** NPS (same lesson plan) and The Sojourner Truth Project (https://www.thesojournertruthproject.com/compare-the-speeches/) both say the *New York Independent*; the Project dates the issue **23 April 1863**. The LC summary's *National Anti-Slavery Standard*, 2 May 1863, remains the other claim. Two sources against one; the outline still says only "published in 1863."
- **The speech's date: a conflict.** The Sojourner Truth Project dates the Akron speech **29 May 1851**; NPS and the bank say **28 May 1851**. The convention ran two days (NPS). Record both; the outline keeps NPS's 28 May.
- **Sojourner Truth's streetcar case, settled in part.** In the mid-1860s in Washington, "when a streetcar conductor tried to violently block her from riding, she ensured his arrest and won her subsequent case" (National Women's History Museum, https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/sojourner-truth); the U.S. Capitol Visitor Center: "she pressed charges against him and won her case" (https://www.visitthecapitol.gov/who-are-the-people-ar/sojourner-truth); Michigan Women Forward dates it 1865 and says she "sued and won a judgment against a streetcar company" (https://miwf.org/celebrating-women/michigan-womens-hall-of-fame/sojourner-truth/). **The dislocated arm or shoulder is still (unconfirmed: search summary only).** Outline updated to say she had the conductor arrested and won.
- **New York's 1860 act on married women's earnings (carry-over "still unchecked," era 7).** After a petition drive in every county, conventions in 40 of the state's 60 counties and lectures in 150 towns (speakers included Frances D. Gage, Antoinette Brown Blackwell and Susan B. Anthony), State Senator **Andrew J. Colvin** brought a bill that Anthony had given him. The senate passed it on 28 February 1860, the assembly on **20 March 1860**, and Governor Edwin D. Morgan signed it. Its words: "the earnings of any married woman, from her trade, business, labor or services, shall be her sole and separate property, and may be used or invested by her in her own name." (Source: *New York Almanack*, Herb Hallas, "1860: Married Women As Wage Slaves," 7 March 2014, https://www.newyorkalmanack.com/2014/03/1860-married-women-as-wage-slaves/ .) **Date conflict:** the *New-York Saturday Press* of 21 April 1860 printed it as "Passed March 30th, 1860" (catalogue record, The Vault at Pfaff's, Lehigh University, https://pfaffs.web.lehigh.edu/node/56182). New York repealed parts in 1862, removing a married woman's equal guardianship of her children (unconfirmed: search summary only). Before 1860, a husband owned his wife's wages: that is coverture, era 3's rule, still in force.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262b): land in era 7
- **Wyoming, 1869.** South Pass City, William Bright's town, was a gold camp that sprang up after the Carissa Lode was found in 1867 on Willow Creek near South Pass, in country the Eastern Shoshone's 1863 treaty had recognized as theirs (a homeland of about 44 million acres). The 1868 Fort Bridger treaty cut that to a reservation of about 3.2 million acres on the Wind River. White prospectors had been "furtively prospecting" there "since the 1850s," despite the treaties. (Source: WyoHistory.org, "Coming to Wind River: The Eastern Shoshone Treaties of 1863 and 1868," https://www.wyohistory.org/encyclopedia/coming-wind-river-eastern-shoshone-treaties-1863-and-1868 .) The same source says Crow, Lakota, Cheyenne and Arapaho hunters also used parts of that country in the 1860s.
- **The Ponca in Indian Territory:** Quapaw land, then former Cherokee land (Ponca PATCH).
- **The Paiute:** Malheur inside Paiute country; Yakama Nation's reservation; Malheur opened to settlers 1882-1883 (Paiute PATCH).
- **Spelman, Vassar, Smith, Wellesley, Bryn Mawr, San Francisco schools:** not researched for prior owners; not a harm the outline states. Left.

Sources checked: Wells, *Southern Horrors* (1892), which says the authorities made "no attempt" to find the lynchers and names none; The Lynching Sites Project of Memphis, which names the victims and the rail yard and no killer; NPS and NWHM (bank above), which name no one; a search summary of Wikipedia (75 masked men, nine entered). No source opened names any member of the mob, and none records an arrest.

Sources checked: NCpedia (Umfleet), NC DNCR "1898 Wilmington Coup," search summaries of the NEH and UNC guides. All name Waddell as leader and the Red Shirts as a group. None opened names a man who fired, and the federal investigation ended in 1900 with no indictments.
