# ERA 8 — 1900 to 1950 — RESEARCHED 2026-09-09

Researched by the era-8 agent. Everything below is fetched and cited per fact. Where a
source could not be fetched, that is said. Eras 9-10 remain untouched.

## 8. 1900 to 1950

### [VERIFY] the NAACP legal campaign — the sequence of cases and their years — CLEARED

The outline said the campaign "works upward from graduate and professional schools toward
the elementary classroom over about twenty years." That is right, and here is the sequence
with dates. The elementary-classroom end of it (*Brown*, 1954) falls in **era 9**.

**Charles Hamilton Houston** — born Washington, D.C., 3 September 1895; died Washington, D.C.,
22 April 1950. First African American editor of the *Harvard Law Review*. Became dean of
Howard University Law School in 1929 and rebuilt it as a training school for civil rights
lawyers. First special counsel of the NAACP in the 1930s. His stated strategy: "start with
the law schools and higher education, and integrate schools from the top down." Thurgood
Marshall was his student at Howard. Houston died four years before *Brown*.
(NPS, https://www.nps.gov/people/charles-hamilton-houston.htm)

**1. Maryland, 1935-36 — *Pearson v. Murray*.** Donald Gaines Murray (1914-1986) applied to the
University of Maryland School of Law on **24 January 1935** and was refused because he was
Black; the Board of Regents refused his appeal. Houston, Thurgood Marshall and the Baltimore
lawyer Nicholas Gosnell took it to the Baltimore City Court, where **Judge Eugene O'Dunne**
ordered university president **Raymond A. Pearson** to admit him (1935). The **Maryland Court
of Appeals affirmed on 15 January 1936**. Murray graduated in **1938**. This was a state case,
not a Supreme Court one.
(University of Maryland Francis King Carey School of Law, Thurgood Marshall Law Library special
collections, https://www2.law.umaryland.edu/marshall/specialcollections/murray/)
*The Maryland State Archives holds a biography of Murray at
https://msa.maryland.gov/megafile/msa/speccol/sc5300/sc5339/000066/000000/000001/unrestricted/dgmurrayfinal.doc — not fetched.*

**2. Missouri, 1935-38 — *Missouri ex rel. Gaines v. Canada*, 305 U.S. 337.** See the story block
below. **Decided 12 December 1938**, argued 9 November 1938. Chief Justice Charles Evans Hughes
wrote for the Court; **Justice McReynolds dissented, joined by Justice Butler**. The holding:
"The State was bound to furnish him within its borders facilities for legal education
substantially equal to those which the State there afforded for persons of the white race."
And: "Manifestly, the obligation of the State to give the protection of equal laws can be
performed only where its laws operate, that is, within its own jurisdiction."
(Cornell LII, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/305/337)

**3. Oklahoma, 1946-49 — *Sipuel v. Board of Regents*, 332 U.S. 631.** **Ada Lois Sipuel** was born
in Chickasha, Oklahoma on **8 February 1924**. She applied to the University of Oklahoma College
of Law on **14 January 1946**. President **George Lynn Cross** refused her and put in writing
that there was **no academic reason to reject her application** — only that state law forbade
teaching Black and white students in the same room. Thurgood Marshall took the case. The
Supreme Court decided it on **12 January 1948**. Oklahoma's answer was to create a **Langston
University School of Law in five days, in rooms in the State Capitol**. She was finally admitted
to the University of Oklahoma on **18 June 1949**, three years and five months after she applied,
and graduated in **August 1952**. She joined the Langston University faculty in 1957, took an
M.A. in history at OU in 1968, retired in December 1987, was appointed to OU's Board of Regents
on **22 April 1992**, and died **18 October 1995**.
(Oklahoma Historical Society, *Encyclopedia of Oklahoma History and Culture*,
https://www.okhistory.org/publications/enc/entry.php?entry=FI009)

**4. 1950 and after belongs to era 9** — *Sweatt v. Painter* and *McLaurin v. Oklahoma State
Regents* (both decided 1950), then *Brown v. Board of Education* (1954). By the boundary rule
they are the era-9 agent's, and this outline hands them forward rather than reaching for them.

**The shape of it, in one line:** eleven years from a Baltimore law school to a Supreme Court
ruling that a state must build the equal thing or open the door, and sixteen more to the
elementary classroom. `education` tells what changed inside the schools; `government-politics`
tells the rulings as law.

### [VERIFY] the California school desegregation case before *Brown* — CLEARED, and there are two, fifteen years apart

**The case the tag meant is *Mendez, et al. v. Westminster School District of Orange County,
et al.*, decided 1946 and affirmed 1947.** `education` already carries **Sylvia Mendez** as a
verified story with the family's side of it — the Seventeenth Street School, the Hoover School,
Judge Paul J. McCormick's ruling of 18 February 1946 and the Ninth Circuit's of 14 April 1947.
**This chapter does not retell that.** This chapter takes the part `education` does not have:
who else joined the fight, and what the case did to the law of the state.

- The lawyer was **David C. Marcus**, who had already won a 1944 case against San Bernardino
  over segregated public swimming pools and parks. The five plaintiff families were **Mendez,
  Guzman, Palomino, Estrada and Ramirez**. (NPS, "Entangled Inequalities,"
  https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/entangled-inequalities-japanese-incarceration-and-mendez-et-al-v-westminster-school-district-of-orange-county-et-al.htm)
- Judge McCormick ordered the districts to stop "discriminatory practices against the pupils of
  Mexican descent" and wrote: **"A paramount requisite in the American system of public education
  is social equality."** (Administrative Office of the U.S. Courts,
  http://www.uscourts.gov/about-federal-courts/educational-resources/educational-activities/mendez-v-westminster-re-enactment/background-mendez-v-westminster-re-enactment)
- **On appeal the case stopped being one family's case.** Friend-of-the-court briefs were filed by
  the **NAACP** — written by **Thurgood Marshall, Robert L. Carter and Loren Miller** — and by the
  **Japanese American Citizens League**, the **League of United Latin American Citizens**, the
  **American Jewish Congress** and the **American Civil Liberties Union**. Marshall used the same
  argument seven years later in *Brown*. (NPS, as above, for the NAACP brief and its three authors;
  the other four organisations are from a search summary of the NPS and Facing History pages and
  are named in the outline as organisations, without further claims about their briefs.)
- **In June 1947 Governor Earl Warren signed the Anderson Bill**, repealing the sections of the
  California school code that allowed segregated schools. California was the first state to
  repeal school segregation. Warren became Chief Justice in 1953 and wrote *Brown* in 1954.
  (NPS, as above.)
- **The farm.** The Mendez family were share-lease farmers on a 40-acre farm on Edwards Street in
  Westminster belonging to **Seima Munemitsu** and his family, who were imprisoned at the **Poston
  Relocation Center from May 1942 to September 1945**. A banker, Frank Monroe, introduced them.
  The lease papers of December 1944 and August 1945-August 1946 survive. The family that broke
  California's school color line was farming land taken out of the hands of a family the same
  government had locked up. (NPS, as above.)

**The earlier case the outline did not know about: *Roberto Alvarez v. the Board of Trustees of
the Lemon Grove School District*, San Diego County, 1931.** It is the first successful school
desegregation case in the United States, fifteen years before *Mendez*.
- The school board planned it in the summer of 1930. On **5 January 1931** the principal of Lemon
  Grove Grammar School, **Jerome T. Green**, stood in the doorway and let every child in except the
  Mexican American ones, and told them to go to a separate two-room building. The school had **169
  pupils: 87 counted as European American, about 75 Mexican American and 7 Japanese American.**
- The parents formed the **Comité de Vecinos de Lemon Grove** — the Lemon Grove Neighbours
  Committee — kept their children out of the separate building, and hired two lawyers, **Fred Noon
  and A. C. Brinkley**. The named plaintiff was one of the pupils, **Roberto Alvarez**. (**His age is not given by the fetched source**; the outline does not state one.)
- **Judge Claude Chambers** of the San Diego County Superior Court ruled for the children on
  **30 March 1931**, ordering the district to admit them "on a basis of equality with other children
  of said school district, and without separation or segregation in a separate school because of
  race or nationality." He said separating "all the Mexicans in one group can only be done by
  infringing the laws of the State of California," and rejected the argument that a separate
  school would teach them English faster.
  (California Legislature, ACR 146 committee analysis, 25 March 2016,
  https://www.leginfo.ca.gov/pub/15-16/bill/asm/ab_0101-0150/acr_146_cfa_20160325_085955_asm_comm.html)
- **One date is not settled.** The legislative analysis gives **30 March 1931**; a search summary of
  other accounts gives **31 March 1931**. The San Diego History Center journal article that would
  settle it returned **403**. The outline gives 30 March 1931 with the legislature as the source.

### [VERIFY] what the law actually permitted when a school refused a disabled child — CLEARED

The tag asked what state laws permitted. The answer is that in most of the country no law had
to permit it, because no law required the school to take the child in the first place, and the
courts had said the school committee's decision was final.

**The ruling everyone built on is *Watson v. City of Cambridge*, 157 Mass. 561, decided by the
Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court on 4 January 1893** — era 7, but it is the rule the whole
of era 8 runs on. Justice Knowlton wrote: "The records of the school committee of the defendant
city set forth that the plaintiff in 1885 was excluded from the schools 'because he was too
weak-minded to derive profit from instruction.' He was afterwards taken again on trial for two
weeks, and at the end of that time again excluded." The committee's record went on, in words a
full-text search of the opinion confirms are in it, that he was "troublesome to other children,
making uncouth noises, pinching others." The court held that the committee's decision was final
and not for a court to review. The plaintiff's name is **John A. Watson**.
(CourtListener search API, opinion cluster 6550666, Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court,
dateFiled 1893-01-04, https://www.courtlistener.com/opinion/6550666/watson-v-city-of-cambridge/ .
**The full opinion text could not be fetched** — the HTML page returned empty to WebFetch and 0
bytes to curl, and the CourtListener opinions API requires a key. Only the indexed opening
paragraph was retrieved verbatim.)

**The era-8 case is *Beattie v. Board of Education of the City of Antigo*, Wisconsin Supreme
Court, 29 April 1919** — Merritt Beattie, thirteen, cerebral palsy, five grades completed,
expelled because his "physical condition and ailment produces a depressing and nauseating
effect upon the teachers and school children." **`education` already carries this in full and
this chapter does not repeat it.**

**What parents did about it — this chapter's angle.** In **1933** parents in Cleveland founded
**the Cuyahoga County Council for the Retarded Child**, described by the county's own successor
agency as "a grass-roots group, led by parents of children with developmental disabilities ...
founded to assist children of the region who had been excluded from their public schools." It
is the earliest of the American parent groups.
(Cuyahoga County Board of Developmental Disabilities, https://cuyahogadd.org/about-us/history/)
**Naming limit:** several secondary accounts say it was started by the mothers of five children,
and one names no one. **The county board's own history names nobody**, so this chapter says
"parents" and names no one. Do not invent the five.

**Disabled adults organised in this era too, and they used a sit-in twenty years before the
lunch counters.** In **1935** in New York City, about **300 people, most of them living with the
effects of polio or cerebral palsy**, formed the **League of the Physically Handicapped**. The
city's Home Relief Bureau stamped every one of their job applications **"PH"** for "physically
handicapped," which marked the applicant unemployable and kept them out of Works Progress
Administration jobs. **Six people started an impromptu sit-in at the Home Relief Bureau in May
1935 after the director refused to see them.** They also picketed, and later sat in at WPA
headquarters. One participant said afterwards in an interview: "What started it was finding out
that jobs were available, that the government was handing out jobs ... everybody was getting
jobs ... those of us who were militant just refused to accept the fact that we were the only
people who were looked upon as not worthy, not capable of work." **By 1936 about 1,500 jobs had
been created in New York City for disabled people.** The league had disbanded by 1938.
In the same period the **Social Security Act of August 1935** defined disability as "inability to
engage in substantial gainful work," so a person drawing disability insurance could not easily
take a job.
(Access Press "History Note," 9 July 2019, sponsored by the Minnesota Governor's Council on
Developmental Disabilities, https://mn.gov/mnddc/past/access_press/Access_Press_07-19.pdf —
extracted with pdftotext.)
**Two limits recorded:** this source calls it the League **for** the Physically Handicapped; other
accounts call it the League **of** the Physically Handicapped, and the outline uses "of" with the
variant noted here. The source gives only "May 1935" for the sit-in; a search summary gives
**29 May 1935** and nine days, which is **not used in the outline** because it was not fetched.

### [VERIFY] Carrie Buck — CLEARED, and the tag understated it: the case was arranged

**`education` already carries Carrie Buck's life and the ruling** in its era-8 span "What the
sorting was for" — her birth, her school record, the pregnancy, the committal, Holmes's words,
Vivian's honor roll, and the Virginia total. **This chapter does not retell that.** What follows
is what `education` does not have: who organised the case, how it was staged, and what happened
to the law afterwards.

**The law.** Virginia's sterilization act was **passed 20 March 1924** and drafted by **Aubrey
Strode**, who then defended it in court. **Albert S. Priddy**, superintendent of the Virginia
State Colony for Epileptics and Feeble-Minded, picked Carrie Buck's case on purpose to test
whether the new law would hold. Priddy died **13 January 1925**, and his successor **Dr John H.
Bell** took his place on the case, which is why it is *Buck v. Bell*.
(Encyclopedia Virginia, https://encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/buck-v-bell-1927/)

**How the case was staged — this is the part that matters here.** Carrie Buck's own lawyer was
**Irving P. Whitehead**, described by Encyclopedia Virginia as "an experienced lawyer, a former
colony board member, and a sterilization supporter." The historian **Paul A. Lombardo** found
that Whitehead "did not aggressively cross-examine any of the witnesses" and "did not call a
single witness" for her. The man paid to defend her wanted the law upheld. The eugenics
researcher **Arthur Estabrook** testified, after **one day of interviews**, that Carrie, her
mother Emma and her baby Vivian were "a defective strain." Emma Buck had been committed in
**April 1920** and called a "low grade moron." Vivian was born **28 March 1924** and a social
worker said of her that she had "a look about it that is not quite normal."
(Encyclopedia Virginia, as above.)

**The court dates.** Trial court **18 November 1924**, Judge **Bennett T. Gordon**. Virginia
Supreme Court of Appeals **12 November 1925**. U.S. Supreme Court argued **22 April 1927**,
decided **2 May 1927, eight to one**. **Dr John H. Bell performed a salpingectomy on 19 October
1927.** Carrie Buck was released from the Colony in November 1927.
(Encyclopedia Virginia, as above.)

**What a salpingectomy is, in plain words** (policy §3b — this must be defined wherever it
appears). A doctor cut out a section of each of the two tubes that carry an egg from an ovary to
the womb. No egg can reach the womb again, so the person can never become pregnant. It is
permanent and cannot be undone.

**Her sister.** **Doris Buck** arrived at the Colony in June 1926, was ordered sterilized in
December 1927 and was sterilized in **January 1928**. (Encyclopedia Virginia, as above.
**Limit:** the entry does not say whether she was told what had been done to her, so this book
does not say she was not told, although that is the usual account.)

**The national scale, and the German connection.** **Harry H. Laughlin** of the Eugenics Record
Office wrote a model sterilization law that states copied. The United States Holocaust Memorial
Museum: "In part due to Laughlin's efforts, dozens of American states enacted laws that forced
certain people to be sterilized against their will," and "more than 62,000 sterilizations had
been performed in the United States by the 1960s," about **60 percent of them on women**. In
**1933 Nazi Germany passed the Law for the Prevention of Offspring with Hereditary Diseases,
"based in part on the practice of forced sterilization in the US."** About **400,000 people were
sterilized in Nazi Germany**. **In 1936 the University of Heidelberg gave Laughlin an honorary
degree**, and in a letter dated **28 May 1936** he wrote that "To me this honor will be doubly
valued because it will come from a nation which for many centuries nurtured the human seed-stock
which later founded my own country."
(USHMM, *Experiencing History*, "Letter from Dr. Harry H. Laughlin to Dr. Carl Schneider,"
https://perspectives.ushmm.org/item/dr-harry-h-laughlin-to-dr-carl-schneider ; USHMM Holocaust
Encyclopedia, "Forced Sterilization: A Form of Nazi Persecution,"
https://encyclopedia.ushmm.org/content/en/article/forced-sterilization-a-form-of-nazi-persecution
— the second URL is from a search result listing and was **not** fetched; the figures used above
all come from the fetched *Experiencing History* page.)

**Afterwards.** Between 1907 and 1927, **8,515 Americans** were sterilized; about **8,300
Virginians** between 1927 and 1972. **Virginia repealed the law in 1974.** The ACLU sued on
behalf of survivors in **1980** and they settled out of court. On **2 May 2002**, the
seventy-fifth anniversary of the ruling, **Governor Mark Warner apologised**, calling Virginia's
eugenics programme "a shameful effort in which state government never should have been
involved." **Carrie Buck married twice and died on 28 January 1983** in a nursing home in
Waynesboro; she is buried in Oakwood Cemetery, Charlottesville. ***Buck v. Bell* has never been
overruled.** (Encyclopedia Virginia, as above.)

**Not settled, and kept out of the outline:** the number of US states with sterilization laws.
USHMM says "dozens"; Encyclopedia Virginia does not give a number; the figure 32 is common in
secondary accounts and was not verified. The outline says "dozens of states" and cites nothing
more precise.

### The suffrage endgame, 1913 to 1920 — sourced, with the prison record from the women themselves

**The 1913 procession.** **3 March 1913**, the day before Woodrow Wilson's inauguration, along
Pennsylvania Avenue in Washington — the same route the inaugural parade would take the next day.
**Alice Paul and Lucy Burns**, newly appointed chairs of the Congressional Committee of the
National American Woman Suffrage Association, organised it. **At least 250,000 spectators.** The
crowd came off the pavements and blocked the route; marchers "found themselves trapped in a sea
of hostile, jeering men who yelled vile insults and sexual propositions," and were "manhandled
and spat upon." **U.S. Army troops arrived about an hour later** to clear the street. Congress
then opened an investigation into why the police had done so little.
**More than forty Black women marched**, in state delegations or with their professions.
**Ida B. Wells-Barnett** came with the Illinois delegation and was asked, as the march was
forming, to go to the back instead. She refused, left, waited on the side of Pennsylvania Avenue
until Illinois came past, and stepped in with two white allies, **Belle Squire and Virginia
Brooks**, one on each side of her. The Chicago *Broad Ax* wrote that she "proudly marched with the
... head Ladies of the Illinois delegation showing that no Color line existed in ... the first
national parade of the noble women who are in favor of equal suffrage."
(NPS, https://www.nps.gov/articles/woman-suffrage-procession1913.htm — fetched. The Wells detail
and the *Broad Ax* quotation come from a search summary of NPS pages including
https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/a-noble-endeavor-ida-b-wells-barnett-and-suffrage.htm , which was
**not fetched directly**; the fetched procession page confirms she was asked to march at the back,
refused, and stepped in with two white allies.)

**The picketing.** **From 10 January 1917** women stood outside the White House gates six days a
week. A dozen of them walked over from National Woman's Party headquarters across Lafayette Park
that first day with purple, white and gold flags. Two banner texts: **"Mr. President, What Will
You Do for Woman Suffrage?"** and **"How Long Must Women Wait for Liberty?"** They were called the
**Silent Sentinels**. It was the first time anyone had picketed the White House. (NPS, from a
search summary of https://www.nps.gov/articles/national-womans-party-protests-world-war-i.htm and
https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/suffrage60seconds-picketing.htm — **neither page was fetched
directly**; the January 1917 start date, the banner wording and the "first time" claim all come
from that summary and should be re-checked against the pages before prose.)

**The arrests and the prison.** The charge was **"obstructing traffic."** In November 1917 **41
women** were arrested on it, among them **Lucy Burns, Dorothy Day, Dora Lewis and Mary Nolan**;
sentences ran from six days to six months. **Alice Paul was given seven months** and **released
after five weeks**. Conditions at the Occoquan Workhouse in Virginia were "brutality from the
guards, poor food, and dirty conditions." On the **Night of Terror, 14 November 1917**, on the
orders of superintendent **W. H. Whittaker**, guards threw the women about, hung them by their
arms, and refused them food and medical care.
(NPS, "Democracy Limited: Prison, Politics, and the National Woman's Party,"
https://www.nps.gov/articles/democracy-limited-prison-politics-and-the-national-woman-s-party.htm)

**The primary source, and it is the one to use.** Doris Stevens, *Jailed for Freedom* (1920),
full text at Project Gutenberg, https://www.gutenberg.org/files/3604/3604-0.txt (fetched and read
directly). It carries the women's own accounts, smuggled out on scraps of paper.

- **Alice Paul on the night of 14 November 1917:** "Seized by guards from behind, flung off my
  feet, and shot out of the room. All of us were seized by men guards and dragged to cells in
  men's part. Dorothy Day was roughly used-back twisted. Mrs. Mary A. Nolan (73-year-old picket
  from Jacksonville, Florida) flung into cell. ... I was handcuffed all night and manacled to the
  bars part of the time for asking the others how they were, and was threatened with a
  straitjacket and a buckle gag."
- **What forcible feeding actually was — Lucy Burns's own account, and this is the passage that
  satisfies policy section 3b:** "Was stretched on bed, two doctors, matron, four colored
  prisoners present, Whittaker in hall. I was held down by five people at legs, arms, and head.
  I refused to open mouth. Gannon pushed tube up left nostril. I turned and twisted my head all I
  could, but he managed to push it up. It hurts nose and throat very much and makes nose bleed
  freely. Tube drawn out covered with blood. Operation leaves one very sick. Food dumped directly
  into stomach feels like a ball of lead. Left nostril, throat and muscles of neck very sore all
  night."
- **Dora Lewis's account of the same:** "I was seized and laid on my back, where five people held
  me, a young colored woman leaping upon my knees, which seemed to break under the weight. Dr.
  Gannon then forced the tube through my lips and down my throat, I gasping and suffocating with
  the agony of it. I didn't know where to breathe from and everything turned black when the fluid
  began pouring in."
- **How often:** "Miss Paul and Miss Winslow, the first two suffragists to undertake the hunger
  strike, went through the operation of forcible feeding this day and three times a day on each
  succeeding day until their release from prison three weeks later."
- **Rose Winslow, smuggled note:** "Alice Paul is in the psychopathic ward. She dreaded forcible
  feeding frightfully ... I heard myself making the most hideous sounds .... One feels so
  forsaken when one lies prone and people shove a pipe down one's stomach." And: "We think of the
  coming feeding all day. It is horrible."
- **The psychopathic ward.** Paul was carried there on a stretcher after Commissioner Gardner told
  Dr Gannon, "Go ahead, take her and feed her." A nurse was ordered to "observe" her every hour
  through the night with an electric light turned into her face. A window was boarded over from
  outside. Two doctors' signatures were all it took to commit a person to St. Elizabeth's Asylum:
  "No trial, no counsel, no protest from the outside world!" The psychiatrist sent to examine her,
  **Dr William A. White**, wrote instead that "I felt myself in the presence of an unusually
  gifted personality" and praised the "most admirable, coherent, logical and forceful way" in
  which she argued her case. She was not committed. **Dudley Field Malone** forced his way in and
  got her out of the ward.
- **Paul to reporters on her way to serve the sentence:** "We are being imprisoned, not because we
  obstructed traffic, but because we pointed out to the President the fact that he was
  obstructing the cause of democracy at home, while Americans were fighting for it abroad."

**A date trap in the primary source, recorded so nobody repeats it.** The biographical roster at
the back of *Jailed for Freedom* says of Alice Paul: "In Oct., 1919, she was sentenced to 7
months for picketing and served 5 weeks before released on account of hunger strike." **That is a
misprint for October 1917.** The book's own narrative places the whole episode in October and
November 1917, and the roster's next two entries for her (August 1918, January 1919) come after
it. The roster entry for **Dr Caroline E. Spencer** gives the same seven-month sentence with the
arrest dated **20 October 1917**. The outline says "October 1917" and does not give Paul a day.

**Alice Paul's life.** Born **11 January 1885** at Mount Laurel, New Jersey, to Quaker parents.
A.B. Swarthmore **1905**; M.A. **1907**; Ph.D. in economics, University of Pennsylvania, **1912**;
LL.B., Washington College of Law at American University, **1922**. Studied at London and
Birmingham. Took part in the militant campaign in England under Emmeline Pankhurst and was
arrested there repeatedly. **Six prison terms for suffrage in all, three in England and three in
the United States.** Chairman of the NAWSA Congressional Committee from 1913; founded the
Congressional Union; **National Woman's Party from 1916**. Wrote the **Equal Rights Amendment**
and had it introduced on **20 July 1923 at Seneca Falls**, where it was called the Lucretia Mott
Amendment. Worked on the United Nations Charter language in 1945 and on the sex clause of the
Civil Rights Act of 1964. **Died 9 July 1977** at Moorestown, New Jersey; buried at Westfield
Friends Burial Ground, Cinnaminson.
(NPS, https://www.nps.gov/people/alice-paul.htm ; Stevens, *Jailed for Freedom*, roster.)

**The amendment.** "The right of citizens of the United States to vote shall not be denied or
abridged by the United States or by any State on account of sex." **House 21 May 1919**;
**Senate 4 June 1919**; **Tennessee ratified as the 36th state on 18 August 1920**; **Secretary of
State Bainbridge Colby certified it on 26 August 1920.** The National Archives adds that "many
women remained unable to vote long into the 20th century because of discriminatory state voting
laws" and that "decades of struggle to include African Americans and other minority women in the
promise of voting rights remained."
(National Archives, https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/19th-amendment)

### Native Americans and the vote, 1924 to 1957 — the thread the brief says not to drop after 1900

The **Indian Citizenship Act** was passed on **2 June 1924** and signed by **President Calvin
Coolidge**. It made American Indians citizens. It did not get them onto the voter rolls, because
states wrote their own rules about who could vote.

- **Arizona, 1928:** the state barred from voting anyone who was an "idiot, insane person,
  person non compos mentis, or under guardianship," and the Arizona Supreme Court held that
  Native people living on reservations were wards of the federal government and so came under
  that clause.
- **New Mexico:** used the phrase "Indians not taxed" — the argument being that people on
  reservations paid no state tax and so could not vote.
- **Arizona, 15 July 1948 — *Harrison v. Laveen*.** The Arizona Supreme Court threw out its own
  1928 precedent. **Justice Levi Udall** wrote the opinion: a Native person who met the other
  requirements could not be kept off the rolls for living on a reservation.
- **New Mexico, August 1948 — *Miguel Trujillo v. Garley*.** A three-judge U.S. District Court for
  the District of New Mexico ruled unanimously that the "Indians not taxed" exclusion was
  unconstitutional.
- **Utah was the last state, in 1957.**

(National Archives, *The Text Message* blog, "The War after the War: the American Indian Fight
for the Vote after WWII," 12 November 2019,
https://text-message.blogs.archives.gov/2019/11/12/the-war-after-the-war-the-american-indian-fight-for-the-vote-after-wwii/ )

**Parked for `native-nations`** — this is that chapter's material as well, from its own angle.
Logged in `workspace/rights-movements.md`.

**Not established:** whether Frank Harrison and Miguel Trujillo were veterans, which is the usual
framing of both cases. The fetched page does not say it, so the outline does not.

### Japanese American incarceration, 1942 to 1945 — this chapter leads (`war` says so in its own outline)

`outlines/war.md` line 117 states in its own text: "**`rights-movements` leads on internment**."
So this chapter tells it fully; `war` keeps the Japanese American soldiers whose families were
held.

**The order.** **Executive Order 9066, signed by President Franklin D. Roosevelt on 19 February
1942.** **Over 120,000 people were removed; two thirds of them were American citizens.**
(Administrative Office of the U.S. Courts, "Facts and Case Summary — Korematsu v. U.S.,"
https://www.uscourts.gov/about-federal-courts/educational-resources/annual-observances/asian-pacific-american-heritage-month/korematsu-v-us-balancing-liberties-and-safety/facts-and-case-summary-korematsu-v-us)

**Ten camps.** About 120,000 people were forced "to move to 10 inland relocation centers across
the nation." **Topaz, the Central Utah Relocation Center**, is the one to describe because two
people in this era's stories were held there. It sat 15 miles northwest of Delta, Utah, on 19,800
acres, **opened 11 September 1942 and closed 31 October 1945**. **More than 11,000 people passed
through it; over 8,000 were there at once at its fullest.** Most came from the San Francisco area.
It had **42 blocks, 12 barracks to a block, 250 to 300 people to a block**. The barracks were pine
planks covered in tarpaper, with sheetrock inside; the furniture issued was pot-bellied stoves,
army cots, blankets and mattress covers. Each block had one recreation room, one combined
washroom-toilet-laundry building and one dining hall. There were **seven watch towers and a
perimeter fence**.
**A named person was killed there. James Hatsuki Wakasa, 63, was shot dead by a military guard in
April 1943 while walking near the perimeter fence.**
(NPS, "Central Utah Relocation Center (Topaz) Site,"
https://www.nps.gov/places/central-utah-relocation-center-site.htm)

**The four Supreme Court cases.**
- ***Hirabayashi v. United States*, 320 U.S. 81 (1943)** — the curfew upheld; the Court reserved
  the exclusion question for *Korematsu*. Conviction vacated by coram nobis in the 1980s.
- ***Yasui v. United States*, 320 U.S. 115 (1943)** — same, vacated in the 1980s.
- ***Korematsu v. United States*, 323 U.S. 214** — **decided 18 December 1944, six to three**,
  Justice Hugo Black writing. Black wrote that "all legal restrictions which curtail the civil
  rights of a single racial group are immediately suspect" and require "the most rigid scrutiny,"
  and then upheld the exclusion anyway. **Justice Frank Murphy** called it "the legalization of
  racism" and compared it to the "abhorrent and despicable treatment of minority groups by the
  dictatorial tyrannies which this nation is now pledged to destroy." **Justice Robert Jackson**
  wrote that Korematsu had been convicted for "being present in the state whereof he is a
  citizen."
- ***Ex parte Endo*, 323 U.S. 283** — **decided the same day, 18 December 1944** — the Court held
  that citizens the government itself conceded were loyal could not be held.
(National Archives, "World War II Japanese American Incarceration: Federal Courts,"
https://www.archives.gov/research/aapi/ww2/courts ; uscourts.gov as above.)

**Mitsuye Endo — the one who won, and almost nobody has heard of her.** Born **10 May 1920** in
Sacramento. She had a clerical job with the California state government. The lawyer **James
Purcell** picked her because she was a state employee, a Christian, spoke only English, had never
been to Japan, and had **a brother in the United States Army** — everything a judge might find
sympathetic. Her **habeas corpus petition was filed on 12 July 1942** in the federal district
court in San Francisco. She was held first at the Sacramento Assembly Center, then Tule Lake, then
Topaz. **The War Relocation Authority offered to let her out immediately if she dropped the case,
and she refused and stayed in the camp**, because leaving would have ended the case for everyone
else. Justice **William O. Douglas** wrote that "Mitsuye Endo should be given her liberty" and that
the government "has no authority to subject citizens who are concededly loyal to its leave
procedure." She moved to Chicago in **May 1945**, became a secretary for the Mayor's Committee on
Race Relations, married Kenneth Tsutsumi, had three children and **died in Chicago on 14 April
2006**.
(NPS, https://www.nps.gov/people/mitsuye-endo.htm)
**An error in that NPS page, recorded here.** It calls Topaz the "Topaz Relocation Center in
Arizona." **Topaz was in Utah** — NPS's own place page for the site says west central Utah, and the
camp's formal name was the Central Utah Relocation Center. The outline uses Utah.
**Also not settled:** the NPS page says "Two weeks after the Endo decision, President Roosevelt
ended west coast exclusion." The common account is that the War Department announced the end of
exclusion on 17 December 1944, the day *before* the ruling was handed down. **That was not
verified**, so the outline says only that the exclusion was lifted within weeks of the ruling.

**Fred Korematsu — every fact.** Born **30 January 1919 in Oakland, California**, and named
**Toyosaburo**; a teacher suggested "Fred." His father **Kakusaburo** arrived in the United States
in **1905**; his mother **Kotsui** came through Angel Island in **1914**. He had three brothers,
and the family ran the **Stonehurst Flower Nursery** in East Oakland. He was with **Ida Boitano**
by 1938. After Pearl Harbor on **7 December 1941** he stayed behind when his family was removed:
he **forged identification papers in the name "Clyde Sarah"** and worked as a welder. **He was
arrested on the street with his girlfriend on 30 May 1942**, for failing to report. **Ernest
Besig** of the ACLU visited him in the Presidio stockade and took the case. He was convicted of
breaking **Civilian Exclusion Order No. 34** and **given five years' probation**. He was moved to
**Tanforan Racetrack on 28 June 1942**, and to **Topaz in Utah in September 1942** with his family.
The Supreme Court heard argument on **11 October 1944** and ruled against him on **18 December
1944**. He was approved for indefinite leave in **January 1944** and had settled in Detroit by
that October. He married **Kathryn Pearson** and raised two children in California.
(NPS, https://www.nps.gov/people/fred-t-korematsu.htm — note the leave date of January 1944 sits
oddly beside the September 1942 move to Topaz and the October 1944 argument date; the page gives
both, and the outline uses only the arrest, conviction and ruling dates.)

**The vindication, 1983.** A pro bono legal team found that the government's own lawyers had
suppressed intelligence reports saying Japanese Americans were no military threat. On **10
November 1983, Judge Marilyn Hall Patel** of the federal district court in San Francisco — the
same courthouse where he had been convicted — vacated the conviction: "the conviction that was
handed down in this court and affirmed by the Supreme Court in *Korematsu v. United States* is ...
vacated and the underlying indictment dismissed." **Korematsu's own words: "I don't want a pardon.
If anything, I should be pardoning the government."** He received the **Presidential Medal of
Freedom in 1998** and **died on 30 March 2005 at Larkspur, California, aged 86**. **30 January is
Fred Korematsu Day in California.**
(NPS, as above; National Archives San Francisco, "Motion to Vacate Conviction and Dismiss
Indictment of Fred T. Korematsu, November 16, 1983,"
https://www.archives.gov/san-francisco/highlights/korematsu — named from a search listing, not
fetched; uscourts.gov, as above, for the 10 November 1983 date and Patel's words.)

**Redress belongs to era 9 and is handed forward.** The Commission on Wartime Relocation and
Internment of Civilians took testimony from more than 750 witnesses over 20 days of hearings
between July and December 1981 and reported under the title ***Personal Justice Denied***. The
**Civil Liberties Act was signed by President Reagan on 10 August 1988**; it called the
incarceration "a grave injustice" "motivated largely by racial prejudice, war hysteria, and a
failure of political leadership," and gave an apology and **$20,000 to each surviving person**.
(From a search summary of justice.gov, archives.gov and history.house.gov pages — **not fetched**.
Era 9's agent should fetch before writing it.)

**Densho could not be used.** `encyclopedia.densho.org` returned **403** on 2026-09-09.

### The radium dial painters' court fight — sourced, and one existing claim in this book looks wrong

**`work-workers` (story `grace-fryer-work-workers`) and `elements` (story `radium-girls-elements`)
already carry this and both are marked verified.** This chapter keeps its own story block because
the workspace shared-events table gives it the lead on the court fight, and writes the part the
other two do not have: what radium did inside the body, and the rule of law that nearly stopped
the case.

**The plaintiffs, by name.** In **May 1927** the lawyer **Raymond Berry** filed suit against the
U.S. Radium Corporation for **Grace Fryer** and four co-workers: **Katherine Schaub, Quinta Maggia
Macdonald, Albina Maggia Larice and Edna Hussman**.

**Grace Fryer.** She worked at the Orange, New Jersey plant **from 1917 to 1920** and then took
other work. In **1924** she went to a dentist, **Theodor Blum**, who found severe damage and
identified atrophy of her jaw bone — "radium jaw" — which he had never seen before.

**What radium did, and by what mechanism.** "Radium, which is in the same alkaline earth family as
calcium, would be taken up in the hydroxy apatite of growing bones and teeth." The body treats
radium as if it were calcium and builds it into bone. It then keeps giving off radiation from
inside. The results named are "aplastic anemia, necrosis of the jaw, and osteosarcomas" — the
blood stops being made properly, the jaw bone dies and breaks apart, and bone cancers grow.
Autopsies found **99 percent of the radioactivity in the skeleton**. Measured amounts in the body
ran from **1 to 180 micrograms**. The painters shaped their brushes by putting them between their
lips; the physicist Robley Evans recorded why: "For wiping and tipping the brush the workers found
that that either a cloth or their fingers were too harsh, but by wiping the brush clean between
their lips the proper erasing point could be obtained."
(Kathryn D. Held et al. / National Institute of Standards and Technology history, "The National
Bureau of Standards and the Radium Dial Painters," PubMed Central PMC10046820,
https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10046820/ ; Oak Ridge Associated Universities,
https://www.orau.org/blog/history/radium-girls-the-health-scandal-of-radium-dial-painters-in-the-1920s-and-1930s.html)

**The settlement.** **On 4 June 1928 federal judge William Clark negotiated a settlement** giving
"substantial funds to the seriously ill women." **The company did not admit it had done anything
wrong.** Berry took a 10 percent share of settlements.
(PMC10046820, as above.)

**The two-year rule.** New Jersey's occupational disease law gave a worker **two years from the
last exposure** to bring a claim, and radium poisoning took longer than two years to show itself,
so the law itself was the obstacle. The women testified in **January and April 1928**, and the
company was then granted an adjournment to **September** — a delay while the plaintiffs were dying.
They settled on **4 June 1928**.
(From a search summary of the Library of Congress *Headlines & Heroes* blog post "Radium Girls:
Living Dead Women," https://blogs.loc.gov/headlinesandheroes/2019/03/radium-girls-living-dead-women/
— **loc.gov and blogs.loc.gov return 403 to this environment and the page was not fetched.** The
two-year limit, the January/April 1928 testimony and the September adjournment come from that
summary and are used in the outline as reported.)

**A CLAIM ALREADY IN THIS BOOK THAT LOOKS WRONG — flagged, not silently fixed.**
The seed text of this chapter's era-8 span, and `work-workers` and `elements`, all say the
settlement terms were **$10,000 each, a $600-a-year pension and medical costs**. **Not one fetched
source in this session states those terms.** PMC10046820 says only "substantial funds." The figures
trace to the Library of Congress blog and History.com, neither of which could be fetched here.
**They are kept out of this chapter's rewritten outline** and left where they already stand in the
other two chapters. **Audit item.**

**And a second one.** The seed text says Catherine Wolfe Donohue "won her Illinois case in 1938,
months before her death." **Oak Ridge Associated Universities says she died in July 1938 at 35 and
that "the final legal victory" came in October 1938, after lengthy arbitration, with her family
receiving modest compensation** — that is, after she was dead. Both cannot be right as written. The
likely reconciliation is that she won before the Illinois Industrial Commission during her life and
the appeals finished after her death, but **that was not established from a fetched source**, so
this chapter's outline says only that she worked for the Radium Dial Company at Ottawa, Illinois,
that she died in July 1938 aged 35, and that the last ruling came in October 1938 after she was
dead and her family received modest compensation. **The claim that she collapsed while giving
evidence is also unsourced here and is left out.** **Audit item for `work-workers` and `elements`.**

### STORY — Lloyd Gaines — **fills the `target` slot "a family in the pre-Brown school cases," and the slot is renamed**

The target slot `pre-brown-school-family` said "a family in the pre-*Brown* school cases,
documented by name in the court record." **The obvious candidate was the Mendez family, and
`education` has already used them** (story `sylvia-mendez`, verified). Rather than tell the same
family twice in two chapters, the slot is filled with **Lloyd Gaines**, whose case is the first the
NAACP won at the Supreme Court and who is documented by name in the court record and in the State
Historical Society of Missouri. **Slug changes from `pre-brown-school-family` to `lloyd-gaines`.
Checked against every `hb-story` slug in `outlines/*.md`: `lloyd-gaines` is unused.**

**His life.** Born **1911 in Water Valley, Mississippi**, son of **Henry Richard Gaines**, a tenant
farmer and former teacher, and **Callie Gaines**. **Ten siblings.** Graduated **Vashon High School,
St. Louis, 1931**; one year at Stowe Teachers College; **graduated Lincoln University (Missouri) in
1935 with a degree in history**, with honors. The Supreme Court's own opinion says he took his
Bachelor of Arts at Lincoln in **August 1935**.
(State Historical Society of Missouri, Historic Missourians,
https://historicmissourians.shsmo.org/lloyd-gaines/ ; Cornell LII, 305 U.S. 337,
https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/305/337)

**The refusal.** He applied to the **University of Missouri School of Law** in 1935. **Registrar
S. W. Canada** sent him to the president of Lincoln University, who pointed him at a state law
paying for Black students to study out of state. Missouri offered to pay his fees at a law school
in **Kansas, Nebraska, Iowa or Illinois** rather than admit him. (Cornell LII, as above.)

**The courts.** Suit filed in circuit court **24 January 1936**; circuit court ruled against him
**July 1936**; the **Missouri Supreme Court** heard it in **May 1937** and ruled against him
**9 December 1937**; the U.S. Supreme Court argued it **9 November 1938** and decided it
**12 December 1938**, with **Chief Justice Charles Evans Hughes** writing and **Justice McReynolds
dissenting, joined by Justice Butler**. The lawyers were **Charles Hamilton Houston** for the NAACP
and **Sidney Redmond** of St. Louis. Hughes: "The State was bound to furnish him within its borders
facilities for legal education substantially equal to those which the State there afforded for
persons of the white race." And: "Manifestly, the obligation of the State to give the protection of
equal laws can be performed only where its laws operate, that is, within its own jurisdiction."
(SHSMO and Cornell LII, as above; Lincoln University of Missouri,
https://www.lincolnu.edu/news/2025/02/lloyd-gaines-lincoln-university-of-missouri.html , for
Houston leading and the 6-2 vote.)

**One thing the sources do not line up on.** SHSMO gives both a **formal denial in March 1936** and
a **suit filed 24 January 1936**. Those two are in the wrong order and the page does not explain
it. The outline says he applied in 1935, was refused, and sued in January 1936, and gives no date
for a formal letter.

**What Missouri did.** Rather than admit him, **Missouri lawmakers created a separate law school
for Black students at Lincoln University in 1939.** (SHSMO, as above.)

**What happened to him.** He was **last seen in Chicago sometime in March 1939**, at a fraternity
house. He **left a bag of his belongings behind**. His absence **was not formally noted until
October 1939**. He has never been found. **The FBI reopened the case in 2007** as part of its civil
rights cold case work. (SHSMO; Lincoln University, as above.)

**No film exists** about Lloyd Gaines that could be verified, so the story block carries no Movie
line.

### Black organizing, 1908 to 1948 — the NAACP, the anti-lynching bills, and the march that never happened

**The Springfield riot, August 1908.** On **14 August 1908** Springfield, Illinois newspapers
reported that a Black man had tried to assault a white woman at her home the night before, and
then reported a second assault. A white mob went looking for the men accused. The violence ran
**nearly three days**. **Two men were lynched, Scott Burton and William Donegan**, neither of whom
had anything to do with any crime. Dozens of homes were destroyed. **Nearly 2,000 Black residents
left the city and never came back.** The state militia restored order. **About 150 people were
arrested and few were ever convicted of anything.** It happened in Abraham Lincoln's own town.
(NPS, "Illinois: 1908 Springfield Race Riot Archaeological Site,"
https://www.nps.gov/articles/illinois-1908-springfield-race-riot-archaeological-site.htm ; NPS,
Springfield 1908 Race Riot National Monument, https://www.nps.gov/spra/ , which confirms the mob
and the two lynchings.)

**The NAACP, 1909.** The riot "became the impetus for the creation of the National Association for
the Advancement of Colored People (NAACP)" on **12 February 1909**, the hundredth anniversary of
Lincoln's birth. (NPS, as above.) A search summary of the Library of Congress NAACP exhibition
adds that the socialist writer **William English Walling** published an article about the riot in
1908; that an interracial group met in his apartment in **January 1909**; that they issued a
**"Call"** for a national conference to be held on Lincoln's centenary; and that the signers
included **W. E. B. Du Bois, Mary Church Terrell and Ida B. Wells-Barnett**, with **Mary White
Ovington** in the early work. (**loc.gov returns 403 to this environment**; the exhibition page
https://www.loc.gov/exhibits/naacp/founding-and-early-years.html was **not fetched** and those
details are used only as reported in the search summary.)

**Anti-lynching bills — thirty years of them, and none passed.**
- **The first federal anti-lynching bill was H.R. 6963, introduced by Representative George Henry
  White on 20 January 1900.** White was the last Black member of Congress of his generation. It
  went nowhere. (U.S. House of Representatives, History, Art & Archives, "Toward a Temporary
  Farewell,"
  https://history.house.gov/Exhibitions-and-Publications/BAIC/Historical-Essays/Temporary-Farewell/Anti-Lynching-Legislation/ )
- **Representative Leonidas Dyer of Missouri introduced his bill on 8 April 1918**, "to protect
  citizens of the United States against lynching in default of protection by the States." It would
  have charged lynch mobs with capital murder, tried the cases in federal court, fined the county
  where a lynching happened, jailed and fined up to **$5,000** any law officer who failed to stop
  one or handed a prisoner to a mob, and kept mob members off juries.
- **The House passed it on 26 January 1922**, with several hundred Black spectators packed into the
  gallery. **The Senate never voted on it.** Southern Democrats obstructed debate and threatened a
  filibuster, and Senate Republicans dropped it.
- The NAACP produced the report ***Thirty Years of Lynching in the United States, 1889-1919*** to
  push Congress, and sent **James Weldon Johnson** to Washington as its lobbyist. Johnson: "I
  tramped the corridors of the Capitol and the two office buildings so constantly that toward the
  end, I could, I think, have been able to find my way about blindfolded."
(U.S. House of Representatives, History, Art & Archives, "A Generation Lost,"
https://history.house.gov/Exhibitions-and-Publications/BAIC/Historical-Essays/Temporary-Farewell/A-Generation-Lost/ )
**Two things left out of the outline for want of a source.** The House vote count of **230 to 119**
appeared only in a search summary, and the House's own essay does not give it. And the era-7 agent
handed forward an **NAACP total of 3,224 people lynched between 1889 and 1918** from a loc.gov PDF
that returned 403; **it is still unverified and is still not used.** The report's title and years
(1889-1919) are now confirmed; the number in it is not.

**A. Philip Randolph and the march that did not happen.**
- Born **15 April 1889 at Crescent City, Florida**; **died 16 May 1979 at his home in New York
  City, aged 90**. Founded the **Brotherhood of Sleeping Car Porters in 1925** and got it into the
  American Federation of Labor — the first Black labour union in the United States. Worked with
  **Bayard Rustin** on the 1941 march plan. **Principal organizer of the 1963 March on Washington**
  and met President Kennedy afterwards. **Presidential Medal of Freedom, 1964.** Founded the A.
  Philip Randolph Institute.
  (NPS, https://www.nps.gov/people/a-philip-randolph.htm)
  **An error on that NPS page, recorded here:** it says the 1941 pressure produced "Executive Order
  8806." **The order is 8802**, as the National Archives milestone-document page shows. The outline
  uses 8802.
- **1941.** Randolph, as president of the Brotherhood, and other Black leaders met **Eleanor
  Roosevelt** and cabinet members, and Randolph threatened to bring **"ten, twenty, fifty thousand
  Negroes on the White House lawn"** if nothing changed. **President Roosevelt signed Executive
  Order 8802 on 25 June 1941**: **"There shall be no discrimination in the employment of workers in
  defense industries and in Government, because of race, creed, color, or national origin."** It
  set up the **Committee on Fair Employment Practice** inside the Office of Production Management,
  to take complaints, investigate them and recommend action. It covered federal agencies, defense
  contractors and unions doing war work. **Randolph called the march off once the order was
  issued.** The National Archives says historians have largely judged the FEPC ineffectual,
  especially in the South, and that **Congress refused to make it permanent after the war**.
  (National Archives, https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/executive-order-8802)
- **1948.** Randolph planned another march, this time against segregation in the armed forces.
  **President Truman signed Executive Order 9981 on 26 July 1948**: **"There shall be equality of
  treatment and opportunity for all persons in the armed forces without regard to race, color,
  religion or national origin."** It created the **President's Committee on Equality of Treatment
  and Opportunity in the Armed Services**. The services resisted, and **nearly all of them were
  integrated by the end of the Korean War, about 1953.**
  (National Archives, https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/executive-order-9981 — the page
  **does not** name Randolph, so the outline credits him for the 1948 campaign to the NPS page and
  not to the National Archives.)

**STORY — A. Philip Randolph. This is a SIXTH story added to era 8, not a replacement.** Era 8 had
no story about Black organizing at all, and the era holds the founding of the NAACP, thirty years
of anti-lynching bills, and the two executive orders. He already has a story in `work-workers`
(slug `a-philip-randolph-work-workers`) about the porters' union. **This block is about the 1941
and 1948 campaigns only, and the slug is `a-philip-randolph-rights-movements`.** Checked against
every `hb-story` slug in `outlines/*.md`: that slug is unused.

- **`education`'s era-8 span and `rights-movements`' seed both said Carrie Buck was sterilized under

**Handed to the era-9 agent**
- **Era 9 opens on cases this era set up.** *Sweatt v. Painter* and *McLaurin v. Oklahoma State
  Regents* were both decided in **1950** and fall in era 9 by the boundary rule. *Brown* (1954) is
  the end of the twenty-year campaign that starts with Donald Gaines Murray in 1935.
- **Charles Hamilton Houston died on 22 April 1950**, four years before *Brown*. Thurgood Marshall,
  his student, is `government-politics`' story (`thurgood-marshall`); do not duplicate it.
- **Redress for the incarceration is yours and is not yet fetched.** The Commission on Wartime
  Relocation and Internment of Civilians heard more than 750 witnesses over 20 days between July and
  December 1981 and reported as *Personal Justice Denied*; the **Civil Liberties Act was signed by
  President Reagan on 10 August 1988**, with an apology and **$20,000** to each survivor. **All of
  that is from a search summary and none of it was fetched.** So is Korematsu's coram nobis team.
- **Fred Korematsu's and Mitsuye Endo's later lives run into your era** and are already sourced
  above; do not re-source them, and do not repeat the era-8 story.
- **Federal anti-lynching law never passes in era 9 either.** The Emmett Till Antilynching Act is
  2022 — **era 10**.
- **A. Philip Randolph organized the 1963 March on Washington.** His `rights-movements` story block
  stops in 1948 on purpose; era 9 should carry 1963 in span prose or give it to Rustin, whose
  `bayard-rustin` slot is already a candidate in era 9.
- **The Equal Rights Amendment was written by Alice Paul in 1923** and is still not ratified. Its
  1972 passage by Congress and the ratification fight are era 9's.
- **The pattern era 8 hands on:** every method the 1960s movement used was invented before 1950 —
  the planned test case (Houston, 1935), the picket line (1917), the sit-in (the League of the
  Physically Handicapped, 1935), and the threatened mass march (Randolph, 1941 and 1948).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262c): the Tulsa Race Massacre, 31 May to 1 June 1921. This chapter LEADS it (DECISIONS #23)

**Boundary.** `crime-justice` era 8 already tells, in its own outline span "Tulsa, 1921: no one convicted": Dick Rowland's arrest, the courthouse, the deputizing, the 10,000 figure, 35 blocks, 50 to 300 dead, Dr. A. C. Jackson, the camps in one sentence, the grand jury, Gustafson, the 2001 report and the 2025 DOJ review. Its era 10 tells the lawsuit's dismissal (July 2023, 12 June 2024), Fletcher's "I still see Black men being shot" quote, her death and the Greenwood Trust. `money` era 8 tells the $1.8 million claims and the insurance "riot" clause. **This chapter's slice:** Greenwood as a Black community, what the attackers did to it and its people, the camps and the pass system, the fight to rebuild (Franklin's tent law office and the fire ordinance), and the survivors' hundred-year campaign to be acknowledged and paid. Writers must not re-tell the grand jury, Gustafson or the dismissal dates at length.

**Main source.** US Department of Justice, Civil Rights Division, *Review and Evaluation: Tulsa Race Massacre*, file 144-59N-731, signed 9 January 2025, posted 10 January 2025, later reissued with some names unredacted (front page note). PDF https://www.justice.gov/crt/media/1383756/dl , **full text extracted with pypdf and read 2026-09-27** (127 pages). Cited below as "DOJ 2025, p. N" (printed page numbers). The DOJ in turn cites the 2001 Oklahoma Commission report (Ellsworth; Franklin and Ellsworth; Brooks and Witten), the American Red Cross report (Loula Watkins, comp.), and Mary Jones Parrish's 1923 *Events of the Tulsa Disaster*.

**Greenwood before 31 May 1921.**
- About 10,000 Black residents; "home to two newspapers, several churches, a library branch, and scores of Black-owned businesses" (Scott Ellsworth, "Tulsa Race Massacre," Oklahoma Historical Society, *Encyclopedia of Oklahoma History and Culture*, https://www.okhistory.org/publications/enc/entry?entry=TU013 , fetched).
- "four well-equipped drug stores," many grocery stores, two "fine hotels," and the Dreamland Theater. **Loula Williams** owned the Dreamland and a confectionery. **J. B. Stradford** owned a hotel (the 2001 Commission calls it a "modern fifty-four room structure," opened 1918). The Nails family owned a shoe shop. "dozens of Black churches" (the Commission: "more than a dozen"). Doctors, lawyers and teachers lived on Detroit Avenue. Families owned pianos, fine furniture and jewelry (DOJ 2025, p. 10 and notes 66-73).
- Greenwood "was not a utopia": white officials controlled Tulsa's tax money, so Greenwood got no money for sanitation or running water and fewer paved streets. "although remembered as 'Wall Street,' Greenwood was not a financial center with brokerage firms or banks. It was, however, a center of shopping and other trade." Black residents spent their money almost entirely in Greenwood because most white businesses would not serve them or humiliated them (DOJ 2025, p. 11). The nickname "Negro Wall Street" is credited to Booker T. Washington by one secondary book cited in DOJ note 6; the DOJ does not confirm it. **Do not state that Washington coined it.**
- Tulsa city ordinances barred Black people from living in white blocks unless they were "domestics" serving white families (DOJ 2025, p. 9).
- Viola Fletcher, written testimony to Congress, 19 May 2021: "The neighborhood I fell asleep in that night was rich, not just in terms of wealth, but in culture, community, and heritage. My family had a beautiful home. We had great neighbors and I had friends to play with. I felt safe." (Her written text reads "feel asleep" and uses a dash after "rich". Quote without the dash.) (https://www.congress.gov/117/meeting/house/112648/witnesses/HHRG-117-JU10-Wstate-FletcherV-20210519.pdf , text extracted and read.)
- **Land.** Tulsa, Greenwood included, lies inside the Muscogee (Creek) Reservation, which the Supreme Court held in *McGirt v. Oklahoma* (9 July 2020) Congress never ended. Sourced in `research/research-crime-justice.md`, PATCH "whose land Tulsa and Osage County were". A writer who mentions the land says it was Muscogee (Creek) land.

**The attack: who did it.** (Rowland and the courthouse are `crime-justice`'s. One sentence of cause is enough here.)
- The "invasion" began "a little after 5:00 a.m. on June 1" when a whistle or siren blew. White men who "had been organized into companies the night before" gathered at three railroad depots southwest of Greenwood and went in (DOJ 2025, p. 26). Red Cross relief director **Maurice Willows** (white) wrote in his memoir: "This was NOT a RIOT ... It was a well-planned, diabolical ouster of the innocent [N]egros from their stamping grounds" (DOJ 2025, p. 26).
- **How many attackers:** about 10,000 (2001 Commission); 8,000 to 10,000 "under arms" (a scholar, 1946); Adjutant General **Charles Barrett** told the *New York Times* "[t]wenty-five thousand," which many take as a misprint for 2,500 (DOJ 2025, p. 26-27). Record all three. Women and boys took part, mostly in looting and arson (DOJ 2025, p. 27).
- **How they burned it:** teams of white men, "some of them deputized by police," entered a home, piled bedding and furniture in the middle of a room, poured kerosene on it and lit a match. They burned "practically every building" in Greenwood, "including a dozen churches, five hotels, 31 restaurants, four drug stores, eight doctor's offices, more than two dozen grocery stores, and the [B]lack public library," and "[m]ore than a thousand homes" (DOJ 2025, p. 27, quoting a journalist's account and the Commission). The first fires were set about 1:00 a.m. near the Frisco tracks. White men with guns threatened the firemen who came and told them to let the Black homes burn (DOJ 2025, p. 23).
- **The looting:** trucks backed up to homes to load "everything moveable and of value"; white women with shopping bags took clothing, silverware and jewelry; safes were opened and legal papers destroyed before buildings were burned (DOJ 2025, p. 30).
- **Killings the DOJ names or describes** (survivors "could not identify any particular perpetrator by name," p. 28): Dr. A. C. Jackson, 70 (told in `crime-justice`, do not retell); a grandfather shot in front of his family in a wagon; a paralyzed old man shot in his home when he said he could not "march"; four white men tied a blind Black double amputee who begged in Tulsa's streets to a car bumper and dragged him to death; an old couple on Greenwood Avenue shot in the back of the head while praying; a man shot as he came out of a burning building and thrown back into the fire (DOJ 2025, p. 29-30). Victims unnamed in the source except Jackson. **Tell these unnamed, in zoom prose.**
- **The National Guard.** Three Guard units were stationed in Tulsa. Company B had six Browning Automatic Rifles. Guardsmen worked under Police Chief Gustafson before martial law. By their own admission they helped police form companies of white men and entered Greenwood to round up Black residents. "The primary role of the National Guard in the massacre was to subdue and capture Black men and take them into custody. This had the effect, intended or not, of facilitating the burning and looting of unprotected Black homes." Guardsmen operated at least one machine gun. Guardsman and police officer **Major Daley** ordered the arrest of Black people in white homes' "servants['] quarters." Some guardsmen tried to hold off or disarm white attackers (DOJ 2025, p. 37-39). A machine gun on a grain elevator covered the attackers (p. 26). Governor **James Robertson** declared martial law at 11:30 a.m. on 1 June (crime-justice bank, DOJ). The DOJ's own narrative elsewhere gives 11:29 a.m. (p. 32). At 1:46 a.m. a telegram signed by the police chief, the sheriff and a judge asked the governor for Guard troops from across the state (p. 23).
- **The airplanes.** "It is undisputed that, during the massacre, planes kept tabs on Black Greenwood residents and informed law enforcement of the movements of Black people." Likely Curtiss JN-4 "Jenny" biplanes from the Curtiss-Southwest airfield, some probably owned by Curtiss Southwest and some by Sinclair Oil. "What is hotly disputed is whether, and to what extent, airplanes dropped incendiary material ... and to what extent the pilots or passengers shot at those fleeing." Many survivors reported it. Lawyer **B. C. Franklin**, an eyewitness, described turpentine bombs. Mary Jones Parrish saw men with rifles board planes (DOJ 2025, p. 42-43). **Prose must say both: planes watched and reported, and whether they bombed or fired is disputed.**
- **Black Tulsans who fought back:** men in the belfry of the new Mount Zion Baptist Church held off attackers until guardsmen used a machine gun on it; J. B. Stradford fired from the second story of his hotel and surrendered only when the attackers promised not to burn it. They burned it (DOJ 2025, p. 31).

**The dead: every count and whose.**
- "credible estimates of deaths range from fifty to three hundred" (Ellsworth, OHS).
- "The massacre killed as many as 300 Tulsans, perhaps even more" (DOJ 2025, p. 51, citing the 2001 Commission). A true count "may never be reached" because bodies went into the Arkansas River, onto flatbed rail cars and into unmarked mass graves, and because many survivors left Tulsa for good.
- Injured: the Red Cross estimated about 700 injured and 163 operations in the week after (DOJ 2025, p. 51).
- **The graves search.** Mayor **G. T. Bynum** launched the city's search in 2018 (NBC News, 17 August 2024, fetched). By September 2026 investigators had exhumed the remains of **55 people** at Oaklawn Cemetery and recovered DNA profiles from 22 (News 9, 25 September 2026, https://www.news9.com/tulsa-race-massacre/tulsa-oaklawn-1921-graves-investigation-2026 , fetched). Two victims have been identified: **C. L. Daniel**, a World War I Army veteran in his twenties, matched by DNA to Burial 3 of the 2021 excavation, announced 12 July 2024 by Mayor Bynum (City of Tulsa press release, fetched: a family lawyer's letter to the Veterans Administration said "C. L. was killed in a race riot in Tulsa Oklahoma in 1921"), and **James Goings**, also a World War I veteran, identified from Veterans Administration records and announced 18 June 2025, whose grave has not been found (News On 6, 18 June 2025, fetched). A sixth field season began at the end of September 2026 (News 9).

**The destruction, counted.**
- The Red Cross estimated white Tulsans "burned more than 1256 houses, looted another 215 houses, and burned and looted businesses" (DOJ 2025, p. 51). "More than one thousand homes and businesses" (Ellsworth, OHS). 35 city blocks (DOJ, crime-justice bank). Bureau of Investigation agent Weiss reported a real estate and insurance firm's estimate of $1,500,000 in losses, which he called "conservative," over an area "two miles long and from two to six blocks wide." A researcher for the 2001 Commission estimated $1.8 million in 1921 dollars. The DOJ converts that to about $32.3 million in December 2024 dollars (DOJ 2025, p. 51). A federal post office substation was burned (p. 51).

**The camps and the passes.**
- Police, special deputies and guardsmen arrested Black residents "regardless of whether they were suspected of committing acts of violence." "detention was not based on probable cause or even reasonable suspicion." Camps: **Convention Hall, McNulty Park (a baseball park), and the Tulsa fairgrounds**. Armed men guarded them. **Between 4,000 and 6,000 people** at their fullest. Some stayed a few days, others up to two weeks. A Black leader described people "compelled to be in the stalls of the fair grounds under a heavy, cruel guard of home-guards" (DOJ 2025, p. 48-49).
- To leave, a Black resident needed an identification card, and a white person had to sponsor it. Workers in white homes and businesses got permanent green passes. Unemployed workers got red cards good for one day. Anyone without a valid card was re-arrested. The system ran at least a month. People forced to live under it said they were treated "as though they [were] dogs" (DOJ 2025, p. 49). "By June 7, 7,500 tags had been issued" (Human Rights Watch, "The Case for Reparations in Tulsa, Oklahoma," 29 May 2020, https://www.hrw.org/news/2020/05/29/case-reparations-tulsa-oklahoma , fetched).
- **Forced labor.** The day after the massacre the Guard commander ordered "all able bodied [N]egro men remaining in the detention camp at the fairgrounds and other places" to "render such service and perform such labor as is required." Detainees cleared debris and buried bodies. The Red Cross paid up to 25 cents an hour, and meals cost 20 cents. Some scholars say some men were never paid. Black people were barred from having or buying guns for several weeks (DOJ 2025, p. 49-50).
- **Homeless.** "The vast majority of Tulsa's African American population had been made homeless" (Ellsworth, OHS). Many lived more than a year in Red Cross tents, many without floors. Booker T. Washington High School became a hospital (DOJ 2025, p. 50).

**Rebuilding, and the officials who tried to stop it.**
- On **7 June 1921** the city commission passed **Fire Ordinance No. 2156**, which put most of Greenwood inside the fire limits and required new buildings to be "of concrete, brick, or steel and ... at least two stories high." The DOJ: "those who drafted the ordinance planned to drive Black people out of Greenwood." The first news story on it said the land would "never again be a [N]egro quarter but will become a wholesale and industrial center" (DOJ 2025, p. 54). **Name the actors as the city commissioners; the DOJ does not name them individually.**
- **B. C. Franklin**, with **I. H. Spears** and **T. O. Chappelle**, set up a law office in a tent on Archer Street. They won a temporary halt and then, in **September 1921**, a permanent injunction against the ordinance as a taking of property rights. Before that, many people were arrested for trying to rebuild (DOJ 2025, p. 55).
- Franklin's firm filed about **$4 million** in claims against the city and insurers. Insurers refused under the "riot clause." In **1926** the Oklahoma Supreme Court shut off suits against insurance companies for the massacre. Suits against the city, Mayor **T. D. Evans**, the police and the airplane company were all dismissed (DOJ 2025, p. 55). (`money` holds the $1.8 million city claims and the one paid claim. Record: $1.8 million to the city, Brookings; about $4 million city plus insurers, DOJ.)
- Greenwood's people rebuilt anyway. By 1925 Greenwood hosted the National Negro Business League conference and by the 1940s had 242 Black-owned businesses (Brookings 2021, in `research-money.md`). From the 1950s the city condemned property for expressways, and the Inner Dispersal Loop and Cherokee Expressway (finished in the 1970s) cut along Greenwood's edges (HRW 2020).

**The reckonings, 1997 to 2026.**
- **1997:** the Oklahoma legislature formed an 11-member commission to study it (HRW 2020). **28 February 2001:** its report went in. It interviewed **118 survivors** (search summary of Oklahoma Voice 2023, unconfirmed: search summary only). It recommended direct payments to survivors and descendants, a scholarship fund, an economic development zone and a memorial (HRW 2020).
- **1 June 2001:** the legislature passed the 1921 Tulsa Race Riot Reconciliation Act. "None of the legislation provided financial compensation to survivors or descendants of survivors" (HRW 2020).
- **2003:** survivors and descendants sued the state and city in *Alexander v. Oklahoma*. Federal courts threw it out as too late (Tenth Circuit, 2004) (DOJ 2025, p. 56).
- **19 May 2021:** Viola Fletcher, her brother Hughes Van Ellis and Lessie Benningfield Randle spoke to the House Judiciary subcommittee on civil rights (Fletcher written testimony).
- **1 June 2021:** President Joe Biden met the three survivors and spoke at the Greenwood Cultural Center, the first sitting president to visit Greenwood about the massacre. He said: "It wasn't a riot, it was a massacre." He did not call for reparations (ABC News, 1 June 2021, fetched).
- **Randle v. City of Tulsa:** filed 2020 by Randle, Fletcher and Van Ellis under Oklahoma public nuisance law against the City of Tulsa, the Tulsa Regional Chamber, the Tulsa County commissioners, Sheriff Vic Regalado and the Oklahoma Military Department (search summary of the Justia and CourtListener listings of 2024 OK 40). It also claimed the city had profited from the name "Black Wall Street" (DOJ 2025, p. 57). Dismissed July 2023 (`crime-justice`). The Oklahoma Supreme Court affirmed on **12 June 2024, 8 to 1** (Justice James Edmondson dissenting in part): the lingering harm "implicates generational-societal inequities that can only be resolved by policymakers, not the courts" (State Court Report, Brennan Center, fetched). Filing day 1 September 2020 (unconfirmed: search summary only).
- **January 2025:** the DOJ review (`crime-justice` tells it).
- **Hughes Van Ellis** died **9 October 2023** in Denver, aged **102**, a World War II veteran who had been a baby in 1921 (CBS and AP via search summaries; NPR *Morning Edition*, 10 October 2023, fetched: "Van Ellis died yesterday. He was 102").
- **Viola Ford Fletcher** died **24 November 2025**, aged **111** (CNN URL dated 24 November 2025; WP in the crime-justice bank; NBC's page was summarized as 25 November, so record the conflict: 24 November has two sources). Her grandson **Ike Howard** announced it (NBC News, fetched). Her memoir with Ike Howard: *Don't Let Them Bury My Story* (2023) (NBC; DOJ note 13).
- **Lessie Benningfield Randle**, born **10 November 1914** in Okmulgee, Oklahoma (Gerontology Research Group profile, fetched), turned 111 in November 2025 and was the last known living survivor in May 2026 (KOLUMN Magazine, 9 May 2026). **No report of her death was found through 27 September 2026** (search 2026-09-27). Refresh before prose.
- **1 June 2025:** Mayor Monroe Nichols's Greenwood Trust (`crime-justice` era 10 tells it).

### STORY source: Viola Ford Fletcher (new story `viola-fletcher`, era 8, kind "ordinary")
- Born **10 May 1914** (WP via crime-justice bank; NBC). Daughter of **Lucinda Ellis and John Wesley Ford** of Tulsa, sister of Hughes Van Ellis. Aged 7 on 31 May 1921.
- Her written testimony, 19 May 2021 (congress.gov PDF, read in full): woken by her family the night of the massacre, "My parents and five siblings were there. I was told we had to leave." "I will never forget the violence of the white mob when we left our house." "I still hear airplanes flying overhead. I hear the screams. I live through the Massacre every day." "When my family was forced to leave Tulsa, I lost my chance at an education. I never finished school past the fourth grade." She worked in the California shipyards in the Second World War, and "for most of my life, I was a domestic worker serving white families." "Today, I am in Washington, D.C. for the first time in my life. I am here seeking justice." "I am 107 years old and have never seen justice." "Open the courtroom doors to us."
- Plaintiff in *Randle v. City of Tulsa* (above). Died 24 November 2025, aged 111.
- Slug `viola-fletcher` checked against every `hb-story:start` in `outlines/*.md` on 2026-09-27: unused. No other outline tells her as a story. `crime-justice` era 10 quotes her once in span prose. The story block must not reuse that quote ("I still see Black men being shot").
- Movie: none about her found. Omit the line.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262c): the NAACP lynching report, checked against the report itself. The title in the outline was wrong
- The NAACP's report is ***Thirty Years of Lynching in the United States, 1889-1918***, published **April 1919**, 105 pages, fifty cents. Its own text: "we have confined ourselves to the story of the past thirty years, from 1889 to 1918, inclusive. During these years 3,224 persons have been killed by lynching mobs. Seven hundred and two white persons and 2,522 Negroes have been victims. Of the whites lynched, 691 have been men and 11 women, of the colored, 2,472 were men and 50 were women." (Internet Archive full text, https://archive.org/details/thirtyyearsoflyn00nati , read via the djvu text 2026-09-27. The report punctuates the last clause with a semicolon, rendered here as a comma.)
- **Correction:** the era-8 bank section above and the outline gave the title as "1889-1919," following history.house.gov. The report's own title page and text say **1889-1918**. The outline is corrected. **The 3,224 figure, handed forward from era 7 as unverified, is now verified** from the report itself.
- **The Dyer bill's House vote was 230 to 119** on 26 January 1922 (Ben Railton, "Considering History: The Dyer Anti-Lynching Bill," *Saturday Evening Post*, February 2022, fetched). Southern Democrats filibustered it in the Senate, and it was filibustered again in the 1923 and 1924 sessions and never became law (same). NAACP: "passage was halted by a filibuster in the Senate by Southern Democrats" (NAACP, "Dyer Anti-Lynching Bill," https://naacp.org/find-resources/history-explained/legislative-milestones/dyer-anti-lynching-bill , fetched). On **14 June 1922** the NAACP held a silent march in Washington with more than 5,000 Black marchers for the bill (*Saturday Evening Post*, as above).
- James Weldon Johnson became NAACP field secretary in 1916 and executive secretary in 1920 (NAACP, "James Weldon Johnson," fetched).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262c): the Red Summer of 1919, and the NAACP's court win that came out of Elaine
No chapter in the book tells the Red Summer (grep of `outlines/` and `research/`, 2026-09-27). This chapter tells it from the angle of the people who organized against it and the NAACP's legal answer. `crime-justice` keeps killing outside the law as crime.
