- **What it was:** "The Red Summer was a pattern of white-on-black violence that occurred in 1919 throughout the United States" (National Archives, "Racial Violence and the Red Summer," https://www.archives.gov/research/african-americans/wwi/red-summer , fetched). "Anti-Black riots erupted in 25 major American cities" (Equal Justice Initiative, "Red Summer of 1919," https://eji.org/news/history-racial-injustice-red-summer-of-1919/ , fetched). EJI: "Black veterans especially were targeted by white mobs and police." The name "Red Summer" is credited to James Weldon Johnson (unconfirmed: search summary only, History.com). The NAACP's Johnson page does not say so. **Do not credit him in prose.**
- **Chicago, 27 July to 3 August 1919.** Eugene Williams, 17, a Black teenager, drowned at the 29th Street beach after George Stauber, a 24-year-old white man, threw stones at the Black boys on a raft. Police officer Daniel Callahan "refused to take Stauber into custody" (Chicago History Museum, "The Murder of Eugene Williams and a Racial Reckoning," https://www.chicagohistory.org/the-murder-of-eugene-williams-and-a-racial-reckoning/ , fetched). "thirty-eight people were killed, twenty-three Black and fifteen white, and some 537 Chicagoans were injured." "Two-thirds of those injured were Black, and yet African Americans also made up two-thirds of the 138 persons indicted." White attackers included "youth gangs and so-called 'athletic clubs'" (Chicago Race Riot of 1919 Commemoration Project, https://chicagoraceriot.org/history/the-riot/ , fetched). Injured count disagreement: 537 (Commemoration Project) and 520 (search summary of another page). Record both. Whether Stauber was ever charged: not in either fetched page.
- **Elaine, Arkansas, 30 September to early October 1919.** About 100 Black sharecroppers met at a church at Hoop Spur for the Progressive Farmers and Household Union of America, led by **Robert Lee Hill**, organized to "obtain better payments for their cotton crops" from white landowners. Shooting broke out between the union's armed guards and men in a car outside. **W. A. Adkins**, a white security officer, was killed and Deputy Sheriff **Charles Pratt** wounded. Governor **Charles Hillman Brough** sent "more than 500 battle-tested troops from Camp Pike," who arrived 2 October. Estimates of Black people killed "ranged into the hundreds." Five white people died. Colonel **Isaac Jenks** recorded only two Black deaths caused by US troops, against other accounts. Phillips County charged **122** Black people. **Twelve men, the "Elaine Twelve," were sentenced to death by 5 November 1919.** One white defense lawyer admitted he "had not interviewed any witnesses." The NAACP's **Walter White** investigated, and the Black Little Rock lawyer **Scipio Africanus Jones** defended the twelve. **Who killed:** "an estimated 500 to 1,000 armed white people, mostly from the surrounding Arkansas counties but also from across the river in Mississippi," came to put down what they called an "insurrection." White witness **H. F. Smiddy** swore in 1921 that "several hundred of them ... began to hunt negroes and shotting [sic] them as they came to them." "Anecdotal evidence also suggests that the troops from Camp Pike engaged in indiscriminate killing." The brothers **David Augustine Elihue, Gibson Allen, Lewis Harrison (L. H.) and Leroy Johnston** were killed on 2 October returning to Helena from a hunting trip. The NAACP sent Field Secretary Walter White to investigate (Encyclopedia of Arkansas, same page, second fetch). The story that White passed as a white man there is not on that page. Do not use it without a source. In January 1925 Governor **Thomas McRae** freed the last of them (Encyclopedia of Arkansas, "Elaine Massacre of 1919," https://encyclopediaofarkansas.net/entries/elaine-massacre-of-1919-1102/ , fetched). National Archives: "it is estimated that over 100 African Americans were killed." **Counts to record:** "into the hundreds" (Encyclopedia of Arkansas), "over 100" (National Archives), 2 by troops (Jenks's report).
- ***Moore v. Dempsey*, 261 U.S. 86, decided 19 February 1923**, Justice Oliver Wendell Holmes for the Court, Justices McReynolds and Sutherland dissenting. The trial "lasted about three-quarters of an hour and in less than five minutes the jury brought in a verdict of guilty of murder in the first degree." "The Court and neighborhood were thronged with an adverse crowd that threatened the most dangerous consequences to anyone interfering." Affidavits said Black witnesses were "whipped, placed in the electric chair and strangled by something put in their noses to make them testify." If "the whole proceeding is a mask," a federal court must step in (Cornell LII, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/261/86 , fetched). **The opinion quotes the affidavit only and does not say what was done to the men in the chair. Say only what the opinion quotes.**
- NAACP membership grew from 9,000 before the war to about 100,000 by the early 1920s (unconfirmed: search summary only, History.com).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262c): Mexican Repatriation, 1929 to 1936. US citizens sent out of their own country
`immigration` era 8 names it in one clause and its bank (T-238 PATCH) holds the full sourcing. This chapter tells it as a removal of citizens and the apology campaign.
- **Who:** US Secretary of Labor **William N. Doak**, under President **Herbert Hoover**, led a national campaign to remove Mexicans, saying they took jobs. In Los Angeles **Charles P. Visel**, head of the city's unemployment relief committee, planned the publicity, and the **Los Angeles County welfare office** ran the "repatriation" trains. The first county train left **23 March 1931**. County welfare officers told families their relief money would stop unless they took free train tickets. Hospital staff carried patients on stretchers to trucks for the border. On **26 February 1931** immigration agents sealed the exits of La Placita park in Los Angeles with about 400 people inside (California Migration Museum, "From Deportation to Repatriation," https://www.calmigration.org/learn-chapter/deportation-to-repatriation , as recorded in `research-immigration.md` T-238).
- **How many (every count):** about 2 million people of Mexican ancestry, "approximately 1.2 million of whom had been born in the United States," and in California about 400,000 "American citizens and legal residents" (State of California, SB 670 findings, Government Code §8721, https://www.leginfo.ca.gov/pub/05-06/bill/sen/sb_0651-0700/sb_670_bill_20051007_chaptered.html , fetched 2026-09-27). About 1 million nationally, about 60 percent US-born (California Migration Museum, following Balderrama and Rodríguez, *Decade of Betrayal*). About 500,000 (the bank's older figure, "Mexican Repatriation references"). About 35,000 from Los Angeles (Migration Museum). **Range for prose: about 500,000 to 2 million, and more than half of them born in the United States by the higher counts.**
- SB 670 findings: "massive raids were conducted on Mexican-American communities, resulting in the clandestine removal of thousands." Families "were forced to abandon, or were defrauded of, personal and real property, which often was sold by local authorities." Citizens "were deprived of their livelihood and United States constitutional rights." **Apology (§8722):** "The State of California apologizes to those individuals described in Section 8721 for the fundamental violations of their basic civil liberties and constitutional rights committed during the period of illegal deportation and coerced emigration." Governor approved it **7 October 2005** (same, fetched). Los Angeles County supervisors apologized in February 2012 (MALDEF, in `research-immigration.md`). No federal apology was found.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262c): Japanese American incarceration, who gave the orders, the count dispute, the end of exclusion, and redress
- **Who ordered it:** President Roosevelt signed Executive Order 9066 on 19 February 1942. Lieutenant General **John L. DeWitt**, commanding the Western Defense Command, issued the curfews "that included only Japanese Americans" and Public Proclamation No. 4, "which began the forced evacuation and detention of Japanese-American West Coast residents on a 48-hour notice." Congress passed **Public Law 503** (21 March 1942), making it a crime to break an exclusion order, punishable by up to a year in prison and a $5,000 fine (National Archives, "Executive Order 9066," https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/executive-order-9066 , fetched; date from NPS Tule Lake timeline). DeWitt issued Public Proclamation No. 1 on 2 March 1942 and Civilian Exclusion Order No. 1 on 23 March 1942, for Bainbridge Island, Washington (NPS, "Timeline: Japanese Americans during World War II," https://www.nps.gov/tule/planyourvisit/timeline-japanese-americans-during-world-war-ii.htm , fetched).
- **The count and the citizens, two figures:** "Approximately 122,000 men, women, and children," of whom "Nearly 70,000 ... were American citizens" (National Archives). "Over 120,000," two thirds citizens (uscourts.gov, above). Record both. The ten camps were in Wyoming, California, Utah, Arizona, Colorado, Idaho and Arkansas (National Archives).
- **The end of exclusion, settled.** Public Proclamation No. 21 was issued **17 December 1944**, the day before the *Korematsu* and *Endo* rulings, lifting the West Coast exclusion effective **2 January 1945**. Tule Lake, the last camp, closed **20 March 1946** (NPS timeline, fetched). This settles item 8 of the 2026-09-09 log.
- **Redress (dates belong to era 9 and 10 by the boundary rule; recorded here because era 8 leads the event).** President Carter signed the act creating the Commission on Wartime Relocation and Internment of Civilians on 31 July 1980. It held 20 days of hearings in 11 places, July to December 1981, with more than 750 witnesses. Its February 1983 report found the causes were "race prejudice, war hysteria and a failure of political leadership." President Reagan signed the **Civil Liberties Act on 10 August 1988**, with an apology and **$20,000** to each surviving person. Senators Daniel Inouye and Spark Matsunaga and Representatives Norman Mineta and Robert Matsui led it in Congress, and the Japanese American Citizens League and the National Council for Japanese American Redress campaigned for it. First apology letters and checks: 1 October 1990 (US House History, Art & Archives, "Long Road to Redress," https://history.house.gov/Exhibitions-and-Publications/APA/Historical-Essays/Exclusion-to-Inclusion/Redress/ , fetched). Another account gives 9 October 1990 for the first nine checks, and a total of **82,219** people paid (unconfirmed: search summary only). The National Archives: "$20,000 cash payment to each person who was incarcerated."

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262c): the Silent Sentinels, now checked on the NPS pages themselves
- NPS, "National Woman's Party Protests During World War I," https://www.nps.gov/articles/national-womans-party-protests-world-war-i.htm (fetched): picketing began **10 January 1917**, "six days a week." "More than 150 women were convicted of obstructing traffic" over the whole campaign. Another banner: "MR. PRESIDENT IT IS UNJUST TO DENY WOMEN A VOICE IN THEIR GOVERNMENT WHEN THE GOVERNMENT IS CONSCRIPTING THEIR SONS."
- NPS, "Suffrage in Sixty Seconds: Picketing," https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/suffrage60seconds-picketing.htm (fetched): "Picketing the White House was actually invented by the American suffragist Alice Paul." Banner texts: "Mr President What Will You Do For Woman Suffrage?" and **"How Long, Mr President, Must Women Wait For Liberty?"** **Correction:** the outline's second banner was "How Long Must Women Wait for Liberty?" It is corrected to the NPS wording. The "first to picket the White House" claim now rests on NPS's statement that Paul invented it.
- **The amendment's own history.** Senator **Aaron Sargent** of California introduced the same wording on **10 January 1878**. The Senate voted it down 16 to 34 on 25 January 1887, 35 to 34 (short of two-thirds) on 19 March 1914, 53 to 31 on 1 October 1918, 55 to 29 on 10 February 1919, and passed it 56 to 25 on 4 June 1919 (US Senate, "Nineteenth Amendment vertical timeline," https://www.senate.gov/about/women-of-the-senate/nineteenth-amendment-vertical-timeline.htm , fetched). Susan B. Anthony died **13 March 1906** in Rochester, New York. The amendment was called the Anthony Amendment "as a final tribute" to her (NPS, https://www.nps.gov/people/susan-b-anthony.htm , fetched). This settles the era-7 handoff item "Anthony's death and the 19th Amendment."

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262c): Isaac Woodard and the road to Truman's 1948 order (the rights-campaign side)
- The beating is sourced in `research-war.md` (T-259b PATCH "Isaac Woodard," South Carolina Encyclopedia): Police Chief **Lynwood Shull** and Officer **Elliot Long** of Batesburg, South Carolina, beat Sergeant Woodard on 12 February 1946, the day of his discharge, and he was blind for life. An all-white federal jury acquitted Shull in November 1946 after thirty minutes.
- **The link to Truman, now with a source.** On **19 September 1946** a delegation led by the NAACP's **Walter White** met President Truman. Truman: "My God! I had no idea that it was as terrible as that! We've got to do something!" On **5 December 1946** he signed **Executive Order 9808**, creating the **President's Committee on Civil Rights** (Truman Library Institute, "To Secure These Rights, Part VI," https://www.trumanlibraryinstitute.org/civil-rights-symposium-history-6/ , fetched; the page shows Woodard's photograph in that section). Its report, *To Secure These Rights*, came out in 1947 (search summary, Truman Library Institute listing; month not confirmed).
- No outline tells Woodard (war T-259b grep). This chapter tells him in one span beside Randolph's 1948 campaign.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262c): the LGBTQ thread in era 8. Henry Gerber's Society for Human Rights, Chicago, 1924 to 1925
- **Henry Gerber** was born **Josef Heinrich Dittmar** on 29 June 1892 in Bavaria, came through Ellis Island in October 1913, served in the US Army and worked on an army newspaper in Germany after the First World War. He founded the **Society for Human Rights** in Chicago on **10 December 1924** and got a State of Illinois charter for it. The charter described its purpose as protecting people "abused and hindered in the legal pursuit of happiness." NPS: "the first official organization with the exclusive purpose being to advocate for homosexual civil rights in the United States." It printed two issues of *Friendship and Freedom*, "the earliest official homosexual periodical in the U.S." No copy survives. Police arrested Gerber and others without a warrant and took his typewriter, writings and diaries, and only the typewriter came back. The Post Office fired him "for conduct unbecoming a postal employee." He died in 1972. His Chicago house became a National Historic Landmark in June 2015 (NPS, "Henry Gerber House National Historic Landmark," https://www.nps.gov/articles/henry-gerber-house-national-historic-landmark.htm , fetched). The society ended in 1925 (DOI press release title and search summary). **Gay** (definition for prose): a man who loves men or a woman who loves women.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262c): era-7 handoffs settled in era 8
- **Ida B. Wells-Barnett's death:** **25 March 1931**, Chicago. The *Chicago Daily News* ran her obituary by Frank L. Hayes that day (Chicago Sun-Times, 25 March 2021, fetched). Cause: uremia, kidney failure (search summary of Encyclopedia.com, unconfirmed: search summary only). The Sun-Times gives her age as 62, but her birth date of 16 July 1862 makes her 68. NWHM's 15 March is the outlier. **Use 25 March 1931.**
- **The Comstock Act into Sanger:** Margaret Sanger was charged under the Comstock laws in 1914 for *The Woman Rebel*, a paper about birth control, and left for England. She opened a birth control clinic in Brooklyn on **16 October 1916**, was arrested "barely a week later," and spent 30 days in jail. A 1918 court ruling let doctors prescribe contraceptives for medical reasons. She founded the American Birth Control League in 1921. In 1936 a federal court let doctors prescribe birth control. She supported parts of the eugenics movement (NWHM, "Margaret Sanger," https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/margaret-sanger , fetched). **Birth control** (definition): ways to keep from becoming pregnant. Bank only. The outline does not tell Sanger, and her eugenics views would need their own sourcing before any story.
- **Chinese exclusion:** repealed by the Magnuson Act, signed **17 December 1943**, with a quota of **105** a year, and Chinese immigrants became "the first Asians eligible to naturalize" (HistoryLink File 8993, search summary. Immigration History, "Repeal of Chinese Exclusion (1943)," https://immigrationhistory.org/item/1943-repeal-of-chinese-exclusion/ , fetched, for the 105 quota, naturalization, and China as a wartime ally).

### SEARCHED, NOT FOUND 2026-09-27 (T-262c): the 1902 and 1904 laws that extended Chinese exclusion, as a fetched source
Sources checked: Immigration History (1943 page, silent on 1902 and 1904), HistoryLink search listing (silent), search summary of the Magnuson Act (silent). How the prose can say it: "Congress renewed the ban on Chinese workers again and again after 1882, and did not repeal it until 1943."

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262c): other "firsts" in the era-8 outline, checked
- **Brotherhood of Sleeping Car Porters, "the first Black labor union in the United States."** NPS says it, and the Chicago History Museum calls it "the first all-Black labor union in the US." The narrower claim both sources support is: "In 1935, the Brotherhood of Sleeping Car Porters became the first African American union organization to be granted membership into the America Federation of Labor" (Chicago History Museum, "The Brotherhood of Sleeping Car Porters," fetched). The outline now uses the narrower claim. Pullman signed a contract with it in 1937 (April 1937, Chicago History Museum. 25 August 1937, search summary. Record both).
- **Cuyahoga County Council for the Retarded Child, "the earliest of the American parent groups."** The county's history page (cited above) calls it a grassroots parent group founded in 1933 and does not say it was the earliest. **Unsupported. Removed from the outline.**
- **League of the Physically Handicapped, "almost nobody has heard of it."** A judgment with no source. Removed from the outline.
- **Eugenics span, "its authors had read the American ones."** USHMM says the 1933 law was "based in part on the practice of forced sterilization in the US," which is already quoted. The claim about what the authors read has no source. Removed.
- **Lemon Grove, "the first school desegregation case won in the United States," and California "the first state to strike school segregation out of its own laws":** both carried by the 2026-09-09 bank above from the California Legislature and are left as they stand.
- **Lloyd Gaines, "the first time the NAACP won a school case at the Supreme Court":** sourced above (SHSMO, State Historical Society of Missouri).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262c): era-8 story checks (Paul, Fryer, Buck, Korematsu, Randolph, Gaines)
Every key fact in the six older era-8 story blocks was matched against the bank sections above. Two results:
- **Alice Paul, "One of her English ancestors had died in prison for being a Quaker."** No bank source. NPS says only "Born to a New Jersey Quaker family in 1885" (https://www.nps.gov/people/alice-paul.htm , fetched). A search summary of her oral history (*Conversations with Alice Paul*, cdlib) says her first Paul ancestor was imprisoned in England as a Quaker and then came to America, which contradicts "died in prison" (unconfirmed: search summary only). **The sentence is removed from the outline.**
- **Alice Paul's boarded window, now sourced in her own words** (Stevens, *Jailed for Freedom*, Gutenberg 3604, fetched): "Dr. Gannon immediately ordered one window nailed from top to bottom. He then ordered the door leading into the hallway taken down and an iron-barred cell door put in its place." Then a prisoner on a ladder "began to nail heavy boards across the window from the outside." He told her he was in prison for drinking and "had never seen anything so inhuman as boarding up this window and depriving a prisoner of light and air." The actor for the story is Dr. Gannon, who gave the order.
- All other story facts (Fryer: Evans, Blum, Berry, Judge Clark, Donohue. Buck: 20 March 1924, Strode, Whitehead, Lombardo, Estabrook, 8,300, Warner 2002, Doris. Korematsu: Stonehurst, Clyde Sarah, Patel, Larkspur. Randolph: 8802, 9981. Gaines: Water Valley, Vashon, Redmond, 1938 vote, 2007 FBI) are in the bank sections dated 2026-09-09. The Randolph "first Black labor union" line is narrowed (PATCH on firsts). The new `viola-fletcher` story is sourced in the Tulsa PATCH.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262c): parked items for era 8, what this chapter did with each
- **Houston, 23 August 1917, and Port Chicago, 17 July 1944:** fully sourced in `research-war.md` (T-259b). They are courts-martial of Black servicemen, the war chapter's angle, and no chapter's outline tells them yet. This chapter takes neither and hands both back to `war`. The rights-side facts: the Army set aside all the Houston convictions in 2023, and the Navy exonerated the Port Chicago 50 on 17 July 2024, after Thurgood Marshall of the NAACP had worked on their appeal (NPS, in the war bank).
- **Isaac Woodard:** taken, one span (PATCH above).
- **1927 flood killings:** the only source is Wikipedia citing John Barry's *Rising Tide* (disasters T-264). Not used. It needs a second source first.
- **Hill-Burton (1946), Japanese American farms (1942), Maggie Walker and Jesse Binga:** each fully sourced in its own bank (`health`, `food-farming`, `money`). None is a campaign by the people denied the right, so none is added here. Hill-Burton's "separate but equal" clause and its end in *Simkins v. Cone* (1963) are era 9's campaign.
- **Scottsboro (1931 to 1937):** `crime-justice` tells the trials and rulings. The defense campaign is this chapter's if a later editor wants it. Not researched here.

# ERA 9 — 1950 to 2000 — RESEARCHED 2026-09-09

## 9. 1950 to 2000

### 9.1 The end of the NAACP's four-step campaign — *Sweatt*, *McLaurin*, *Brown*
*(Handed forward by the era-8 agent, who sourced steps one to three: Pearson v. Murray 1936, Gaines 1938, Sipuel 1948.)*

**Sweatt v. Painter, 339 U.S. 629.** Decided **5 June 1950**, unanimous, opinion by Chief Justice
Fred Vinson. Heman Marion Sweatt applied to the University of Texas Law School in **February 1946**
and was rejected only because he was Black. Texas then set up a separate law school for Black
students, opened **February 1947**, in Austin: **four part-time faculty** borrowed from UT, about
**10,000 volumes** ordered of which few had arrived, **no full-time librarian**, and no
accreditation. Vinson: "With such a substantial and significant segment of society excluded, we
cannot conclude that the education offered petitioner is substantially equal to that which he would
receive if admitted to the University of Texas Law School." And: "Equal protection of the laws is
not achieved through indiscriminate imposition of inequalities." Sweatt had to be admitted.
— law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/339/629

**McLaurin v. Oklahoma State Regents, 339 U.S. 637.** Decided the **same day, 5 June 1950**,
unanimous, Vinson again. George W. McLaurin held a master's degree and applied for a doctorate in
education at the University of Oklahoma. The university let him in and then set the terms. Verbatim
from the opinion: "Thus he was required to sit apart at a designated desk in an anteroom adjoining
the classroom; to sit at a designated desk on the mezzanine floor of the library, but not to use
the desks in the regular reading room; and to sit at a designated table and to eat at a different
time from the other students in the school cafeteria." Before the argument the rules were loosened:
a designated row in the classroom, an assigned table on the library's main floor, the cafeteria at
the same time as everyone else but still at a separate table. The Court struck the whole
arrangement down. — law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/339/637

**Brown v. Board of Education of Topeka, 347 U.S. 483.** Decided **17 May 1954**, unanimous,
opinion by Chief Justice Earl Warren. "Separate educational facilities are inherently unequal.
Therefore, we hold that the plaintiffs and others similarly situated for whom the actions have been
brought are, by reason of the segregation complained of, deprived of the equal protection of the
laws guaranteed by the Fourteenth Amendment." — archives.gov/milestone-documents/brown-v-board-of-education

**The five cases the Court took in the fall of 1952** (NPS, Brown v. Board of Education NHP):
*Oliver Brown et al. v. Board of Education of Topeka* (Kansas) · *Briggs v. Elliott* (South
Carolina) · *Davis v. Prince Edward County School Board* (Virginia) · *Belton v. Gebhart*
(Delaware) · *Bolling v. Sharpe* (District of Columbia).
— nps.gov/brvb/learn/historyculture/fivecases.htm (via NPS search summary)

### 9.2 What signing the petition cost — Clarendon County, South Carolina
NPS, Brown v. Board of Education NHP, "Briggs v. Elliott"
(nps.gov/brvb/learn/historyculture/socarolina.htm):
- Clarendon County School District 22 ran **more than 30 buses for white students and none for
  Black students.** The parents began "with a simple request, a school bus."
- The all-white Summerton High School is described in the case record as "modern, safe, sanitary,
  well equipped, lighted and healthy." The Black schools: "inadequate…unhealthy…old and
  overcrowded and in a dilapidated condition."
- **Rev. Joseph A. DeLaine**, an educator and minister, "fostered a meeting between the National
  Association for the Advancement of Colored People and the Pearson brothers." He was later
  "attacked by six white men" and escaped "by pretending he had a gun in his pocket and leaping
  into the passing truck of another petitioner."
- The petition went round for signature on **11 November 1949**; "By the end, 107 parents and their
  children had signed."
- The suit was filed **16 May 1950**. It is named for **Harry Briggs**, the first signatory.
- Reprisals, in the page's own words: petitioners were "fired from their jobs, denied service when
  trying to purchase supplies for their farms, or being turned down for loans on their properties."
- **NOT on this page and therefore not claimed:** the 1947 bus request date, any statement by
  Superintendent Roderick Elliott, Harry and Eliza Briggs's own jobs, and the burning of DeLaine's
  house and church. Named nowhere in what was fetched.

### 9.3 Barbara Rose Johns and the Moton strike — fills the `target` story slot
Encyclopedia Virginia, "Barbara Rose Johns Powell"
(encyclopediavirginia.org/entries/barbara-rose-johns-powell/):
- Born **6 March 1935, New York City.** Died **25 September 1991, Philadelphia**, of bone cancer,
  aged 56.
- **Sixteen** in April 1951. Robert Russa Moton High School, Farmville, **Prince Edward County,
  Virginia.**
- The building was **designed for 180 students and held 360 to 450.** Classes met "in the
  auditorium, school buses, and three temporary wood-frame outbuildings" wrapped in tar paper,
  which people compared to "chicken coops." **No cafeteria, no science laboratory, no gymnasium.**
- What decided her: **a half-empty white school bus passed her without stopping.**
- **23 April 1951.** A student, **John Watson**, disguised his voice and telephoned the principal's
  office saying students were making trouble at the downtown bus station. **Principal M. Boyd Jones
  left the school to investigate**, and the strike began while he was out. **It lasted two weeks.**
- She wrote to the NAACP lawyers **Oliver W. Hill** and **Spottswood William Robinson III.** Their
  condition for taking it: the parents had to sue to end segregation itself, "not just unequal
  facilities."
- ***Davis v. County School Board of Prince Edward County*, filed 23 May 1951.** **74 parents
  representing 118 Moton students** were plaintiffs. It became one of the five cases decided as
  *Brown*.
- Afterwards her parents sent her to **Montgomery, Alabama** for her senior year, to her uncle
  **Vernon Johns**, a pastor, and his wife **Altona**. **The family home later burned down and the
  family suspected arson.**
- **Not stated by the source and therefore not claimed:** the number of students who walked out on
  23 April; her grade.

Encyclopedia Virginia, "Moton School Strike and Prince Edward County School Closings":
- **Rev. L. Francis Griffin** called a mass meeting at the Black First Baptist Church in Farmville
  on **3 May 1951** to put the NAACP's offer to the parents.
- The county board of supervisors **voted to cut off public school money on 26 June 1959.**
  **Schools reopened 8 September 1964.** **About 2,000 Black students** were left with no formal
  schooling, for **five years**. The white private school was **Prince Edward Academy**.
- ***Griffin v. County School Board of Prince Edward County*, decided 25 May 1964.**
- **Attorney General Robert F. Kennedy, 1963, verbatim:** "the only places on earth not to provide
  free public education are Communist China, North Vietnam, Sarawak, Singapore, British Honduras —
  and Prince Edward County, Virginia."
- **Not addressed by the source:** whether Black families refused the state tuition grants.
- **Note on overlap:** `education` already carries the closure in full, including the 1,700-2,000
  range, the 1963 free schools, and the 2004 Virginia scholarship fund. This chapter takes **the
  student strike as a campaign** and the reprisals, not the closure narrative.

### 9.4 Little Rock — the campaign side, and the price the adults paid
`education` already tells the school year inside Central High in full (Elizabeth Eckford's walk, the
101st Airborne, Minnijean Brown's expulsion, the Lost Year of 1958-59). **This chapter does not
repeat it.** What belongs here is the organizing and the reprisals.
- NPS, "Home of Daisy and L.C. Bates" (nps.gov/places/home-of-daisy-and-l-c-bates.htm): Daisy Bates
  was **president of the Arkansas State Conference of the NAACP** and co-publisher, with her husband
  L. C. Bates, of the ***Arkansas State Press***. Their house was the pick-up and drop-off point for
  the nine students every school day and the press gathering place. **In August 1957 a rock came
  through the living room window with a note on it: "STONE THIS TIME. DYNAMITE NEXT."** Crosses were
  burned in the front yard on two occasions and shots were fired into the house from a passing car.
  The page gives no dates for the crosses or the shots and **none are claimed**. It says nothing
  about the newspaper's fate, an advertiser boycott, or her arrest, and **none of that is claimed
  here**.
- ***Bates v. Little Rock*, 361 U.S. 516**, decided **23 February 1960**, opinion by Justice Potter
  Stewart. Little Rock and North Little Rock had ordinances making organizations file with the City
  Clerk their name, headquarters, officers and their salaries, purpose, and financial detail
  including "dues...and/or contributions paid, by whom paid" — **all of it made public.** Daisy
  Bates, custodian of the Little Rock NAACP branch records, and a Mr. Williams of the North Little
  Rock branch, were convicted for refusing to hand over the membership lists. Stewart: "Inviolability
  of privacy in group association may in many circumstances be indispensable to preservation of
  freedom of association, particularly where a group espouses dissident beliefs." Convictions
  reversed; "petitioners cannot be punished for refusing to produce information which the
  municipalities could not constitutionally require." **The opinion does not state the vote or the
  fine amount; neither is claimed.** — law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/361/516

### 9.5 New Orleans, 14 November 1960 — four girls, not one
64 Parishes (Louisiana Endowment for the Humanities), "New Orleans School Crisis":
- Federal district judge **J. Skelly Wright** ordered New Orleans to desegregate in **spring 1960**.
- **The schools were desegregated on 14 November 1960.** **Four** Black six-year-old girls went in,
  to two schools: **Ruby Bridges** alone at **William Frantz Elementary**, and **Leona Tate, Tessie
  Prevost and Gail Etienne** together at **McDonogh 19** in the Lower Ninth Ward.
- "by the end of the month, fewer than ten white children attended McDonogh and William Frantz
  combined."
- From **January to May 1961 Ruby Bridges was the only student at William Frantz**, because white
  parents kept their children out.
- NPS, "Louisiana: Leona Tate Foundation for Change": the three girls at McDonogh 19 were escorted
  in by **federal marshals** on the same morning; Leona Tate was **six**. McDonogh 19 reopened as the
  **Tate, Etienne, and Prevost Center** on **4 May 2022**.
- **Not stated by these sources and therefore not claimed:** the screening test given to Black
  applicants, how many applied, how long the crowds gathered, and whether the girls' parents lost
  jobs or homes.
- **Overlap note:** `education` owns Ruby Bridges as a story (`ruby-bridges`). This chapter says
  plainly that there were four girls and names the other three, which the single-child version of
  the story leaves out.

### 9.6 Busing, and the ruling that stopped it at the city line
- ***Milliken v. Bradley*, 418 U.S. 717**, decided **25 July 1974**, **5 to 4**, opinion by Chief
  Justice Warren Burger; Justices Douglas, White, Brennan and Marshall dissenting. The lower court's
  plan would have covered **54 school districts** — Detroit plus 53 suburban ones. Burger: "Before
  the boundaries of separate and autonomous school districts may be set aside by consolidating the
  separate units for remedial purposes or by imposing a cross-district remedy, it must first be
  shown that there has been a constitutional violation within one district that produces a
  significant segregative effect in another district." Justice White, dissenting: "The State's
  default is 'the condition that offends the Constitution,' and state officials may therefore be
  ordered to take the necessary measures to completely eliminate from the Detroit public schools
  'all vestiges of state-imposed segregation.'" — law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/418/717
- **Boston.** NPS, "The Fight for Equal Education Continues: Morgan v. Hennigan"
  (nps.gov/articles/000/morgan-v-hennigan.htm): **Tallulah Morgan**, "a Black mother of three
  students," was the first-named plaintiff; **fourteen families** sued the Boston School Committee.
  Filed in U.S. District Court in **1972**. Judge **W. Arthur Garrity** ruled on **21 June 1974**
  that "the entire school system of Boston was unconstitutionally segregated" and that school
  authorities "had knowingly carried out a systematic program of segregation affecting all of the
  city's students, teachers and school facilities." Phase 1, beginning **9 September 1974**, paired
  **South Boston** with **Roxbury**. **Of 800 Black students assigned to South Boston that first day,
  56 came.** Protesters "threw rocks and bottles at the buses, breaking windows and yelling racial
  slurs." Garrity handed monitoring to the Massachusetts State Board of Education in **1982** and
  gave his final order in **1985**.
- **Not claimed:** injury counts, the December 1974 stabbing at South Boston High, the school's
  closure, and any white-flight enrollment figures. The NPS page carries none of them.

### 9.7 Title IX, 1972 — the sponsors and the wording
- **The wording, verbatim:** "No person in the United States shall, on the basis of sex, be excluded
  from participation in, be denied the benefits of, or be subjected to discrimination under any
  education program or activity receiving Federal financial assistance." **Enacted 23 June 1972**,
  as Title IX of the Education Amendments of 1972.
  — history.house.gov/Education/NHD/NHD-2025/NHD-Education/
- **Who wrote and carried it:** **Rep. Patsy Takemoto Mink** of Hawaii, **Rep. Edith Green** of
  Oregon, who "led the hearings in the House of Representatives advocating passage of Title IX," and
  **Sen. Birch Bayh** of Indiana. The House history office's Mink material says the three worked
  together in the 92nd Congress (1971-73) to get the votes. Mink died **28 September 2002** in
  Honolulu, and the act was renamed the **Patsy Takemoto Mink Equal Opportunity in Education Act**.
  — history.house.gov (Mink profile and blog; the Bayh detail and the renaming come from the House
  history office **search summary**, not from a page fetched directly — flagged)
- **The House page does not say who signed it**, does not give Green's hearing dates or
  subcommittee, and carries no quotation from any of the three. None of that is claimed.
- **What changed in school sport.** obamawhitehouse.archives.gov (22 June 2012): before Title IX
  "fewer than 300,000 girls competed in high school sports"; in 2012 "over 3 million." The post says
  the number "has risen ten-fold in the past 40 years," that "six times as many women now compete in
  college sports," and that in 2012 there were still "1.3 million fewer opportunities for girls than
  boys to participate in high school athletics."
- **What had not changed by 2013-14.** GAO (blog, 6 February 2019, reporting GAO-17-754R on
  Department of Education data): "girls made up 49% of students at public high schools that offer
  sports and 43% of sports participants at those schools."
- **The often-quoted 1971-72 figure of 294,015 girls** comes only from a **search summary** here and
  was not fetched from any source page. **Not used.**

### 9.8 The two cases behind the 1975 act — PARC and Mills
- ***Pennsylvania Association for Retarded Children v. Commonwealth of Pennsylvania*, Civil Action
  No. 71-42, U.S. District Court, Eastern District of Pennsylvania.** Two orders, both confirmed
  directly from the CourtListener search API: **334 F. Supp. 1257, filed 8 October 1971**, and
  **343 F. Supp. 279, filed 5 May 1972**, before a three-judge panel of **Adams, Masterson and
  Broderick**. Plaintiffs' lawyer: **Thomas K. Gilhool** of Philadelphia. The 1971 caption is
  "PENNSYLVANIA ASSOCIATION FOR RETARDED CHILDREN, **Nancy Beth Bowman**, et al., Plaintiffs."
- **The statutes, verbatim**, from M. White, *Right to Education — Civil Action No. 71-42*, December
  1972, ERIC ED082394 (a U.S. Department of Health, Education and Welfare / National Institute of
  Education document), read with pdftotext:
  - **24 P.S. Pa. Sec. 13-1304:** "The board of school directors may refuse to accept or retain
    beginners who have not attained a mental age of five years, as determined by the supervisor of
    special education or a properly certificated public school psychologist in accordance with
    standards prescribed by the State Board of Education."
  - **24 P.S. Pa. Sec. 13-1375** provided for the "Temporary or permanent exclusion from the public
    school of children who are found to be uneducable and untrainable in the public schools."
  - Children with IQ scores "of less than 30 were considered uneducable and were assigned to the
    Department of Public Welfare for placement and perhaps training."
  - The statutes challenged were **24 P.S. Pa. Secs. 13-1304, 13-1326, 13-1330, 13-1375.**
- **The scale:** "the Pennsylvania Association for Retarded Children (P.A.R.C.) estimated that
  approximately **100,000** mentally retarded children in Pennsylvania were being denied free
  education and training." The suit was brought on **7 January 1971**, "on behalf of **fourteen**
  mentally retarded" children. The same document reports litigation then running in **20 states**.
- **The word.** The name of the organization and the language of the statutes are the words used at
  the time. The education chapter handles this the same way in its own era-9 span. State the word as
  the government's own word of the period and say what it meant.
- ***Mills v. Board of Education of the District of Columbia*** — decided **1 August 1972** by Judge
  Joseph Waddy. **The education chapter owns this case in full**, with all seven named children.
  This chapter does not retell it and does not use a story block for it (the slug
  peter-mills-education belongs to education).
- **A source error found:** mn.gov/mnddc/parallels2/three/003.html dates *Mills* to **1971** and says
  it "affirmed the Pennsylvania decision." *Mills* was decided **1 August 1972**, as the education
  chapter's own sourced span states. **Not repeated.**
- **How many children were out of school:** the same mn.gov page states that "by 1975, the Bureau of
  Education for the Handicapped was also saying that an estimated one million — children with
  significant disabilities were still totally excluded from the education system, and at least
  another 300,000 were not receiving adequate services."

### 9.9 Section 504, the 1977 sit-in, Gallaudet 1988, and the ADA
- **Section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act of 1973, verbatim** (NPS, "504 Protest: Disability,
  Community, and Civil Rights"): "No otherwise qualified individual with a disability in the United
  States...shall, solely by reason of his or her disability, be excluded from the participation in,
  be denied the benefits of, or be subjected to discrimination under any program or activity
  receiving Federal financial assistance."
- **The regulations went unsigned for four years.** They had been drafted under President Ford;
  **HEW Secretary Joseph A. Califano Jr.** would not sign them. Schools and hospitals objected to
  what compliance would cost.
- **5 April 1977: ten demonstrations at once** — San Francisco, Washington D.C., Seattle, Los
  Angeles, Denver, Chicago, Dallas, Atlanta, New York City, Boston and Philadelphia.
- **About 100 disabled protesters occupied the HEW offices at 50 UN Plaza, San Francisco, for 26
  days.** The **Black Panther Party** brought one hot meal a day plus ingredients for two more. The
  **International Association of Machinists** paid for things, including flying a delegation to
  Washington. The **Brick Hut**, a lesbian cooperative, and **Glide Memorial Church** helped.
  Organizers inside: **Judy Heumann, Kitty Cone and Dennis Billups.** Heumann and Billups went to
  Washington. Califano signed.
- **CONFLICT, stated not resolved.** NPS's "504 Protest" article says **26 days**; NPS's "Disability
  History: The Disability Rights Movement" article says **April 5-28, 1977** and **24 days**.
  Neither gives the signing date. The outline says the occupation ran into its fourth week and gives
  no signing date.
- **Gallaudet, Deaf President Now, 1988.** National Archives *Pieces of History* blog (3 August
  2015): an **eight-day** protest in **March 1988**; the Board of Trustees chose the hearing
  candidate, **Elisabeth Zinser**. The four demands: Zinser must resign and a deaf president be
  chosen; **Jane Spilman** must resign from the Board; deaf members must be at least **51 percent**
  of the Board; and no reprisals against protesters. Students "marched, boycotted classes, and
  deflated school bus tires in front of university gates." After eight days the board gave way and
  **Dr. I. King Jordan** became Gallaudet's first deaf president. Jordan: "A Deaf person can do
  anything a hearing person can, except hear." The blog says the coverage "may well have pushed
  passage of the [ADA] forward."
  — prologue.blogs.archives.gov/2015/08/03/deaf-president-now-and-the-25th-anniversary-of-the-ada/
- **From a Gallaudet search summary only, flagged:** protest dates **6-13 March 1988**; Zinser
  resigned **11 March**; the Board met **13 March**, Spilman left the Board entirely, **Philip
  Bravin** became the first deaf Board chair, and Jordan was appointed. **gallaudet.edu returns 403
  to this environment**, so none of that was fetched from the university's own pages. The outline
  uses only the archives.gov facts plus "March 1988" and the eight days; it does not state Zinser's
  resignation date or Bravin's appointment.
- **A source conflict on the finalists.** The archives.gov blog says "three deaf candidates and one
  hearing candidate" were in contention. The usual account gives three finalists — I. King Jordan
  and Harvey Corson, both deaf, and Zinser, hearing. **Not settled here.** The outline says only
  that the board chose a hearing candidate over deaf ones and does not give a number.
- **The Capitol Crawl and the ADA.** NPS, "Disability History: The Disability Rights Movement":
  protesters "tossed aside their wheelchairs, walkers, and crutches and ascended the steps" of the
  Capitol. **NPS dates this to July 1990.** It is commonly dated **12 March 1990**, four months
  before the signing, and the NPS date looks like a conflation with the signing. **Not settled; the
  outline gives no date for the crawl and says so.** NPS names no child; **Jennifer Keelan is not
  claimed.**
- **The ADA was signed on 26 July 1990 by President George H. W. Bush** — acl.gov/ada/timeline
  ("1990: ADA Passed House ... July 26: ADA Signed") and NPS. **The House and Senate vote dates were
  not found and are not claimed.**

### 9.10 Judy Heumann — fills the judy-heumann candidate slot and clears the licence [VERIFY]
NPS, "Judy Heumann" (nps.gov/people/judy-heumann.htm):
- Born **18 December 1947, Philadelphia**. **Polio at eighteen months**, which paralysed her; she
  used a wheelchair from early childhood.
- **New York City public schools refused to enrol her as a child.** Her mother **Ilse** worked for
  years to get her in. She got in around fourth grade, into **Health Conservation 21**, a separate
  programme for disabled students. **NPS gives no stated reason for the refusal.** The much-repeated
  "fire hazard" reason is **not in this source and is not claimed.**
- **1970: the New York Board of Education refused her a teaching licence because she used a
  wheelchair.** The ACLU turned her down, saying a medical ground was not discrimination. The *New
  York Times* put her story on the front page in 1970; the lawyer **Roy Lucas** then offered to
  represent her. She sued at **twenty-two**. The judge was **Constance Baker Motley**. The case
  **settled out of court and she got the licence.** She then co-founded **Disabled in Action**.
- **[VERIFY] CLEARED.** The outline's line "she was refused a teaching licence and sued" is correct.
- She was **Assistant Secretary of the Office of Special Education and Rehabilitative Services,
  1993-2001**; adviser on disability and development at the **World Bank, 2001-2005**; and **Special
  Advisor on International Disability Rights, 2010-2017**. She died **4 March 2023, Washington D.C.**
- Verbatim: "Some people say that what I did changed the world. But really, I simply refused to
  accept what I was told about who I could be. And I was willing to make a fuss about it."
- **From the search summary, flagged and not claimed in the outline:** that she was "the first
  person in a wheelchair hired to teach in New York City."
- **A counting error inside the NPS 504 page:** it says "Ten simultaneous demonstrations" and then
  lists **eleven** cities. The outline lists the cities and gives no number.

### 9.11 Montgomery, 1955-56 — Parks, Colvin, Robinson, King
**Rosa Parks** — NPS, "Rosa Parks" (nps.gov/people/rosa-parks.htm):
- Born **4 February 1913, Tuskegee, Alabama.** Died **24 October 2005, Detroit, Michigan.**
- Married **Raymond Parks** in **1932**.
- **Secretary of the Montgomery NAACP, 1943-1956** — twelve years. She founded the Montgomery NAACP
  Youth Council in the early 1940s and later became secretary of the Alabama State Conference of the
  NAACP.
- **Arrested 1 December 1955.** NPS gives **no** driver's name, charge, trial detail or fine, and
  **none is claimed.**
- The boycott was "launched by 17,000 Black citizens" and ran **13 months**. "A Supreme Court ruling
  and declining revenues forced the city to desegregate its buses."
- **Afterwards: "Parks lost her tailoring job and received death threats."** She moved to **Detroit
  in 1957** and worked for Representative **John Conyers from 1965 to 1988**.
- **NPS carries nothing about Highlander Folk School and no quotation from her. Neither is claimed.**

**Claudette Colvin** — Encyclopedia of Alabama, "Colvin, Claudette":
- Born **5 September 1939, Birmingham, Alabama.** **Fifteen** and a student at **Booker T. Washington
  High School** in March 1955.
- **2 March 1955.** She boarded a Montgomery bus at **Bibb and Commerce Streets** and refused to give
  up her seat when white passengers boarded. She told the police "she had a constitutional right to
  sit anywhere on the bus, just as white passengers did."
- **What the police did, verbatim:** "as the officers swore at her and hit her with a nightstick,
  Colvin was dragged off the bus." She was charged with **disorderly conduct and violating the city's
  segregation laws.** The article does not say whether she was handcuffed or held in an adult jail,
  and **neither is claimed.**
- **Why her case was not used:** **E. D. Nixon** and others thought a "different 'face'" was needed.
  Nixon thought her **"too young"**, and when she became pregnant later that year he decided
  "someone more in line with the more conservative social mores of the time was needed." Colvin
  believed the decision had to do with her family's standing outside Montgomery's Black middle class.
- She was **one of the four plaintiffs in *Browder v. Gayle*, filed 1 February 1956**, with **Aurelia
  Browder, Susie McDonald and Mary Louise Smith**. Lawyer: **Fred Gray**.
- She moved to New York and worked as a nurse's aide for fifty years, returned to Alabama in 2004,
  and **her 1955 arrest record was expunged on 16 December 2022.**
- **NOT USED, flagged:** the fetched page reports a **death date of 13 January 2026**. That was not
  confirmed against any second source in this session, and the outline says nothing about her death.

**Jo Ann Robinson and the Women's Political Council** — Encyclopedia of Alabama, "Montgomery Bus
Boycott" and its Robinson material:
- The **Women's Political Council** of Montgomery, "an activist group of black professional women,"
  formed in **1949** and had been pressing white officials about the buses for six years before
  Parks's arrest.
- Robinson (**1912-1992**) was "perhaps the individual most instrumental in planning and publicizing
  the 1955 Montgomery Bus Boycott, proposing the idea more than a year before it was implemented."
- After Parks was arrested the council ran off **"50,000 flyers that called for a one-day bus boycott
  on Monday, December 5."**

**The boycott** — Encyclopedia of Alabama, "Montgomery Bus Boycott":
- **First day: 5 December 1955.** The **Montgomery Improvement Association** was formed the same day
  and "unexpectedly elected King as MIA president."
- Montgomery had **40,000 Black residents**. "Most walked to work or school, carpooled with friends,
  took taxis, or hitchhiked."
- "city leaders indicted **nearly 100** boycott leaders on conspiracy charges" — the article gives no
  date.
- **King's house was bombed** and **E. D. Nixon's home was also bombed, with little damage** — no
  dates given, and none are claimed.
- ***Browder v. Gayle***: district court ruled **June 1956**; the Supreme Court upheld it in
  **November 1956**. **Buses desegregated 21 December 1956.**
- **A one-day date conflict, stated not resolved:** the Encyclopedia of Alabama's *Browder v. Gayle*
  entry (via search summary) says the ruling "took effect December 20, 1956"; its Montgomery Bus
  Boycott article gives the desegregation date as **21 December 1956**. The outline uses 21 December
  for the buses and says the federal order reached Montgomery the day before.
- **The one-year figure:** NPS says 13 months; 5 December 1955 to 21 December 1956 is twelve and a
  half months. The outline gives the two dates and does not give a month count.

**Martin Luther King Jr.** — NPS, "Dr. Martin Luther King, Jr." (nps.gov/people/martinlutherkingjr.htm)
and NPS, "Frequently Asked Questions about Dr. Martin Luther King, Jr." (nps.gov/malu/faq_dr_martin_luther_king_jr.htm):
- **1929-1968**, born in **Atlanta, Georgia**. Morehouse College, Crozer Theological Seminary, Boston
  University. **NPS does not give his exact birth date on these pages; the outline gives the year
  only.**
- He led **Dexter Avenue Baptist Church** in Montgomery (now Dexter Avenue King Memorial Baptist
  Church) while finishing his doctorate at Boston University.
- **Nobel Peace Prize 1964, at age 35.**
- **Died 4 April 1968 at 7:05 PM at St. Joseph's Hospital, Memphis, Tennessee, aged 39.**
- **Age 26 in December 1955** — arithmetic from the 1929 birth year, stated as "twenty-six."

### 9.12 The March on Washington, 28 August 1963
NPS, "March on Washington" (nps.gov/articles/march-on-washington.htm):
- **28 August 1963.** **About 250,000 people** — the page gives 190,000 Black and 60,000 white.
- **A. Philip Randolph** thought of it in the 1940s and was its head; **Bayard Rustin** did the daily
  organizing. "The March was organized in less than 3 months."
- Sponsors, the "Big Six" and others named on the page: **Roy Wilkins** (NAACP), **Whitney Young**
  (National Urban League), **Martin Luther King Jr.** (SCLC), **James Farmer** (CORE), **John Lewis**
  (SNCC), plus **Walter Reuther, Joachim Prinz, Eugene Carson Blake and Matthew Ahmann.**
- The sponsors issued **"Ten Demands"** for fair living wages, fair employment policies and school
  desegregation.
- **No woman gave a formal speech.** **Dorothy Height** of the National Council of Negro Women helped
  plan it, "but she operated in the background of this male dominated, leadership group."
- **John Lewis**: "we do not want our freedom gradually but we want to be free now." His remarks were
  "toned down...at the request of white liberals and moderate black allies."
- **The page does not give the hour King spoke; it is not claimed.**

### 9.13 Bayard Rustin — fills the bayard-rustin candidate slot
NPS, "Bayard Rustin" (nps.gov/people/bayard-rustin.htm):
- Born **17 March 1912, West Chester, Pennsylvania.** Died **24 August 1987, New York City**, of a
  ruptured appendix, aged **75**.
- "Rustin was raised a Quaker and his family was engaged in civil rights activism." Wilberforce
  University, Cheyney State Teachers College, City College of New York.
- **1947, the Journey of Reconciliation**, organized with **George Houser** — "the first of the
  Freedom Rides," testing the Supreme Court's ban on race discrimination in interstate travel.
  Rustin "was arrested for violating state laws regarding segregated seating on public transportation
  and **served twenty-two days on a chain gang.**" A chain gang was prison labour: prisoners worked
  outdoors, usually on roads, chained at the ankles.
- He "served as a mentor to Martin Luther King, Jr. in the practice of nonviolent civil resistance"
  and worked with A. Philip Randolph. Randolph "appointed Rustin as Deputy Director and overall
  logistical planner" of the 1963 march.

**The 1953 conviction — from California's own primary documents**, both fetched as PDFs and read with
pdftotext: **Governor Gavin Newsom, Pardon of Bayard Rustin, 4 February 2020**
(gov.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/2020/02/Pardon-Certificate-Bayard-Rustin-02.04.2020.pdf) and
**Executive Order N-24-20, 4 February 2020**
(gov.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/2020/02/EO-N-24-20-Bayard-Rustin-02.04.2020.pdf):
- **"On January 22, 1953, the Los Angeles County Superior Court convicted 41-year-old Mr. Rustin of a
  misdemeanor violation of Penal Code section 647.5 (vagrancy). The court sentenced Mr. Rustin to 60
  days in jail and ordered him to register as a sex offender."**
- The executive order states the conviction was "for engaging in consensual sexual activity with
  other men."
- **"Mr. Rustin was sentenced pursuant to a charge commonly used to punish gay men for engaging in
  consensual adult sexual conduct. His conviction is part of a long and reprehensible history of
  criminal prohibitions on the very existence of LGBTQ people and their intimate associations and
  relationships."**
- **"an injustice that was compounded by his political opponents' use of the record of this case to
  try to undermine him, his associates, and the civil rights movement."**
- California's own account of what it did to LGBTQ people: "This discrimination has taken many forms
  including social isolation and shaming, surveillance, intimidation, physical violence, and unjust
  arrest and prosecution."
- The pardon also records: **"While in California in 1942, he visited Japanese Americans imprisoned in
  the Manzanar internment camp and reported on the camp's humanitarian conditions."**
- **"On August 8, 2013, President Barack Obama posthumously awarded Mr. Rustin the Presidential Medal
  of Freedom,"** describing him as **"an unyielding activist for civil rights, dignity, and equality
  for all."**
- In **2018 the California Legislature passed ACR 172**, apologizing "for the enactment of past
  discriminatory laws and constitutional provisions that resulted in the persecution and oppression of
  LGBT individuals and their communities, and which forced them to live in fear of unjust
  prosecutions and public shaming."
- **A source error, stated not repeated:** the pardon certificate calls Rustin **"a native of New
  York."** NPS gives his birthplace as **West Chester, Pennsylvania**, on 17 March 1912. The outline
  uses West Chester.
- **NOT SOURCED HERE and therefore not claimed:** the number of days Rustin actually served (search
  summaries say 50, the sentence was 60); who read the arrest record aloud and where; the arrest's
  location in Pasadena; and Rustin's partner's name.

### 9.14 Fannie Lou Hamer — fills the fannie-lou-hamer candidate slot
**Mississippi History Now** (Mississippi Department of Archives and History),
"Fannie Lou Hamer: Civil Rights Activist," April 2007
(mshistorynow.mdah.ms.gov/issue/fannie-lou-hamer-civil-rights-activist):
- Born **6 October 1917, Montgomery County, Mississippi**, the **youngest of twenty children** of
  **Ella and James Lee Townsend**.
- **Started work at six**, weeding and then picking cotton.
- Married **Perry "Pap" Hamer in 1944**.
- Worked on the **W. D. Marlow plantation outside Ruleville** as **timekeeper for eighteen years.**
- **1961: "While hospitalized, she was sterilized without her knowledge or consent."**
- **31 August 1962:** she led **seventeen** people to the Sunflower County courthouse in **Indianola**
  to register. **She lost her job on the Marlow plantation that same day.**
- **June 1963, Winona:** arrested and beaten by police on a bus trip home from citizenship training.
  Her own words: **"After I got out of jail, half dead, I found that Medgar Evers had been shot down
  in his own yard."**
- **1964:** vice chair of the Mississippi Freedom Democratic Party delegation that challenged the
  all-white Mississippi delegation at the Democratic National Convention in Atlantic City.
- **1969: Freedom Farm**, to give local families food and a living.
- **Died 14 March 1977**, of heart failure brought on by cancer, diabetes and high blood pressure.

**National Archives, "NARA Black History Basic Training — Feb 1: Fannie Lou Hamer"** (PDF, read with
pdftotext, rediscovering-black-history.blogs.archives.gov):
- Born 6 October 1917 in Mississippi; first tried to register in 1962 and was fired from her
  plantation job for it; co-founded the Mississippi Freedom Democratic Party in 1964.
- **"In her nationally televised testimony before the Democratic National Convention, Hamer shared
  her personal stories of mistreatment and terror at the hands of racists throughout MS. The
  testimony was interrupted by President Lyndon Johnson, in an effort to suppress the truth and
  stabilize the Democratic Party."**
- Her words, as NARA gives them: **"Nobody's free until everybody's free."**

**Her testimony to the Credentials Committee, Democratic National Convention, Atlantic City, 22
August 1964.** Transcript at crmvet.org/docs/flh_ac.htm (Civil Rights Movement Archive) and
americanradioworks.publicradio.org/features/sayitplain/flhamer.html (American RadioWorks / American
Public Media). Both were fetched; the passages below agree between them.
- She gives herself as **"Mrs. Fanny Lou Hamer," of "626 East Lafayette Street, Ruleville,
  Mississippi, Sunflower County."**
- **31 August 1962:** she and **seventeen others** went **twenty-six miles** to the courthouse in
  **Indianola**. Police limited who could go in; **only two were allowed to take the literacy test.**
- The plantation owner: **"If you don't go down and withdraw your registration, you will have to
  leave."** Her reply: **"I didn't try to register for you. I tried to register for myself."**
- **10 September 1962: sixteen bullets were fired into the Tucker home**, and other houses were shot
  at the same night.
- **9 June 1963:** returning from a voter registration workshop, ten of them travelling by
  Continental Trailways bus, they stopped at **Winona, Montgomery County, Mississippi.** Four went to
  the washroom and two tried to use the restaurant. She was arrested.
- **The beating, from the testimony:** the **State Highway Patrolmen ordered the first Negro prisoner
  to take the blackjack**; she was ordered to **"lay down on a bunk bed on my face"**; the first
  prisoner beat her until he was exhausted; **the State Highway Patrolman then ordered the second
  Negro prisoner to take the blackjack**, and made the first sit on her feet so she could not move
  them. **"I began to scream and one white man got up and began to beat me in my head and tell me to
  hush."** A blackjack is a short club, usually leather with lead or shot inside it, made to knock a
  person down.
- Her close: **"All of this is on account of we want to register, to become first-class citizens.
  And if the Freedom Democratic Party is not seated now, I question America. Is this America, the
  land of the free and the home of the brave, where we have to sleep with our telephones off the
  hooks because our lives be threatened daily, because we want to live as decent human beings, in
  America?"**
- **Injuries.** The archives.gov search summary says the beating left "permanent damage to her eye,
  legs, and kidneys." **That is a search summary, not a fetched page, and the outline does not list
  the organs.** It says she was beaten with a blackjack by two prisoners under a patrolman's orders
  and never fully recovered — which the MDAH page supports with her own "half dead."

**On the 1961 sterilization.** MDAH states plainly that she "was sterilized without her knowledge or
consent" while in hospital in 1961. **PMC-hosted medical literature** (pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov) states
the operation was a **hysterectomy** — the surgical removal of the womb — and that Hamer's own name
for such operations, **"Mississippi appendectomy,"** entered use for them. **What is NOT settled:**
what she had gone into hospital for, whether she was ever told, and the surgeon's name. **None of
that is claimed.** Under the clinical-word rule the outline says what a hysterectomy is (the womb,
the organ a baby grows in, cut out), what it did to her (she could never become pregnant again, and
this cannot be undone), and that she was not asked.

### 9.15 The sit-ins, 1960
NPS, "North Carolina: F.W. Woolworth Building"
(nps.gov/places/north-carolina-fw-woolworth-building.htm):
- **1 February 1960.** "four freshmen from the Agricultural and Technical College of North
  Carolina...took vacant seats at the store's 'whites-only' lunch counter." They "sat down, ordered
  coffee and waited."
- Named on the page: **Franklin McCain, Joseph McNeil, David Richmond and Ezell Blair, Jr.** (Blair
  later took the name Jibreel Khazan — from a search summary, not claimed in the outline.)
- The waitress and the store manager ignored them, a policeman walked up and down nearby, and some
  white customers taunted them while others encouraged them.
- **Day two: 19 supporters. Day three: 85, from several colleges. "Before the week was out, there
  were 400."** They took it in shifts so they could still go to class. Local officials asked for a
  two-week pause.
- Sit-ins started in **seven other North Carolina cities**, plus **Hampton, Virginia** and
  **Nashville, Tennessee**.
- **"By summer, 33 southern cities, including Greensboro, had integrated their restaurants and lunch
  counters. One year later, 126 cities had taken the same step."**
- **The NPS page does not give the date the Greensboro counter was desegregated.** A search summary
  gives 25 July 1960; **not claimed.**

### 9.16 The Freedom Rides, 1961
- **4 May 1961: thirteen Black and white riders, including CORE's national director James Farmer,
  left Washington, D.C. by bus for New Orleans**, to test *Boynton v. Virginia* (1960), which had
  held that segregation in bus and railway terminals serving interstate passengers was illegal.
  — from an NPS/LOC **search summary**; flagged.
- **14 May 1961, Anniston, Alabama.** NPS, "Greyhound Bus Burning Site"
  (nps.gov/places/greyhound-bus-burn-site.htm): the mob **"firebombed the bus"**; the riders were
  **"Trapped on the burning bus"** and **"emerged after an explosion"**, and then **"the mob beat
  them."** **Reverend Fred Shuttlesworth** organized a convoy that got them to Birmingham within a
  few hours. The page gives **no mob size and no injury detail**, and none is claimed.
- From the same search summary (NPS event listing, flagged, used in the outline only for the
  sequence of what the mob did): a white mob of about **150 to 200** met the bus at the Anniston
  Greyhound station and hit it with bats and pipes; the mob followed the bus out of town, slashed its
  tyres, forced it off the road, broke the windows with chains, sticks and iron rods, held the exit
  door shut, and threw a firebomb inside; the back of the bus exploded when the fire reached the fuel
  tank. **The outline states only what the fetched NPS page states, plus the firebomb through the
  window and the door being held, which the outline attributes as "reported."** *If a later agent can
  fetch the NPS event page directly, this can be tightened.*

### 9.17 Birmingham, 1963 — the children's marches and the church bombing
From NPS search summaries of nps.gov pages on Kelly Ingram Park and the Birmingham Civil Rights
National Monument (flagged as summaries):
- **2 May 1963:** students **aged eight to eighteen** gathered at the **16th Street Baptist Church**
  and marched downtown to talk to the mayor about segregation. The marches are called **D-Day** or
  the **Children's Crusade**.
- Under orders from Public Safety Commissioner **Bull Connor**, Birmingham police **arrested 600
  child picketers on 2 May, some as young as six.** **By the next day 1,000 children had been
  jailed.**
- **"Firemen blasted the protestors, including children, with high-pressure fire hoses, and police
  used their nightsticks indiscriminately. Police K-9 units loosed their dogs into crowds of peaceful
  demonstrators, pinning down one woman and severely wounding three teenagers."**
- **What a fire hose does to a child is not stated by NPS and is therefore not described in the
  outline beyond what the source says** — high pressure, knocking people off their feet. No invented
  physiology.

NPS, "16th Street Baptist Church Bombing (1963)" (nps.gov/articles/16thstreetbaptist.htm) and NPS,
"Four Little Girls" (nps.gov/articles/fourlittlegirls.htm), both fetched:
- **15 September 1963, "just before 11 o'clock"**, a bomb exploded **under the steps of the church**.
- **The four girls killed, ages from the "Four Little Girls" page:** **Addie Mae Collins, 14 ·
  Denise McNair, 11 · Carole Robertson, 14 · Cynthia Wesley, 14.**
- **A FETCH ERROR TO RECORD:** an earlier fetch of the bombing page returned "Denise McNair, age 14"
  and "Cynthia Wesley, age 11." The "Four Little Girls" page gives McNair as **11**. The outline uses
  the "Four Little Girls" ages. Anyone re-checking should read that page, not the summary.
- **"Addie's sister Sarah survived, but lost her right eye."** Seventeen others were injured (from
  the search summary; the fetched page gives no count).
- The girls **"had been dressed in their Sunday best, ready to sing and act as ushers at the adult
  service, and only a few minutes before the explosion they had been together in the basement women's
  room, talking about the new school year."**
- The church **"served as headquarters for civil rights mass meetings and rallies"** and **"became
  the departure point for many of the demonstrations"** — including the children's marches.
- **Convictions: Robert Chambliss, 14 November 1977 · Thomas Blanton, 1 May 2001 · Bobby Frank
  Cherry, 2002 · Herman Cash died in 1994 and was never prosecuted.** Fourteen years passed before
  the first conviction and thirty-nine before the last.

### 9.18 The organisers who were murdered
**Medgar Evers.** NPS, "Medgar Evers" (nps.gov/people/medgar-evers.htm) and NPS, "Assassination"
(home.nps.gov/memy/learn/historyculture/assassination.htm), plus the Mississippi Department of
Archives and History timeline:
- Born **2 July 1925, Decatur, Mississippi.** Served in **Germany and France in the Second World
  War**. **NAACP field secretary for Mississippi from 1954** until his death. He "tirelessly led
  marches, prayer vigils, voter registration drives and boycotts," and worked to get **James Meredith
  into the University of Mississippi in 1962.**
- He was shot **in the back** in the driveway of his own home in Jackson. **Myrlie Evers and the
  children "had waited up and were watching TV together"**; the children **"immediately responded as
  they had practiced — they fell to the ground and made their way to the bathroom."** Myrlie found
  him **"collapsed and bleeding, clutching t-shirts he had carried from the car."** He **"died at
  University Hospital from trauma and blood loss."**
- **DATE CONFLICT, stated not resolved.** MDAH's timeline says **12 June 1963**. NPS's assassination
  page as fetched says **"June 11, 1963, just after midnight"** — the same night, counted differently.
  A fetch of the NPS "Medgar Evers" people page returned **"July 12, 1963"**, which is wrong by a
  month and does not match NPS's own "31 years and three trials later ... 1994." **The outline says
  "in the early hours of 12 June 1963" and follows MDAH.**
- Killer: **Byron De La Beckwith.** NPS: **"In 1994 - 31 years and three trials later - Evers' killer,
  Byron De La Beckwith, was convicted and sentenced to life in prison."** The two earlier trials were
  in 1964; **NPS gives no dates for them and none are claimed.**
- MDAH (search summary): more than **four thousand people** came to his funeral at the Masonic Temple
  on Lynch Street in Jackson on **15 June 1963**; the NAACP gave him its **Spingarn Medal** on **4
  July 1963**.

**James Chaney, Andrew Goodman and Michael Schwerner.** National Archives, *Rediscovering Black
History* blog, "Three Civil Rights Workers" (12 August 2014), fetched:
- **James Chaney, 21**, a Black Mississippi college student. **Andrew Goodman, 20**, and **Michael
  Schwerner, 24**, both white New Yorkers with CORE.
- **Freedom Summer**, also called the Mississippi Summer Project: Freedom Schools, registering Black
  voters, and building the Mississippi Freedom Democratic Party.
- On **21 June 1964** the three **"investigated the burning of a Black church, where a civil rights
  rally took place days earlier."** They were **"arrested and placed in jail for 'speeding' by the
  local police"** and **"released after dark into the hands of the Ku Klux Klan."**
- **4 August 1964:** they were **"found near Old Jolly Farm in Neshoba County, Mississippi."**
- The autopsies: **"Goodman and Schwerner were both shot in the head with a single bullet, while
  Chaney suffered two broken arms, trauma to the groin area, a broken jaw, and a crush right
  shoulder."**
- **Seven of twenty-one arrested** were charged with violating the three men's civil rights. **Edgar
  Ray Killen was convicted of manslaughter in 2005 and sentenced to sixty years** in the Mississippi
  State Penitentiary.
- From the FBI's own case page via search summary (fbi.gov returns **403** to direct fetch): the
  arrest was by **Neshoba County Deputy Sheriff Cecil Price at 5 p.m.**, they were released about
  **10:30 p.m.** and followed by Klan members "by preordained plan"; the burned station wagon gave
  the case its FBI name, **MIBURN**, for Mississippi Burning; **Killen's conviction came on 21 June
  2005, the forty-first anniversary.** Flagged as a summary; the outline uses the arrest and release
  times and the case name and attributes them to the FBI.

### 9.19 Selma, 1965
NPS, Selma to Montgomery National Historic Trail (nps.gov/semo/learn/historyculture/index.htm):
- **Jimmie Lee Jackson** was **"shot in the abdomen while protecting his mother and grandfather"**
  during a protest at **Marion, Alabama on 18 February 1965**, and **died on 26 February 1965.**
- **Bloody Sunday, 7 March 1965:** about **600 marchers**, most of them Black, left **Brown Chapel**.
  At the **Edmund Pettus Bridge**, **Alabama State Trooper Major John Cloud** gave them two minutes
  to leave, and then they were **"attacked by the law enforcement officers with nightsticks and
  teargas."** **"at least 50 protestors required hospital treatment."**
- **9 March 1965: the "Ministers March," about 2,000 people, led by Dr. King** — "Turnaround
  Tuesday." **Rev. James Reeb** was attacked by white men that evening and **died on 11 March 1965.**
- **21 March 1965: more than 8,000 protesters** set out. **25 March:** the crowd reaching the state
  capitol in Montgomery **"had grown to nearly 25,000."**
- **The Voting Rights Act was signed on 6 August 1965.**
- **Viola Liuzzo is not on this page and is not claimed.**

### 9.20 The Voting Rights Act, 6 August 1965
archives.gov/milestone-documents/voting-rights-act:
- **Signed 6 August 1965 by President Lyndon Johnson.**
- It **outlawed literacy tests and suspended their use in covered jurisdictions**; provided for
  **federal examiners with power to register qualified citizens**; and under **Section 5** required
  covered places to get **"preclearance"** from the District Court for the District of Columbia or
  the U.S. Attorney General before changing any voting practice. **Section 2** applied "a nationwide
  prohibition of the denial or abridgment of the right to vote on account of race or color."
- **Coverage formula:** states or subdivisions that had a literacy test on **1 November 1964** AND
  where **fewer than 50 percent** of voting-age residents were registered or voted in the 1964
  presidential election.
- **The Fifteenth Amendment was ratified in 1870** and "prohibits denial or abridgment of voting
  rights based on race or color."
- **By the end of 1965 a quarter of a million new Black voters were registered, a third of them by
  federal examiners. By the end of 1966 only four of thirteen Southern states still had fewer than
  50 percent of African Americans registered.**
- **Preclearance is what the Supreme Court gutted in *Shelby County v. Holder* (2013) — era 10's.**

---

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): Viola Liuzzo, 25 March 1965, the killers named and what happened to them
- NPS, "Viola Liuzzo" (https://www.nps.gov/people/viola-liuzzo.htm , fetched): born **11 April 1925, California, Pennsylvania**. A **39-year-old mother of five** from Detroit, a Unitarian Universalist, active in the Detroit NAACP. Arrived in Selma **8 March 1965**. Marched across the Pettus Bridge on **21 March**, worked the registration desk at Brown Chapel, drove airport shuttle runs, worked at the first-aid station at the campsite, and walked the last four miles to the capitol barefoot. Killed **25 March 1965** while driving between Selma and Montgomery (Lowndes County) by Ku Klux Klan members **"Collie Leroy Wilkins, Jr., William Orville Eaton, and Eugene Thomas."** Her passenger **Leroy Moton** "escaped relatively uninjured." Buried Holy Sepulchre Cemetery, Southfield, Michigan. The NPS page says nothing about trials.
- Encyclopedia of Alabama, "Liuzzo, Viola Gregg" (https://encyclopediaofalabama.org/article/viola-gregg-liuzzo/ , fetched): killed at **7:37 p.m.** on Highway 80, about 20 miles outside Selma; Klansmen fired into her car and she "was killed instantly." (The page as fetched also places the start of the chase "at a traffic light in front of the Edmund Pettus Bridge." The outline says only that they chased and shot her on Highway 80.) Moton was **19**, a local Black activist. Indicted **6 April 1965**: **Collie LeRoy Wilkins (21), William Orville Eaton (41), Eugene Thomas (42) and Gary Thomas Rowe (42)**. Rowe was a paid FBI informant who was in the car. **State trials:** Wilkins's first trial at Hayneville, **3 May 1965**, ended in a hung jury. Later state trials acquitted Wilkins, Eaton and Thomas. **Federal trial:** federal juries convicted the three of violating Liuzzo's civil rights and sentenced each to **10 years**. **Eaton died in March 1966** before serving. Rowe got full immunity and the federal witness protection program. **J. Edgar Hoover** called her an "outside agitator," questioned her husband's background, suggested she and Moton had a "romantic interlude," and leaked speculation to the press portraying her as "an unstable woman." Her family sued the FBI in **1979**. Judge **Charles W. Joiner** dismissed the suit on **27 May 1983**, finding "no evidence of an FBI conspiracy."
- Search summary (Encyclopedia.com and the Michigan Advance, not opened): the federal conviction came on **3 December 1965** for conspiring to deprive her of her civil rights, the maximum sentence, and Thomas and Wilkins each served about five years (unconfirmed: search summary only). The outline gives no federal verdict date and no time served.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): Selma. Who shot Jimmie Lee Jackson and who killed James Reeb
- **Jimmie Lee Jackson.** PBS *Frontline*, "Unresolved: Jimmie Lee Jackson" (https://www.pbs.org/wgbh/frontline/interactive/unresolved/cases/jimmie-lee-jackson/ , fetched): Jackson was **26**. Shot **18 February 1965** in Marion, Alabama, **inside a cafe** during a voting rights march, **in the stomach**, by **Alabama State Trooper James Bonard Fowler**. Died **26 February 1965**, eight days later, of infection from the wound. Fowler was indicted in **May 2007** for first- and second-degree murder, pleaded guilty in **2010** to **misdemeanor manslaughter**, and was sentenced to **six months**. He served five. A search summary (CNN, 15 November 2010, would not open: 451) gives the plea date as 15 November 2010 and Fowler's age as 77 (unconfirmed: search summary only).
- The outline's "a young man named Jimmie Lee Jackson" is corrected by this PATCH: he was 26. NPS (bank 9.19) says he was shot "while protecting his mother and grandfather." Both are true together.
