- **James Reeb.** US Department of Justice, Civil Rights Division, "James Reeb, Notice to Close File" (https://www.justice.gov/crt/case-document/james-reeb-notice-close-file , fetched): on **9 March 1965** in Selma, Reeb, a minister from Boston, left a restaurant with **Orloff Miller** of Boston and **Clark Olsen** of Berkeley, California. Four men attacked them, and "One of the four attackers struck Reeb in the head with a club." He died in a Birmingham hospital two days later. Police arrested **Elmer L. Cook**, a novelty store manager, **William Stanley Hoggle**, a salesman, **Namon O'Neil Hoggle**, an auto mechanic, and **R. B. Kelley**. Three were tried. The trial "began on December 7, 1965 and ended three days later with acquittals for all three men on December 10, 1965." Solicitor McLeod "permitted a man whose brother was a suspect in the attack and another man, a well-known racist, to sit as jurors." DOJ closed the file on **20 May 2011**: the statute of limitations had run, key suspects were dead, and the surviving defendant had been acquitted.
- Search summary (NPR, 2019, not opened): a fourth attacker, **William Portwood**, was identified in 2019 (unconfirmed: search summary only). Not used in the outline.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): Medgar Evers's killer, the trials, and his death in prison
- Mississippi Department of Archives and History, "Byron De La Beckwith Papers (Z/2306)" finding aid (https://finding.mdah.ms.gov/manuscripts/z2306000 , fetched): Beckwith was born in Colusa, California, on **9 November 1920**. He joined the **White Citizens' Council in 1954** and became involved with the Ku Klux Klan in the late 1960s. He was tried twice for Evers's murder in **1964**, "both trials ending in hung juries." "On February 5, 1994, Beckwith was convicted and sentenced to life in prison." He died **21 January 2001**, aged 80, at the University of Mississippi Medical Center.
- A search summary (Biography.com, CRDL) says the two 1964 juries were **all white**, and that the 1994 jury had eight Black members (unconfirmed: search summary only). The outline says "two juries" without their make-up.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): Neshoba County. The 1967 federal verdict, who was convicted, and Killen's death
- ABA Journal, "Oct. 20, 1967: A jury rules against the KKK" (https://www.abajournal.com/magazine/article/united_states_price_mississippi_burning , fetched): on **20 October 1967** a federal jury convicted **seven of 18** defendants, among them **Cecil Price**, the Neshoba County deputy sheriff, and **Samuel Bowers**, imperial wizard of the White Knights of the Ku Klux Klan of Mississippi. Sentences ran from **three to ten years**. Judge **William Harold Cox** presided. In **March 1966** the Supreme Court had unanimously reversed Cox's dismissal of the charges. **Edgar Ray Killen** went free when one juror would not convict a preacher. The three men "were stopped again, shoved into a sheriff's vehicle, carried to a desolated country road and shot dead."
- Search summary (JURIST famous trials, Wikipedia "Cecil Price," not opened): Bowers got 10 years and Price 6, and **Alton Wayne Roberts** fired the shots (unconfirmed: search summary only). The outline names Price and Bowers and the range of sentences only.
- Southern Poverty Law Center, Hatewatch, 12 January 2018 (https://www.splcenter.org/hatewatch/2018/01/12/killer-ex-klansman-edgar-ray-killen-dies-prison-92 , fetched): Killen, a Klan leader and former preacher, organized the killings. The 1967 case against him ended in a hung jury. He was convicted of manslaughter on **21 June 2005**, 41 years to the day, and sentenced on **23 June 2005** to six decades. He died in the prison hospital at the **Mississippi State Penitentiary at Parchman** at 9 p.m. on **11 January 2018**, aged **92** (a search summary gives 93: record both).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): the Birmingham church bombing. Who the bombers were and what happened to each
- EBSCO Research Starters, "16th Street Baptist Church bombing" (https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/history/16th-street-baptist-church-bombing , fetched): the bombers were members of the Ku Klux Klan. The FBI identified **Robert Chambliss** as the main suspect, but FBI Director **J. Edgar Hoover** closed the investigation in **1968** "without charging anyone, while also ordering that all evidence be sealed." The case was revived after Hoover's death in 1972. **Chambliss** was convicted in 1977 and **died in prison in 1985**. **Thomas Blanton** (2001) and **Bobby Frank Cherry** (2002) were convicted of four counts of murder, using secret 1964 FBI recordings. **Herman Cash** died before prosecution.
- Wikipedia, "Thomas Edwin Blanton Jr." (fetched): convicted May 2001, **four life sentences**, died **26 June 2020** at the William E. Donaldson Correctional Facility, Bessemer, Alabama, aged 82. (The same article says Cherry was convicted "alongside Blanton in May 2001." That is wrong: Cherry's trial was 2002, per NPS in bank 9.17 and EBSCO. Not repeated.)
- Search summary (Wikipedia "Bobby Frank Cherry," not opened): Cherry got life and died **18 November 2004**, aged 74 (unconfirmed: search summary only).
- **The NPS arrest counts disagree, both fetched this session.** NPS "Kelly Ingram Park" (https://www.nps.gov/places/kelly-ingram-park.htm): "On May 2, under orders from Public Safety Commissioner Bull Connor, police arrested 600 child picketers (some as young as six years of age); by the next day, 1,000 children had been jailed." NPS "Theophilus Eugene 'Bull' Connor" (https://www.nps.gov/people/bull-connor.htm): on 2 May more than 1,000 students gathered and "By the end of the day 1,200 children had been arrested"; on 3 May Connor ordered "the use of fire hoses and attack dogs to disperse the marchers, eventually incarcerating over 3,000 demonstrators." **This PATCH upgrades bank 9.17's hose-and-dog quotation from search summary to fetched (Kelly Ingram Park page).** The outline gives both NPS figures.
- NPS "Bull Connor" also states: "In 1961, during the Freedom Rides, Connor ordered Birmingham police to stay away from the bus station while Klansmen attacked the arriving buses and Freedom Riders." Connor died of a stroke on 10 March 1973.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): Anniston, 14 May 1961, now checked on NPS pages
- NPS, "Alabama: Freedom Riders National Monument" (https://www.nps.gov/places/freedom-riders-national-monument.htm , fetched): the Greyhound bus left Atlanta on **Sunday 14 May** and at the Anniston station "a segregationist mob, including members of the Ku Klux Klan, violently attacked the Freedom Riders."
- NPS, "Greyhound Bus Depot" (https://www.nps.gov/places/greyhound-bus-depot.htm , fetched): "a white mob ambushed the bus," "slashed tires," and followed it, "firebombing the bus."
- NPS, "Anniston Trailways Station" (https://www.nps.gov/places/anniston-trailways-station.htm , fetched): the same day, on the second bus, "a group of young white men came aboard to enforce segregated seating ... The men beat the Riders, forcing them to segregate."
- **Still from search summary only:** the mob size of 150 to 200, the chains and iron rods, and the door held shut (bank 9.16). No fetched NPS page gives arrests or convictions for Anniston. **SEARCHED, NOT FOUND** is not declared: the question was not pursued past NPS.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): Fannie Lou Hamer's jailers, the federal trial, and the injuries
- SNCC Digital Gateway, "Beatings in Winona Jail" (https://snccdigital.org/events/beatings-winona-jail/ , fetched): beaten in the Winona jail in June 1963: **June Johnson (15), Annell Ponder, Fannie Lou Hamer, Euvester Simpson, James West, Rosemary Freeman**. June Johnson's "dress was soaked with blood." Ponder was beaten with "blackjacks, a belt, fists, and open palms." In **December 1963** in **Oxford, Mississippi**, the Justice Department tried the Winona police chief and the Montgomery County sheriff and others on seven counts of conspiring to deprive the activists of their civil rights. An all-white local jury found them **not guilty on all counts**.
- Fannie Lou Hamer's America, "Montgomery County Jail" (https://www.fannielouhamersamerica.com/driving-tour/montgomery-county-jail , fetched): five white Winona law officers; Hamer, Ponder and Johnson were beaten by **two Black prisoners, Roosevelt Knox and Sol Poe**, "forced by the law enforcement officials." "The five men were tried and found not guilty by an all-male and all-white federal jury."
- Equal Justice Initiative (https://eji.org/news/remembering-1963-fannie-lou-hamer-arrested-and-beaten-winona-mississippi/ , fetched): Winona Police Chief **Thomas Herrod** ordered the arrests. Hamer "lost vision in one of her eyes and suffered permanent kidney damage, which contributed to her death in 1977 at age 59." **This settles the injuries left open in bank 9.14 (eye, kidneys) from a fetched page.**
- Search summary (University Press of Mississippi; Fannie Lou Hamer's America timeline, not opened): the defendants were **William Surrell, John L. Basinger, Earle Wayne Patridge, Thomas J. Herrod Jr. and Charles Thomas Perkins**, and Hamer testified in Oxford on **2 December 1963** (unconfirmed: search summary only). The outline says five officers, names the chief, and does not list the other four.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): the gay rights movement, 1953 to 1979, and Stonewall (the chapter's open gap)
- **The federal firings.** Executive Order 10450, signed by President Eisenhower on **27 April 1953** (National Archives, Federal Register codification, https://www.archives.gov/federal-register/codification/executive-order/10450.html , fetched). Section 8(a)(1)(iii) lists as grounds for removal "Any criminal, infamous, dishonest, immoral, or notoriously disgraceful conduct, habitual use of intoxicants to excess, drug addiction, sexual perversion." TIME, "History You Didn't Learn About the Anti-Gay Lavender Scare" (https://time.com/5922679/lavender-scare-history/ , fetched): historian **David K. Johnson** estimates "somewhere between 5,000 and 10,000 people lost their jobs." **Frank Kameny** was fired from the Army Map Service in **1957** and fought it as a civil rights case. On **21 May 1965** protesters picketed the White House against the firings. In **1975** the Civil Service Commission announced rules against firing federal workers for being gay. In **1995** President Clinton signed an order barring the same discrimination in security clearances.
- Search summary (FIRE, NBC, Wikipedia "Frank Kameny," not opened): Kameny held a Harvard doctorate in astronomy, was a World War II combat veteran, petitioned the Supreme Court himself in 1961, and received a government apology in 2009 (unconfirmed: search summary only).
- **Stonewall.** NPS, Stonewall National Monument, "History & Culture" (https://www.nps.gov/ston/learn/historyculture.htm , fetched): "In the early hours of June 28, 1969, a police raid on the Stonewall Inn provoked a spontaneous act of resistance." In New York then, "Serving alcoholic beverages to homosexuals was prohibited"; people "were arrested for wearing fewer than three articles of clothing that matched their sex"; people arrested in raids were "photographed and humiliated." Police "were often paid off in return for information about planned raids." When the crowd formed, police retreated and barricaded themselves inside the bar. The crowd threw "pennies, metal garbage cans, bricks, bottles, an uprooted parking meter, and burning trash" and chanted "Gay Power!" The NYPD's **Tactical Patrol Force**, "trained for riot control," came in. "Before Stonewall...fifty to sixty gay groups in the country. A year later there were at least fifteen hundred. By two years later...twenty-five hundred."
- NYC LGBT Historic Sites Project, "Stonewall Inn" (https://www.nyclgbtsites.org/site/stonewall-inn-christopher-park/ , fetched): the raid was led by NYPD **Deputy Inspector Seymour Pine**. The bar was opened in **1967** by the Mafia member **"Fat Tony" Lauria** as a "private" gay club. The uprising ran **six nights, 28 June to 3 July 1969**. Arrests: **13 the first night, 3 the second, 5 the sixth.** Eyewitness estimates of the crowd: "five to six hundred" the first night, "about two thousand" the second, "five hundred to a thousand" the last.
- NPS, "Stonewall National Monument: Rising for Equality" (https://www.nps.gov/articles/stonewall-national-monument-rising-for-equality.htm , fetched): the crowd "included many people of color." President Obama designated the Stonewall National Monument on **24 June 2016** (search summary of the NPS site: unconfirmed).
- **The first march.** Search summary (EBSCO, NYC LGBT Sites, Wikipedia "Christopher Street Day," not opened): the Christopher Street Liberation Day march, **28 June 1970**, the first anniversary, from Sheridan Square to Central Park, organized in part by **Craig Rodwell**, with marches in Chicago and San Francisco on 27 June 1970. Crowd estimates vary (unconfirmed: search summary only). The outline gives the date and says marches were held in several cities, with no count.
- **Named people present at Stonewall** (Marsha P. Johnson, Sylvia Rivera, Stormé DeLarverie): **not on any page fetched.** Not claimed.
- **Harvey Milk.** EBSCO Research Starters, "White Murders Politicians Moscone and Milk" (https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/social-sciences-and-humanities/white-murders-politicians-moscone-and-milk/ , fetched): on **27 November 1978** Dan White, a former San Francisco supervisor, shot Mayor **George Moscone** and Supervisor **Harvey Milk** at City Hall. He shot Milk "five times." Charged with first-degree murder, White was convicted of **voluntary manslaughter** and sentenced to **seven years and eight months**. He served about five. White's lawyer argued that sugary food had left him unable to be held fully responsible. The riot at City Hall after the verdict (the White Night) did "more than $1 million in damage." White killed himself on **21 October 1985**. EBSCO calls Milk "one of the first openly gay elected officials in the United States." **Date conflict:** EBSCO gives the verdict as **22 May 1979**. Search summaries (Wikipedia, History.com) give **21 May 1979** for the verdict and the riot. The outline gives "May 1979."
- **AIDS and ACT UP** are told in `health` (its outline line 578). Not repeated here.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): the women's movement, NOW and the Equal Rights Amendment, 1966 to 1982
- **NOW.** *Ms.* magazine, "Today in Feminist History: The National Organization for Women (June 30, 1966)," David M. Dismore (https://msmagazine.com/2020/06/30/feminist-history-july-30/ , fetched with curl): 28 people founded NOW on **30 June 1966** in Washington, D.C., at the Third National Conference of the Commissions on the Status of Women. Delegates wanted a resolution demanding that the **Equal Employment Opportunity Commission** enforce the ban on sex discrimination at work. They were told "government commissions cannot take action against other government departments," and the motion was ruled "out of order." They met in **Betty Friedan**'s hotel room that night. The purpose: "To take action to bring women into full participation in the mainstream of American society NOW, assuming all the privileges and responsibilities thereof in truly equal partnership with men." Search summary (NOW's own site, 403): Friedan was the first president, and **Pauli Murray** co-wrote the Statement of Purpose (unconfirmed: search summary only).
- **The ERA through Congress.** US Senate Historical Office, "The Senate Passes the Equal Rights Amendment" (https://www.senate.gov/artandhistory/history/minute/Senate_passes_ERA.htm , fetched): text, "Equality of rights under the law shall not be denied or abridged by the United States or by any State on account of sex." The Senate passed it on **22 March 1972, 84 to 8**. **Alice Paul** wrote the amendment (the page says 1922, first introduced 1923). Representative **Martha Griffiths** of Michigan pushed it through the House in **1971**, with a seven-year deadline. Senator **Sam Ervin** of North Carolina led the objections. **Hawaii** ratified first, in 1972. When the deadline ran out in **1982** it was three states short. Nevada (2017) and Illinois (2018) became the 36th and 37th.
- **Ratification and the reversal.** Brennan Center, "The Equal Rights Amendment, Explained" (https://www.brennancenter.org/our-work/research-reports/equal-rights-amendment-explained , fetched): **35 states by 1977**. Congress extended the deadline by three years and no more states ratified. Lawmakers in **Nebraska, Tennessee, Idaho, Kentucky and South Dakota** voted to take back their ratifications. Virginia became the 38th state in January 2020 (era 10's). Search summary (Wikipedia, CRS): the extension ran to **30 June 1982** (unconfirmed: search summary only; the Senate page's "1982" is fetched).
- **The opposition.** National Women's History Museum, "Phyllis Schlafly" (https://www.womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/phyllis-schlafly , fetched): Schlafly (15 August 1924 to 5 September 2016) led the STOP ERA campaign, "STOP" standing for "Stop Taking Our Privileges." She argued the amendment would end women's exemption from the military draft, "dependent wife" Social Security benefits for widows, and mothers' preference in custody cases. The page dates the Eagle Forum to 1972. A search summary (EBSCO) says STOP ERA was founded in 1972 and renamed the Eagle Forum in 1975 (unconfirmed: search summary only). The outline says she founded STOP ERA in 1972.
- **Credit.** The Equal Credit Opportunity Act, October 1974, barred lenders from refusing credit because of sex or marital status, and the March 1976 amendments added race, color, religion, national origin, age and public assistance (CFPB blog, parked from `marketplace`, full text in `research/research-marketplace.md`, T-253 PATCH). Used in one outline sentence with that citation.
- **What is era 10's:** Virginia 2020 and everything after.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): the Chicano movement, as a rights campaign (farmworkers stay with `work-workers`)
- ***Hernandez v. Texas*, 347 U.S. 475**, decided **3 May 1954**, unanimous, Chief Justice Earl Warren (Cornell LII, https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/347/475 , fetched): in Jackson County, Texas, about 14 percent of the people had Mexican or Latin American surnames, and "for the last twenty-five years there is no record of any person with a Mexican or Latin American name having served on a jury commission." More than 6,000 jurors had been called in that time with none from that group. The courthouse had one men's toilet marked "Colored Men" and "Hombres Aqui" ("Men Here"). Children of Mexican descent were put in separate classes for the first four grades. The Court held that shutting people of Mexican descent out of juries broke the Fourteenth Amendment. Decided two weeks before *Brown*. Petitioner: **Pete Hernandez**, convicted of the murder of Joe Espinosa and sentenced to **life imprisonment** (same page, second fetch). Exact wording: "On the courthouse grounds at the time of the hearing, there were two men's toilets, one unmarked, and the other marked 'Colored Men' and 'Hombres Aqui' ('Men Here')."
- **The East Los Angeles walkouts.** Library of Congress, "A Latinx Resource Guide: 1968 East Los Angeles Walkouts" (https://guides.loc.gov/latinx-civil-rights/east-la-walkouts , fetched): **1 to 8 March 1968**, "around 15,000 students walked out" of seven schools. Mexican American students faced a "60% dropout rate from high school," bans on speaking Spanish, and being steered into trade classes instead of college classes. They asked for "curriculum changes, bilingual education, and hiring of Mexican-American administrators." The Board of Education turned them down, citing money. On **31 March 1968** thirteen organizers were arrested on felony conspiracy charges. Twelve were soon released. The teacher **Sal Castro** was held longer, released on bail **2 June**, and fired. After months of sit-ins at the Board of Education, with **35 arrested on 2 October**, he got his job back.
- **The Chicano Moratorium.** Library of Congress, "1970: National Chicano Moratorium" (https://guides.loc.gov/latinx-civil-rights/national-chicano-moratorium , fetched): **29 August 1970**, East Los Angeles, "20,000 to 30,000 demonstrators" marched against "the disproportionate number of Mexican American troops drafted and killed or injured during the Vietnam War." "1,500 police officers arrived at Laguna Park, shooting tear-gas canisters into the crowd and beating protestors." Los Angeles later renamed the park Rubén Salazar Park.
- PBS SoCal / KCET, "August 1970: Chicano Moratorium Protests in East L.A." (https://www.pbssocal.org/kcet-50th-anniversary/august-1970-chicano-moratorium-protests-in-east-l-a-journalist-ruben-salazar-killed , fetched): "An L.A. County Sheriff's deputy fired a 10-inch tear gas round inside" the Silver Dollar Bar on Whittier Boulevard, "hitting Salazar in the head and killing him." The coroner ruled it a homicide. The deputy was never prosecuted. **Four people died** that day, and **150** were arrested.
- Online Archive of California, finding aid for the Los Angeles County Sheriff's Department records on the Salazar homicide (https://oac.cdlib.org/findaid/ark:/13030/c8rn3g7z , fetched): "Salazar was killed instantly when he was struck by a tear gas projectile fired into the café by **Deputy Thomas Wilson**." A **16-day** televised coroner's inquest followed. District Attorney **Evelle Younger** said he would not charge Wilson. The county's Office of Independent Review reported in **2011**: "It was not an era of openness and public transparency. The Sheriff's Department ... offered few explanations and no apologies."
- **Projectile length conflict:** KCET says 10-inch. A search summary says 8-inch. The outline gives no length.
- **Rubén Salazar** was a *Los Angeles Times* journalist (LOC).
- **The other three dead are not named on any page fetched.** Not claimed.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): Japanese American redress, told in era 9
- The facts are in era 8's PATCH "Japanese American incarceration, who gave the orders, the count dispute, the end of exclusion, and redress" (T-262c, US House History, Art & Archives, "Long Road to Redress," fetched): commission created 31 July 1980, 20 days of hearings in 11 places in 1981 with more than 750 witnesses, the February 1983 report ("race prejudice, war hysteria and a failure of political leadership"), the Civil Liberties Act signed by President Reagan on 10 August 1988 with an apology and $20,000 each, Inouye, Matsunaga, Mineta and Matsui, the JACL and the NCJAR, first letters and checks 1 October 1990. The 9 October 1990 date and the 82,219 count remain unconfirmed. **Era 9's outline now carries it as a span.**

- `health` tells the Relf sisters as its own story (`relf-sisters`), Willowbrook, Medicare's hospital desegregation, *Simkins v. Cone*, and the 1976 Indian Health Service count. Full sourced text: `research/research-health.md` era 9 (T-263 PATCHes, lines about 700 to 790).

Weinberger*); the court found "an estimated 100,000 to 150,000 poor people were sterilized annually under federally funded programs" (SPLC case page). Willowbrook State School, Staten Island, built for 4,000, held 6,000 by 1965; **parents of 5,000 residents sued New York State on 17 March 1972** (health bank, Wikipedia, cross-checked there by the health agent). The surgeons who operated are unnamed: health's SEARCHED, NOT FOUND stands.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): "firsts" and outline claims in era 9, checked
- "Section 504 ... the first federal law that made it illegal to shut a disabled person out": supported. DREDF, "Short History of the 504 Sit-in" (https://dredf.org/short-history-of-the-504-sit-in/ , fetched): "In 1973 the first federal civil rights protection for people with disabilities, Section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act was signed into law." The same page says the San Francisco sit-in, "the only one that endured, lasted 26 days," from 5 April 1977. A search summary (Zinn Education Project) gives **28 April 1977** as the day Califano agreed to sign (unconfirmed: search summary only). The outline gives no signing date.
- "Until 1975, an American public school could turn a disabled child away and no law said otherwise": **wrong as written.** Section 504 (1973) already barred it for any school taking federal money (its rules were unsigned until 1977), and the *PARC* orders of 1971 and 1972 barred it in Pennsylvania. The outline sentence is rewritten to what the bank supports.
- "the Journey of Reconciliation ... the first of the Freedom Rides": NPS's own words (bank 9.13).
- "I. King Jordan became Gallaudet's first deaf president": archives.gov (bank 9.9).
- "the first man was convicted of the bombing ... 1977": bank 9.17 and EBSCO above.
- Era-zoom claim "the country outlawed ... sex discrimination in schools, and the refusal to let a disabled person into a building" between 1954 and 1990: supported by Title IX 1972, Section 504 and the ADA 1990 in the bank. The sentence is rewritten so that people, not "the country," act.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262d): era-9 story checks (rosa-parks-mlk, bayard-rustin, judy-heumann, fannie-lou-hamer, barbara-johns)
- **Rosa Parks.** NPS "Rosa Parks" (https://www.nps.gov/people/rosa-parks.htm , fetched again): "as secretary of the Alabama State Conference of the NAACP, she traveled throughout the state interviewing victims of discrimination and witnesses to lynchings." She had disobeyed bus segregation rules repeatedly over the years and had once been removed from a bus for it. The outline's earlier line "Part of that job was writing down what happened to Black people in Montgomery, including on the buses" was not on the page and is replaced with these two facts.
- **Claudette Colvin's age in the outline was wrong.** The story called her "a fifteen-year-old" as a *Browder* plaintiff (filed 1 February 1956). Born 5 September 1939 (bank 9.11), she was sixteen then. She was fifteen at her arrest on 2 March 1955. The outline now says so, and gives her death (13 January 2026, perishable PATCH above).
- **The Montgomery bombings.** Equal Justice Initiative calendar, 30 January (https://calendar.eji.org/racial-injustice/jan/30 , fetched): King's house was bombed on **30 January 1956** with **Coretta Scott King, seven-week-old Yolanda and a neighbor** inside. The front was damaged and no one was hurt. The mayor and police commissioner promised a full investigation, but "no one was ever prosecuted or held accountable." King Institute, Stanford, "E. D. Nixon's yard bombed" (https://kinginstitute.stanford.edu/encyclopedia/e-d-nixons-yard-bombed , fetched): **1 February 1956**, "A bomb explodes in the yard of Nixon, the MIA treasurer." A search summary (History.com, others) says no one was ever convicted for either bombing (unconfirmed: search summary only for Nixon). **SEARCHED, NOT FOUND in effect:** no source names the bombers. The prose can say: "No one was ever prosecuted for the bombing."
- **King's killer.** US Department of Justice, Civil Rights Division, "Overview of Investigation of Allegations Regarding the Assassination of Dr. Martin Luther King, Jr." (June 2000, https://www.justice.gov/crt/overview-investigation-allegations-regarding-assassination-dr-martin-luther-king-jr , fetched): **James Earl Ray** pleaded guilty in **March 1969** and got **99 years**. He "repeatedly attempted to withdraw his plea" for 30 years until his death in **April 1998**. In the 1999 civil case *King v. Jowers*, brought by the King family, "the jury adopted a verdict ... finding that Jowers and 'others, including government agencies' participated in a conspiracy to assassinate Dr. King." DOJ found the Jowers and Donald Wilson allegations not credible and "no reliable evidence that a Raoul participated." Search summary (UPI archive, Wikipedia): plea on **10 March 1969**, Ray died **23 April 1998** (unconfirmed: search summary only). **Both positions are recorded. The outline states the plea, the civil verdict and the DOJ finding.**
- **Bayard Rustin.** NPS "Bayard Rustin" (fetched again): "a life-long pacifist, due to his Quaker upbringing." The outline's "Quakers refuse to fight in wars" is replaced by that sourced wording. The outline's "whose own state had jailed him" was wrong (born in Pennsylvania, convicted in California): corrected.
- **Judy Heumann.** Every outline fact matches bank 9.10. The Who line's "longest sit-in of the disability rights movement" was an unsourced superlative, replaced with "the 26-day sit-in" (NPS, DREDF). "Brooklyn" was not on the NPS page and was removed.
- **Fannie Lou Hamer.** Matches bank 9.14. Added from the Winona PATCH above: the two prisoners' names, the eye and kidney damage (EJI), the December 1963 federal trial and acquittal.
- **Barbara Rose Johns.** Every outline fact matches bank 9.3 (Encyclopedia Virginia). No change.

### ERA 9 bank check, T-262d, 2026-09-27: coverage
- Find 1 (hard subjects): Liuzzo, Jackson, Reeb, Evers, Neshoba, Birmingham, Anniston, Winona now carry named killers or named officers and what happened to each. Stonewall, the Lavender Scare and Milk added. Women's movement and ERA added. Chicano movement added (Hernandez, walkouts, Moratorium, Salazar's killer named). Redress told. Health's parked items placed. Till left with crime-justice. AIM and the farmworkers pointed to their lead chapters.
- Find 2 (actor, cause, count): see each PATCH. Remaining unnamed: the Anniston mob (no convictions found on NPS), the three other people killed on 29 August 1970, the Stonewall participants.
- Find 3 ("firsts"): all checked, one outline claim ("until 1975 ... no law") rewritten.
- Find 4 (perishable): Colvin's death confirmed. Killers' deaths dated.
- Find 5 (land): no era-9 land-taking in this chapter's events. Pointers recorded.
