# ERA 10 — 2000 to Today — RESEARCHED 2026-09-09

**Standing rule for this era: every figure carries the year it describes AND the year of the
source that reports it.** Nothing in this section says "today," "currently" or "in recent years."
Where the newest confirmable number is several years old, the entry says so. Where a fight is
unresolved, the entry says it is unresolved and gives the date it was checked (2026-09-09).

## 10. 2000 to Today

### 10.1 *Shelby County v. Holder*, 25 June 2013 — clears the first `[VERIFY]` tag
Source: Legal Information Institute, Cornell Law School, full text of *Shelby County v. Holder*,
No. 12-96 (law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/12-96), fetched 2026-09-09; docket and argument date
from LII's Supreme Court Bulletin preview for No. 12-96 (law.cornell.edu/supct/cert/12-96).

- **Docket No. 12-96. Argued 27 February 2013. Decided 25 June 2013. 5-4.**
- **Chief Justice John Roberts wrote the majority**, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Alito.
  **Justice Ruth Bader Ginsburg wrote the dissent**, joined by Breyer, Sotomayor and Kagan.
- **What was struck down: Section 4(b) of the Voting Rights Act — the coverage formula**, the list
  of which states and counties had to get federal permission before changing a voting rule. Holding
  as printed: **"Section 4 of the Voting Rights Act is unconstitutional; its formula can no longer
  be used as a basis for subjecting jurisdictions to preclearance."**
- **Section 5 — the preclearance requirement itself — was NOT struck down.** Roberts: **"We issue
  no holding on §5 itself, only on the coverage formula."** And: **"Congress may draft another
  formula based on current conditions."**
- Roberts's stated reason: **"the Act imposes current burdens and must be justified by current
  needs."**
- Ginsburg quotes **"what's past is prologue"** and invokes Santayana in dissent.
- **NOT CLAIMED:** the widely repeated Ginsburg line about throwing away an umbrella in a rainstorm
  does not appear in the LII text of this case as fetched on 2026-09-09. Two separate targeted
  fetches for the word "umbrella" came back empty. **Do not put it in the outline.**
- **The 1965 coverage formula this replaced is in the era-9 bank at 9.20**: a literacy test on
  1 November 1964 plus fewer than 50 percent of voting-age residents registered or voting in 1964.

### 10.2 What happened to voting rules after 2013
Source: U.S. Department of Justice, Civil Rights Division, "Jurisdictions Previously Covered by
Section 5" (justice.gov/crt/jurisdictions-previously-covered-section-5), fetched 2026-09-09, and
DOJ's "About Section 5 of the Voting Rights Act" via a justice.gov-restricted search, 2026-09-09.

- **Who had to ask permission before 2013.** Nine states were covered in full, with the date their
  coverage began: **Alabama, Georgia, Louisiana, Mississippi, South Carolina and Virginia from
  1 November 1964; Alaska, Arizona and Texas from 1 November 1972.** Parts of five more states were
  covered — named counties in **California** (Kings, Monterey, Yuba), **Florida** (Collier, Hardee,
  Hendry, Hillsborough, Monroe), **New York** (Bronx, Kings, New York), **North Carolina** and
  **South Dakota** (Shannon, Todd) — plus two **Michigan** townships, Clyde Township in Allegan
  County and Buena Vista Township in Saginaw County.
- **A FETCH ERROR TO RECORD:** the fetch summary of that DOJ page said "North Carolina: 31 counties
  listed individually" and then listed **forty** county names. The count in the summary is wrong.
  **The outline gives no count for North Carolina** and names the state only.
- **DOJ's own statement of the effect**, from its Section 5 page: the jurisdictions identified by
  the Section 4(b) formula **"no longer need to seek preclearance for the new voting changes, unless
  they are covered by a separate court order entered under Section 3(c) of the Voting Rights Act."**
- **STILL OPEN, checked 2026-09-09.** Congress has not written a replacement coverage formula. See
  10.3 for what is and is not established about what states did next.

### 10.3 How segregated schools are now — clears the second `[VERIFY]` tag
**The tag asked for "the measure and its source year." The honest answer is that the comparison the
seed wanted — now against 1970 — cannot be made from one source, because the two best series do not
both reach back that far.** What can be stated is below. Every figure carries the school year it
describes and the year the source was published.

**(a) The federal measure, for school year 2020-21, published 2022.** U.S. Government Accountability
Office, GAO-22-104737, *K-12 Education: Student Population Has Significantly Diversified, but Many
Schools Remain Divided Along Racial, Ethnic, and Economic Lines* — **issued 16 June 2022, publicly
released 14 July 2022** (gao.gov/products/gao-22-104737, fetched 2026-09-09):
- In **school year 2020-21**, more than a third of students — **about 18.5 million** — **"attended
  schools where 75 percent or more students were of a single race or ethnicity."**
- **"14 percent of students attended schools where 90 percent or more of the students were of a
  single race/ethnicity."**
- The report covers **school years 2014-15 through 2020-21** and **does not compare with 1970.**

**(b) The long series, from a university research centre, published 2024.** Gary Orfield and Ryan
Pfleger, *The Unfinished Battle for Integration in a Multiracial America — from Brown to Now*, the
Civil Rights Project / Proyecto Derechos Civiles, UCLA, **April 2024**. Downloaded as PDF and read
locally on 2026-09-09 (civilrightsproject.ucla.edu/wp-content/uploads/2024/04/National-Segregation-041624-CORRECTED-for-1.pdf).
**This is an academic research centre, not a government agency — label it that way in the book.**
Its numbers come from the federal Common Core of Data, and its 1968 figures come from Orfield's own
1983 analysis of Office for Civil Rights data.
- The report defines an **"intensely segregated" school as one that is 90-100 percent non-white.**
- **The series on those schools starts in 1988, not 1970.** In **1988**, about **7.4 percent** of
  schools were 90-100 percent students of colour — "out of the approximately 67,000 schools in the
  data, approximately 5,000." By **2021**, **19.8 percent** of schools were. The report's own
  summary sentence: the proportion **"nearly tripled over the last 30 years, rising from 7.4% to
  20%."**
- **In 2021, 78 percent of the students in those schools were poor.**
- **The South, on a different measure that does reach back to 1968.** Figure 13, "Percent of Black
  Students in Majority White Schools, by Southern State, 1968-2020." The narrative: the proportion
  of Black students in majority-white schools in the South **"reached a peak of 43%"** by the
  **mid-1980s**, and was **"around 16% as of 2021."** The report ties the turn to
  *Board of Education of Oklahoma City v. Dowell*, 498 U.S. 237 (1991), which let courts end
  desegregation orders, and says the decline ran for **"nearly 30 consecutive years."**
- **For Latino students in the South, on the same measure:** the share in majority-white schools
  **fell from 30.4 percent in 1968 to 15.4 percent in 2021.**
- **Who is in the public schools, 1968 and 2021** (Table 1; source NCES Common Core of Data via the
  Urban Institute Education Data Portal, with 1968 from the 1983 OCR analysis): white **80.7 percent
  in 1968, 45.3 percent in 2021**; Black **14.7 percent in 1968, 14.9 percent in 2021**; Latino
  **4.7 percent in 1968, 28.2 percent in 2021.** Asian, American Indian and multiracial students
  are not counted separately in the 1968 column at all.
- The report states plainly that this is not just a population effect: **"the proportion of the
  population that is white was also decreasing during the 1950s and 1960s when we saw a rapid
  decrease in segregation."**

**What the outline may therefore say:** the federal 2020-21 measure with its publication year; the
1988-to-2021 rise in 90-100-percent-non-white schools, attributed to the UCLA centre; and the
Southern peak-and-decline, dated to the mid-1980s peak and 2021, not to 1970. **The outline must not
say "compared with 1970."** No fetched source makes that comparison.

### 10.4 Marriage, 2003 to 2022 — the whole chain, from primary sources
All court text below was fetched on 2026-09-09 from the Legal Information Institute at Cornell Law
School (law.cornell.edu) unless another source is named. **mass.gov returned 403 or a JavaScript
shell to every attempt**, so nothing rests on it; the two Massachusetts facts come instead from
federal and state court opinions retrieved through the CourtListener search API, which is open
without a key (`courtlistener.com/api/rest/v4/search/`).

**1. *Lawrence v. Texas*, No. 02-102, decided 26 June 2003.** LII syllabus
(law.cornell.edu/supct/html/02-102.ZS.html):
- **6-3.** Justice Anthony Kennedy wrote the majority; Justice Sandra Day O'Connor concurred in the
  judgment; Justice Antonin Scalia dissented, joined by Chief Justice Rehnquist and Justice Thomas,
  and Justice Thomas dissented separately.
- The Texas statute made it a crime for **two people of the same sex** to have sex in private.
  Holding: **"The Texas statute making it a crime for two persons of the same sex to engage in
  certain intimate sexual conduct violates the Due Process Clause."**
- It overruled *Bowers v. Hardwick* (1986): **"*Bowers* was not correct when it was decided, is not
  correct today, and is hereby overruled."**
- **This is the starting point for the era.** Until this day, in the states that still had such
  laws, a gay couple could be arrested for what they did at home.

**2. Massachusetts, 2003-2004.** From the Supreme Court's own opinion in *Obergefell* (below):
**"In 2003, the Supreme Judicial Court of Massachusetts held the State's Constitution guaranteed
same-sex couples the right to marry. See *Goodridge* v. *Department of Public Health*, 440 Mass.
309, 798 N. E. 2d 941 (2003)."** CourtListener gives the decision date as **18 November 2003.**
- **The court delayed its own order.** *Doyle v. Goodridge*, 444 Mass. 1006 (2005), quotes the
  *Goodridge* order: **"entry of judgment shall be stayed for 180 days to permit the Legislature to
  take such action"** as it saw fit.
- **When the stay ran out:** *Largess v. Supreme Judicial Court for the State of Massachusetts*
  (U.S. District Court, District of Massachusetts, 13 May 2004): **"The stay expires on May 17,
  2004."** And *Charron v. Amaral*, Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court, 10 July 2008, describing
  a couple who applied **"the first day the Commonwealth permitted it, May 17, 2004."**
- **So Massachusetts is first, and the date is 17 May 2004** — fifty years and three days after
  *Brown v. Board of Education* was decided on 17 May 1954. The outline may give both dates; it
  draws no conclusion from the coincidence.

**3. *United States v. Windsor*, No. 12-307, decided 26 June 2013.** LII full text:
- **5-4.** Kennedy wrote the majority. Roberts, Scalia (joined by Thomas) and Alito (joined in part
  by Thomas) dissented.
- The facts, in the opinion's words: **"Two women then resident in New York were married in a lawful
  ceremony in Ontario, Canada, in 2007."** They were **Edith Windsor and Thea Spyer**. **"When Spyer
  died in February 2009, she left her entire estate to Windsor."** Because the federal government
  did not count them as married, **"Windsor paid $363,053 in estate taxes and sought a refund."**
- **Struck down: Section 3 of the Defense of Marriage Act**, which defined marriage and spouse as
  one man and one woman for **more than 1,000 federal statutes and regulations**.
- **Same week as *Shelby County*** (10.1): *Shelby County* on 25 June 2013, *Windsor* on 26 June
  2013. One ruling narrowed a right and the other widened one, on consecutive days.

**4. *Obergefell v. Hodges*, No. 14-556, decided 26 June 2015.** LII full text:
- **5-4.** Kennedy wrote the majority, joined by Ginsburg, Breyer, Sotomayor and Kagan. **Four
  separate dissents**: Roberts (joined by Scalia and Thomas), Scalia (joined by Thomas), Thomas
  (joined by Scalia) and Alito (joined by Scalia and Thomas).
- **The named plaintiff's story, in the Court's own words — quote this, do not paraphrase it:**
  "Petitioner James Obergefell, a plaintiff in the Ohio case, met John Arthur over two decades ago.
  They fell in love and started a life together, establishing a lasting, committed relation. In
  2011, however, Arthur was diagnosed with amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, or ALS. This debilitating
  disease is progressive, with no known cure. Two years ago, Obergefell and Arthur decided to commit
  to one another, resolving to marry before Arthur died. To fulfill their mutual promise, they
  traveled from Ohio to Maryland, where same-sex marriage was legal. It was difficult for Arthur to
  move, and so the couple were wed inside a medical transport plane as it remained on the tarmac in
  Baltimore. Three months later, Arthur died. Ohio law does not permit Obergefell to be listed as
  the surviving spouse on Arthur's death certificate."
- **ALS in plain words, for the reader:** amyotrophic lateral sclerosis destroys the nerve cells
  that carry orders from the brain to the muscles. The muscles stop getting the message, waste away
  and stop working — hands and legs first, then swallowing, then breathing. The Court's own phrase
  is **"progressive, with no known cure."**
- **What the case was actually about:** not a wedding, a **death certificate.** Obergefell sued to
  have his name put on it.
- **NOT CLAIMED:** the opinion gives no dates for the wedding or the death, only "two years ago" and
  "three months later." **No fetched source gave the exact days**, so the outline gives none.
- Before the ruling, the opinion says, **"voters and legislators in eleven States and the District
  of Columbia have changed their definitions of marriage to include same-sex couples."**
- **The closing paragraph**, which Congress later copied from: "No union is more profound than
  marriage, for it embodies the highest ideals of love, fidelity, devotion, sacrifice, and family.
  In forming a marital union, two people become something greater than once they were. As some of
  the petitioners in these cases demonstrate, marriage embodies a love that may endure even past
  death. It would misunderstand these men and women to say they disrespect the idea of marriage.
  Their plea is that they do respect it, respect it so deeply that they seek to find its fulfillment
  for themselves. Their hope is not to be condemned to live in loneliness, excluded from one of
  civilization's oldest institutions. They ask for equal dignity in the eyes of the law. The
  Constitution grants them that right."

**5. The Respect for Marriage Act, 13 December 2022.** Primary text: **Public Law 117-228**, 136
Stat. 2305, downloaded from the Government Publishing Office through congress.gov
(congress.gov/117/plaws/publ228/PLAW-117publ228.htm) and read locally on 2026-09-09.
- **H.R. 8404.** Full title: **"To repeal the Defense of Marriage Act and ensure respect for State
  regulation of marriage, and for other purposes."** The note on the law reads **"Dec. 13, 2022."**
- **Congress opened the law by quoting the Supreme Court back to itself.** Finding (1) reads, word
  for word, the first sentence of Kennedy's last paragraph in *Obergefell*: **"No union is more
  profound than marriage, for it embodies the highest ideals of love, fidelity, devotion, sacrifice,
  and family."**
- **What it does.** Section 3 repeals 28 U.S.C. 1738C, the section the Defense of Marriage Act had
  added. Section 4 inserts a new 1738C: **"No person acting under color of State law may deny full
  faith and credit to any public act, record, or judicial proceeding of any other State pertaining
  to a marriage between 2 individuals, on the basis of the sex, race, ethnicity, or national origin
  of those individuals."** The Attorney General may sue anyone who breaks it, and so may the person
  harmed.
- **What it does NOT do:** it does not require a state to issue a marriage licence. It requires
  every state to **recognise** a marriage performed somewhere else.
- **The votes, from the roll calls themselves.** Senate, senate.gov roll call vote 362 of the 117th
  Congress, 2nd session (senate.gov/legislative/LIS/roll_call_votes/vote1172/vote_117_2_00362.htm),
  fetched 2026-09-09: **"Vote Date: November 29, 2022, 05:52 PM ... Required For Majority: 3/5 ...
  YEAs 61 NAYs 36 Not Voting 3."** Counting the names on that page, **twelve Republican senators
  voted yes: Blunt, Burr, Capito, Collins, Ernst, Lummis, Murkowski, Portman, Romney, Sullivan,
  Tillis and Young.**
- House, Office of the Clerk roll call 513 (clerk.house.gov/Votes/2022513), fetched 2026-09-09:
  **"Dec 08, 2022, 11:11 AM ... Vote Question: On Motion to Concur in the Senate Amendment ...
  yea: 258 nay: 169 present: 1 not voting: 4."** By party: **Democratic 219 yea, 0 nay; Republican
  39 yea, 169 nay, 1 present, 4 not voting.**
- **Shared with:** `government-politics` (the rulings and the statute as law) · `home-family` (who
  counts as a family). **This chapter's angle is the people and the campaign.**

### 10.5 Ferguson, 2014-2015 — what a federal investigation found
Source: **United States Department of Justice, Civil Rights Division, *Investigation of the Ferguson
Police Department*, 4 March 2015.** Downloaded as PDF from justice.gov and read locally on
2026-09-09
(justice.gov/sites/default/files/opa/press-releases/attachments/2015/03/04/ferguson_police_department_report.pdf).
**This is a federal government finding, not a claim by protesters.** All quotations below are from
the report itself.

- **The investigation opened on 4 September 2014**, under the pattern-or-practice provision of the
  Violent Crime Control and Law Enforcement Act of 1994. Michael Brown was shot by a Ferguson
  officer on **9 August 2014** (the report's own date, at footnote 1457 of the extracted text).
- **What the investigators did:** interviewed the city manager, the mayor, the police chief, the
  municipal judge, the court clerk, the finance director and **half of the department's sworn
  officers**; spent **"approximately 100 person-days onsite"**; read **"over 35,000 pages of police
  records"**; sat through four sessions of the municipal court.
- **The finding, in the report's words:** **"a pattern or practice of unlawful conduct within the
  Ferguson Police Department that violates the First, Fourth, and Fourteenth Amendments to the
  United States Constitution, and federal statutory law."**
- **The money finding:** **"Ferguson's law enforcement practices are shaped by the City's focus on
  revenue rather than by public safety needs."** In March 2010 the city finance director wrote to
  the police chief that **"unless ticket writing ramps up significantly before the end of the year,
  it will be hard to significantly raise collections next year."** In March 2013 he wrote to the
  city manager: **"Court fees are anticipated to rise about 7.5%. I did ask the Chief if he thought
  the PD could deliver 10% increase. He indicated they could try."**
- **The numbers, from the department's own data, 2012 to 2014.** African Americans were **67% of
  Ferguson's population** and accounted for **"85% of vehicle stops, 90% of citations, and 93% of
  arrests made by FPD officers."** They were **more than twice as likely** to be searched at a
  traffic stop and were found with something illegal **26% less often** than white drivers. From
  2011 to 2013 they were **95% of "Manner of Walking in Roadway" charges** and **94% of "Failure to
  Comply" charges.**
- **Force:** **"Nearly 90% of documented force used by FPD officers was used against African
  Americans. In every canine bite incident for which racial information is available, the person
  bitten was African American."**
- **The court:** African Americans were **"92% of cases in which an arrest warrant was issued by the
  Ferguson Municipal Court in 2013"** and, of people arrested only because of an outstanding
  municipal warrant, **"96% are African American."**
- **THE PART THAT BELONGS TO THIS CHAPTER RATHER THAN TO `crime-justice`: the right to protest and
  the right to record.** The report has a whole section headed **"FPD Engages in a Pattern of First
  Amendment Violations."**
  - An officer who saw a couple recording him said **"you don't videotape me!"**, arrested the
    father, then pulled the mother over and arrested her too, saying **"no more mercy, since she
    wanted to videotape,"** and **"nobody videotapes me."** He took the phone from their daughter.
    **"After posting bond, the couple found that the video had been deleted."**
  - **In November 2014 a federal judge entered a consent order** forbidding Ferguson officers from
    interfering with people **"lawfully and peacefully"** recording police. The same month the city
    settled a second suit over using its loitering rule to arrest peaceful protesters on public
    sidewalks.
  - **It kept happening.** On **9 February 2015**, the six-month anniversary of Michael Brown's
    death, people protested outside the police station. Two police vehicles **"abruptly accelerated
    from the police parking lot into the street"** and an officer announced, **"everybody here's
    going to jail."** A man recording the arrests was pushed to the ground and handcuffed while
    officers said **"stop resisting or you're going to get tased"**; the report says the video shows
    **"the man was neither interfering nor resisting."** **"A protester in a wheelchair who was live
    streaming the protest was also arrested."**
- **NAMING RULE:** every person in these accounts is unnamed in the report. **All of it goes in
  `hb-zoom` prose, never in an `hb-story` block.**
- **Parked to `research-crime-justice.md`:** the revenue-driven ticketing, the municipal court
  findings, the dog-bite case of the 14-year-old boy in December 2011 (which the report says it
  cannot resolve between the officer's account and the boy's), and the use-of-force findings. Those
  are that chapter's angle.

### 10.6 Black Lives Matter — where the phrase came from
Source: University of Michigan Library research guide, "Black Lives Matter" (AAS 201)
(guides.lib.umich.edu/c.php?g=1433354&p=11146055), fetched 2026-09-09, which quotes the movement's
own account. **A university library guide, not a government record — say so.**
- **"In 2013, three radical Black organizers — Alicia Garza, Patrisse Cullors, and Opal Tometi —
  created a Black-centered political-movement-building project called #BlackLivesMatter"**, made
  **"in response to the acquittal of Trayvon Martin's murderer, George Zimmerman."**
- **NOT CLAIMED and NOT USED:** the date of Trayvon Martin's death, the date of the acquittal, and
  the account that the phrase began in a Facebook post by Garza which Cullors turned into a hashtag.
  Those come from a search summary only; **no primary source for them was retrieved on 2026-09-09.**
  The outline names the three founders, the year 2013 and the acquittal, and stops there.

### 10.7 George Floyd, 25 May 2020 — from the officer's own admissions
Source: **the signed federal plea agreement of Derek Chauvin**, United States District Court for the
District of Minnesota, case 0:21-cr-00108-PAM-TNL, document 142, **filed 15 December 2021**.
Downloaded as PDF from justice.gov and read locally on 2026-09-09
(justice.gov/archives/opa/press-release/file/1456711/dl?inline=). **Every fact below is something
Chauvin himself admitted in writing, under his own signature.** That is why this source is used and
not a news account.

- **"On the evening of May 25, 2020, the defendant and his partner, Officer Thao, responded to a
  dispatch call regarding a counterfeit bill. Officers Kueng and Lane were already on scene."**
- **"After an attempt to seat Mr. Floyd in a squad car, the defendant and Officers Kueng and Lane
  maneuvered Mr. Floyd, who was handcuffed and requesting to be placed on the ground, out of the
  vehicle and face-down on the street. Mr. Floyd remained restrained, prone and handcuffed on the
  ground for approximately ten minutes."**
- **"During this entire period, the defendant held his left knee on Mr. Floyd's neck, back, and
  shoulder area and his right knee on Mr. Floyd's left arm and upper back."**
- **"After the initial restraint, Mr. Floyd stopped resisting officers."** Chauvin admitted that
  from the point the officers decided not to use a hobble strap, **"the defendant's continued use of
  force became objectively unreasonable and excessive."**
- **"The defendant also knew there was no legal justification to continue his use of force because
  he was aware that Mr. Floyd not only stopped resisting, but also stopped talking, stopped moving,
  stopped breathing, and lost consciousness and a pulse."**
- **"The defendant also heard Mr. Floyd repeatedly explain that he could not breathe, was in pain,
  and wanted help."**
- **"The defendant was aware that civilian bystanders repeatedly asked him to check for a pulse,
  stated that Mr. Floyd was unresponsive and not breathing, and asked him to get off of Mr. Floyd.
  The defendant also heard Officer Kueng, who checked Mr. Floyd at least twice for a pulse, twice
  say that he could not find one."** Chauvin admitted he **"did not observe Officer Thao or Officer
  Keung do or say anything to try to get the defendant off of Mr. Floyd,"** and that Officer Lane
  twice asked whether Floyd should be rolled onto his side.
- **A DISCREPANCY TO STATE, NOT RESOLVE.** The plea agreement says **"approximately ten minutes."**
  The figure most often repeated in public is 9 minutes 29 seconds, which comes from the state
  murder trial and was **not sourced here.** **The outline uses the plea agreement's own words.**
- **The same document covers a second victim, four years earlier.** On **4 September 2017** Chauvin
  and an officer he was training answered a call from a mother who said her 14-year-old son had
  assaulted her. Chauvin admitted he **"held Juvenile 1 by the throat and struck Juvenile 1 multiple
  times in the head"** with a flashlight, causing **"a wound near Juvenile 1's left ear"**, and that
  he held **"his knee on Juvenile 1's neck, shoulders, and upper back for between fifteen and
  sixteen minutes"** while the boy was **"face-down on the floor, handcuffed, and unresisting"** and
  **"at times, cried from pain."** Chauvin admitted his report of that night **left out** the
  flashlight blows, the throat hold and the knee. **The boy is unnamed in the record and stays
  unnamed here.**
- **What happened to Chauvin, from a search of justice.gov on 2026-09-09** — **flagged, because
  justice.gov press-release pages returned an empty body to WebFetch and a blocked page to curl.**
  The press-release titles and the search summary give: **pleaded guilty 15 December 2021**
  (confirmed by the filed document above) and **sentenced on 7 July 2022 to 252 months in federal
  prison.** **The state murder conviction and its sentence were not sourced and are not claimed.**
  `crime-justice` should settle both before prose.
- **Parked to `research-crime-justice.md`:** the 2017 juvenile admissions and the sentencing gap.

### 10.8 How many people protested in 2020
Source: **Pew Research Center**, "Amid Protests, Majorities Across Racial and Ethnic Groups Express
Support for the Black Lives Matter Movement," **published 12 June 2020**, survey of **9,654 U.S.
adults conducted 4-10 June 2020** (pewresearch.org), fetched 2026-09-09. **Pew is a survey
organisation — a pollster. Label it that way in the book; this is what people told a survey, not a
head count.**
- **"Two-thirds of U.S. adults say they support the movement, with 38% saying they strongly support
  it."**
- **6% of U.S. adults said they had "attended a rally or protest focused on these issues"** in the
  past month. Among Black adults the figure was **10%**, among Hispanic adults **9%**, among Asian
  adults **10%**.
- **NOT CLAIMED:** the widely quoted estimate that 15 to 26 million Americans marched in 2020. It
  comes from polling extrapolations reported in newspapers and **no source for it was retrieved on
  2026-09-09.** Do not put a headcount in the outline.

### 10.9 The Emmett Till Antilynching Act, 29 March 2022
Primary text: **Public Law 117-107**, 136 Stat. 1125, from the Government Publishing Office through
congress.gov (congress.gov/117/plaws/publ107/PLAW-117publ107.htm), read locally 2026-09-09.
Committee history: **House Report 117-251**, Committee on the Judiciary, PDF from
congress.gov/117/crpt/hrpt251/CRPT-117hrpt251.pdf, read locally 2026-09-09.

- **H.R. 55.** Long title: **"To amend section 249 of title 18, United States Code, to specify
  lynching as a hate crime act."** Short title: **"Emmett Till Antilynching Act."** Note on the law:
  **"Mar. 29, 2022."** The law's own last line: **"Approved March 29, 2022."**
- **Legislative history printed on the law itself:** **"Feb. 28, considered and passed House. Mar.
  7, considered and passed Senate."**
- **What it does:** it adds paragraph (5) to 18 U.S.C. 249(a): **"Whoever conspires to commit any
  offense under paragraph (1), (2), or (3) shall, if death or serious bodily injury ... results from
  the offense, be imprisoned for not more than 30 years, fined in accordance with this title, or
  both."**
- **Why it is named for Emmett Till**, from the House report: **"H.R. 55 is named in honor of Emmett
  Till, a 14-year-old African American youth from Chicago who was lynched in 1955 while visiting an
  uncle in Mississippi."**
- **A DISAGREEMENT INSIDE ONE DOCUMENT — state it, do not resolve it.** House Report 117-251
  contains two accounts of when the first federal anti-lynching bill was introduced, in two
  different sections of the same report:
  - Committee section: **"The first federal antilynching legislation was introduced by Congressman
    George Henry White, then the only African American Member of Congress, in 1900."** And:
    **"During the first half of the 20th century, nearly 200 antilynching bills were introduced in
    Congress, but none were successful."**
  - A later section: **"In 1918, Representative Leonidas C. Dyer, a Missouri Republican, introduced
    the first bill in Congress to make lynching a federal crime. Over the next century, Members of
    Congress introduced more than 200 antilynching bills."**
  - Both agree that **between 1920 and 1940 the House passed three anti-lynching bills** and that
    none became law. Era 8 of this chapter already carries the Dyer bill of 1922 and the Senate
    filibusters.
  - **The outline says: the first bill was introduced either in 1900 by George Henry White or in
    1918 by Leonidas Dyer — Congress's own 2022 report says both — and about 200 more followed
    before one passed in 2022.**
- **A SECOND DISAGREEMENT INSIDE THE SAME DOCUMENT, on the Tuskegee lynching count.** One section:
  Tuskegee Institute began recording in 1892 and **"reported that 4,742 reported incidents had taken
  place by 1968, of which 3,445 of the victims were African-Americans."** Another section:
  **"Between 1882 and 1968, 4,743 people were lynched in the United States, including 3,446 African
  Americans and 1,297 whites."** The two counts differ by one in both figures. **Parked as an audit
  item to `research-crime-justice.md`**, which owns lynching as killing outside the law and must
  name a victim.
- **A LOOSE END FROM ERAS 7 AND 8 PARTLY CLOSED.** The era-7 and era-8 agents could not verify the
  NAACP report *Thirty Years of Lynching in the United States, 1889-1918* because loc.gov returns
  403. House Report 117-251 cites it in a footnote with its publisher, its span and its date —
  **"National Association for the Advancement of Colored People, Thirty Years of Lynching in the
  United States 1889-1918 (April 1919)"** — and gives the loc.gov URL. **The report's existence,
  title and April 1919 date are now confirmed from a congressional document. The figure of 3,224
  people lynched is still not confirmed and is still not used anywhere in this chapter.**

### 10.7b Darnella Frazier — the recording, and what it is sourced to
**pulitzer.org returned 403 to both WebFetch and curl on 2026-09-09** (a Cloudflare challenge page).
The citation text below comes from journalism reporting the Pulitzer Prize Board's announcement —
Poynter, CNN and Variety, 11 June 2021 — retrieved through a web search on 2026-09-09. **The
AGENT-BRIEF allows journalism for recent items provided it is labelled. It is labelled here and it
must be labelled in the book.**
- **The 2021 Pulitzer Prize Board special citation** was given to Darnella Frazier **"for
  courageously recording the murder of George Floyd, a video that spurred protests against police
  brutality around the world, highlighting the crucial role of citizens in journalists' quest for
  truth and justice."**
- She was **17** and walking with her **nine-year-old cousin** when she recorded it. She testified at
  Chauvin's state trial.
- **From the same reporting, and NOT from a court record:** Chauvin was found guilty in **April
  2021** of **second-degree murder, third-degree murder and second-degree manslaughter.** No state
  court document was retrieved. **The outline attributes this to the reporting and does not give a
  day or a sentence.**
- **Darnella Frazier is named in `hb-zoom` prose in this chapter and is not taken as a story block.**
  `news-communication` has an open `target` slot in era 10 and citizen recording is that chapter's
  angle. **Parked to `research-news-communication.md`.**

### 10.2b *Louisiana v. Callais*, 29 April 2026 — the second cut to the Voting Rights Act
**This is the newest thing in the chapter and it happened four months before this era was
researched.** Primary source: the slip opinion, **No. 24-109, *Louisiana v. Callais***, 608 U.S. ___
(2026), downloaded from supremecourt.gov (opinions/25pdf/24-109_21o3.pdf) and read locally on
2026-09-09. Neutral explanation: **Congressional Research Service Legal Sidebar LSB11431,
"Congressional Redistricting: High Court Narrows Voting Rights Act in *Louisiana v. Callais*,"
dated 14 May 2026**, downloaded from congress.gov and read locally the same day.

- **Argued 15 October 2025. Decided 29 April 2026.** Consolidated with No. 24-110, *Robinson v.
  Callais*.
- **Line-up, from the opinion itself:** **"ALITO, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which
  ROBERTS, C. J., and THOMAS, GORSUCH, KAVANAUGH, and BARRETT, JJ., joined. THOMAS, J., filed a
  concurring opinion, in which GORSUCH, J., joined. KAGAN, J., filed a dissenting opinion, in which
  SOTOMAYOR and JACKSON, JJ., joined."** So **6-3**.
- **A SOURCE ERROR FOUND AND NOT REPEATED.** The CRS Legal Sidebar says **"Justice Kagan wrote a
  dissent, joined by Justices Sotomayor and Kagan."** Kagan cannot join her own dissent. The
  opinion's own line-up names **Sotomayor and Jackson.** The outline follows the opinion.
- **What was at stake.** Louisiana drew one majority-Black district out of six after the 2020
  census. A federal judge held in *Robinson v. Ardoin* that the map likely broke Section 2 of the
  Voting Rights Act. Louisiana then drew a second majority-Black district, and other voters sued
  saying that map was an unconstitutional racial gerrymander.
- **The holding:** **"Because the Voting Rights Act did not require Louisiana to create an
  additional majority-minority district, no compelling interest justified the State's use of race in
  creating SB8, and that map is an unconstitutional racial gerrymander."**
- **The new rule for every future Section 2 case:** Section 2 **"imposes liability only when the
  evidence supports a strong inference that the State intentionally drew its districts to afford
  minority voters less opportunity because of their race."** CRS's plain-language summary: a
  challenger **"now must show that a state drew a redistricting map based on racial, and not
  political, considerations and present evidence of current-day, intentional voting discrimination
  that is based on race."**
- **What that changes.** Congress rewrote Section 2 in **1982** precisely so that a challenger would
  *not* have to prove intent — the opinion itself records this at pages 385-392 and 476-482 of the
  extracted text, noting Congress acted **"shortly after Bolden"** to abrogate *Mobile v. Bolden*.
  *Thornburg v. Gingles*, 478 U.S. 30 (1986), then set the three-part test used for forty years.
  Callais keeps the *Gingles* name and changes what it requires.
- **Kagan's dissent explains vote dilution in words a child can follow, and it is quotable.** She
  imagines a round county district that is 90 percent Black in a state that is otherwise 90 percent
  white, and a legislature that slices it **"into six pie pieces"** so that Black voters are spread
  thin: **"Election after election, Black citizens' votes are, by every practical measure, wasted."**
  She names the practice: **"'cracked' is the usual term."** She writes that the majority made
  **"a nullity of Section 2"** and threatens **"a half-century's worth of gains in voting
  equality"** (that phrase is quoted in the CRS sidebar, page 6).
- **What happened next, and it is not over.** ***Allen v. Milligan***, Nos. 25A1314, 25A1315 and
  25A1316, **per curiam, 2 June 2026**, slip opinion downloaded from supremecourt.gov
  (opinions/25pdf/25a1314_7m58.pdf) and read locally 2026-09-09. After *Callais* the Court vacated
  the district court's orders that had stopped Alabama using its 2023 congressional map — a map with
  **one** majority-Black district. The district court entered a new order on much the same grounds,
  and on **2 June 2026** the Supreme Court stayed that order too, saying Alabama **"is likely to
  succeed on the merits as to both claims."**
- **CRS's own statement of where this leaves things, 14 May 2026:** **"Existing majority-minority
  districts that were designed to comport with Section 2 might be challenged as unconstitutional
  racial gerrymanders in view of Callais. More immediately, prompted by the ruling, some state
  legislatures are considering or have already enacted modifications to their redistricting maps
  that eliminate majority-minority districts for the upcoming 2026 congressional elections."**
- **STATUS AS OF 9 SEPTEMBER 2026: unresolved.** Congress has written no new coverage formula since
  *Shelby County* and no new Section 2 standard since *Callais*. **The outline says so and gives the
  date it was checked.**
- **Shared with:** `government-politics` (**lead** on the rulings and on redistricting as law) ·
  `crime-justice` (no share). **This chapter's angle is what happened to a right that people
  organized for eighty years to win.**

### 10.10 Disability, 2008 to 2022 — narrowed by courts, restored by Congress, still unfinished
**(a) The ADA Amendments Act of 2008.** Primary text: **Public Law 110-325**, 122 Stat. 3553, from
the Government Publishing Office through congress.gov
(congress.gov/110/plaws/publ325/PLAW-110publ325.htm), read locally 2026-09-09.
- **S. 3406. Signed 25 September 2008.** Its long title is the whole point: **"To restore the intent
  and protections of the Americans with Disabilities Act of 1990."**
- **Congress said in the law itself which court rulings it was overruling.** Finding (4): **"the
  holdings of the Supreme Court in Sutton v. United Air Lines, Inc., 527 U.S. 471 (1999) and its
  companion cases have narrowed the broad scope of protection intended to be afforded by the ADA,
  thus eliminating protection for many individuals whom Congress intended to protect."** Finding
  (5) says the same of *Toyota Motor Manufacturing, Kentucky, Inc. v. Williams*, 534 U.S. 184
  (2002). Finding (6): **"as a result of these Supreme Court cases, lower courts have incorrectly
  found in individual cases that people with a range of substantially limiting impairments are not
  people with disabilities."**
- **What *Sutton* was about, from LII's syllabus (law.cornell.edu/supct/html/97-1943.ZS.html),
  fetched 2026-09-09.** Decided **22 June 1999, 7-2**, opinion by Justice O'Connor, Stevens and
  Breyer dissenting. The petitioners were **twin sisters** who applied to be commercial airline
  pilots at United Air Lines. **Without glasses their vision was 20/200 or worse; with glasses it
  was 20/20 or better.** United required uncorrected vision of 20/100 or better and turned them
  down. The Court held that **"the determination whether an individual is disabled should be made
  with reference to measures, such as eyeglasses and contact lenses, that mitigate the individual's
  impairment."** So the sisters were too blind to be hired and not blind enough to sue.
- **What the 2008 law put back.** The definition of disability **"shall be construed in favor of
  broad coverage of individuals under this Act, to the maximum extent permitted."** **"An impairment
  that is episodic or in remission is a disability if it would substantially limit a major life
  activity when active."** And the question is decided **"without regard to the ameliorative effects
  of mitigating measures"** — medication, prosthetic limbs, hearing aids and cochlear implants,
  mobility devices, oxygen equipment, assistive technology — with **ordinary eyeglasses and contact
  lenses expressly excluded from that list.**
- The law also wrote out what counts as a major life activity, including **"caring for oneself,
  performing manual tasks, seeing, hearing, eating, sleeping, walking, standing, lifting, bending,
  speaking, breathing, learning, reading, concentrating, thinking, communicating, and working"** and
  **"the operation of a major bodily function,"** naming the immune system, normal cell growth,
  digestion, bowel, bladder, neurological, brain, respiratory, circulatory, endocrine and
  reproductive functions.

**(b) *Olmstead* and the people still in institutions.** Sources: DOJ, **"Statement of the
Department of Justice on Enforcement of the Integration Mandate of Title II of the ADA and
*Olmstead v. L.C.*"** (ada.gov/resources/olmstead-mandate-statement/, last updated **28 February
2020**) and the archived DOJ page **"Olmstead: Community Integration for Everyone — About"**
(archive.ada.gov/olmstead/olmstead_about.htm). Both fetched 2026-09-09.
- **Decided 22 June 1999**, so the ruling itself belongs to era 9 by the boundary rule. **Era 9 of
  this chapter does not carry it**, and era 10 uses it only as the background to what happened after
  2000, the way the voting span uses the 1965 Act.
- **The two women, named on DOJ's own page:** **"Lois Curtis and Elaine Wilson, who had mental
  illness and developmental disabilities, and were voluntarily admitted to the psychiatric unit in
  the State-run Georgia Regional Hospital."** After **"mental health professionals stated that each
  was ready to move to a community-based program,"** the women **"remained confined in the
  institution, each for several years after the initial treatment was concluded."** **"They filed
  suit under the Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) for release from the hospital."**
- **The Court's reasoning, quoted by DOJ:** **"institutional placement of persons who can handle and
  benefit from community settings perpetuates unwarranted assumptions that persons so isolated are
  incapable or unworthy of participating in community life,"** and **"confinement in an institution
  severely diminishes the everyday life activities of individuals, including family relations,
  social contacts, work options, economic independence, educational advancement, and cultural
  enrichment."**
- **What it requires:** public bodies must provide community-based services when the services are
  appropriate, the person does not object, and it can be reasonably accommodated given the
  resources and the needs of others.
- **It is still being enforced, which is how we know it is still happening.** DOJ letter of findings
  to **Governor Jared Polis of Colorado, 3 March 2022**, PDF from archive.ada.gov
  (olmstead/documents/colorado_lof.pdf), read locally 2026-09-09:
  - **"We have determined that Colorado is violating the ADA by administering its long-term care
    system in a way that unnecessarily segregates individuals with physical disabilities in nursing
    facilities and places others with physical disabilities at serious risk of unnecessary
    institutionalization."**
  - **"For some, what begins as a brief rehabilitative stay in a facility becomes indefinite when
    they are unable to access the supports they need to live independently."** And: **"Some
    individuals have remained in nursing facilities for many years with no movement toward
    discharge."**
  - Scale, as of the letter: **"more than 200 nursing facilities across the state, ranging from
    approximately 25 to 250 total beds,"** and **"More than 15,000 Coloradans receive these
    Medicaid-funded facility-based services in a given year."**
  - The letter's own opening reason for urgency: **"nearly 150,000 nursing home residents nationwide
    have died of COVID-19, including more than 1,900 in Colorado"** — DOJ citing CMS COVID-19
    Nursing Home Data, **last visited 24 February 2022.** **That figure is as of February 2022 and
    must be dated that way.**
  - **The letter names no individual.** Nothing from it goes in an `hb-story` block.

**(c) How many disabled Americans there are, and the trap in the number.** U.S. Census Bureau,
**"Anniversary of Americans with Disabilities Act: July 26, 2026," released 22 July 2026**
(census.gov/newsroom/facts-for-features/2026/disabilities-act.html), fetched 2026-09-09, using the
**2024 American Community Survey 1-year estimates**:
- **45.8 million, or 13.7 percent** — **"The total U.S. civilian noninstitutionalized population
  with a disability in 2024."**
- **19.6 percent** in West Virginia, the highest of any state; **11.0 percent** in New Jersey and
  Utah, two of the lowest. **10.5 million** disabled people aged 18 to 64 were employed in 2024.
  Median earnings for disabled workers aged 16 and over: **$34,114** in 2024 dollars.
- **THE TRAP, AND IT IS WORTH TEACHING.** Every one of those figures describes the **civilian
  noninstitutionalized population** — the count leaves out people living in institutions, which is
  exactly the group the *Olmstead* fight is about. **The outline says so.**
- The 2023 edition of the same Census page (released for 26 July 2023) gave **42.5 million or 13
  percent for 2021** and median earnings of **$28,438**, on the same noninstitutionalized basis.
  Both editions were fetched 2026-09-09; the 2026 edition is the newer figure and the outline uses
  it with its year attached.

**(d) Alice Wong — fills a story slot.** Sources, all fetched 2026-09-09: **National Council on
Disability, "NCD remembers former Councilmember Alice Wong," 18 November 2025**
(ncd.gov/2025/11/18/ncd-remembers-former-councilmember-alice-wong/) · **Obama White House archive
photo page, 20 July 2015**
(obamawhitehouse.archives.gov/photos-and-video/photo/2015/07/president-obama-greets-alice-wong-robot)
· **UCSF Disability Resource Center, "In memory of Alice Wong"** (disability.ucsf.edu).
- **Born 1974. Died 14 November 2025, aged 51** (NCD).
- **Appointed to the National Council on Disability by President Barack Obama on 25 January 2013**,
  and served **2013 to 2015** (NCD). The Council is a federal agency that advises the President and
  Congress on disability policy.
- **Founded the Disability Visibility Project in 2014** (NCD).
- **20 July 2015, the Blue Room of the White House, the Americans with Disabilities Act 25th
  Anniversary reception.** The official caption on the White House photograph, taken by Pete Souza:
  **"President Barack Obama greets Alice Wong, Disability Visibility Project Founder and Project
  Coordinator via robot."** She could not travel, so she took part through a wheeled screen on a
  stand that carried her face and voice into the room.
- **Her body, in plain words, from UCSF:** she used a **wheelchair** and had a **tracheostomy
  connected to a ventilator.** A tracheostomy is a hole cut through the front of the neck into the
  windpipe, with a short tube held in it; a machine pushes air through that tube into the lungs so
  the person keeps breathing. **UCSF does not name her underlying condition and this bank does not
  guess at one.**
- **UCSF:** she earned a **master's degree in Medical Sociology from UCSF's School of Nursing in
  2004**, founded the campus **Disability Interest Group in 2002** as a student, sat on the
  Chancellor's Advisory Committee on Disability Issues, and worked at three research centres — the
  National Center for Personal Assistance, the Disability Statistics Center and the Community Living
  Policy Center.
- **Her own words, quoted by UCSF:** **"Disability is part of the greater continuum of diversity,
  and we need to allow for variation and not get so focused on what is the norm."**
- **Books:** *Disability Visibility: First-Person Stories from the Twenty-First Century* and
  *Year of the Tiger: An Activist's Life* (2022, per NCD).
- **NOT CLAIMED:** the Disability Visibility Project's partnership with StoryCorps appears in a
  search summary and on pages that were not fetched directly. **Not used.** No film exists that this
  agent could verify, so `movie=""`.
- **Slug `alice-wong` checked against every `hb-story:start` slug in `outlines/` on 2026-09-09 and
  is unused.** (`anna-may-wong` in `art`/`storytelling-evolution` and `wong-kim-ark` are different
  people and different slugs.)

### 10.11 "Me too," 2006 to 2018 — and the founding date nobody agrees on
Sources, all fetched 2026-09-09: **National Women's History Museum biography of Tarana Burke**
(womenshistory.org/education-resources/biographies/tarana-burke) · **Tarana Burke's own biography
page** (taranaburke.com/bio) · **Colorado State University, Department of English, "Women's History
Month: Tarana Burke"** (english.colostate.edu) · **Pew Research Center, "How social media users have
discussed sexual harassment since #MeToo went viral," 11 October 2018** (pewresearch.org).

- **Born 12 September 1973 in the Bronx, New York** (NWHM). **At 14 she joined the 21st Century
  Youth Leadership Movement** (Colorado State). She went to **Alabama State University** and then
  **Auburn University**, and afterwards **moved to Selma, Alabama** and kept working for 21st
  Century there (both).
- **She is a survivor of sexual abuse herself** (NWHM).
- **THE FOUNDING YEAR IS DISPUTED THREE WAYS AND MUST NOT BE RESOLVED SILENTLY.**
  - **Her own site: "in 2005, she created Just Be, Inc., an organization committed to the
    empowerment and wellness of black girls."**
  - **NWHM: JustBe, Inc. was "founded in 2007."**
  - **Colorado State: "In 2006, she created a Me Too MySpace page — long before there was such a
    thing as a hashtag."**
  - **The outline gives the range and names who says what.** It does not pick a year.
- **How the phrase started.** NWHM traces it to **1996**, when a young camper told Burke about being
  sexually abused, and says that years later Burke **"began using the phrase 'me too' to promote the
  idea of 'empowerment through empathy.'"** Burke's own page says her career **"took an intentional
  turn toward supporting survivors of sexual violence while living in Selma, Alabama and working for
  21st Century,"** and that the movement was born **"shortly thereafter as an entry to healing for
  survivors and a mechanism for developing survivor leaders."** **The 1996 camp story rests on one
  source and the outline attributes it to NWHM rather than stating it flat.**
- **What happened in 2017.** NWHM: **"During the 2017 Harvey Weinstein sexual abuse scandal, Burke's
  hashtag #metoo went viral."** Burke's own page: **"In 2017, when 'me too.' as a hashtag (#metoo)
  went viral, Tarana emerged as a global leader in the evolving conversation around sexual
  violence."**
- **The date and the size, from Pew Research Center, published 11 October 2018.** Pew is a survey
  and data organisation; label it. The hashtag **went viral on 15 October 2017**, when the actress
  **Alyssa Milano** used it. Pew counted publicly available English-language tweets and found the
  hashtag **"more than 19 million times on Twitter"** between **15 October 2017 and 30 September
  2018** — **"an average of 55,319 uses of the hashtag per day."** Pew's peak day was **9 September
  2018.**
- **Time magazine named Burke among "the Silence Breakers," its Person of the Year for 2017**
  (Colorado State).
- **In 2018 Burke founded "me too." International** (her own page).
- **NOT CLAIMED:** the count of 53,000 replies in the first six hours and the 180,700 tweets on the
  first day appeared only in a search summary and **are not used.** Pew's own figures are used
  instead.
- **Slug `tarana-burke` checked against every `hb-story:start` slug in `outlines/` on 2026-09-09 and
  is unused.**

### 10.12 Transgender rights, 2020 to 2026 — won at work, lost at school, still open
**(a) *Bostock v. Clayton County*, No. 17-1618, decided 15 June 2020.** LII full text
(law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/17-1618), fetched 2026-09-09.
- **6-3.** Justice Neil Gorsuch wrote the majority. Justice Alito dissented, joined by Justice
  Thomas; Justice Kavanaugh dissented separately.
- **Holding: "An employer who fires an individual merely for being gay or transgender violates Title
  VII."** Title VII is the part of the Civil Rights Act of 1964 that bans job discrimination because
  of sex.
- **The three people:** **Gerald Bostock** worked as a child welfare advocate for Clayton County,
  Georgia, for ten years and was fired for conduct **"unbecoming"** a county employee shortly after
  he joined a gay recreational softball league. **Donald Zarda** was a skydiving instructor at
  Altitude Express in New York and was fired days after telling his employer he was gay.
  **Aimee Stephens** worked at R.G. & G.R. Harris Funeral Homes in Michigan; she was hired
  presenting as male, and six years later told her employer she intended to **"live and work
  full-time as a woman."** The funeral home fired her before she left on holiday, saying the
  arrangement **"is not going to work out."**
- **NOT CLAIMED:** that Zarda and Stephens both died before the ruling. Widely reported, **not
  sourced here.**

**(b) *United States v. Skrmetti*, No. 23-477, decided 18 June 2025.** LII full text
(law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/23-477), fetched 2026-09-09.
- **Line-up, verbatim:** **"Roberts, C. J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which Thomas,
  Gorsuch, Kavanaugh, and Barrett, JJ., joined, and in which Alito, J., joined as to Parts I and
  II-B. Thomas, J., filed a concurring opinion. Barrett, J., filed a concurring opinion, in which
  Thomas, J., joined. Alito, J., filed an opinion concurring in part and concurring in the judgment.
  Sotomayor, J., filed a dissenting opinion, in which Jackson, J., joined in full, and in which
  Kagan, J., joined as to Parts I-IV. Kagan, J., filed a dissenting opinion."**
- **What the Tennessee law does.** It stops doctors giving **puberty blockers or hormones** to
  anyone under 18 for two stated purposes: **"(1) enabling the minor to identify with, or live as, a
  purported identity inconsistent with the minor's biological sex, or (2) treating purported
  discomfort or distress from a discordance between the minor's sex and asserted identity."** The
  same drugs stay legal for minors for other reasons — a birth defect, puberty arriving very early,
  disease or injury.
- **Holding:** **"Tennessee's law prohibiting certain medical treatments for transgender minors is
  not subject to heightened scrutiny under the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment
  and satisfies rational basis review."** In plain words: the Court did not treat the law as a
  suspect kind of line-drawing, and once it applied the easiest test, the law passed.
- **What the Court said it was NOT deciding:** **"We have not yet considered whether Bostock's
  reasoning reaches beyond the Title VII context."**
- **Plain words for the reader:** a puberty blocker is a drug that pauses the body's release of the
  hormones that start puberty, so the changes of puberty do not begin while it is taken. Hormone
  treatment gives a person the hormone associated with the sex they identify as, which changes the
  body in that direction. **State that neutrally and do not go further than the record.**

**(c) *West Virginia v. B.P.J.* and *Little v. Hecox*, decided 30 June 2026.** **This is four
months before this chapter was researched and is the most recent item in the whole book.** Primary
source: slip opinion, **No. 24-43**, 609 U.S. ___ (2026), downloaded from supremecourt.gov
(opinions/25pdf/24-43_2b35.pdf) and read locally 2026-09-09.
- **Argued 13 January 2026. Decided 30 June 2026.** Consolidated with **No. 24-38, *Little,
  Governor of Idaho, et al. v. Hecox et al.***
- **Line-up, verbatim:** **"KAVANAUGH, J., delivered the opinion of the Court, in which ROBERTS,
  C. J., and THOMAS, ALITO, GORSUCH, and BARRETT, JJ., joined. THOMAS, J., and GORSUCH, J., filed
  concurring opinions. SOTOMAYOR, J., filed an opinion concurring in the judgment in part and
  dissenting in part, in which KAGAN and JACKSON, JJ., joined. JACKSON, J., filed an opinion
  concurring in the judgment in part and dissenting in part."**
- **The question, in the Court's own words:** **"whether, under Title IX and the Equal Protection
  Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment, schools may maintain women's and girls' sports teams for
  biological females."**
- **Scale, from the syllabus:** **"In the past six years, 27 States have enacted laws that maintain
  female sports for biological females."**
- **The two laws.** Idaho's **Fairness in Women's Sports Act, 2020**, and West Virginia's **Save
  Women's Sports Act, 2021**. Both define sex by biology and bar students the law counts as male
  from girls' and women's teams.
- **Held:** **"Title IX allows schools to provide separate women's and men's sports teams defined by
  biological sex,"** and the two state laws **"did not violate the Equal Protection Clause."** The
  Court also held that **"Title VII and Bostock are not relevant in this very different statutory
  and factual context of sports."**
- **The Court's reasoning on why separate teams are reasonable:** **"the inherent physical
  differences between the sexes,"** plus **"the safety and competitive fairness issues that can
  arise when females are forced to compete against males,"** and the fact that **"27 States and
  various sports-governing bodies have all drawn the same line."**
- **The dissenting opinion states the other side, and it is the clearest short account of the
  student.** Justice Sotomayor, joined by Kagan and Jackson: **"Respondent B. P. J. is a transgender
  girl who wants to live her life consistent with her gender identity. When B. P. J. was 11 years
  old, she sought the opportunity to do what she and so many other children love to do: play
  sports."** B.P.J. wanted the cross-country and track teams. **"In the five years since the ban has
  been in place, B. P. J. is the only transgender girl publicly identified in the State who has
  sought to play sports with other girls."** Sotomayor agreed the Title IX claim failed but said the
  equal protection question should have gone back to the district court for fact-finding: **"because
  unresolved factual questions prevent the Court from assessing the merits ... the Court should
  allow the District Court to address those factual questions in the first instance."**
- **NAMING.** The record identifies the student only as **B.P.J.** The case caption names her
  mother, **Heather Jackson** — **"B. P. J., by her next friend and mother, Heather Jackson."** The
  other student is named in the record as **Hecox**, who competed for a women's club soccer team and
  tried out for the women's Division I track and cross-country teams at a university in Idaho. **The
  book uses exactly these names and invents nothing.** No `hb-story` block is built on initials.
- **STATUS AS OF 9 SEPTEMBER 2026.** This is settled law on these two questions and is not settled
  as an argument. **The outline says what each side argued, who won, and on what ground, and stops
  there.**
- **Shared with:** `government-politics` (the rulings as law) · `education` (what schools now do) ·
  `sports-play` (school and college sport). **This chapter's angle is the fight and the people in
  it.**
