### 10.13 Late corrections made to the era-10 outline during the agent's own self-audit (2026-09-09)
- **An unsourced specific was caught and removed.** A draft sentence said that after *Sutton* and
  *Toyota* lower courts "began deciding that people with epilepsy, cancer and diabetes were not
  disabled either." **Public Law 110-325 names no conditions.** Its finding (6) says only that
  lower courts **"incorrectly found in individual cases that people with a range of substantially
  limiting impairments are not people with disabilities."** The outline now quotes that instead.
  **Do not put named conditions back without a source that names them.**
- **A number was overstated and corrected.** A draft era-zoom line said about one school in five
  "had almost no white children in it." The UCLA measure is **90 to 100 percent non-white**, which
  allows up to one child in ten. The outline now says **"at least 90 percent non-white."**
- **The statute's own list was restored.** The outline's list of things ignored when deciding
  whether someone is disabled now matches Public Law 110-325: medication, prosthetic limbs, hearing
  aids and cochlear implants, **mobility devices** (the statute's phrase, not "wheelchairs") and
  oxygen equipment, with ordinary eyeglasses and contact lenses expressly left off the list.
- **A note on how the outline states the 1965 coverage formula.** Era 9's bank at 9.20 sources the
  **original** formula from archives.gov: a literacy test on **1 November 1964** plus **fewer than
  50 percent** of voting-age residents registered or voting in the 1964 presidential election. DOJ's
  list of covered places shows coverage beginning on **1 November 1964** for one group of
  jurisdictions and **1 November 1972** for another, so the same kind of rule was applied twice, and
  by the later round the "test or device" being looked for was broader than a literacy test.
  **The outline therefore says "a test to decide who could vote," names both dates, and does not
  claim that the 1972 round used a literacy test.**

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262e): Standing Rock and the Dakota Access Pipeline, 2016 to 2026. Who ordered the police response, what the officers did, what the courts said, and where it stands
`native-nations` LEADS Standing Rock (its outline, era 10, span "Standing Rock and land back", tells the camps, the youth runs, the 4 December 2016 halt and the 2017 approval). It does NOT tell the police response, the court rulings on it, or the 2025-2026 outcomes. This chapter carries the protest as a rights campaign: the right to protest and what happened to the people who did.

**Who organized the police response.** United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit, *Dundon v. Kirchmeier*, No. 22-1246, filed 3 November 2023, opinion by Judge Steven Colloton (PDF ecf.ca8.uscourts.gov/opndir/23/11/221246P.pdf, read locally 2026-09-27):
- "The governor of North Dakota organized a 'Unified Incident Command' to respond to the protests. The command was composed of the Morton County Sheriff's Office and other law enforcement agencies. The Stutsman County Sheriff's Office and the City of Mandan's police department volunteered to assist in the effort. The chief of each law enforcement agency remained in control of his agency's officers, and left decisions about using force in response to the protests to the discretion of the individual officer."
- The chiefs sued, as named in the caption and opinion: **Morton County Sheriff Kyle Kirchmeier, Stutsman County Sheriff Chad Kaiser, and City of Mandan Chief of Police Jason Ziegler.**
- The governor in 2016 was **Jack Dalrymple.** He signed an **emergency declaration on 19 August 2016** for southwest and south-central North Dakota, saying the state protected "the right to lawful protests" but that recent events created a "significant public safety concern." About 1,500 people were at the protest then, and 28 had been arrested (Forum News Service / InForum, "Gov. Dalrymple issues state of emergency in response to pipeline protests," inforum.com/news/4098242, fetched 2026-09-27).

**20 November 2016, Backwater Bridge, from the court's own account (Dundon, 2023):**
- The North Dakota Department of Transportation closed the bridge on Highway 1806. Officers built a barricade of two dump trucks chained to concrete barriers and barbed wire. Protesters used a semi-trailer truck to drag one dump truck away. The crowd grew to "the hundreds."
- "The officers fired tear gas canisters into the crowd." "The officers also deployed rubber bullets and lead-filled bean bags." Officers got a fire truck from the City of Mandan to put out brush fires and "also sprayed the protestors with the hose in an effort to disperse them."
- **The count of injured: not in the opinion.** The Civil Rights Litigation Clearinghouse summary of the case (clearinghouse.net/case/43321, fetched 2026-09-27) says force was used "without warning, injuring over 200 demonstrators and arresting approximately 100 people." That is the plaintiffs' side as the Clearinghouse summarizes it. PBS NewsHour (2016) reported "more than 200 people" injured (search summary only, unconfirmed).
- **Outcome.** Judge Daniel M. Traynor granted summary judgment to all defendants on 29 December 2021 (Clearinghouse). The Eighth Circuit affirmed on 3 November 2023. Its ground: "it was not clearly established" in November 2016 that using force to disperse a crowd was a Fourth Amendment seizure, so the officers had qualified immunity. **No officer or agency was held liable. The injured protesters got nothing.**

**Sophia Wilansky, the most seriously injured person that night.** Eighth Circuit, *Wilansky v. Morton County*, Nos. 24-1911 and 24-1919, submitted 16 December 2025, **filed 24 June 2026**, opinion by Judge Jonathan Kobes, with Judges Loken and Lavenski R. Smith (PDF ecf.ca8.uscourts.gov/opndir/26/06/241911P.pdf, read locally 2026-09-27). These are her alleged facts, which the court accepts for the appeal:
- Police ordered her and another protester away from the remaining truck: "Get out now. Return to the south side of the bridge. Otherwise, we will use less-lethal munitions." Officers Dvorak, Arndt and Hanson fired less-lethal munitions at her as she ducked behind a metal sheet. One hit her arm. She ran. About 30 yards away, as she bent to pick up a piece of plywood, "she was hit with an aerial warning munition fired by Officer Moll. It tore the flesh off her arm, and she fell to the ground." Protesters carried her away. "Officers laughed and congratulated Officer Moll on his 'marksmanship.'" (The defendants dispute that last point, footnote 5.) "Her left hand and forearm are permanently and severely injured."
- Plain words, from the opinion's footnotes: less-lethal munitions "include rubber and sponge bullets, pepper spray, pepper balls, lead beanbags, tear gas, and flashbangs." "An aerial warning munition is a flashbang embedded in a less-lethal bullet. It is designed to be fired above crowds for dispersal."
- **Outcome: the court affirmed dismissal of both her suits with prejudice.** She had not pleaded a clearly established seizure, and the force did not meet the "shocks the conscience" standard. The officer who fired was named in court (Jonathan R. Moll) and was not held liable.

**Arrests and charges.**
- CBS News / Associated Press (late 2016, "Dakota Access pipeline Standing Rock protest arrests leave North Dakota courts struggling to cope," cbsnews.com, fetched 2026-09-27): "Police have made nearly 575 arrests since August." Fewer than 8 percent of those arrested were from North Dakota, by Morton County Sheriff's Office data.
- **761 arrests in all, by the count of the law enforcement joint information center** (unconfirmed: search summary only). North Dakota Monitor, 17 September 2026, says only "Hundreds of arrests resulted ... though many of the charges were later dismissed."
- InsideSources (May 2017, fetched 2026-09-27): many trespass cases were dismissed by Morton County District Judge Allan Schmalenberger because no one had told the protesters to leave private land. **A later tally of 835 state cases, 333 dismissed and 17 convicted at trial** appears in the same search summary (unconfirmed: search summary only).

**What the federal government decided, with dates.**
- **4 December 2016:** the Army's Assistant Secretary for Civil Works denied the easement to cross under Lake Oahe (memorandum of that date, named in the presidential memorandum below; Harvard Environmental and Energy Law Program tracker, eelp.law.harvard.edu/tracker/dakota-access-pipeline, fetched 2026-09-27).
- **24 January 2017:** President Donald Trump's memorandum to the Secretary of the Army, "Construction of the Dakota Access Pipeline," 82 Fed. Reg. 8661 (30 January 2017, federalregister.gov full text, fetched 2026-09-27), ordered the Army to "review and approve in an expedited manner" the approvals, and to consider rescinding the 4 December 2016 memorandum and withdrawing the notice of an environmental impact statement. It says: "I believe that construction and operation of lawfully permitted pipeline infrastructure serve the national interest."
- **8 February 2017:** the Corps granted the easement (EELP). Camps cleared 22-23 February 2017. **Oil has flowed since June 2017** (North Dakota Monitor, 19 December 2025).
- **6 July 2020:** a federal district judge (James Boasberg) vacated the easement and ordered a full environmental impact statement. **26 January 2021:** the D.C. Circuit upheld the finding that the law had been broken but reversed the shutdown order. **22 February 2022:** the Supreme Court declined to hear Dakota Access's appeal (EELP). The pipeline then ran for about five years without an easement (North Dakota Monitor, 2025).
- **19 December 2025:** the Corps issued its final environmental impact statement, preferring to grant the easement with added conditions (North Dakota Monitor, Mary Steurer and Amy Dalrymple, 19 December 2025, fetched 2026-09-27). Standing Rock Chairman **Steve Sitting Bear**: "At every turn, our rights and the health and safety of our people and the environment are ignored ... We will continue to fight for our homelands and the promises the United States made to our Tribe in Treaty."
- **Record of Decision:** "the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers signed a Record of Decision selecting an alternative that grants an easement with additional conditions," notice signed by Brigadier General William C. Hannan, 91 Fed. Reg. 34227, **5 June 2026** (federalregister.gov, fetched 2026-09-27). EELP dates the signing to **21 May 2026.**
- **June 2026:** the Standing Rock Sioux Tribe asked the D.C. Circuit to dismiss its own appeal, and the court granted it (EELP gives 23 June 2026; Law360 and Bloomberg Law headlines; unconfirmed: search summary only for the Bloomberg and Law360 detail).
- **STATUS, 27 September 2026:** the pipeline runs under Lake Oahe with a federal easement. North Dakota Monitor, 17 September 2026, quotes Standing Rock council member Nola Taken Alive: "The oil is still flowing. The pipeline hasn't been shut down."

**The company's lawsuit against Greenpeace.** North Dakota Monitor, Mary Steurer, 27 February 2026 (fetched 2026-09-27): Energy Transfer, the pipeline's developer, sued Greenpeace USA, Greenpeace Fund and Greenpeace International in Morton County in 2019. **On 19 March 2025 a Morton County jury ordered Greenpeace to pay about $667 million** for harms during the 2016-17 protests and for defamation. **Southwest Judicial District Judge James Gion cut the award to $345 million in October 2025 and finalized it in February 2026**, with 11 percent interest. Greenpeace says it had six employees at the camps and calls the suit an attempt to punish it for supporting the protests. Appeals were expected to the North Dakota Supreme Court.

**Land.** The pipeline "crosses underneath Lake Oahe less than a mile north of the Standing Rock Reservation. It passes through unceded land that was recognized as belonging to the Sioux Nation in 19th century treaties with the U.S. government" (North Dakota Monitor, 19 December 2025). `native-nations` carries the treaties and the Oahe Dam.

**Parked:** the police response and court outcomes to `research-native-nations.md` (below this chapter's heading there).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262e): immigrant rights, the Dreamers and DACA, 2010 to 2026, and the Minneapolis killings of 2026
`immigration` leads the laws and the enforcement numbers (its bank, era 10, "Enforcement, 2025-2026", already carries the shootings of Silverio Villegas González, Renée Nicole Good and Alex Pretti in one line each). Its outline does not tell DACA. This chapter carries the Dreamers as a movement and what happened to the program they won.

**The organizing.** Swarthmore College, Global Nonviolent Action Database, "Miami college students march to U.S. Capitol in support of immigrant rights (Trail Of Dreams), 2010" (nvdatabase.swarthmore.edu, fetched 2026-09-27):
- The DREAM Act (Development, Relief, and Education for Alien Minors) was first introduced in 2001 by Senator Richard Durbin of Illinois and Representative Howard Berman of California. It would have given people brought into the country as children a path to citizenship through two years of college or military service. It had not passed by 2010.
- **On 1 January 2010 four Miami-Dade College students, Gaby Pacheco (24), Carlos Roa (22), Felipe Matos (23) and Juan Rodriguez (20), began a 1,500-mile walk from Miami to Washington.** Three were undocumented. They told the public their status as they went, which risked arrest and deportation. They gathered 30,000 signatures. The White House refused them a meeting with President Obama, and a Secret Service agent declined to accept their petition on 28 April 2010. They walked the last four miles to the White House on 1 May 2010.
- **18 December 2010:** the Senate vote to end debate on the House's DREAM Act amendment to H.R. 5281 failed, 55 yeas to 41 nays, with 60 needed (senate.gov roll call vote 278, 111th Congress, 2nd session, fetched 2026-09-27: "Vote Result: Cloture Motion Rejected ... Required For Majority: 3/5"). The bill's official titles include "DREAM Act of 2010" (govinfo BILLSTATUS-111hr5281.xml, fetched 2026-09-27).

**The program and the fight over it.** *Department of Homeland Security v. Regents of the University of California*, No. 18-587, argued 12 November 2019, **decided 18 June 2020** (LII, law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/18-587, fetched 2026-09-27):
- "In 2012, the Department of Homeland Security (DHS) issued a memorandum announcing an immigration relief program known as Deferred Action for Childhood Arrivals (DACA), which allows certain unauthorized aliens who arrived in the United States as children to apply for a two-year forbearance of removal. Those granted such relief become eligible for work authorization and various federal benefits. Some 700,000 aliens have availed themselves of this opportunity." Forbearance of removal means the government agrees, for now, not to deport the person.
- The rules, from the opinion: under age 31 in 2012, in the country continuously since 2007, in school or a high-school graduate or an honorably discharged veteran, and no serious criminal record.
- "In September 2017, Attorney General Jefferson B. Sessions III sent a letter to Acting Secretary of Homeland Security Elaine C. Duke" advising that DHS "should rescind" DACA. Duke did. District courts in California, New York and the District of Columbia ruled for the challengers.
- The Court held, in Chief Justice Roberts's opinion (5-4 on the main holding, per FWD.us and the line-up), that the rescission was reviewable and broke the Administrative Procedure Act as "arbitrary and capricious." **DACA stayed. The Court did not decide whether DACA itself was lawful** (FWD.us, "DACA Court Case Updates," fwd.us/news/daca-court-case, fetched 2026-09-27; FWD.us is an immigration advocacy group).
- The program was announced on **15 June 2012** and created by Secretary of Homeland Security Janet Napolitano (National Immigration Forum explainer; FWD.us).
- **The Texas case.** Texas and other states sued. Judge Andrew Hanen of the Southern District of Texas ruled DACA unlawful in 2021 and stopped new applications, while letting current holders renew. On **17 January 2025** the Fifth Circuit held the work-permit and "lawful presence" parts unlawful, kept the protection from deportation, and limited the order to Texas. As of 1 July 2026 Judge Hanen had not issued the order carrying that out, so Texas DACA holders could still renew. **82,410 DACA recipients live in Texas** (National Immigration Forum, "Current Status of DACA: Explainer," forumtogether.org, fetched 2026-09-27; an immigration advocacy group).
- **How many, dated.** **About 455,000 DACA recipients as of 31 March 2026**, down 40,000 from 31 December 2025, per USCIS data as reported by the National Immigration Forum. USCIS was processing renewals only and accepted but did not process new applications. It approved 53,855 renewals in the first half of fiscal 2026, against nearly 145,000 in the first half of fiscal 2025, and 120,000 renewals were pending, the most ever (same source). The Board of Immigration Appeals ruled on 24 April 2026 that DACA alone does not stop deportation proceedings (same source).
- FWD.us reports that in the first year of the second Trump administration "ICE arrested 261 DACA recipients and deported 86 of them" (FWD.us, fetched 2026-09-27; advocacy group, label it).
- **Congress has never passed the DREAM Act.** FWD.us: "Congress has failed to pass any meaningful legislation for Dreamers in the 14 years since DACA was established."

**Minneapolis, January 2026: two citizens killed by federal immigration agents, and who has been held responsible.** Minnesota Reformer, 13 July 2026, "Feds turn over evidence in killings of Renee Good, Alex Pretti by immigration agents" (minnesotareformer.com, fetched 2026-09-27) and NPR, 10 April 2026 (npr.org, fetched 2026-09-27):
- Renée Macklin Good and Alex Pretti, both U.S. citizens, were killed by federal immigration agents during "Operation Metro Surge" in Minneapolis in January 2026 (the `immigration` bank dates them 7 and 24 January 2026). **Good was shot by ICE officer Jonathan Ross**, named first by the Star Tribune. **ProPublica named the agents who shot Pretti as Border Patrol agent Jesus Ochoa and Customs and Border Protection officer Raymundo Gutierrez** (both as reported by Minnesota Reformer).
- NPR: after Good's death "federal officials said she impeded law enforcement and weaponized her vehicle. Local officials and others say video evidence contradicts that." After Pretti's death "federal authorities labeled him a domestic terrorist and said that federal officers shot him because they feared for their safety. Video evidence contradicts that."
- Federal officials kept state investigators away from the evidence. At the Pretti shooting "federal authorities physically blocked state investigators from accessing the scene" (NPR). Hennepin County Attorney Mary Moriarty and Minnesota Attorney General Keith Ellison sued the federal government for the evidence in March 2026 and received it in late June and July 2026 (Minnesota Reformer). Acting Attorney General Todd Blanche said there would be no civil rights investigation into Good's killing (Minnesota Reformer).
- **STATUS, 19 September 2026: no one has been charged in either killing.** The charging decision may fall to the next Hennepin County Attorney, chosen in the November 2026 election (The Intercept, 19 September 2026, theintercept.com, fetched 2026-09-27). One agent, Christian Castro, was charged by Moriarty's office with four counts of second-degree assault for shooting and wounding a Venezuelan man, Julio Sosa-Celis (Minnesota Reformer).
- Minnesota Reformer, July 2026: a man killed by an ICE agent in Maine that week was "at least the ninth killing by federal agents since the start of Trump's immigration crackdown" (the paper's count).

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262e): the women's movement since 2017, Dobbs, and the Equal Rights Amendment after 2000
**The Women's March, 21 January 2017.** Britannica, "Women's March" (britannica.com/event/Womens-March-2017, fetched 2026-09-27): the day after President Trump's inauguration. Teresa Shook, a grandmother in Hawaii, proposed a march on Washington on Facebook the day after the 8 November 2016 election. **Between 3.2 million and 5.3 million people marched in the United States**, with about 500,000 in Washington. The Crowd Counting Consortium, run by Jeremy Pressman (University of Connecticut) and Erica Chenoweth (Harvard Kennedy School), was founded to count it, and its data showed it was "at that time ... the largest single-day demonstration in US history" (Harvard Ash Center, ash.harvard.edu/programs/crowd-counting-consortium, fetched 2026-09-27). The consortium's range for U.S. marchers is 3,336,865 to 4,633,725 (unconfirmed: search summary only). Britannica: the record was passed by the "No Kings" protests of 14 June 2025, "in which about five million people marched."

**Dobbs v. Jackson Women's Health Organization, No. 19-1392, argued 1 December 2021, decided 24 June 2022** (LII, law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/19-1392, fetched 2026-09-27):
- Mississippi's Gestational Age Act banned abortion after 15 weeks of pregnancy except in a medical emergency or for a severe fetal abnormality.
- **Justice Samuel Alito wrote for the Court, joined by Thomas, Gorsuch, Kavanaugh and Barrett. Chief Justice Roberts concurred only in the judgment. Breyer, Sotomayor and Kagan dissented together.** So 6-3 to uphold the Mississippi law and 5-4 to overrule *Roe v. Wade* (1973) and *Planned Parenthood v. Casey* (1992).
- The holding: "We therefore hold that the Constitution does not confer a right to abortion. Roe and Casey must be overruled, and the authority to regulate abortion must be returned to the people and their elected representatives."
- **State laws after Dobbs, as of 10 August 2026** (KFF, "Abortion in the U.S. Dashboard," kff.org, last updated 10 August 2026, fetched 2026-09-27; KFF is a health-policy research group): **13 states ban abortion in almost all cases** (Alabama, Arkansas, Idaho, Indiana, Kentucky, Louisiana, Mississippi, North Dakota, Oklahoma, South Dakota, Tennessee, Texas, West Virginia). 7 more ban it after about 6 to 12 weeks (Florida, Georgia, Iowa, South Carolina and Wyoming at 6 weeks, Nebraska and North Carolina at 12). 4 set limits at 15 to 22 weeks. 18 states and D.C. limit it at or near viability. 9 states and D.C. set no limit by stage of pregnancy. KFF notes more ballot measures slated for November 2026.

**The Equal Rights Amendment after 2000.** Virginia Mercury (States Newsroom), Jennifer Shutt, 17 January 2025 (virginiamercury.com, fetched 2026-09-27), quoting a Congressional Research Service report and both statements:
- Congress sent the ERA to the states in 1972 with a seven-year deadline, later extended to 1982. Only 35 states ratified by then. Its text: "equality of rights under the law shall not be denied or abridged by the United States or any State on account of sex."
- CRS: "In 2017, acting upon the notion that the ratification deadline could be disregarded, **Nevada became the 36th state** to ratify the amendment." **Illinois ratified in 2018 and Virginia in 2020.** The three states asked the Archivist to certify it. The Archivist did not. They sued in January 2020. The District Court for D.C. dismissed the suit for lack of standing, and **the D.C. Circuit upheld the dismissal in February 2023.**
- Mid-December 2024: Archivist Colleen Shogan and Deputy Archivist William J. Bosanko: "the Equal Rights Amendment (ERA) cannot be certified as part of the Constitution due to established legal, judicial, and procedural decisions." The Justice Department's Office of Legal Counsel had said in 2020 and 2022 that the deadline "is valid and enforceable."
- **17 January 2025:** President Biden: "I affirm what I believe and what three-fourths of the states have ratified: the 28th Amendment is the law of the land." The statement ordered no one to do anything, and the Archivist did not publish the amendment.
- **STATUS, 27 September 2026:** the ERA is not published as part of the Constitution. Joint resolutions to declare it ratified sit in the 119th Congress (H.J.Res. 80 and S.J.Res. 38, titles on congress.gov; search summary only for their status). The era-9 PATCH (T-262d) carries the 1972 Senate vote and the rescissions.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262e): LGBTQ rights, 2015 to 2026. Stonewall made a monument, the marriage ruling challenged, and the 2025-2026 federal actions on transgender Americans
- **Stonewall National Monument: Proclamation 9465 of 24 June 2016** by President Barack Obama, 81 Fed. Reg. (29 June 2016), full text from federalregister.gov, fetched 2026-09-27. It covers Christopher Park, "an approximately 0.12-acre triangle," and recounts "In the early morning hours of June 28, 1969, a riot broke out in response to a police raid on the Stonewall" Inn. It also records that on 26 June 2015, "within moments of the issuance of the" *Obergefell* ruling, LGBT people gathered in Christopher Park. **This settles the date the era-9 checkpoint flagged as search-summary-only.**
- **February 2025: the National Park Service removed "transgender" and "queer" from the monument's website** (ABC7 New York, 14 February 2025, fetched 2026-09-27), after President Trump's executive orders on "gender ideology." The home page, checked on nps.gov on 2026-09-27, reads: "Before the 1960s, almost everything about living authentically as a lesbian, a bisexual person or a gay man was illegal." Earlier wording, per CBS New York and ABC7: "lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, or queer (LGBTQ+)" (the earlier wording itself is search summary only).
- **Executive Order 14168, "Defending Women From Gender Ideology Extremism and Restoring Biological Truth to the Federal Government," signed 20 January 2025**, 90 Fed. Reg. 8615 (federalregister.gov API, fetched 2026-09-27). **EO 14183, "Prioritizing Military Excellence and Readiness," signed 27 January 2025**, 90 Fed. Reg. 8757. **EO 14201, "Keeping Men Out of Women's Sports," signed 5 February 2025**, 90 Fed. Reg. 9279.
- **The military ban.** SCOTUSblog, May 2025 (scotusblog.com, fetched 2026-09-27): the Defense Department issued the ban on 26 February 2025. It "generally disqualifies anyone who either has gender dysphoria or has undergone medical interventions to treat gender dysphoria." Commander Emily Shilling, a naval aviator for nearly two decades, led the suit. District Judge Benjamin Settle blocked the ban. **On 6 May 2025 the Supreme Court, in an unsigned order in *United States v. Shilling*, let it take effect**; Justices Sotomayor, Kagan and Jackson would have refused.
- **Passports.** *Trump v. Orr*, No. 25A319, **6 November 2025** (slip opinion, supremecourt.gov/opinions/25pdf/25a319_i4dj.pdf, read locally 2026-09-27): the Court stayed a Massachusetts district court's injunction, so the policy "requiring all new passports to display an individual's biological sex at birth" took effect: "Displaying passport holders' sex at birth no more offends equal protection principles than displaying their country of birth." Justice Jackson dissented, joined by Sotomayor and Kagan (search summary; the PDF's page 2 header confirms Jackson's dissent).
- **Conversion therapy.** *Chiles v. Salazar*, No. 24-539, argued 7 October 2025, **decided 31 March 2026** (LII, law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/24-539, fetched 2026-09-27). Colorado's 2019 law barred licensed counselors from "conversion therapy" with minors, defined as "any practice or treatment ... that attempts ... to change an individual's sexual orientation or gender identity." Kaley Chiles, a licensed counselor who uses only talk therapy, sued. **Justice Gorsuch wrote for eight justices that the law, as applied to her talk therapy, regulates speech by viewpoint; the case went back to the lower courts. Justice Kagan concurred with Sotomayor. Justice Jackson dissented alone.**
- **The marriage ruling challenged and left standing.** Kim Davis, the former Kentucky county clerk who refused marriage licences to same-sex couples after *Obergefell*, asked the Court to reconsider *Obergefell* in *Davis v. Ermold*, No. 25-125, after the Sixth Circuit upheld damages against her. **"Nov 10 2025 Petition DENIED"** (supremecourt.gov docket 25-125, fetched 2026-09-27). ABC News, 10 November 2025 (abcnews.com, fetched 2026-09-28): Davis "refused to issue a marriage license to a gay couple on religious grounds in open defiance of the high court's ruling and was subsequently jailed for six days. A jury later awarded the couple emotional damages plus $260,000 for attorneys fees." The couple were David Ermold and David Moore. The petition sought to overturn a "$100,000 damages verdict" and was "the first since 2015 to formally ask the court to overturn" *Obergefell*.
- **STATUS, 27 September 2026:** same-sex marriage is legal in every state under *Obergefell* and the 2022 Respect for Marriage Act. The 2025 executive orders, the military ban and the passport rule are in force.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262e): voting rights, 2021 to 2026. The bill that failed and the maps redrawn after Callais
- **The John Lewis bill.** H.R. 5746, whose titles include "Freedom to Vote: John R. Lewis Act" and "John R. Lewis Voting Rights Advancement Act of 2021" (govinfo BILLSTATUS-117hr5746.xml, fetched 2026-09-27). The Advancement Act would have written a new coverage formula to replace the one struck down in *Shelby County*. The CRS summary of H.R. 4 (117th Congress), the same Act as a stand-alone bill: "This bill establishes new criteria for determining which states and political subdivisions must obtain preclearance before changes to voting practices may take effect," for example a state with "15 or more voting rights violations" in the previous 25 years would be covered for 10 years (govinfo BILLSTATUS-117hr4.xml, fetched 2026-09-28). **On 19 January 2022 the Senate vote to end debate failed, 49 to 51** (senate.gov roll call vote 9, 117th Congress, 2nd session: "Vote Result: Cloture Motion Rejected," fetched 2026-09-27). A second vote that night, 52-48, kept the Senate's 60-vote rule in place (roll call vote 10, "Decision of Chair Sustained").
- **After *Callais*, Louisiana.** NBC News, Jane C. Timm, late May 2026 (nbcnews.com, fetched 2026-09-27): Louisiana's Republican legislature passed a new congressional map "eliminating one of the state's two majority-Black districts," expected to elect five Republicans and one Democrat. A third of Louisiana's people are Black. State Senator Jay Morris wrote the bill. State Representative Beau Beaullieu: "We focused on the Democrat numbers, not the racial numbers when drawing." Democratic State Representative Kyle Green Jr.: the map takes Black Louisianians "from 33% of the population to 16% of the representation." Louisiana delayed its House primaries set for 16 May 2026 and threw out "some 40,000 votes" already cast.
- **Other states.** Tennessee eliminated its majority-Black Memphis district after *Callais*, and the governors of Georgia, Mississippi and Alabama signalled similar plans (unconfirmed: search summary only). South Carolina Republicans ended a redistricting bill in early June 2026 (NBC News).
- **STATUS, 27 September 2026:** Congress has passed no new coverage formula and no new Section 2 standard. The outline's 9 September line still holds.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262e): Black Lives Matter, what the movement won and lost, 2012 to 2026, and the line with `crime-justice`
- **The line between the chapters.** `crime-justice` era 10 ("Police killings and what happened in court") tells the cases: Michael Brown, Eric Garner, Tamir Rice, Breonna Taylor, George Floyd with Chauvin's state and federal sentences and the other three officers, Tyre Nichols. It names this chapter as lead on "the protests, Black Lives Matter, and Chauvin's admitted facts." **So this chapter tells the movement and the protests and does not repeat the verdicts and sentences.** The crime-justice parked note (Chauvin convicted 20 April 2021, 22.5 years on 25 June 2021) closes the gap 10.7 left open; the outline here needs no sentence number.
- **Trayvon Martin.** Britannica, "Shooting of Trayvon Martin" (britannica.com, fetched 2026-09-27): George Zimmerman, "a neighborhood-watch volunteer," shot and killed Martin, "a 17-year-old African American" who "was returning from a convenience store," in Sanford, Florida, on **26 February 2012**. "Martin was unarmed." The Sanford police chief said on 12 March 2012 that no charge could be filed. After nationwide demonstrations, Governor Rick Scott appointed a special prosecutor in April 2012, who charged second-degree murder. **The jury found Zimmerman not guilty on 13 July 2013.** The Justice Department announced in 2015 that it would not bring federal hate-crime charges. Protests after the verdict "led to the formation of the prominent Black Lives Matter social movement." **This closes item 6 of the era-10 log.**
- **The federal bill.** H.R. 1280, the George Floyd Justice in Policing Act, **passed the House 220 to 212 on 3 March 2021** (roll call 60) and was received in the Senate on 9 March 2021 (govinfo BILLSTATUS-117hr1280.xml, fetched 2026-09-27). No Senate vote followed in that record. It did not become law.
- **The federal oversight, begun and ended.** NPR, 21 May 2025 (npr.org, fetched 2026-09-27): Assistant Attorney General for Civil Rights **Harmeet Dhillon announced that the Justice Department would dismiss its lawsuits against the Minneapolis and Louisville police departments**, close the investigations and retract the earlier findings: "Today, we are ending the Biden Civil Rights Division's failed experiment of handcuffing local leaders and police departments with factually unjustified consent decrees." A consent decree is a court-enforced agreement to change how a department works. Dhillon said Minneapolis was already under a court-ordered plan with the Minnesota Department of Human Rights and that Louisville had agreed to hire its own monitor. The Division also closed investigations and retracted findings for Phoenix, Trenton, Memphis, Mount Vernon (New York), Oklahoma City and the Louisiana State Police (Washington Post and others, search summary only).
- **How big 2020 was.** Harvard Ash Center (fetched 2026-09-27): Crowd Counting Consortium data "underlie an influential New York Times analysis demonstrating that, at that time, protests in 2020 following George Floyd's murder constituted the largest and broadest mobilization in U.S. history," and "support the claim that the 2020 Black Lives Matter uprising was overwhelmingly peaceful, especially relative to the heavy-handed police response." The Pew 6 percent figure (10.8) stays the outline's count.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262e): disability, 2026. The federal government drops the integration rule
DREDF (Disability Rights Education and Defense Fund, an advocacy group), "Texas v. Kennedy (formerly Texas v. Becerra)" (dredf.org/protect-504, fetched 2026-09-27), and Disability Belongs, September 2026 (disabilitybelongs.org, fetched 2026-09-27):
- **September 2024:** 17 states (Texas, Alaska, Alabama, Arkansas, Florida, Georgia, Indiana, Iowa, Kansas, Louisiana, Missouri, Montana, Nebraska, South Carolina, South Dakota, Utah, West Virginia) sued the federal government over the 2024 Section 504 rules. Section 504 of the 1973 Rehabilitation Act bars disability discrimination by programs that take federal money.
- **25 January 2026:** nine states filed a new complaint, *Texas v. Kennedy*, aimed at the rules' "most integrated setting" provision, the right to get services at home or in the community rather than in an institution. Indiana (1 May), South Dakota (12 May), Kansas (10 June) and Missouri (20 July 2026) dropped out.
- **18 June 2026:** the Justice Department's Office of Legal Counsel issued an opinion saying that federal law, including Section 504 and Title II of the ADA, **"does not include an integration mandate"** (DREDF's summary). That reverses the position the Justice Department took in its Olmstead enforcement, including the 2022 Colorado letter at 10.10.
- **31 August 2026:** the Justice Department, for HHS, joined the five remaining states (Texas, Alaska, Louisiana, Montana, Florida) in asking the judge to strike the integration provisions. **23 September 2026: the judge granted it**, vacating the definition of "most integrated setting" and 45 C.F.R. § 84.76 nationwide, and closed the case. DREDF: "The law itself has not changed ... Section 504, the ADA, and Olmstead remain the law." (The judge is not named in the pages fetched.)
- **For the outline:** the line "the Justice Department is still writing to governors about it" is out of date as of June 2026 and is rewritten.

### PATCH 2026-09-27 (T-262e): era-10 story checks (jim-obergefell, tarana-burke, alice-wong)
- **jim-obergefell.** The cases joined with his came from **Michigan, Kentucky, Ohio and Tennessee**, and the petitioners were "14 same-sex couples and two men whose same-sex partners are deceased" (LII syllabus and opinion, 14-556, fetched 2026-09-27). The outline's "Arthur could not sit up or walk" goes beyond the Court's words, "It was difficult for Arthur to move." Rewritten to the Court's words. "Hired" a plane is not in the opinion. Removed.

- **alice-wong.** Every outline fact is in 10.10(d). No change.

- **Firsts.** "the first federal law against lynching" rests on House Report 117-251 (10.9), which says none of about 200 earlier bills became law. Kept. "the largest count anyone had ever put on this" (the Pew #MeToo figure) is unsourced. Removed from the outline.
